FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1968 WEDNESDAY, MARCH 13, 1968 UNITED STATES SENATE, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN RELATIONS, Washington, D.C. 

The committee met, pursuant to recess at 10:05 a.m.

, in room 318, Old Senate Office Building, Senator John Sparkman, presiding. 

Present: Senators Sparkman, Lausche, Symington, Pell, Hickenlooper, Aiken, Williams, Mundt, and Cooper. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Let the committee come to order, please.

We anticipate other Senators being able to attend, but I think we had better start without them.

The committee's hearings on the foreign aid bill, S. 3091, will be continued this morning with the Honorable William S. Gaud, Administrator of the Agency for International Development, as a witness. 

Mr. Gaud, we are glad to have you back with us today. 

I want to change the order of questioning because Senator Symington must go to a meeting of the Preparedness Subcommittee of the Armed Services Committee. He has some questions he particularly would like to ask, so I will recognize Senator Symington at this time. 

Senator SYMINGTON. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Administrator, it is a privilege to see you again. 

Mr. GAUD. Thank you, Senator.

AID PROGRAMS TRANSFERRED TO DEFENSE DEPARTMENT Senator SYMINGTON. In accordance with the Chairman's approval, I would ask for the following figures: Secretary of Defense 

McNamara testified before us last January that several of the programs previously under AID, your organization, have been transferred to the Defense Department. I would appreciate your listing what those are, for the record, how much they were when you handled them, and what are the programs remaining in South Vietnam that AID is conducting, detailed to the extent you think would be of interest. You and I have worked together on these things before. I would appreciate your putting in the detail you feel advisable.

DEPARTMENT OF DEFENSE FUNDING OF FORMER AID PROJECTS 

During the Fall of 1966, AID and DOD agreed that certain of AID's activities directly related to the military effort were properly a DOD responsibility. Funding arrangements were made during FY 67 to suit different situations and the extent of DOD responsibility. 

A. Two AID activities were turned entirely over to DOD for full FY 67 and future funding management and programming responsibility.

D. AID and DOD each fund 50 percent of the cost of medical supplies under 

AID's Health Logistic Support project. These medical supplies are drawn in largest part from DOD stocks, with AID reimbursing DOD for one-half the cost. DOD reimbursed AID for one-half of the cost of medical supplies secured elsewhere by AID with AID funds ($1,350,000 in FY 67).

E. During FY 1967. AID and DOD also had two funding arrangements which were not continued into FY 1968: (1) Saigon Electrification: DOD reimbursed $9.4 million to AID as the war related share of AID's FY 1967 obligations to meet emergency replacement requirements and necessary increases in Saigon electrical power. AID is continuing to fund without DOD participation, a project to improve Saigon electric power over the longer term. (2) Vietnam television: AID and USIA support a project to improve the information services of the GVN. DOD agreed to fund one part of this projectto improve Vietnamese television for psychological warfare purposes-and reimbursed $473,000 to AID for this FY 1967. Beginning in FY 1968 funding for assistance to Vietnamese television is by USIA and DOD with AID participating only in other portions of the information services project.

237 In all, DOD reimbursed $43,147,000 to AID under the arrangements described above during FY 1967. 

In FY '69, the arrangements under B, C, and D above, are expected to continue, with AID and DOD each requesting its share of the funding under separate appropriations. DOD also has agreed to fund fully three additional military-related AID activities in FY 1969.

AID PROGRAM IN VIETNAM 

The AID Program in Vietnam proposed for FY 1969 is concentrated under four major goals: 1) Economic Stabilization; 2) War Support and Relief; 3)

Support of Pacification; and 4) National Development, as discussed in the printed presentation to the Congress.

Following is a listing of the projects and programs, by functional sector, showing estimated FY 1968 and proposed FY 1969 funding. This listing excludes projects which have been transferred to the Department of Defense for funding. It should be noted that these estimates were prepared prior to the Viet Cong counter-offensive begun during the Tet holiday. Though some adjustments have been made to meet emergency conditions arising from the attacks, it is anticipated that additional substantial changes will be made.

Agricultural general support... 

4,849 4,987 The agriculture program has been re-oriented to place the primary emphasis of most projects on supporting the GVN rice production program, which if security conditions permit, promises to bring Vietnam to self-sufficiency in 1971. Production of protein foods is a secondary priority. Agricultural credit organizations, cooperatives, and the GVN agriculture statistical service are also being aided because of their importance in supporting the rice and protein production efforts. Assistance to the GVN's land reform program is provided through senior advisors to the Government and consultants working on policy and program matters.

[In thousands of dollars] Fiscal year 1968 estimated Fiscal year 1969 proposed 10,045 13, 106 3,676 3,540 1,335 2,093 366 1,353 400 893 2,517 2,490 177 426 809 1,700 765 611 The principal emphasis of our assistance to the education sector is the maintenance and expansion of primary education through classroom construction, teacher training and textbooks production and distribution. The current goal is to enroll about 85% of the elementary school-age population in schools by 1970, compared to the current 66%. A system of junior vocational, agricultural, and technical schools is also receiving assistance to increase the supply of trained manpower. AID assistance to secondary education is directed at increasing the supply of secondary teachers and limited classroom construction. 

Assistance to higher education concentrates on faculties of Pedagogy, Agriculture, and Medicine. Approximately one hundred students with leadership potential are selected annually for degree training in the U.S.

AID and DOD are expending a massive effort to improve and expand hospital and health facilities and to furnish the physicians and supplies needed to meet the emergency. Assistance to the urban sector has become especially critical since the recent Viet Cong attacks on the cities. Sector activities include support of the GVN emergency reconstruction programs now being launched by the GVN. The total magnitude of assistance required for this effort is not yet known, and may require the shifting of additional resources from other programs. Two major long-term capital investment projects are being financed in the Saigon Metropolitan area: a project for expansion of electric power generating and distribution capacity of 350 MW by the end of 1971; and provision of an effective potable water supply system. A water supply system for the city of DaNang is planned for initiation in FY 1969. Interim improvement of power and water facilities in a large number of provincial cities is also being carried out, together with assistance in fire protection, sanitary services, and street repair. Advisors in municipal administration are coordinating such assistance and helping local governments mobilize their resources. Total, other...….

Youth Affairs.-AID provides personnel, training equipment, and construction materials to a number of youth programs of a community development nature.

Village and Local Development.-AID is conducting a pilot program in several villages to strengthen local self-government by giving advice and financial support to local initiatives. It is proposing a new project under this title in FY 69.

AID recruits and funds personnel to work in the MAC/V Civilian Operations and Revolutionary Development Support Organization (CORDS) as province and district managers, provincial assistants, communications and logistics technicians, and staff officers at the regional and Saigon levels. Other advisors provided to CORDS work with the provincial government efforts to provide logistics support to the Revolutionary Development program and to administer its self-help project program, AID also provides advisors to CORDS to work with economic and social development programs for Highlanders and with the Chieu Hoi ("Open Arms" returnee) program. Financial responsibility for the latter program will be transferred to DOD in FY 1969. AID also funds commodities required by those CORDS programs.

[In thousands of dollars] 

Fiscal year 1968 estimated 

Fiscal year 1969 proposed 18, 331 22,547 17,688 21,612 643 935 This program provides relief assistance to refugees and support in reintegrating them into Vietnamese society. AID will provide food, clothing, education, skills training and personnel to work with GVN Ministry and refugee camp officials. The recent VC attacks will probably require substantial increases in funding. It is in this program that the many voluntary agencies are making a substantial contribution in relief and resettlement.

Social Welfare. The main emphasis of this project is to assist displaced and other needy persons who are not benefited by the refugee program. AID will provide three technical advisors at the national level, and will support a contract which provides approximately 47 health and welfare specialists to staff teams which will demonstrate and help establish critically needed services.

The Public Safety Programs assist the Vietnamese National Police in main taining law and order in urban and pacified rural areas and in collecting and acting upon information about the enemy. AID provides advisors and commodities for a variety of police programs, a telecommunication project and a prison rehabilitation program. It also furnishes substantial amounts of equipment.

The GVN plans an overall increase in police manpower from 73,371 in 1967 to about 114,000 in 1969.

THE COMMERCIAL IMPORT PROGRAM 

In addition to the project program described above, AID provides funds to meet the foreign exchange costs of goods imported into Vietnam under the Commercial Import Program (CIP). This program, designed to control runaway inflation, supply needed commodities and generate local currency, has proven successful in reducing inflation from 68% in 1966 to 34% in 1967.

Mr. GAUD. Mr. Chairman, I am here, as you said, to support the 

President's request for economic assistance under the Foreign As sistance Act. 

OUTLINE OF PROPOSAL 

For fiscal year 1969, the President is requesting $2.498 billion for economic aid-$132 million less than requested last year. 

This is a tight budget tailored to fit a tight situation. Particularly in view of the cuts in the fiscal year 1968 budget, we believe it is the minimum necessary to maintain the development momentum now underway in the developing world. 

This year for the first time we are presenting our proposed funding levels for each country on an unclassified basis. All of the economic aid figures for each country are public, they are not classified. 

This entails some risks. But we decided to adopt this course in order to permit a fuller and franker discussion of our proposals and I would like to add here if I may, Mr. Chairman, this year for the first time we have prepared a presentation book-this very large volume which is prepared every year-in an unclassified version, and a copy of this will be given to every Member of the Congress, so that all Members of the Congress will be able to study our program in their offices and not have to go to the committee rooms to look at the material.

We propose programs in 55 countries for fiscal year 1969 compared with 64 in fiscal year 1968. In addition, some 13 African countries will be eligible for small self-help funds. 

Most of the funds requested will be spent in a relatively few countries. The following 15 will get nearly 90 percent of all country program funds and these countries in alphabetical order are: Bolivia, Brazil, Chile, Colombia, Dominican Republic, India, Indonesia, Korea, Laos, Pakistan, Peru, Thailand, Tunisia, Turkey, and Vietnam.

Eight of these fifteen countries India, Pakistan, Turkey, Korea, Indonesia, Brazil, Colombia, and Chile-are scheduled to get more than four-fifths of development loan country programs. Four of them-Vietnam, Korea, Laos, and Thailand-are expected to get 95 percent of all supporting assistance.

Development loans DEVELOPMENT LOAN REQUEST 

Let me say a word about our development loan request. The President is requesting $765 million for development loans $9 million less than last year's appropriation request. This amount plus repayments

on prior loans and estimated deobligations will fund a development loan program of $850 million. There is a table here as you see which shows where that $850 million is scheduled; $660 million of it to 

India, Pakistan, and Turkey, with a very small amount to Jordan; $100 million to East Asia-Indonesia, Korea, and the Philippines; and $90 million to Africa, mainly concentrated in three countries, Tunisia, Ghana, and Morocco a very highly concentrated development loan program.

Three of these countries India, Pakistan, and Turkey-are scheduled to receive three-quarters of all of our development loan funds. 

Nearly half the people whom AID seeks to help live in these three countries.

As you know, our assistance to each of them is channeled through multilateral consortia, led by the World Bank for India and Pakistan and by the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development 

(OECD) for Turkey. Through these consortia AID works with other donor countries and international aid institutions to focus influence and assistance, to support development programs effectively, and to encourage needed self-help. The countries themselves provide the bulk of development investment. Turkey, for example, puts up 92 percent invested in development, India puts up 78 percent and in Pakistan, 64 percent comes from the taxes and savings of these peoples themselves. 

The rest of the world puts the balance of external assistance and the United States share is somewhat less than one-half of the total outside aid which is going to these three countries. 

INDIA 

India, spurred by a record food grain crop which is now estimated at 100 million tons or more, by sound agricultural policies, and by a more pragmatic approach to current economic problems and longer range development planning, has the opportunity to break through to a significantly higher growth rate than in the past. It will take more outside aid than we can provide in fiscal year 1968 (only $236 million in development loans) to achieve this breakthrough. We propose to make available $408 million in development loans: $200 million for imports of fertilizers from the United States, and let me say, India will purchase another $200 million worth of fertilizer with other funds; $20 million for imports of better seeds and pesticides; $23 million for development projects, including $15 million to le help finance a new fertilizer plant; 

Pod $165 million for raw materials, spare parts, and equipment 2301 needed for industrial production-mostly in the private sector.

Expanded industrial production is needed to meet increased demand for goods generated by this year's bumper crop. Both sides of the economy-industry and agriculture-must expand if India is to move to a higher growth rate. GNP growth is expected to reach 

9 percent this year. Without these imports covered by our development loans and without those provided by other donors-India's economy will fall back toward stagnation. The opportunity for her to move forward will be lost.

PAKISTAN AND TURKEY 

Pakistan is making steady development progress. The development budget has been increased, and agricultural progress is at the point where self-sufficiency in food grains looks only a year away. A vigorous family planning program is under way, and private investment is steadily expanding. We propose $177 million for development loans. Nonproject agricultural and industrial production loans of $140 million will support:

Increased use of fertilizer-only 30,000 nutrient tons were used in 1960, but more than 420,000 tons will be needed in fiscal year 1969;

Pakistan's trade reform program-raw materials and spare parts are needed by private firms to increase production above its current level of 60 percent of capacity. $37 million in project loans are planned for agriculture, water development, and transportation.

Turkey is making good use of aid. Her GNP has grown on an average of 6 percent a year since 1962, industrial output has grown 9 percent a year, and food production is growing faster than population. 

By the early 1970s, Turkey plans to reach the point where she will no longer need concessional economic aid. We believe that $60 million is a minimum development loan level for fiscal year 1969 to keep 

Turkey moving.

EAST ASIA 

Substantial development loan programs are contemplated for two countries in east Asia-Indonesia and Korea. As you know, Mr. 

Chairman, the United States has agreed, subject to appropriation of funds, to help meet Indonesia's requirements as determined by the seven-nation Inter-Governmental Group, the IMF, and the World Bank, provided the other donors do their part. We estimate that $55 million in development loans, plus Public Law 480 sales, will be needed in fiscal year 1969.

In the past few years Korea has been making excellent progress in agriculture, in industry, in education, and in reducing population growth. GNP growth has averaged 8.5 percent a year since 1961. We believe $40 million in development loans is essential in fiscal year 1969 to enable Korea to maintain her present momentum and capitalize on the remarkable progress she has made to date. 

AFRICA 

Appropriation cuts in the current fiscal year have sharply reduced the level of development loans for Africa-from $98 million in fiscal year 1967 to $52 million in fiscal year 1968. More than this latter

amount is needed in fiscal year 1969 to support regional development efforts, help the reform Government of Ghana and support Tunisia's strong development effort. We propose a total of $90 million for development loans for Africa for fiscal year 1969. 

Of this more than $34 million is proposed for loans to support regional development. In Ghana, a $15 million loan will finance goods for industry and agriculture as part of a multidonor effort led by the IMF to help restore economic stability. Just over $20 million is proposed for Tunisia, including a $10 million loan for import of agricultural and industrial commodities and a $5 million loan for ground water development. AID assistance to Tunisia is provided in coordination with the consultative group led by the World Bank. 

TECHNICAL ASSISTANCE 

Technical assistance.-The President requests $235 million for technical assistance in fiscal year 1969-$8 million less than last year's request. With recoveries, it will fund a program of $253 million. 

All developing countries, as the members of this committee know, need technical assistance to provide skills, develop trained manpower, build basic institutions, and prepare for capital investment. 

The deep fiscal year 1968 cut in technical assistance (from $243 million to $180 million) has largely restricted us to continuing ongoing activities. In fiscal year 1969 we need to do more in institutional grants, in research, and to speed progress in agriculture, family planning, education, and other fields.

In Africa, $80 million is needed to train teachers, provide skilled manpower, and expand regional development activities in response to African initiatives.

In the Near East and south Asia, $46 million is needed for population programs, for secondary and higher education, and to help governments carry on priority programs in agriculture. 

In East Asia, $63 million is needed to spread rural development, education and health programs, and to assist Asian initiatives for regional development. 

SUPPORTING ASSISTANCE 

Supporting assistance.-We are requesting $595 million for supporting assistance. This will enable us to carry out a $629.5 million program. Programs are planned for seven countries: Vietnam Thailand Laos [In millions] $480. 0 50.0 39.0 25.0 20.0 8.0 2.5 Korea 

Dominican Republic 

Congo (K) Haiti

For Vietnam, $480 million is proposed. The program was planned before the Vietcong offensive which began January 30. In the months to come, changes in scope and composition may well be required as the situation develops.

For other countries, countries other than Vietnam, proposed programs total $144 million-compared to $653 million for fiscal year 1961 for countries other than Vietnam. 

In Korea, we propose $25 million for fiscal year 1969 to help that nation shoulder her large defense burden. The proposed program will continue the steady reductions from the fiscal year 1960 level of $195 million. In Laos ($39 million) and Thailand ($50 million) supporting assistance is needed to help maintain internal security and promote rural development. In the Dominican Republic $20 million in supporting assistance loans will be used to attack unemployment and support fiscal reforms.

ALLIANCE FOR PROGRESS 

The Alliance for Progress.-The President is requesting $625 million for the Alliance for Progress, $110 million of which is for technical assistance grants. With recoveries and receipts, this will fund a program of $685 million. About 70 percent of these funds are planned for Brazil, Chile, Colombia, and Central America.

Brazil is making progress in containing inflation and resuming economic growth. In 1967 real GNP growth exceeded 5 percent for the first time in 5 years. AID assistance for fiscal year 1969 is planned at $215 million. A $75 million program loan will support continuing economic stabilization efforts. Sector loans in agriculture and education will total $60 million. The agricultural loan will provide credit to help farmers invest in on-farm storage facilities, fertilizers, seed, and pesticides needed to increase earnings and production. The loan for education will help finance the beginning of a free secondary school system. 

Chile, under the administration of President Frei, has achieved an annual per capita growth rate of nearly 2.5 percent-the Alliance target.

For fiscal year 1969, $83 million is planned. A $35 million program loan will support the economic stabilization program. Over the past 3 years, inflation has been cut in half to about 20 percent; $30 million is needed for sector loans in agriculture and education. 

In Colombia, we plan a $95 million program in fiscal year 1969. 

The Colombian economy has been hurt by falling coffee prices. The resulting drop in her foreign exchange earnings threatens to choke off growth. Sixty-five million dollars in program lending will finance the 

U.S. goods and raw materials that Colombia's private sector needs to grow. A $15 million agricultural sector loan will provide fertilizer and machinery to help Colombia boost food production. A $10 million education sector loan will begin an attack on the secondary education bottleneck which now denies educational opportunity to Colombia's people.

CENTRAL AMERICAN ECONOMIC INTEGRATION 

Central America has made rapid strides toward economic integration in the last few years. But the increase in traditional exports which spurred growth has stopped, and now the Central American governments must expand their investment and raise more revenue. For fiscal year 1969, $15 million in Alliance technical assistance and $68 million in Alliance loans are planned. The keystone of the program is a $25 million loan to the Central American Integration Fund to continue to build physical links among the Central American nations. The World 91-392-68-pt. 2- -2

Bank and the Inter-American Development Bank are also expected to provide substantial assistance to Central America. 

OTHER REQUESTS 

Other requests.-The President is also requesting in the bill that is before you: $154 million for voluntary contributions to international organizations; $100 million in authorization for the contingency fund, although the appropriation request is only for $45 million; $58.8 million for AID administrative expenses; and $20.5 million for American sponsored schools and hospitals abroad, investment surveys, and administrative expenses, State Department.

INCREASED FOOD PRODUCTION 

Increased food production. Mr. Chairman, may I say I would like to talk a few minutes to the two problems to which we in AID give top priority in our programs. Yesterday and the day before, this committee addressed what is certainly the most important problem before the world today-how to achieve and maintain an equitable peace. 

The next most important program in the world today, as I see it, is whether the world can continue to feed itself, whether the world can maintain a balance between the demand for food and the supply of food. 

For some years the less developed nations have not been growing enough food to feed themselves. Their food deficits are met largely with the food surpluses of the developed nations. This cannot continue and it need not-and in an effort to meet this problem, as I say, we are giving top priority to helping the developing countries increase agricultural production.

The means are at hand to deal with the problem. New seeds, more fertilizer, effective pesticides and herbicides, the better use of water, farm credit, price incentives, better storage facilities, improved marketing and distribution facilities, sound Government policies-these can produce an agricultural revolution. And there are signs that—at least in Asia-we are on the verge of just that, an agricultural revolution.

For example in May 1967, Pakistan planted 600,000 acres to new high-yielding wheat seed. This spring, a year later, the farmers of 

Pakistan will harvest the new wheat from an estimated 3.5 million acres. They will bring in a total wheat crop of 72 to 8 million tons a new record. Pakistan has an excellent chance of achieving self-sufficiency in food grains in another year. 

In 1967 new high-yielding wheats were harvested from 700,000 acres in India. This year they will be planted to 6 million acres. 

Another 10 million acres will be planted to high-yield varieties of rice, sorghum and millet. India's food grain crop (100 million tons fat or thereabouts) is an alltime record. She hopes to achieve selfsufficiency in food grains in another 3 or 4 years, and has the capability to do so.

Turkey has demonstrated that she can raise yields by two and three times with new wheat varieties. Last year's crop set a new

record. In 1968 Turkey will plant the new seed to one-third of its coastal wheat growing area.

The Philippines have harvested a record rice crop wtih only 14 percent of their ricefields planted to new high-yielding seeds. 

This year more land will be planted to the new varieties. The 

Philippines are clearly about to achieve self-sufficiency in rice. 

Although it is much too early to say that the world will avert a food crisis, these encouraging results give clear evidence that this crisis can be averted.

New seeds, more fertilizer, better water management, and improved pesticides and insecticides are important weapons in the fight to achieve self-sufficiency in food production. But they will not do the job alone.

Not only must these be available to the farmer, but he must have credit to buy them, he must know how to use them properly, he must be able to get a decent price for his crop and he must be able to get it to market. 

AID TECHNICAL ASSISTANCE 

Outside technical and capital assistance are needed to accomplish these objectives in the developing countries. They can not do this job alone, and AID technical assistance helps get the news about modern methods to the farmers and helps train them in their use. It helps build agricultural extension services. It helps finance demonstrations which illustrate that better methods mean larger crops. AID development loans finance imports or production of indispensable inputs such as fertilizer, pesticides and herbicides.

In a number of countries, higher production costs of modern farming are being financed on reasonable terms through agricultural credit programs. AID technical assistance helps provide know-how to run these small banking operations. Local currency proceeds from AID loans provide some of the capital. 

Developing countries are investing more funds in irrigation and, where possible, opening up new lands for agricultural use. They are building farm-to-market roads. In some countries land reform programs are giving the farmer a larger stake in his farm and in his future. In each of these areas, AID assistance is helping to speed progress.

Since the early 1960s, the United States has presssed the developing nations to invest more in agriculture, and to adopt policies which favor and stimulate agricultural production such as price incentives; private rather than Government distribution of fertilizer; agrarian reform; and liberal import quotas on raw materials for fertilizer production.

This advice has been increasingly heeded. In a number of countries, the influence of AID program loans has helped improve Government policies with respect to agriculture. 

For example, in Pakistan, until recently, Government agencies managed outlets for fertilizer, even at the retail level. They were inadequate. AID urged that private dealers be allowed to distribute fertilizer. One of our fertilizer loans for $25 million-induced the 

Government of Pakistan to expand distribution of fertilizer by the private sector. By l

By last summer there were 10,668 private fertilizer web Lu Hf T

dealers in East Pakistan-up from 7,000 at the end of August 1966and more are being encouraged. 

The developing nations their governments, their institutions and their farmers cannot sustain a satisfactory rate of agricultural growth without outside support. They lack the skills to do the necessary adaptive research. They lack the foreign exchange to import fertilizer. They lack the capital to build fertilizer plants. They lack the facilities and the technicians needed to train their people in the new ways.

If the developing countries are to succeed in their efforts to avoid a food crisis, they can only do so as the result of a working partnership between themselves and the advanced nations. 

We believe it is right to give priority to agriculture. That we are doing so is evident from the fact that $800 million of our fiscal year 1969 budget request is intended for direct support of agriculture. But it is equally essential to stimulate general economic growth in those countries which are working to increase their agricultural production. 

Agricultural development cannot be achieved in any country if the rest of its economy is permitted to stagnate or worse still-to deteriorate. The farmer must be able to buy fertilizer. The farmer must be able to sell his product at a price which makes it worthwhile for him to grow more food. This can be achieved only through overall economic development. Now, for the next 20 years, I think most of the critics agree the principal answer to the food crisis is to grow more food in the developing countries. But this is not the answer in the long run.

POPULATION GROWTH Population Growth.-As the President's Science Advisory Commit tee has pointed out, a longrun solution to the world's food problems requires the developing countries not only to grow more food, but also to reduce the present rate of population growth. 

AID population programs are based upon four fundamental principles. First, overpopulation and underdevelopment go hand in hand. A country cannot move ahead when production gains are matched by population gains. Second, we believe that the government of every nation with a population problem, whether developed or developing, should do its utmost to increase the availability of family planning to its citizens. Our role is to assist developing countries in this task. 

Third, we must respect the sovereignty and sensibilities of the nations we assist. Family planning programs must be host nation programs-not our programs. We do not provide assistance unless we are asked to do so. Fourth, we will not support any family planning program unless it is voluntary. It must be freely chosen by the developing country, and we will not force any particular type of program on any country. Nor will we support any family planning program unless each individual is free to decide whether he or she wants to participate in it. We want no part of either international or individual coercion. 

We do not make family planning a condition of aid. 

AID's assistance in this area has increased sharply in the last few years. In fiscal year 1965 we obligated $2.5 million for population and family planning. This fiscal year we have already obligated or committed over $23 million in dollars plus over $10 million in local currency. We will fund additional projects before the end of the fiscal 4431

year, and hope to reach the $35 million figure contained in title X of the Foreign Assistance Act. As a measure of our increased attention to this subject, in 1966 there were eight people in the Agency spending all or a major part of their time on population matters, whereas today there are 52.

We are increasing support for population research. Our first two grants under section 211(d), totaling $1.4 million, will finance increased population work at the Carolina Population Center of the University of North Carolina and the Johns Hopkins Population Unit.

A substantial amount of our family planning assistance is channeled through private organizations. For example, in October 1967 we granted the International Planned Parenthood Federation $3 million for contraceptives, vehicles and other family planning supplies and for support of IPPF family planning programs in Latin America, Asia, and Africa. Last fiscal year we provided the Population Council with $775,000 for expansion of its family planning activities in East Asia and for its highly successful postpartum family planning program in 27 hospitals in 15 countries around the world. 

As is the case with food production, the direct responsibility for developing family planning programs rests squarely with the developing countries themselves. Today, some 26 developing countries have official family planning policies. A number of them, notably Pakistan, India, and Korea, are making substantial progress. 

Mr. Chairman, some people believe that development is not a pressing task for the United States, or that it can be put off for a whilethat foreign aid can wait. 

But foreign aid cannot wait. Development is not a luxury, it is a driving necessity that absorbs the attention, the energy, and the resources of more than half the people in the world. Their problems will not go away, and they will not wait until tomorrow to try to solve them.

I urge the committee to recommend full authorization of our request for fiscal year 1969. We must get on with the job of development today, if we are to have any real hope of living in a world of peace tomorrow. 

Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

We do run a chance of some embarrassment on that score, but it seemed to us that the opportunity for fuller and freer discussion of the program is a greater advantage than it is a disadvantage. 

DECREASE OF DEVELOPMENT LOANS Senator SPARKMAN. The 1968 Economic Report of the President listed average annual foreign economic and military loans and grants during fiscal years 1962 to 1966 as $6,200 million. The total for fiscal 

1967 was $6,900 million of which $5.7 billion was for loans or grants to less developed countries. 

Does this indicate that we are drawing away from the problem of the world's poor? What is the comparable estimate for fiscal 1968? 

Mr. GAUD. I am not familiar with those figures. I wonder if they include the export-import loans, Mr. Chairman? I think they must. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Yes; they do include the Export-Import Bank loans. They are given at page 310 in the Economic Report.

Mr. GAUD. The volume of our loans to the developing countries has been decreasing in recent years. For fiscal year 1967, for example, under the Foreign Assistance Act, the Congress appropriated $500 million for development loans. In fiscal year 1968 it was a still lower figure $435 million for development loans. And this, I think, is one of the most serious problems that we face in trying to carry out this program. 

We have concentrated our development loan program in a very few countries now, as I said earlier, India, Pakistan, Turkey, Jordan, Indonesia, Korea, and a few countries in Africa. This is where these loans go.

These are all countries which are working on development in a serious way. They are countries which are being helped by others. They are countries which have an opportunity to move forward from where they are at present and it is pretty hard to run a program like this on a stopand-go basis. India and Pakistan, for example, adopted new policies to increase their agricultural production, to free up their imports, to liberalize their economy, to encourage the growth of the private sector. 

If these policies are to be successful, if they are to work out, India, Pakistan, Korea, and Turkey all need outside sources of foreign exchange.

In this current year, fiscal year 1968, we have had to reduce our loans to these countries by roughly 40 percent. If we have to continue at this lower level, it will be, I think, impossible for these countries to go ahead with their development plans. It is going to lengthen the time that it will take them to achieve self-sufficiency. 

Korea and Turkey, for example, have official Government policies of trying to be free of AID assistance by 1972 or 1973. They can only do this if they continue to receive assistance from outside, and to the extent that we reduce our assistance, to the extent that any other aid donors reduce their assistance because we do and I think this is a real fear there will be a stretchout of the time that it will take these countries to achieve self-sufficiency and, in the long run, this is going to cost us more money because they will be unable for a longer period of time to get along on their own.

ARMS PROGRAMS IN INDIA AND PAKISTAN 

Senator SPARKMAN. I don't want to take any more time, but I do want to ask a question about India and Pakistan which you have mentioned two or three times. Is there any problem now as to these two countries because of their respective arms programs? 

Mr. GAUD. Do you mean the percentage of their budgets they are devoting to defense expenditures? 

Senator SPARKMAN. What they are doing in the field of increasing arms, and the impact that that has on their being able to support themselves economically.

Mr. GAUD. Right. This has been a subject of continuing concern to us, and it is a subject to which we have to give close attention and to which we do give close attention not only in the case of India and 

Pakistan, but to all other countries.

Last year, as you will remember, Mr. Chairman, section 620 (s)-the 

Symington amendment-was added to the Foreign Assistance Act which provides, in effect, that if any country is devoting an undue

proportion of its resources to military expenditures at the expense of development, the President should suspend aid to that country. 

Now, pursuant to that amendment, we have been engaged in the last 2 or 3 months in obtaining as full and as complete information as we can from all of the countries which receive our aid, looking at the amount of their defense expenditures in relation to their budget, looking at what percentage of their GNP and of their budget they are devoting to defense. Are these going up or are they going down? What are their requirements with reference to their neighbors? What is their situation? How badly do they need the defense expenditures that they are making? And in every case we have to arrive at a judgment before we can decide whether we can go ahead with aid to a country. 

Now, as far as India and Pakistan are concerned, Pakistan has been reducing her defense expenditures and reducing the percentage of her budget which is going for defense for several years now, and we have concluded on the basis of the information that is available to us, that Pakistan does not present a problem under the Symington amendment, and we have assurances from the Government that she proposes to continue this trend of devoting a larger percentage of her expenditures to development.

India did not increase her defense expenditures in real terms in any major way after the Indian-Pakistan war while the Pakistanis did. 

India did increase her expenditures very substantially after she was invaded by China in 1962. But in the last few years the expenditures for defense in India have increased some, but somewhat less than prices have increased. So that on a real basis you might say there has not been an increase, and on the basis of the information that is available to us today again it seems to us that the defense expenditures in 

India are not out of line.

MIXED FEELINGS ABOUT DECLASSIFICATION Senator HICKENLOOPER. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Gaud, your decision to declassify the amounts proposed for each country, I think, has benefits. However, you mentioned some of the disadvantages that it has. I have mixed feelings about it. For the last 15 years the committee in general has asked why these figures haven't been declassified, and I have generally followed the idea that perhaps it was a good thing not to declassify them, rather to do so on a regional basis and give some discretion in the area for the shifting of funds from one to another country, depending on conditions. 

I am not objecting to declassification. I think probably it is all right. We are demanding that everybody do, and maybe it is a good thing to do. But it is going to cause you trouble, I am sure, when a certain country reads that country X is entitled to so much money, and they begin to think they have a vested interest in it. If they don't get it, it causes more hard feelings than the money causes happiness. 

But I think perhaps it is just as well that you do declassify it. 

Mr. GAUD. I might say on that point, this is obviously a troublesome point. What we did was to send messages to all of our missions before we did this, and we talked to the ambassadors here about it. 

We asked our missions overseas to talk to the governments about it,

and we have taken such action as we can to prevent misunderstandings. There probably will be some. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Of course, one country looks at the amount the next country is getting, and says "They don't deserve it as much as we do and we are not getting enough." I can see a lot of trouble for you there. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Jealousies and things of that sort arise. I 

found out you don't make any friends by giving people money. You just make them a little anxious to get more.

U.S. SHARE IN DEVELOPMENT ASSISTANCE Senator HICKENLOOPER. Mr. Gaud, I notice what appears to be an encouraging statistic with reference to Turkey, India, and Pakistan that the bulk of development investment in Turkey will be paid 92 percent by the Turks themselves? 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Does that mean the other 8 percent will come out of this assistance?

Mr. GAUD. The other 8 percent comes from the consortium led by the OECD which provides aid to Turkey, and we put up about 45 percent, a little under 45 percent of the total amount of aid provided by that consortium.

Senator HICKENLOOPER. So roughly 4 percent would come from us? 

Mr. GAUD. That is correct.

Senator HICKENLOOPER. I notice 78 percent of their development in vestment will be paid by India. 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir.

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Does the same situation prevail there? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, our percentage of the consortium there has been roughly 40 percent.

Senator HICKENLOOPER. And 64 percent in Pakistan? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir.

SIGNIFICANCE OF LOWER PERCENTAGE PAID BY PAKISTAN Senator HICKENLOOPER. What is the significance of the lower per centage contributed by Pakistan to its own development investments? 

Mr. GAUD. It means less in taxes, less in internal savings. The percentages vary. You might say there are several factors involved. One is how well off is the country, how much taxation can it stand. 

Second, how effective a job is the country doing in terms of collecting taxes and in terms of encouraging investments on the part of its citizens.

Turkey, of course, has a much higher per capita GNP, a much higher per capita income than either India or Pakistan. The opportunities for savings and taxation are much greater, and Turkey has done a rather remarkable job in the last 3 or 4 years in increasing her tax collections. So that one would expect to see a lower percentage of domestic investment in both India and in Pakistan and as between the two, India has done more of a job in mobilizing local resources for investment than Pakistan has.

SOURCE OF INDIA'S "OTHER FUNDS" FOR FERTILIZER PURCHASES Senator HICKENLOOPER. I notice you mention that we propose to make available $408 million for development loans as follows, of which will be $200 million for fertilizer from the United States. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. India will purchase another $200 million worth with other funds? From what other funds will India purchase another $200 million worth?

Mr. GAUD. If past practice is any experience, some of that will come from countries such as England or Japan which are giving her aid. India will use some of the loans from the British, French, or 

Japanese to purchase fertilizers in those countries.

SOURCE OF RAW MATERIALS AND SPARE PARTS FOR INDIA 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. The last item there, the $165 million for raw materials, spare parts and equipment needed for industrial production, mostly in the private sector, where will those raw materials, spare parts, and equipment come from, purchased with that $165 million? 

Mr. GAUD. One hundred percent from the United States.

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Thank you. 

SUPPORTING ASSISTANCE FOR HAITI 

One thing I find a little bothersome, where you have listed under supporting assistance-well, it is a comparatively small amount—you have $2.5 million for Haiti. 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir.

Senator HICKENLOOPER. What is encouraging about Haiti in its political and economic situation?

Mr. GAUD. I am afraid there is nothing encouraging about Haiti in either its political or economic situation. The bulk of that two and a half million dollars will go to support a malaria eradication program which has been going on for a great many years and our rationale for continuing it is that Haiti is after all on the same island as the Dominican Republic in the Caribbean and we think it would be a mistake to let that program drop.

Senator HICKENLOOPER. I think the malaria eradication program is a good one; I am not opposed to it.

Mr. GAUD. Yes. But, unfortunately, I see nothing encouraging in the picture in Haiti.

Senator HICKENLOOPER. It would seem to me that any substantial economic aid proffered to Haiti might find its home some place else than with the Haitian people. 

Mr. GAUD. I think you are right.

PUBLIC LAW 480 PROGRAM IN HAITI Senator HICKENLOOPER. Isn't there another million dollars in Pub lic Law 480 for Haiti? 

Mr. GAUD. I think that is right.

Under the title, I think it is the title II program. I think that pro-

gram is carried on by one or more of the voluntary agencies in Haiti, Senator Hickenlooper.

Senator HICKENLOOPER. One of the voluntary agencies? 

 I don't know whether

Senator HICKENLOOPER. With Public Law 480 funds? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir;

title II of the Public Law 480 authorizes volun tary agencies-Church Welfare Services, Catholic Welfare Relief 

Society and American Red Cross, and others-authorizes them to carry on these programs in these countries, authorizes them to furnish food for relief of schoolchildren and mothers, this sort of business. 

There are quite a number of such programs around the world. 

SHORTCOMINGS IN CERTAIN AID PROGRAMS Senator HICKENLOOPER. One more thing that I am concerned about, as I am sure you are, regarding the aid program and that is the revelations in the news publications by individual investigators about the shortcomings in certain aid programs amounting to either malfeasance or downright corruption on the part of certain officials. I will say at the outset, Mr. Gaud, that I think you are vigorous in trying to ferret out those things and trying to correct them, so I give you whatever praise I can for vigor in that.

But I do wonder if the AID apparatus has been sufficiently vigorous in taking a lesson from the past, in the military assistance in the Point IV program, in other services abroad, where these instances of corruption have occurred with great frequency, and in failing to put enough inspectors in these places and not calling the fire department until the house has burned down. 

I have here just particularly a story by David Willis, staff correspondent of the Christian Science Monitor about the 1st of March, pointing out some of these things, and naming individuals who apparently have resigned. There are other individuals who had shortcomings, aren't there, besides the ones mentioned in the article? Do you recall this particular article?

Mr. GAUD. I think you are talking about our excess property contract in Belgium.

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Belgium and another in Japan. 

Mr. GAUD. Right. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. I don't like to get into a statement on individuals here, but have you ever prosecuted anyone for stealing 

Government money or for defrauding the Government?

Mr. GAUD. We haven't had very many cases of that in AID since 

I have been here, and I have been here since 1961. Let me say a word about that case- 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Then you haven't had a lot of cases of what you-if you don't want to call it that-call it utter stupidity and inefficiency in handling of these things.

Mr. GAUD. We have pulled a number of boners in our time, I am sorry to say.

Senator HICKENLOOPER. I guess every department has. 

Mr. GAUD. So far as I know, we have had very few cases of what I would call corruption. This is clearly a bad case and let me say a word about it, if I may, Senator Hickenlooper.

EXCESS PROPERTY CONTRACT IN BELGIUM AND OTHER COUNTRIES 

In 1964, we let a contract in Belgium to a firm to rehabilitate excess equipment which was going to go to the various developing countries after rehabilitation.

Prior to that time, worldwide, the armed services had been doing this rehabilitation work for us. In November 1964, we first went into the business ourselves directly, and we let a contract in Antwerp, Belgium. 

The invitations for bid were prepared for us by the Defense Department.

We subsequently let three other contracts of the same sort-one in Okinawa, one in Korea, and one in Japan. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Were Americans involved in the perform ance of these contracts?

Mr. GAUD. In Okinawa only, not in the other three. They were mainly to foreign firms. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. How about Belgium. 

Mr. GAUD. Belgium was a foreign firm.

Senator HICKENLOOPER. I see. 

Mr. GAUD. Now, we made what turned out to be a grievous mistake in all four of these cases. We did not, as you suggest, put enough inspectors or auditors on the ground to supervise the operations of the contractors. Two things happened. One, we were overcharged by all four of these contractors. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. That is par for the course; isn't it?

Mr. GAUD. Well, we should have caught it sooner than we did. 

Now, this was first discovered in our contract in Tokyo, and we made an investigation of that. We discovered that we were overcharged to the tune of some $59,000 and we have collected that claim in full.

When we discovered what was going on in Tokyo we started an investigation of the contract in Antwerp because that was the biggest of the four. We quickly discovered two things: One, that we were being overcharged there, that we were not supervising the contract properly and, second, it appeared to us that some of our employees had been engaged in some monkey business. We also started investigations- 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Is that another word for scullduggery?

Mr. GAUD. Sure. 

Senator MUNDT. Is scullduggery another word for stealing?

Mr. GAUD. No- Senator HICKLENLOOPER. We will get to that in a minute. 

Mr. GAUD. I will come to that in a minute. 

I will clear it up in a little bit, Senator Mundt. 

Senator MUNDT. It is still vague.

Mr. GAUD. Let me take care of the other two, the two in Okinawa and Korea first, and then I will come back to Belgium. 

We discovered no evidence of any monkey business, scullduggery, or any other term that you would like either in Tokyo, Okinawa, or in Korea, but we have filed claims- Senator HICKENLOOPER. How did they overcharge you, falsify the books?

Mr. GAUD. Yes, they charged us for work they had not done. They overcharged us by the number of hours they said they put on a particular job. They would go out and buy spare parts for $5 and charge us $10, this sort of business, a straight case of overbilling and overcharging.

Senator HICKENLOOPER. A straight case of corruption, I would think.

NO CORRUPTION BY AID EMPLOYEES 

Mr. GAUD. But the point I am trying to make, coming back to your first suggestion, so far as we can say in the case of all three there was no corruption on the part of AID employees. The contractor was overcharging us, and we did not have close enough supervision of the contract to catch it at the outset. 

We have filed claims against the Okinawa contractor and against the Korean contractor and we have withheld payments from them in an amount sufficient to cover our claims as we see them, and we are now negotiating a settlement of those claims. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. To about what do those claims amount? 

Mr. GAUD. About $100,000 in each case and $59,000 in the case of 

Tokyo which has been collected.

As far as Belgium is concerned, two points: one as I said, overcharging and overbilling. We have filed a claim for approximately $250,000 in that case and again we have withheld from the contractor payments in an amount sufficient to cover the full amount of our claims so that Uncle Sam, as we see it, will not be out any money. 

In addition, there is this question of the misbehavior of our empolyees. 

Now, there are four employees who have resigned, as you say. We called them in and we told them that as we saw it they had been guilty of misconduct. They denied this. They all resigned.

We have turned our records on this case over to the Department of Justice for such action as they see fit. They in turn have turned the papers over to the U.S. District Attorney here in the District, and, as 

I understand it, a grand jury is sitting on this thing.

I don't feel free under those circumstances to discuss the details. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. All right. 

Mr. GAUD. But I would like to say this, if I may, there have been some indications in the newspaper and elsewhere that there were kickbacks in the case, that these four men had been stealing our money.

We have seen no evidence of that. Our $250,000 worth of claims is based entirely on overcharging and excessive billing. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. I would suggest, Mr. Gaud, if this is in the hands of a grand jury that you not say much more about the particular details until there has been a resolution of this matter one way or the other.

Mr. GAUD. Right. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. It might prejudice the case on either side there.

Mr. GAUD. Exactly. 

CLOSER MANAGEMENT AND SUPERVISION URGED BY AID 

Now, the essential point, as I see it, Senator Hickenlooper, is what have we done about it and what are we doing about it. As far as the

specific cases concerned we have already advertised for, put out a new tender for, bids to get a new contractor in Europe. We have increased our staff authorizations in Belgium from one man to 11. We are putting inspectors and auditors on the job, so hopefully we will not run into that again.

In addition to that I have made considerable effort in the last few weeks to induce my staff, both here and abroad, to devote more effort to what might be called the followthrough side of our business, the management side of our business. I held a staff meeting of all of our officers that I could get in one place, some 800, and talked about the situation. I have sent out a personal message to all of my mission directors all over the world, and I am doing everything I can to impress on the AID staff the importance of closer supervision, better management. It is something that we have to be after all the time. 

REMOVAL OF INEFFICIENT AID EMPLOYEES 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Let me ask you one more question: Do you feel in some cases you are handicapped by your inability to remove incompetent or inefficient employees? 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. That has been the trouble. 

Senator MUNDT. By what, Civil Service rules? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir.

We came to you, you will remember, Senator Mundt, we came to the Congress, a couple of years back, maybe it is 3 now, possibly even 4, for authority to select out our marginal civil service employees, and we were not successful in getting that authority. We can select out our Foreign Service reserve people by going through a process of boards and the like, which is a perfectly reasonable, sensible process, I don't object to it at all. But we do not have the same authority with respect to our Civil Service employees. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Mr. Chairman, I have taken more time than I should here. I don't want to interfere with other members' time.

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir.

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir.

Mr. GAUD. That is right, sir.

Senator LAUSCHE. Senator Aiken. 

Senator AIKEN. I enjoyed listening to your statement in the quiet atmosphere of this committee room this morning, and I haven't much in the way of questions, but just one or two.

U.S. INVESTMENTS ABROAD 

Our balance of payments situation is growing more critical and if additional troops are sent to Vietnam this trend will accelerate. The 

Administration is apparently still encouraging U.S. investments in developing countries through the investment guaranty and other programs.

How much in new investments abroad were covered under the guaranty program in the last year?

Mr. GAUD. I will have to give you the figure. I am afraid I will have to put that figure in the record. I don't know it offhand. The total amount of our guaranties, Senator Aiken, was $1.9 billion. But this covered investments which will actually be made over a period of years.

Senator AIKEN. This $1.9 billion was under the guaranty program. 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir, but that cannot be taken as representing the amount of investments overseas because a man making an investment may want coverage for war risk, he may want it for expropriation, he may want it also for protection against inconvertibility. So that the dollar value of the guaranties that we issue will be far in excess of the actual investment that is made in any given case. 

Senator AIKEN. I see. 

Mr. GAUD. And I am afraid I haven't got here a figure of just what the investment was. (The information referred to follows:)

NEW INVESTMENT COVERED BY INVESTMENT GUARANTIES 

Specific risk guaranties issued in calendar year 1967 totalled $1,927,067,428. Į 

These guaranties covered an estimated $719 million in private U.S. investment.pr 

The actual investment covered is lower than the total coverage issued because investors can, and frequently do, elect to take out more than one of the available coverages on the same investment, and because coverage is available for the original investment plus up to 100% of that amount in retained earnings. prorob 

It should be noted that the actual investment is not necessarily made during the year in which the guaranty is issued. The investment may actually be made before the guaranty is issued, provided the investor has received a "waiver letter" from AID. (The "waiver letter" provides that if the guaranty application is filed within the time period authorized the investor may proceed with his investment without prejudicing consideration of his completed application on the grounds that the investment is no longer new.) Also, the guaranty may be issued to cover total investment in the project which may be made over a period of several years.

INVESTMENT GUARANTY PROGRAM 

Senator AIKEN. I believe our total foreign investment last year was nearer $5 billion, wasn't it, including the Western Hemisphere. 

Mr. GAUD. I am sorry; I just don't know. 

Senator AIKEN. Do you expect to increase this investment guaranty program this coming year? 

Mr. GAUD. Well, Senator Aiken, the President's foreign direct investment program, which he announced on January 1, allows investment in the developing countries up to 110 percent of the average investment in the base years of 1965 and 1966, so that there is a limit put on investment to that extent. 

As you probably will remember, there were three categories of countries for private investment abroad. In Western Europe and a few other countries, no investment is permitted. 91-392-68-pt. 2- -3

Senator AIKEN. There was very substantial overseas investment until 

Congress stopped it. 

Then another category of countries in which the limit, the allowable amount, is 65 percent of the base figure and then the developing countries where the target is 110 percent. 

Senator AIKEN. Yes. 

Mr. GAUD. So that the President's present policy does not contemplate a reduction in private investment in the developing countries.

The projects are a condensed milk plant, a pulp and paper mill, an oil storage and distribution facility, a vehicle assembly and repair plant and two commercial bank branches.

AID PERSONNEL LOSSES IN VIETNAM 

Senator AIKEN. Did you ever pay protection money for pacification crews? 

Mr. GAUD. Not to my knowledge. 

Senator AIKEN. You don't. 

Mr. GAUD. Not to my knowledge.

Senator AIKEN. There were no serious personnel losses, were there, in the pacification program?

Mr. GAUD. As far as AID is concerned, we lost relatively few people, but we did lose some. We have lost, all told, speaking of AID personnel-six of our employees have been captured, 30 have been killed, and 41 have been injured. Now, many of these go back several years. Of that number during the Tet offensive we had three employees captured, 11 killed, and 7 injured, and in terms of the number of people out there and the fact that many of them were living in very dangerous places-living dangerously, you might say that I think is really a fairly small percentage. 

Senator AIKEN. Would those losses come from gunfire or stepping on mines, accidentally, you might say?

Mr. GAUD. Both. 

Senator AIKEN. Driving over a mined field?

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir, that happened in some cases. There were some that were killed in Hué when the Viet Cong took over. 

CONSIDERATION OF PRIORITIES 

Senator AIKEN. I think that is a remarkable record of security that our investors in Vietnam have had, investing $141 million without a loss. I wish we could do it here.

In a speech last Friday before the Society for International Development you said: "The real answer why foreign aid is in trouble lies in the single word 'frustration,'" and after pointing out how you indicated the world is, you then said, "Vietnam is the epitome of these problems, and Vietnam has a great deal to do with the troubles in foreign aid." 

A sentence later you then said: "Disagreement with U.S. policy in 

Vietnam also serves as a pretext for opposing foreign aid. This strange mixture of apples and oranges is no basis on which to make national policy.' 

Now, as members of the Senate we have to make decisions establishing a priority list for allocation of our budget resources. Do you believe that the consequences throughout our society of this war should have no bearing on a decision on foreign aid levels? 

Mr. GAUD. No, sir.

Senator AIKEN. Should we make the foreign aid appropriations just as if there were no war?

Mr. GAUD. No, sir; I don't feel that at all. I feel that the Congress, the Senate, obviously has to consider priorities. There are a great many pressing problems that the U.S. has to deal with. 

But the point that I had in mind was perhaps best exemplified by a statement made by a member of the Senate, a few days before I made that speech, to the effect that he was not prepared to vote on the Asian Development Bank bill unless he had more assurance than he had then that we would get an honorable peace settlement in Vietnam. 

It seemed to me, when I made my speech, and it seems to me now, that the foreign aid program should be dealt with on its merits, that it should not be treated as irrelevant, that it should not be decided on the basis of one's views on Vietnam. And my plea in that speech, as you will recall, was a plea that foreign aid be looked at on its merits and be judged on its merits and not merely be shoved to one side because we have a difficult problem in Vietnam or because we have a

difficult problem somewhere else. That there should be, as you say, a weighing of the relative priorities, and it seems to me that in the minds of some there isn't a weighing of priorities, that in effect foreign aid—which I would say is an innocent bystander as far as Vietnam is concerned that the innocent bystander is being shot; plea is, don't shoot the innocent bystander.

Senator AIKEN. I think, on the whole, foreign aid has been one of our better programs in the last 20 years or so that it has been in existence. But I do think as Members of Congress we have to see how each program fits in with all the other programs. 

Mr. GAUD. No question about that.

Senator AIKEN. Domestic and foreign. 

Mr. GAUD. No question about that. 

Senator AIKEN. And that is the reason you are likely to find cuts in programs where we would rather not make cuts. But if the majority wants to make the cuts in a certain place, the cuts are made. 

Senator AIKEN. That is all, Mr. Chairman.

Senator LAUSCHE. Senator Williams?

DETECTING CASES OF ABUSE 

Senator WILLIAMS. Many of us are bothered about this sizable budget deficit-this continuous increase. Some of us get concerned with these glaring cases of abuse in the AID program to which you refer, regardless of what adjective you may use, and sometimes there is disappointment that there is not more disciplinary action taken on the part of the officials who may be responsible. 

I recognize that you may need more inspectors, but surely a competent administrator, and I am not referring to you personally, but speaking of the man in the field, administering this program, checking it, should detect these abuses and these overcharges long before they are found out. You realize that.

Mr. GAUD. The trouble here was that the man in the field was one of the men who was in trouble and who has resigned. 

Senator WILLIAMS. That is correct.

Mr. GAUD. So was his boss here at home. This was my problem. 

Senator WILLIAMS. That is correct.

Mr. GAUD. Now, as far as taking action is concerned, if I may, Senator Williams, we called these men in, presented them with the case as we saw it, and said we were going to prefer charges. They resigned. 

We cannot stop them from resigning. They have a right to resign, as you know, under the Civil Service regulations. Our Constitution forbade slavery some years back. We cannot make them continue to work for the Government when they do not want to. So they resigned. 

We could not stop them.

We referred the case to the Department of Justice. I do not know really what more we can do in a case like this than what we did. 

Senator WILLIAMS. I realize the delicacy of discussing this case, as 

Senator Hickenlooper pointed out.

LEGAL PROCEDURES UNDERTAKEN 

Did I understand you to say that the case of these four men is before a grand jury? 

Mr. GAUD. That is my understanding; yes, sir.

Senator WILLIAMS. When did they present it to the grand jury? 

Mr. GAUD. I do not know.

Senator WILLIAMS. When did you present it to the Department of Justice?

Mr. GAUD. I think I am right in saying we sent the files to the Department of Justice on the 26th of January.

 I think that was the 26th of January.

Mr. GAUD. That is correct. That was a coincidence, however, Senator Williams. 

Senator WILLIAMS. I am sure it was quite a coincidence. And the fact that they are in the Department of Justice, you do not feel free to discuss the bills with the committee until after the Department of 

Justice has acted, is that correct, sir? 

Senator WILLIAMS. Now, after Justice has acted, and if there is a question in the minds of any of the members of the committee on this, you would then make all these files available? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir. 

Senator WILLIAMS. I think that one point may be misunderstood.

I understood you to say there was no suggestion of payments to the AID employees. I do not think you meant quite clearly that. We do not want to discuss it, but I do not think, by the same token, you want the record to stand that you are either condemning or exonerating. You are not quite firm to make that statement anyway, are you?

Mr. GAUD. You are right. I made a rather narrow and precise statement, and it does not cover all the grounds. The statement that I made was that the $250,000 of claims that we are asserting against the con-

tractor are based on overcharging and excessive billing. They do not represent kickbacks to these employees. 

Senator WILLIAMS. $250,000 you are speaking of?

Mr. GAUD. Yes, our claims. Our claims against the contractor are not based at all on kickbacks but based on overcharging. I did not mean to say anything about what these individuals had done by way of misconduct. I just call it misconduct.

Senator WILLIAMS. Yes. Well, that is the point that I did not think you meant to make, a broad statement exonerating them on that point entirely. 

Mr. GAUD. No, sir.

Senator WILLIAMS. And I was fearful if it was made it would be misunderstood.

TIMING OF SENDING FILES TO JUSTICE DEPARTMENT 

I, for the moment, will let it go. I will say this, however, that while 

I accept it as a coincidence, as I understand that word, it is stretching it pretty far because I was a little disturbed that this cloak of secrecy was invoked by sending the files to the Department of Justice just 45 minutes before our meeting, but we will excuse that, and- 

Mr. GAUD. May I say a word on that?

Senator WILLIAMS. Certainly. 

Mr. GAUD. Because I feel I am distressed that you feel the way you do about it. 

As I think you already know, I left for Vietnam on the 13th of January, and I had given orders before I left that these files were to to the Department of Justice. go

It took some time to collect the files. When I got back the night before I saw you, I checked to see what the status of this particular case was, and, as I understood it, the files had not gone to the Department of Justice, which is what I told you the next morning.

I discovered subsequently that they had gone to the Department of Justice, and they had gone that very morning. But as far as putting any cloak of secrecy about this thing is concerned, before I went to Vietnam on the 13th of January I directed that these files go to the Department of Justice, which is where they belong. The action in this case can only be taken by the Department of Justice, and there was no connection between my desire to get those files to the Department of Justice and my conversations with you.

Senator WILLIAMS. I accept that as just a coincidence, but I think you can understand why the question was raised because I was discussing this for several days with the Agency. I was waiting until you returned for our meeting, but we will pass over that for the moment. 

This is but an example of what we do when we find abuses in various phases of the program, where it is admitted by you and the Administration that there is abuse in the program, we correct it by filing claims for refund. But far too often we do not see that the officials who are responsible for it are identified and removed from Government service.

DOMINICAN REPUBLIC CASE 

I refer to some of the cases that were discussed with Mr. Mansfield, the Inspector General, by the Chairman, and other members of the

committee. I have been reviewing this. We found a voucher which was paid for with foreign aid funds. I would like to know-I realize that the answer is going to be, "Well, we filed claims and this has happened in the past. 

We can only deal with the past in correcting inequities, as you realize. But somebody in your Agency, someone, not you, approved this, and I would like to know the someone, where he is and what has happened to him. 

Mr. GAUD. Which case are you referring to, Senator?

Senator WILLIAMS. I will refer to the case in the Dominican Republic on page 8 of this report. There is an item here for television sets, $8,265. Now, television sets again, $2,816. Surely we do not need those under the aid program. 

A stereo set for the Embassy down there, $786. Now, we think of these foreign aid programs as helping the underdeveloped. 

We have cars here, we have dolls' eyes, $2,305; and dolls' hair, $1,086, auto cushions, $2,672, sandwich spread, $365; Tam Tam crackers, $1,300 and $900, both together. Those are hard to rationalize to the taxpayer who is paying for all these things. Why are they buying all these things? 

Apple juice, $2,700; colored plumbing fixtures, $9,100; glasses, glass animals and candy dishes, $1,147; electric can openers and electric knives, $767. We have a lot of people in this country who do not have electric knives. 

Mr. GAUD. Can I say something about this? 

Senator WILLIAMS. Yes, I wish you would.

Mr. GAUD. In order to understand this situation, I think you have to understand how AID works. 

Senator WILLIAMS. If you can explain that, that would be good, too. 

Mr. GAUD. I sometimes think I know, but I am not always sure.

But in this particular instance we made $27 million available to the Dominican Republic at the time when we felt that their economy urgently needed support by way of foreign exchange. 

How do we operate in a situation like this which existed in the Dominican Republic? This money is put in an American bank, let us say the Chase Bank. It is used in the Dominican Republic by private businessmen to order goods from the United States. They are entitled to order certain types of goods and not other types of goods. 

A businessman in the Dominican Republic sends an order to a businessman in the United States. That order is filled. The American businessman goes, say, to the Chase Bank with shipping documents, presents them and is paid.

The goods are then shipped to the Dominican Republic. The businessman pays for them through the central bank in local currency, and he sells them in his business. 

Now, what do we do? We audit those transactions after they take place, and when we find that, as sometimes happens, the goods which have been ordered were ineligible, which was clearly the case here, we file a claim for the refund, and in this case we collected every single cent that is represented by ineligible items. 

So that, Senator Williams, no one in AID approved these purchases at any time. We financed transactions between private businessmen in the Dominican Republic and private businessmen in the United States,

and if they break the rules we go after them and get our money back. 

That is the way it works. 

Senator WILLIAMS. In other words, if you can get away with it and do not get caught, good. But you still are employing these same people and having $55 million going to the same country, and still doing business through the same bank. This was not picked up, as I understand it, and a claim filed until the Inspector General caught it and brought it to your attention. That is the point I do not understand in continuing this.

Outboard motors, bubblegum. Now, they are not items that would be in the aid program. 

Now, white sidewall tires, several thousand dollars; sherry wine, $3,430.

HOW CAN SUCH MATTERS GO UNNOTICED? 

I do not understand how these can go unnoticed until they are picked up by the Inspector General. There is surely someone in AID who has some authority of approval over these transactions, and if you do not have such a person, if you are just shoveling it out with a scoop shovel and saying, "Gentlemen, I hope you are spreading it carefully," no wonder the aid program is the way it is. I thought you exercised some control over it. 

Are you telling me they can do the same thing with $55 million and all you can do is file a claim if you catch it?

Mr. GAUD. They can buy certain items that are on the list—what we call a positive list-and these purchases are made by private businessmen in the Dominican Republic from private businessmen in the Dominican Republic from private businessmen in the United States, and we rely on an audit to see if they live up to the rules. 

The only alternative to that, Senator Williams, would be for us to inject ourselves into the picture and require or compel the Dominican Republic (a) to let no one import anything into the Dominican Republic without an import license, and then (b) to let the U.S. 

Government, AID, pass on those import licenses before they were issued.

We are trying to encourage the private sector in these countries. 

We are not trying to make them tighten up on their imports and put everything in the hands of the government. 

Now, there is another thing we do when we discover these things have happened. As of March 15 we have suspended 67 U.S. suppliers who have violated our rules. We have suspended about the same number of importers in the countries, mostly in Vietnam, who have violated our rules. We have referred 38 cases to the Department of Justice where there have been violations of our rules. We have debarred altogether 27 suppliers who have violated our rules. 

What we are trying to do is to make this money available to these countries, rely on the operation of the market, leave things in the private sector, audit the transactions, and when we find there has been monkey business, suspend the supplier or the importer, as the case may be, and get him out of the business. 

Senator WILLIAMS. Will you furnish for this committee, and have it put at this point in the record, a list of all the suppliers that were suspended as a result of these purchases, and also file with this com-

mittee a list of the items which were prohibited as being eligible under the aid program, both prior to this being called to your attention, along with the list which came after the fact. It is my understanding that your list in barring these items was published after and not before the fact. (The information referred to follows:)
