Senator WILLIAMS. I would like to read one paragraph from the report that was sent to you calling this to your attention, and quoting from the Inspector General's letter: "We came across things like the makings of a fairly complete AIDfinanced cocktail party (sherry, cocktail peanuts, cocktail napkins, and many different types of expensive glasses-champagne, wine, oldfashioned, handled whiskey, and the like) a good dinner to follow (sirloin strip, smoked Virginia ham, smoked salmon, and cheese) large amounts of toys, colored bidets, and a good deal of bubblegum." 

Now, I am sure that improved our relations with the masses of the underprivileged people in that country. I wish you would explain how the U.S. Government is financing what went into this same country when you have thousands of people who do not even have the necessities of life.

When you were speaking to the Senator from Vermont, you pointed out the program in these areas, of malaria control and various other programs, all programs which we support; we are glad to finance them. 

JUSTIFICATION FOR DOMINICAN PROGRAM 

But you do not speak of these kinds of programs, and yet these are the ones that are called to our attention, and we just cannot explain them to our constituents.

I do not understand this lady's electric razors, $76.50. We have a lot of women in America who would like to have an electric razor, but where does it come under the foreign aid program? 

Mr. GAUD. The program here was a different kind of program from the one you are speaking of. 

Senator WILLIAMS. I will agree with that.

Mr. GAUD. The purpose of making this program available was to help the Dominican Republic stabilize its economy with those supplies there for the people. There were no supplies in stores. Inflation was beginning to run rampant. The need for that economy was to have supplies.

FIRMS We put up this money to enable the businessmen in the Dominican Republic to buy goods in the United States which they could sell to their people. It was a program designed for economic stabilization. 

Now, two points: One, we agree that some things were purchased here which were not on our eligible list, and we have filed a claim and recovered for that.

Two, a lot of these things that you speak of as sounding so horrible and they do sound horrible when you put them in the context of an aid program--they were ordered by the Ambassador Hotel which

was damaged considerably in the fighting in the Dominican Republic. 

The Dominican Republic was trying to restore its economy and trying to attract tourists, and you cannot in this day and age do that without getting equipment for the bar and that kind of stuff. So they were buying that stuff for that purpose.

We did not authorize it. It was ineligible under our rules, and we filed a claim for it and got paid back. But the reason why this stuff was in here was not because anybody wanted to throw a giant cocktail party. It was because the best hotel in the Dominican Republic was trying to get back in business. 

Senator WILLIAMS. Do I understand that this $12,000 worth of television sets and about $20,000 worth of whitewall tires, $36,000 worth of outboard motors, electric razors, $76.50, and $3,400 of sherry wine was to restock the Ambassador Hotel in the Dominican Republic? 

Mr. GAUD. No.

Mr. GAUD. Not all of them.

PROPOSED CONSTRUCTION OF WATER SUPPLY TERMINAL IN SAIGON PORT 

I would like to ask you a question about the report on page 51, you have a copy of it.

Senator WILLIAMS. I would like to read from that: if In order to alleviate the water supply problem of cargo ships in the Port of Saigon, last spring the U.S. AID had airlifted into Vietnam materials to be used to construct a water supply terminal, also a self-propelled water barge was chartered from the Luzon Stevedoring Company to carry the water from the terminal to the ships. To date and I might say this is about a year later— 

AID has spent over $100,000 (at the rate of $490 per day) on the barge charter, and they had spent $15,000 to airlift $12,000 worth of imported commodities needed for construction of the supply terminal. However, no water had been delivered because the Saigon water supply authorities had not given permission for a connection to be made to its system (even though AID financed the Saigon water system.) As a result, the airlifted commodities have been held for several months in the port waterhouse, while the barge with a crew of four has been tied up at anchor and has not done a day's work since its arrival in Vietnam. 

That is about 200 days later.

We were informed because of lack of water, several ships have had to depart from Saigon without being unloaded and go to Manila and take on water. 

Now, there is a war going on, and surely we are financing all of those programs, we financed the water system, and somebody in AID knew about this, and if he did not, what kind of a party is going on over there? 

Mr. GAUD. We knew about it. 91-392-68-pt. 24

Senator WILLIAMS. You knew about it? Why didn't you do something about it?

Mr. GAUD. We knew about it and we had made arrangements with the port director to dig the well and to pipe the water to the river bank so that the barge could carry it to the ships. 

The work was going very well. In July 1, 1966, the port director was changed. His successor came in: he would not have anything to do with anything that had been done by his predecessor and he said that he did not have any budget to go ahead with the work. The work collapsed.

The result was unfortunately, as you say, that the barge was there, and it could not make a water connection. We spent several months trying to get the Vietnamese Government to do the work which would give us the water connection, they never did it. We finally had to give up.

It was a most unfortunate business, but I do not see any occasion for taking any AID man and doing anything to him for it. It was one of those situations, Senator Williams, where we could not get the cooperation of the local Vietnamese authority to put in that water connection. Most certainly it was not corruption, and it was not stupidity on the part of the AID man. 

Senator WILLIAMS. I accept the word "corruption," but I am not sure it was not correct.

You had $12,000 for the construction of this supply terminal. This program was so important that you could not wait for the normal shipping channels, and you spent $15,000 airlifting it so you could be ready for it a year before you could use it. Surely somebody endorsed this and somebody should have known about it.

Mr. GAUD. I do not know his name.

Mr. GAUD. I will try to.

Mr. GAUD. No, I do not mean that.

PROCEDURES FOR AWARDING CONTRACTS 

Senator WILLIAMS. I would like to refer you to page 49 of this report. There is considerable material here that I do want to discuss with you further. One report calls attention to the fact that contracts of $10,000 and less can be awarded apparently in the field where others have to have more supervision.

Ro

Would you describe for the committee the different procedures of awarding a contract for goods in excess of $10,000 and one for goods below $10,000?

Mr. GAUD. That I can describe. There is no difference in procedure now, Senator Williams. During fiscal year 1967 a contract or an import license for goods under $10,000 did not have to go through our small business procedure, where notice of the requirement would be given to small business throughout the United States. In this way bids would be solicited and there would be an opportunity to compare prices and the like. Whereas this sort of advertising was formerly required only if the order was for more than $10,000 in size, we have wiped out this distinction today.

To begin with, we will not as a general rule accept any transaction that is under $5,000, and everything over $5,000 must go through the small business procedure. 

Senator MUNDT. Will the Senator yield?

Senator WILLIAMS. Yes. 

WHY CONTRACT AWARD PROCEDURE WAS CHANGED 

Senator MUNDT. I wonder if, Mr. Gaud, you would not complete the record by pointing out why that procedure was changed. 

Mr. GAUD. Well, it was changed 

Senator MUNDT. I agree it is not what it used to be. But I think you should fill the record out by showing why it was changed and from where the impetus came. 

Mr. GAUD. It was changed because it was quite clearly subject to too much abuse. There were too many people who were breaking down their orders into smaller than $10,000 lots and thereby evading the regulations which now apply. 

Senator MUNDT. Was it not changed, as a matter of fact, as the result of the disclosure provided by the Senate permanent subcommittee on investigations headed by Senator John McClellan ? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, through that committee.

Senator MUNDT. I sat through the hearings, and I think it is not quite forthright on your part to imply it was changed unilaterally by your organization. It was changed because of the objection of the 

Congress in this situation. 

Senator MUNDT. I think you should be grateful for the change. 

Mr. GAUD. No question about it. 

Senator MUNDT. You should be grateful. But I think you should be forthright in telling us why it was changed. 

Senator WILLIAMS. The Senator is quite correct, and that is the point I am making. These changes were made after Congress or the public found out about the abuse, and that is what bothers us. 

Senator COOPER. Will the Senator yield? 

Senator WILLIAMS. Yes.

He had not taken them into account. (The article about Gunnar Myrdal's study referred to follows:) [From the New York Times, Mar. 11, 1968] MYRDAL FINDS THE OUTLOOK FOR SOUTH ASIA IS GLOOMY (By Peter Kihss) 

In a long, often gloomy study of South Asia and its 748 million people—a fourth of mankind-the Swedish economist Gunnar Myrdal holds that the major need is for changes in attitudes and institutions by the South Asians themselves. 

Dr. Myrdal spent 10 years studying 11 nations from India to the Philippines for the 20th Century Fund, which made his report public here. He found the masses apathetic, social and economic inequities extreme, a population growing at unprecedented rates, agriculture underdeveloped despite widespread hunger and manual labor and wage employment held in low regard.

AID A MARGINAL FACTOR 

Rather than stressing aid from the West, he called for strong campaigns for birth control by the South Asian governments, quick agricultural improvements through land reform giving incentives to the landless and to sharecroppers and tenants, and an overhaul of an education system now oriented "for poverty." "State intervention," Dr. Myrdal held, must overcome development difficulties more formidable than Western nations ever faced. He contended that South 

Asia needs more Mohandas Gandhis "who would sway the upper classes and would walk the country roads and inspire the people in their villages." 

Aid from the West, Dr. Myrdal argued, could be only a marginal factor. But, he added, even marginal aid is crucial to the destitute. He urged more-developed countries to increase the flow of capital and to help even more by preferential buying of South Asian products.

Without basic institutional changes in Southeast Asia, Dr. Myrdal foresaw increasing misery and "explosive potentialities." He downgraded any idea of widespread Communist takeover, but he held that the war in Vietnam was likely to increase Communism and racism in the area and he urged "orderly retreat" by the United States.

Dr. Myrdal's three-volume, 2,284-page study is titled “Asia Drama: An Inquiry Into the Poverty of Nations." The 20th Century Fund is publishing a hardcover edition in 4,200 copies at $25 a set, and Pantheon Books is publishing 25,- 

000 paperback copies at $8.50 a set.

The New York philanthropic foundation has contributed $250,000 since 1957 to the project, which has also been supported since 1961 by Dr. Myrdal's In stitute for International Economic Studies in Stockholm. 

Now 69 years old, Dr. Myrdal in 1944 wrote a classic study of Negro-white relations, "An American Dilemma," which contended that Government intervention would be needed to end the conflict between discrimination and American ideals. Before the new study, he served for 10 years as executive secretary of the United Nations Economic Commission for Europe. 

STUDY CALLED "DISTURBING" 

M. J. Rossant, director of the 20th Century Fund, called the study "blunt, frank and disturbing" and predicted "an important effect on development here and in Asia." The new work was not made available to governments before publication, and informal comment in Washington indicated that in any case the full impact of the study would come over the years. 

In a news conference last week at the fund office, 41 East 70th Street, Dr. 

Myrdal said, "I wanted to be undiplomatic; I'm not afraid of hurting feelings." "Illusions are a danger, opportunistic illusions are even more dangerous, and truth is wholesome," he added. "I'm not a defeatist," he insisted, "because I draw the radical conclusionsthese things must be done."

The study deals mainly with India, Pakistan, Ceylon, Burma, Thailand, Malaysia, Indonesia and the Philippines and to a lesser extent with Vietnam, Laos and Cambodia. 

During the news conference, Dr. Myrdal tried not to emphasize his views on the war in Vietnam. His suggestion of an "orderly withdrawal" by the United 

States came in response to a question. 

In his book, in which he devotes about 20 pages to the war and its background, he wrote that "for many centuries Vietnam defended itself against Chinese encroachment and sought a distinct identity." "There is no reason," he went on, "to suppose that this tradition would not be kept alive under a Communist regime-unless, of course, people felt that they were the object of a relentless attack from the West. 

SEES RACIAL ISSUE 

"To the Vietnamese people, a Communist state, intent on preserving a maximum of independence from China, could hardly be a worse alternative than a prolongation of the misery they have suffered these past 20 years." "Although Westerners, as well as the upper strata in South Asia, generally prefer to ignore it," the report says, "the racial issue is increasingly evident. "To the people in the villages and the paddyfields, the Americans coming in their helicopters and jet planes to spread fire and death are more than powerful

and dangerous strangers: they are 'white devils'—a concept with a long tradition in this part of the world.” 

Dr. Myrdal contended that "each increase in the war effort has left the situation as bad as it was in the beginning, if not worse." "The very weakness of the Saigon Government," he wrote, increases United States dependence on it. Thus the usual disadvantage of having satellites and puppet governments-that the powerful supporting government easily becomes the hostage of its client and dependent on his precarious circumstances-is one more factor in the automatic accelerating escalation of the war in Vietnam." 

URGES POPULATION BRAKE 

Surveying the needs of the South Asian region, Dr. Myrdal urged that birth control education be expanded to brake a population upsurge of 2 per cent a year between 1950 and 1960, double the rate of Europe and Asian Russia. Otherwise, he said, South Asia faces a 60 per cent increase in population between 1960 and 1980 to 1.15 billion or 1.25 billion people.

Agriculture, he wrote, is where long-term development in South Asia "will be won or lost." He called for distribution of land to the landless and to renters to give them dignity and new income. He proposed that owners not cultivating farms be subjected to penalty taxes on incomes, and that future land purchases be forbidden to them.

Without any innovations and even without any investment other than longer and more efficient work, agricultural yields could be raised very substantially," 

Dr. Myrdal said.

South Asia's exports of rice, sugar, cotton and jute have been rising sluggishly for varying reasons, he said, and prospects for tea, tin and rubber are gloomy. 

South Asia's imports, he reported, have been soaring-4.3 per cent a year between 1950 and 1960 against a 1.9 per cent annual rise in exports. The region has turned into a net importer of food since World War II. 

MARKETS NEEDED 

Dr. Myrdal urged that rich countries "initiate effective international action to stabilize their imports of primary products." He proposed that they create markets for South Asian manufactured goods, even by subsidizing imports. 

Increasing Western purchases of South Asian manufactured goods, he argued would be more stimulating than uncertain grants and burdensome loans. "The employment effects of industrialization cannot be expected to be very large for several decades ahead," he asserted, and they may even first cause a loss of jobs in older manufacturing fields.

The "gap is widening" between "lofty aspirations" and "abysmal reality" in South Asia, Dr. Myrdal wrote. He called all the countries "very poor." 

Among a number of special technical studies, one with considerable warning of error-offered estimates of "average yearly income per head," derived by weighting selected retail prices and agricultural output. This produced estimates for 1954 to 1956 in Indian rupees (then worth 21 cents each) as follows: 

Pakistan, 220; India, 260; Indonesia, 300; Burma, 300; South Vietnam, 350; 

Philippines, 380; Thailand, 400; Ceylon, 460; Malaya, 780.

LACK OF ENFORCEMENT 

All the countries, Dr. Myrdal wrote, are "soft states" in which "policies decided on are often not enforced." Changes, he said, will require "putting obligations on people and supporting them by force." 

To questioners at his news conference, he commented that the changes could be achieved within a democratic framework. "I am not talking for dictatorships," he said. Even Communist regimes, he added, have carried out "many of these compulsions not by terrorism but by education and getting people involved." 

Three-fourths of South Asia's labor force is tied up in agriculture. But Dr. 

Myrdal's report estimated that the output of agricultural land was "probably only about half as much as China or Europe."

Poor health is "a very serious deterrent" to labor efficiency throughout the region, Dr. Myrdal added.

Except in Ceylon and Malaya, he reported, "the vast majority of children in South Asia either receive no schooling at all or terminate their schooling before they have even approached functional literacy."

As to individual countries, Dr. Myrdal wrote, “ "India is ruled by a select group of upper-class citizens who use their political power to secure their privileged position."

PAKISTANI REGIME CITED 

73497 Hope for progress in Pakistan, he said, "must be attached to the present quasidictatorial regime: to its ability, despite its very narrow class basis, to advance national goals of planning, equality and consolidation and to purge the state of corruption.”

In Ceylon, he said, parliamentary democracy since the early nineteen-fifties "has operated in an irresponsible and disorderly fashion." 

Burma's officer corps, he wrote, looked to the national interest after seizing power in 1962 "without regard to the interests of the upper class of landlords, businessmen and officials to which they belonged or were closely related." "Any future government in Indonesia that wants to come to grips with its grave problems will have to be a dictatorship," he said. "The country has experienced a long period of virtual lawlessness, and even to restore a minimum of order will require firm measures." 

In Malaya he reported "great economic inequalities between the several ethnic groups and, especially, between the different classes within them." "It is the almost baronial power wielded by the landed interests," Dr. Myrdal wrote, "that largely explains why, despite its comparative prosperity, the Philippines has experienced so little industrial progress, agrarian reform and democratization of its society in depth."

In Thailand, he said, "concern with the need for internal stability and a strong central government" has provided a convenient justification for the methods by which a ruling oligarchy has maintained an unchallenged hold on the spoils of power."

He found "no fundamental efforts to modernize Cambodia or to arouse its people out of their accustomed torpor," and "no hope of peace and stability in Laos unless there is a settlement of the more bloody struggle in Vietnam." 

REQUIRING A PREAUDIT 

Senator MUNDT. Will you yield for a question?

I do not think, Mr. Gaud, we should leave the conclusion with the public that the only alternative to the kind of thing which Senator Williams is discussing in the Dominican Republic is precisely what you tell us, when you say the only other thing we can do is require a system of import licenses and that would shut off some of this. 

I think you would have to agree on the basis of the long hearings we held in the McClellan committee, where we dealt primarily with the Saigon situation where these problems are obviously, understandably more difficult than they would be in the Dominican Republic, that there are at least two things you could do and should do, it seems to me, to stop this kind of nonsense, this absolute squandering of funds that Senator Williams has mentioned, going into a place like the Dominican Republic, and it is not only there, in a peacetime area. 

You could do one or two things: You could require a preaudit instead of a postaudit, which we finally induced you to do in Saigon, which is going to help you there. It is not going to take any more staff to look at these invoices before the fact than it is after the fact. 

If you are going to do the job thoroughly it is not going to take more employees to do the job in advance than to do the job afterward. Thereby you save the money, you provide the kind of thing that they need in the Dominican Republic instead of spinning our wheels and spending our money simply recapturing funds which have been squandered without any great significance as far as building up an underdeveloped country is concerned. 

Why don't you do that?

Mr. GAUD. Well, as I see it, sir, that is obviously a way in which it could be done 

Senator MUNDT. Yes. 

Mr. GAUD. But I do not see how you are going to have a preaudit if you have it in a country without import license applications to audit. 

Senator MUNDT. You do not have to have them in the country at all. 

You are dealing with American manufacturers.

Mr. GAUD. If we do it here. 

Senator MUNDT. You are running it through an American bank.

The bank makes money, the American seller makes money, and you would simply channel your order through your shop and approve it or disapprove it in advance. It does not take a single extra employee. 

It just takes a little managerial genius and a little prudence from the standpoint of protecting the American public, as we see it. 

We got you to do it in Saigon.

Senator MUNDT. All right.

There is nothing available in the recipient country to audit. We could, as you say

VALIDATION SYSTEM AUTHORIZING BANK DISBURSEMENTS

through an American bank, before the bank puts out the money, you can say, "Mr. banker, any money you authorize the payments for which does not carry with it a stamp approved by AID in advance, you pay for." The problem is solved in 15 minutes.

Mr. GAUD. That would mean, sir, when the bank received the shipping documents they would have to come to us in Washington for an okay before they could pay out, right?

Senator MUNDT. Unless you insisted that the people who are trying to receive the goods channel their request, their requisition through you again, which is a very simple device.

PUBLISHING AN ELIGIBLE LIST OF SUPPLIES 

The other way-maybe you do not like that--you can do it by publishing an eligible list and sticking to it. You can say only this and nothing more. Here is a list. Uncle Sam will finance in the Dominican 

Republic or country X anything on this great big list of supplies. 

It would be very simple to do that, and say if there are to be any exceptions, those exceptions must be cleared in advance by AID. 

That is not cumbersome, that is very easy. 

It can be done one of two ways. But if you just sit there and tell us it is bad and nobody in it is corrupt, but the system is such that it

just happens, I think it most discouraging to those of us who have supported aid in the past. We just do not have money to throw away. 

I know the Administration is unwilling to establish a priority, as I said yesterday or the day before, between expenditures of an economic sense and expenditures for winning the war in Vietnam. But at least this Senator is not unwilling to establish a priority. Certainly we have agreed this morning as part of our responsibility, we have to determine, in light of the full picture, how much we can put out for aid. 

Senator MUNDT. And we are trying to be helpful, but you resist it at every step of the game. You did in Saigon. You yielded after we proved it. We learn as we grow. 

I am not saying that you deliberately contrived a system that prevented it, but we have to have some answers, and we have to have them in the record, Mr. Chairman, before we vote another dime for aid because it is within the realm of possibility. 

IMPOSING A PENALTY CHARGE 

Senator WILLIAMS. I would like to suggest one other method that you can enforce, too, Mr. Gaud, and I think the suggestion of the 

Senator from South Dakota has merit; I think it would work. But in the enforcement of our income tax laws, the manner in which they are enforced, is on the premise that if you or I fail to pay our taxes, the government does not just move in and collect what we owe them, they collect what we owe them plus a sizable penalty. It is the danger of that penalty being invoked that causes our voluntary reporting of income and payment of taxes. 

Now, under this system, as I understand it, the only penalty is if you get caught with buying a product which is not on the eligible list, you pay for it and even then when they pay for it, it is only reimbursement. They have had the use of our money for 6 months or 2 years while we are getting our money back, if we get it back. 

Now, couldn't you put the provision in, that if they buy and finance through AID, having authority for a project which is not available, they would have to reimburse us for 150 percent of the value? That would put a stop to it.

Under this system as it is, they have all to win and nothing to lose. 

Mr. GAUD. As I said, this year we have suspended and debarred from further business through us quite a number of suppliers and importers, Senator Williams.

I would doubt very much that we have the legal authority to impose the kind of a penalty that you speak of. I think the law could do this, but I do not think we could do it in a contract. It is much more effective to debar them, is it not, sir? 

Senator WILLIAMS. You could get the law, couldn't you? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, but isn't debarment sufficient punishment? It would seem to me it would be.

Senator WILLIAMS. No, it is not, and I will tell you why it is not. 

Just as we find that if you disbar them for a very few dollars, they can get up a new company and put the same operating head in it. I

think these people appear in various parts of the world under various names; they are repeaters. 

Senator LAUSCHE. Will the Senator yield?

AMENDMENT TO THE LAW PROPOSED 

Mr. Gaud, in my opinion, there was great strength in what Senator 

Williams has just said. These people ought to be deterred from practicing frauds upon the United States or indulging in misconduct that is detrimental to us. 

Regarding his argument that you examine what has been done, find out that it was wrong, then make them reimburse us, I think we ought to submit an amendment to the law to make certain that these transgressors are made to realize that when they try to take advantage of our government they are not only going to reimburse us for the peculations which they have committed, but they are also going to pay a penalty.

Senator WILLIAMS. I agree with that. I understand, Mr. Gaud, we are going to have to recess these hearings shortly. Perhaps you can come back tomorrow. I am going to suggest that overnight you see if you cannot come up with a recommended amendment to this bill which would give to you the adequate authority that you may think is necessary, and which you may not have under existing laws. If you do not have this authority, I think the Congress will give it to you. If there is no way to solve the problem, I personally am very skeptical about pouring this money out knowing that all we can do is to maybe be reimbursed, Mr. Gaud. 

In an underdeveloped area, these outboard motors and bubblegum and other items which have been pictured here today-and I think you will agree with me-should never have been authorized under any type of an aid program of any description. 

TOTAL U.S. GIFTS AND LOANS TO DEVELOPING NATIONS 

Senator LAUSCHE. Mr. Gaud, I would like to have the record show what our total program is in providing American dollars by way of gifts and loans to the different nations of the world in their efforts to improve their economy.

I wish that you would provide for the record what, if any, amount we are to give to the Asian Bank.

Two, what we are to provide new for the Inter-American Bank. 

Three, what we are to provide for the International Development 

Association. 

Four, what aid we will give under Public Law 480. 

I do not know whether there are other programs that are used to provide aid in addition to the program that we are considering here this morning, but those figures ought to be submitted. 

Mr. GAUD. I think that we have already given those to the committee, sir, but if we have not I will see that we do. (The information referred to follows:)

AID EMPLOYEES RESPONSIBLE FOR DISCREPANCIES 

58, 400 45,000 

Senator WILLIAMS. Mr. Gaud, may I make this suggestion: You are familiar with the report which you furnished my office, and I appreciate your cooperation in obtaining it through Mr. Mansfield. I would want to discuss some of these specific items further tomorrow, but if, in the meantime, you could check your records so that you can tell us tomorrow whether you can identify the individuals who were responsible for these discrepancies it would be helpful. If you cannot, if it takes more time, maybe you can furnish them later, but I am interested in examining these items.

I realize that you are talking about locking the barn after the horse has been stolen, but I want to make sure that the same horse thieves are not on the public payroll and, therefore, I do want to discuss these specific items.

We cannot ignore them, as we discuss them. I also want to know whether or not the AID officials who are responsible are still on the payroll, and I want to emphasize, again, as I referred to them, that my questions are not in any way intended to be interpreted as a reflection upon you or your integrity as an individual. I realize this is a big program. You have to delegate authority, and oftentimes, unfortunately, those to whom you delegate it may abuse that trust. 

So this is not a personal matter, as I expressed to you privately. 

I want to make that expression public, but I think that working together we can find out who is responsible, and if not only these individuals know that they are going to be held responsible, and that they can be held accountable for what they did wrong or failed to do, but, at

the same time, their actions will serve as a deterrent on other officials who later on figure, "I may be caught, too, so I will behave myself." 

Mr. GAUD. Thank you, Senator Williams. I think it is also important to keep in mind here that while none of us is happy about this situation in the Dominican Republic that you are talking about, at the same time we ought to remember it is four-tenths of 1 percent of the $27 million we made available to the Dominican Republic that is involved here. It does not represent a large part of the funds that we supplied to the Dominican Republic at that time. Now,

I do not say this to condone it. I do not condone it, but I think it is well to keep this in mind to provide more perspective in what we are talking about.

Senator WILLIAMS. I realize that. I certainly do not mean to leave the impression that all the foreign aid money has been spent for bubblegum. 

Senator WILLIAMS. Not at all. 

But it is these glaring examples which shock not only the Congress but the American people, and it is the abuse which is responsible for some of the excessive spending. These are certainly items which we can take out of the program in reducing it, to hold it down to a realistic level and I think, with your cooperation, we can maybe get to the bottom of what has happened and, at the same time, find out who is responsible and take the appropriate steps thereafter. 

Thank you.

GAP BETWEEN HAVE AND HAVE-NOT NATIONS INCREASING 

Senator PELL (presiding). Mr. Gaud, I think one of the most interesting developments in our Nation's history is happening on the Hill today, because we find being discussed the two great battles facing the country, one at home and one abroad, and the corollary split between haves and the have-nots.

I have the most interesting statement of Secreary Rusk in the last two rather heated days which were, well lit but, perhaps not as well observed. He pointed out that the GNP differences between the havenot nations and the haves is going just the wrong way, the gap between the two is increasing. 

I just left the Labor and Public Welfare Committee meeting where we had witnesses on the riot report, and legislation to correct the manifest need for employment. Their statement was the same thing, that at home the differences between the haves and the have-nots is increasing. Thus there are many common factors which are increasing the polarization of the world. I know you are just as conscious of that as I am, and I commend you in general for the work you are doing on a larger level to try to decrease this polarization. 

MULTIYEAR AUTHORIZATION PREFERRED 

A couple of specific queries: One, you have requested this year a one-year authorization. I know your problem because as I understand it, usually when the Administration asks for a one-year, we like two, and when you ask for two, we like one. It complicates it. 91-392-68-pt. 2-5

I am one of those who likes a longer authorization period because I think you can plan more sensibly and do a better job that way. 

FLOW TO EUROPE OF U.S. MONEY THROUGH PAYMENTS IN VIETNAM 

We touched rather briefly a couple of days ago on the question of scrip money.

Mr. GAUD. Right. 

Senator PELL. I made an incorrect assumption, in my question asking why don't we use scrip. I understand we do use it for a good deal of soldiers' payments. 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, that is correct.

Senator PELL. But there is still a problem in that certain green dollars or checks or I O Us or some equivalent that is expended by individuals there, be it in brothels or stores or wherever it is, and that money finds its way to the money marts in Europe. 

I have heard stories that some of the establishments in Vietnam are owned by non-Vietnamese, in fact.

What can we do to tighten up this flow of money to help us fight the gold drain? 

Mr. GAUD. I do not think I have any satisfactory answer to your question, Senator Pell.

As you say, since 1965 our military personnel in Vietnam have been paid in military payment certificates that portion of their salary which they are to receive in Vietnam. 

Senator PELL. May I interrupt here? My recollection is, if it is correct, that while there may be regulations saying they cannot cash a check, I do not think there is anything in fact preventing them from depositing their salary and drawing a check.

Mr. GAUD. Depositing in the States and then drawing a check-I suspect you are right about that. I do not know. I cannot answer so far as the regulations on the part of our military personnel are concerned, and I am afraid I will have to duck that one, and ask you to talk to someone in the Defense Department. They will be up here to testify on this bill, I think, early next week. 

Senator PELL. Right. 

Do you have any thought as to how much money is leaving Vietnam not for the United States but for other countries in Europe, what the flow amounts to? 

Mr. GAUD. No, sir; I do not. 

Senator PELL. Do you think there is any way of ascertaining it?

That is more a civilian responsibility, maybe a Treasury responsibility, but could you try to secure that figure for the record? 

Mr. GAUD. Let me see what I can do. 

Senator PELL. For the record. (The information referred to follows:) 

---

PERCENTAGE OF SUPPLIES LOST THROUGH GRAFT IN VIETNAM 

Senator PELL. One not only hears, but is pretty confident, there is a pretty good amount of graft and corruption; it is a way of life in certain parts of the world. Newspaper reports indicate that the figures are high. I think I once read 50 percent of the aid that is tendered for the individual farmers, the individual citydwellers, is not reaching them but sticks to the pockets of somebody on the way down. 

What would be your rough estimate as to what the actual percentage we lose through graft is in Vietnam? This might be of interest to the press because so many figures are published of such a large nature. 

Mr. GAUD. Well, here again I have not got very solid ground to walk on.

The last study that we made of this was about a year ago in early 1967 when we tried to get an accurate figure on the amount of the loss of supplies that were imported into Vietnam. 

We came up with an average figure of 5 to 6 percent. 

Now, there were two principal components of our program at that time, and there still are. Well over half of the total amount of commodities at that time which were going into Vietnam were for the commodity import program-goods which went in through the port to Saigon and were turned over to the merchants, most of them right in Saigon.

That was at a time when the port was very badly overcrowded, when there were literally hundreds of barges in the river with goods on them, when there was insufficient warehousing, when there were too few customs inspectors and too few auditors. 

Today the conditions of the port are very different. There is not the congestion that there used to be. There are more auditors, more inspectors, more warehouses, and everything we know would indicate that the loss of that particular kind of goods is way down below what it had been.

Senator PELL. Right. Excuse me, I think in New York or any of our ports, there is a loss from pilferage. 

Mr. GAUD. That is correct.

Senator PELL. And this is taken into account. It should not be, but it is. 

Mr. GAUD. That is correct.

Senator PELL. But the loss I am talking about is when the rice is shipped out from the cities to the countryside. 

Mr. GAUD. I was coming to that because, as you say, apart from the commodity import program, the goods that we imported to Vietnam for our projects, and under Public Law 480-a lot of it has to be distributed through the countryside and go into all kinds of towns and villages where there may or may not be warehouses, and there may or may not be protection.

We figured at that time that our project commodities were being lost at the rate of about 10 to 15 percent, some more than others. 

HIGHEST LOSS ON SUPPLIES TO REFUGEES 

The highest loss, we reckoned, was on the supplies to the refugees which we thought ran as high as 20 percent. 

Now, as I say, this was about a year ago. Today we have more warehouses, we have more guards, we have better protection accorded to these supplies, and my guess would be that our record is better now than it was a year ago.

Still there are isolated instances, I am sure, where we suffer very heavy losses in a particular locality. As a result of the Tet offensive, I am sure that we lost a great many supplies that we would not ordinarily have lost.

So that I cannot give you any real solid estimate, but I have no evidence that suggests except in a specific isolated case in a particular locality--that the losses of these refugee supplies are anything like 50 percent. I think it is much lower than that. But this is only a guess based on many reports that I have seen, and I cannot document it.

Again, perhaps, to put the matter in perspective, AID-funded refugee supplies in fiscal year 1968 come to a total of about $13 million, out of a program of $480 million. So if you take 20 percent of that it is a relatively small part of the total. There will also be about $15 million in relief supplies under Public Law 480. 

Senator PELL. I recognize the problems. I ran a refugee program of sorts for a voluntary agency in Europe, and I recognize the difficulties there.

But, in general, there is a substantial diversion of our goods, I had understood, not in the sale of the goods, but in the pocketing of the proceeds. 

Mr. GAUD. In the sale of the goods?

Senator PELL. In the sale of the goods and the pocketing of the proceeds by the so-called distributors. 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, I am sure this happens.

As far as the commodity import program is concerned, of course, these goods are sent to Vietnam for the express purpose of being sold in the marketplace, and we are paid for those in local currency, so

that these goods are supposed to be sold. There has been a good deal of confusion, I think, over that fact. 

But it is also true, as you say, that other commodities which we sent into Vietnam for refugees-roofing and cement that we sent in for building supplies, or fertilizers, is sometimes stolen, hijacked, or taken by corrupt officials and sold. No question about that, it happens. 

CONFLICT BETWEEN MILITARY WAR AND ECONOMIC WAR 

Senator PELL. You would agree, I am sure, that we are really fighting two wars in Vietnam, one for military security, and the other for the political security of the area, of the securing of the hearts and minds. 

Senator PELL. Do you see any conflict between the military war and the fact that the more successful, the more widespread, the more overwhelming our military presence is there, the more difficult is made your political war? To be specific, when to secure an area we declare that it is for open bombardment, and the people have to come into the cities and become refugees, does this not create a conflict between the military war and the other war of which you are in charge? 

Mr. GAUD. Well, there certainly can be. I think that this is one of the reasons why it made sense to create CORDS. 

Senator PELL. To create what?

Mr. GAUD. I always forget what those initials stand for. It is called 

Civil Operations and Revolutionary Development Support, the or ganization under Ambassador Komer who is in charge of pacification, if you will. He is a deputy to General Westmoreland. He is in charge of the pacification programs that are carried on in the countryside. He is on the staff of General Westmoreland, and this enables him to coordinate his activities with the security operations that are being carried on by the military to a degree that would not have been possible if the civilian program of pacification were under separate command, as it used to be.

The creation of CORDS represents an effort to meld these two programs more closely together, to see that military support, that security, is provided to those villages where the pacification teams are working, to see that military operations are carried out with due regard to the requirements of taking care of refugees and the like. 

It is an effort to decrease the abrasiveness of these two programs. 

Senator PELL. Right. 

I recognize well your position. But one of the reasons I am opposed to the direction of our present policy is that I see this conflict puts us in a dilemma. The greater the military securing of the area, the devastation of the area, and the greater the American presence, the more complicated it makes some of your problems. 

Mr. GAUD. No question about it.

Senator PELL. I think this is really the guts of our problem. 

EXTENT OF PACIFICATION PROGRAM IN THAILAND 

Now, also in connection with Thailand, because I am quite concerned, I see the start of another Vietnam there, starting out rather modestly and rather gently with a slight increase in the aid pro-

gram, with a few advisors, but it can escalate. How much pacification, counterinsurgency work are we presently doing in Thailand? 

How many people do we have going in there and how successful are we?

Mr. GAUD. The situation in Thailand is this, Senator Pell. We are supporting programs of the Thai Government. They are carrying on these programs themselves. Their contribution in terms of money is about twice what ours is.

Their contribution of people is far beyond ours. They have an accelerated rural development program. They have health programs and education programs, a whole series of programs that are being carried on in Northeast Thailand-road building, trying to open up the country-and we are supporting them to some extent with supplies and commodities. We are supporting them with equipment, supporting them to some extent with people. 

Senator PELL. May I interpolate here? I realize in 1962 you were in New York wearing another hat. But would you not say there is a certain similarity between our position in Thailand and what we are contributing and doing to the total economy and the life of the nation today, and where we were in Vietnam in 1962? 

Mr. GAUD. There is a similarity in the sense that we were in Vietnam in 1962 acting as advisers and supporters, and that is what we are doing today in Thailand.

There is an important difference, I think, in the two countries. Thailand is a country, as you know, which has been independent for hundreds of years. It has a much more effective government. It has a much more homogeneous population. It is much more of a national entity than Vietnam has ever been.

I share your fear that the situation in Thailand will develop beyond what it is today. But I think there are important elements of strength in the Thai situation which were not present in the Vietnam situation. 

REVOLUTIONARY PROGRAM IN THAILAND 

Senator PELL. I think we can all agree, as every nation moves along the road to what we consider progress, it goes through a great revolutionary program. I never have known a country that has not. In Thailand this has yet to come, and this is, perhaps, a certain weakness there. 

Mr. GAUD. Yes. And yet in Northeast Thailand I think the revolution is coming-not a dramatic revolution. I was there in January in 

Northeast Thailand. I had not been in that part of the country for a couple of years, and it was quite interesting to see the extent to which the country has been opened up, more roads, more schools, more health clinics, better water, better wells, more evidences of Thai central government intervention.

I talked to the governors of two or three provinces in Northeast Thailand. Their budgets are rapidly increasing. They are getting more money in taxes from their own people. They are getting more money, by way of help from the central government. You can see signs of motion there, and all you can do is hope that this revolution that you speak of is going to be a peaceful, gradual revolution, and that we can do something to help it along. Of course, that is what we are trying to do.

Senator PELL. One other thing. One other pious hope is that we do not consider it part of Communist aggression when the inevitable revolution comes in these countries. This is what concerns me. 

AID PROGRAM IS AN INCREASING PROCESS 

Another more general query to you along the same line: One of the reasons why some of us have reservations about aid is because we see it as a gradually increasing process. It starts out with a little bit of economic aid, and then it moves on into a military assistance program of sorts, and then we find some more formalization, more formal arrangements are concluded, and then some problems come along and we send in a couple of advisers, and then the end result of it is that you find a Vietnam. This is why, I think, you have the problem you have in selling the program to the Hill because each of us, whether we articulate these thoughts or not, have these thoughts in our minds. 

1969 PROGRAM CONCENTRATED IN FIVE COUNTRIES 

In your program this year am I correct in saying you are limiting the program to fewer countries than last time? 

 But I think that you can look at the program in another way.

Take the five countries which are the principal recipients of development loans under the fiscal year 1969 program that I am proposing to the Congress, India, Pakistan, Turkey, Korea, and Indonesia. 

In all five of these countries we are working not alone but with a dozen other aid donors, with the World Bank and with the IMF. 

It is not as though Uncle Sam were going it alone in these countries, and is getting deeply involved in them. We are working with a group of other nations all toward the same goal, trying to help these countries pull themselves up by their bootstraps. 

Senator PELL. And yet, I think it might be of interest to the committee, I do not mean to say this in any embarrassing way to you, if you could submit for the record in the different places where we have aid programs the number of American AID personnel we have and the number of people in multilateral agencies including those in technical assistance programs. One of my concerns is, that sometimes these programs are not quite as closely coordinated as they might be. 

Mr. GAUD. I am not sure I got it.

Senator PELL. The figures that we have, the total number of Americans.

Mr. GAUD. Right. 

Senator PELL. And the total number of noncountry employees, put it that way.

Mr. GAUD. I see. 

Senator PELL. Also the number of technical assistance program people or World Bank people in the same communities. 

Mr. GAUD. From other countries, you mean? 

Senator PELL. No, I am talking about multilateral aid.

Mr. GAUD. Of course, the multilateral institutions do not go in for technical assistance very often. That is one of the problems. The World 

Bank has only recently started doing work in agriculture and education. They do not do that.

Senator PELL. But Paul Hoffman's organization is entirely devoted to that.

WORLD BANK OVERSEAS PERSONNEL 

The World Bank maintains resident representatives in several countries and conducts advisory work through visiting missions and seconded personnel in many other countries. The extent of the latter activities varies from year to year. 

In fiscal year 1967, Bank staff members visited Ethiopia, Ghana, Haiti, Iceland, Ivory Coast, Mauritania, Morocco and the Sudan to provide advisory assistance to governments in evolving suitable organizations for formulating and implementing development programs. In the case of Mauritania, a Bank team also assisted in the preparation of a development plan. 

Members of the Bank staff were seconded to advise on development planning in Libya, the operations of a development finance company in Nigeria, project preparation and planning in Peru, economic policies in the Sudan, and the preparation of annual economic budgets in Tunisia. Similar advisory assignments were completed in Chile and Tanzania. The Bank has continued to maintain resident representatives in India and Pakistan. The Bank's resident representative in Ethiopia ended his term of service at the end of June.

U.N. DEVELOPMENT PROGRAMS 

Senator PELL. One of the things that worries me is that our AID people do not even know the name of the U.N. man who is in the same country. They just know the U.S. technical assistance program and the World Bank. But way down on the totem pole is the U.N. 

This is something we must change. 

Mr. GAUD. You are right. I do not think of the United Nations program as I do the others. The United Nations does not participate in the consortia or consultative groups that we belong to in the same way the Germans, the British, the French, the Canadians and the World Bank do. They run quite a separate program. 

Senator PELL. I realize that, and I think, frankly, in many ways

I am inclined towards it as being a very healthy program. 

OBJECTIVES ARE THE SAME 

As you know, I oppose military assistance in any undeveloped country, except in Southeast Asia; I support a longer aid authorization; I support basically multilateral aid as opposed to bilateral aid. I think each of us on the committee has developed his own views. I hope the majority of us will come up with a voting pattern that somewhat ac-

cords with your views. I think basically our objectives are absolutely the same without any difference at all. 

I understand you will be kind enough to come back tomorrow at half past ten?

Prior to adjourning-I would like to announce that the committee will meet at 10:00 tomorrow morning, in room 4221 of the New Senate

Office Building. 

Mr. Ackley, Ambassador-designee to Italy will be heard at that time, and we hope that you, Mr. Gaud, will be able to be back at half past ten. 

Mr. GAUD. Yes. 

Senator PELL. Thank you, sir. 

The meeting is adjourned. (Whereupon, at 12:45 p.m., the committee was in recess, to reconvene at 10:00a.m., Thursday, March 14, 1968.)
