FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1968 THURSDAY, MARCH 14, 1968 UNITED STATES SENATE, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN RELATIONS, 

Washington, D.O.

The committee met, pursuant to recess, at 10 a.m., in room 4221, New Senate Office Building, Senator John Sparkman presiding. 

Present: Senators Sparkman, Clark, Pell, Hickenlooper, Aiken, Williams, and Mundt.

Senator SPARKMAN. Now, we proceed to the hearings on the foreign aid bill.

Mr. Gaud, I shall be very brief because some members of the committee, I know, do want to question you at length today. Some of them have not had an opportunity heretofore. 

AFRICAN DEVELOPMENT BANK 

I think I can ask you this question while you are going ahead with your papers there. The President's message on foreign aid stated that the African Development Bank was in the process of forming a soft loan window. Would you describe the Bank's operations to date, the present and expected U.S. relationship to the Bank and estimate how much we may be asked to contribute to a special fund? 

STATEMENT OF HON. WILLIAM S. GAUD, ADMINISTRATOR, AGENCY FOR INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENT-Resumed 

Mr. GAUD. Mr. Chairman, the bank to date has no special funds at all. Member countries have paid in about $50 million for capital of the 

Bank so far.

The capital has all been put up by African countries. There are no members of the Bank which come from outside Africa. This is by design. The Africans want it this way. They asked no one to participate in the capital of the Bank.

To date the Bank has got itself organized, it has a staff, it has made one loan, and the loans that are to be made from the funds which it now has at its disposal are made at relatively hard terms. 

The Bank is interested in creating a special fund which would be a soft loan window equivalent to the IDA of the World Bank. The president of the Bank, Mr. Beheiry, has talked to us and other aid-giving nations about the possibility of setting up such a fund. 

We have not been asked for any specific amount of money. We haven't made any specific assurances. We have suggested to Mr. Beheiry that when he has finished his talks with other countries, he come (307)

back to us, and we would then get down to more detailed and specific discussions with him.

If we go ahead with this venture, we will submit to the Congress a separate authorization bill to cover the contribution to the special funds of the bank. We don't know whether that will happen at this session or not. We don't have any definitive idea today as to what the amount would be and I am afraid I just can't be any more specific than that.

Senator SPARKMAN. I think you have given us a good picture of it. 

In other words, we are not obligated to anything yet, and the amount needed has not even been finally determined. 

Mr. GAUD. That is correct, sir. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Our participation will not be automatic? There would be special authorization requested? 

Senator SPARKMAN. Thank you. 

U.S. ECONOMIC AID WITHIN MULTILATERAL FRAMEWORK 

Let me ask this, because this is something our committee has been interested in through the years, as you know, and that is the multilateral participation. I gathered from the initial statement of either yourself or of Secretary Rusk or maybe both, that there was an increase in the multilateral operations. How much of our economic aid will be within the multilateral framework during the next fiscal year? 

Mr. GAUD. Of the funds for which we are asking in this bill, the development loan fund and the Alliance loan fund, roughly 90 percent will be loaned in what we call a multilateral framework. 

Now, I mean by that that the countries to which these loans will be made are members of consortia headed by the World Bank or OECD, they are members of consultative groups headed by the World Bank or in the case of Latin America, they belong, all of them, to CIAP. 

Now, in these consortia and consultative groups the member nations get together and through the leadership of the World Bank, in all cases except the one headed by the OECD for Turkey, they get together and on the basis of studies made by the World Bank, jointly review the requirements of the recipient countries, jointly decide what policies these countries should take to make our aid more effective, what self-help measures they should adopt, and then having decided, having agreed on the size of the requirements, on the necessary self-help measures, each country decides for itself how much aid it is prepared to give against those requirements. 

The classic example of this which I guess most of you are more familiar with than the others, or the latest example of this, I should say, is the case of Indonesia. Indonesia has come on the scene rather late and instead of going ahead on a purely bilateral basis with Indonesia we have joined with some seven or eight other nations which formed together an intergovernmental group so-called with the IMF and the World Bank, and Indonesia's requirements are being handled in that way.

We are doing everything we can to persuade the World Bank to create additional consultative groups for other countries, and within the last 2 or 3 years the number has been increasing quite considerably.

HAS TRANSFER AUTHORITY BEEN USED THIS FISCAL YEAR? 

Senator SPARKMAN. Has the authority of section 205 which authorizes transfers of up to 10 percent of the development loan fund to multilateral lending institutions been used during this fiscal year? 

Mr. GAUD. No, it has not, Mr. Chairman.

Senator SPARKMAN. Why? 

Mr. GAUD. For this fiscal year we are very unlikely to use it because of the very severe cut in our development loan funds. We asked the 

Congress last year, as I recall it, for about $760 million in development loan funds and the authorization was $435 million, and that leaves us pretty strapped.

Now, in fiscal year 1967 we made an effort to use section 205 to make available funds to the World Bank to be used in Africa. 

I discussed the matter with Mr. Woods, the President of the Bank, a number of times. He was agreeable to such a transfer under section 

205, provided that we would make the funds available on an untied basis, in other words, provided those funds could be used anywhere in the world.

Now, two points troubled us. One, all of our loan funds today are tied and we think that the balance of payments situation that we are in requires us generally to tie our funds. 

Two, at the time that I was discussing this with Mr. Woods, the question of IDA replenishment was up, and the United States was taking the position in the negotiations for IDA replenishment that we could not turn over funds to the World Bank on an untied basis. 

So that Mr. Woods and I met an impasse on that question of tying. He was not prepared to take the funds unless they were untied and I was not prepared-or the United States was not prepared-to make them available unless they were tied. So we were not able to go through with our hope of making a transfer under section 205. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Does that condition still exist? 

Senator SPARKMAN. Then there is not much likelihood that it will be utilized during the next fiscal year, is there? 

Mr. GAUD. That would be my guess.

Senator SPARKMAN. All right, sir. 

REFLECTION IN VIETNAM AID PROGRAM OF DOUBLED DOLLAR HOLDINGS 

Now, according to the AID briefing book, South Vietnam had $334 million in foreign exchange as of November 1967, nearly double what her dollar holdings, her dollar reserves were as of 1965. How has this favorable situation been reflected in the AID program for Vietnam? 

Mr. GAUD. It has resulted in the United States financing a much smaller volume of commodity imports into Vietnam. 

Two years ago, for example, we financed roughly $400 million worth of commodities in support of the Vietnamese stabilization program. 

We have reduced that to the neighborhood of $200 million, both last year and this year.

There are certain other arrangements that we have entered into with the Vietnamese in connection with their foreign exchange reserves which I could only talk about in executive session, Mr. 

Chairman.

Senator SPARKMAN. Very well.ama yum

Mr. GAUD. But that is only one very specific and concrete result. 

Senator SPARKMAN. As a matter of fact, I was going to ask you the next question about the possibility of reduction which you have just explained. i wetend 

Senator SPARKMAN. Senator Hickenlooper. 

SOVIET FOREIGN AID COMMITMENTS Senator HICKENLOOPER. Mr. Gaud, I believe the Soviet aid program over the world from somewhere around 1954 on has been around $6 billion. But there has been quite a gap between the Soviet promises of aid and the amount of aid that they have actually come through with, hasn't there?

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir, there has. They are rather slow in committing or obligating funds. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Can you give us the figures on approxi mately how much has actually been given to recipients under this approximately $6 billion economic program that they proposed? 

Mr. GAUD. I have a figure that goes back beyond 1960, and if I may use that 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. I am starting at 1956. 

Mr. GAUD. Beg your pardon, 1955. According to my figures here, since 1955 their cumulative commitments are about $9 billion and their expenditures are about $3 billion, in other words, just one-third of their commitments.

Now, their expenditures in 1967 ran as far as we can tell, between $400 and $500 million.

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Well, my understanding of the figures is a little different from yours. I don't have the $9 billion figure; the figure I have is somewhere around $6 billion since 1954. 

Mr. GAUD. Well, I had better check my figures again, let me do that. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. As of this year, the first of this year, there probably is less than $2.5 billion that has actually been drawn down by the recipients.

Mr. GAUD. I am sorry, the figures that I have here in front of me are the ones I gave you, I will be glad to check them back at the office. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. As a matter of fact, I will be glad to show you the figures I have here.

Mr. GAUD. Good. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. And the source of them. 

Mr. GAUD. Good. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. I understand that the total of Soviet de liveries of goods and services under their so-called aid program in 1967 was less than $300 million. 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir.

They jump around quite a lot. They don't follow a steady curve at all, and, according to the figures I have here my figures agree with you on this commitments were less than $300 million last year. 

There have been years when they have been substantially above that. (The information referred to follows:)

SUMMARY OF COMMUNIST AID-1955–1967 

It is estimated that the Communist countries have extended about $9 billion in economic credits and grants from 1955 through 1967 to non-Communist countries in the less developed areas. Of this cumulative total, some $3 billion, or one-third of the amount extended, has been drawn down by the aid-recipient countries in the form of actual deliveries of goods and services. The annual flow of Communist economic aid extensions has varied considerably. In some years, aid extensions have totalled well over $1 billion; in others, from $200-300 million. 

In 1966, economic aid credits reached an estimated high of $1.6 billion; in 1967, it fell to some $200 million. The sharp drop in aid commitments from a previous high is not unusual. It appears to reflect the fact that the major aid recipients were still digesting, in terms of specific earmarking and expenditures of funds, the relatively heavy credit extensions of the previous two or three years. 

Economic aid expenditures have been running about $500 million a year. 

The Soviet Union has accounted for roughly two-thirds of the $9 billion worth of total economic aid commitments, or about $6 billion. The East European countries have accounted for roughly 25 percent and Communist China for the remainder. By the end of 1967, over 40 non-Communist countries were recipients of Communist economic aid. It should be noted that the Soviet Union has, in addition, extended such aid to over a dozen countries in the Communist areas since 1955.

It is also estimated that the Communist countries, primarily the Soviet Union, have extended over $4 billion in military aid of all types to non-Communist countries through 1967. The bulk of the military aid-some 75-80 percent has been delivered.

ARE EXPECTATIONS OF AID PROGRAM UNFULFILLED? 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. In the last 22 years the United States has spent some $130 billion in foreign aid of one type or another. Now the gap between the world's rich and poor is getting wider, and the United States foreign policy objectives seem to be losing support steadily all around the world. There is little that a practicing politician can point to in the way of solid accomplishment within the foreign aid program. 

Do you think it is possible that the program has been oversold both to the recipients and to our own citizens, and that expectations have been aroused which cannot be fulfilled? 

Mr. GAUD. Well, I think that we have had a number of aid programs over the years. If you take that gross figure that you gave, sir, that includes a good many different things. It includes, for example, the 

Marshall plan, which cost $13 billion, and which I think was clearly not oversold, and which was highly successful. It includes the program of aid to Turkey and Greece in the 40s, which was highly successful, to prevent the communist domination of those two countries. 

It includes some $38 billion worth of military assistance over the years. It includes loans made by the Export-Import Bank, which are not really developmental-the American taxpayer makes money off those loans. And it includes Food for Freedom, some $15 billion, most of which was provided in a period when we were not exercising the same tight control and insisting upon the same self-help measures that we are insisting upon today.

Now, if you take the kind of economic aid programs that we are carrying on today, these are relatively new. We calculate the total amount that has gone into them at around $30 billion. 

Now, how successful have they been, have they any chance of success?

There are some specific countries, some few countries with which we are both familiar which have done extremely well and which are 91-392-68—pt. 2— -6

now off of the aid rolls, such as Taiwan, Iran, Greece, Israel, one or two others, not many.

There are a number of other countries today which I think are making extraordinarily good progress, such as Korea and Turkey, both of which are planning to get off of economic aid in the early 

1970's. 

There are other countries, for example, Pakistan, and several countries in Latin America, where we can see, I think, real substantial signs of progress.

The question that to me is more fundamental and more important than whether a specific country has gotten off of aid is whether the aid program is dealing with, and is helping to meet, fundamental problems.

AGRICULTURAL REVOLUTION 

Yesterday I talked in my statement about this business of food. I think it is the most important problem that the world faces except peace. I think the evidence is clear that in Asia, in Brazil, in Morocco, in Tunisia, in a number of countries, real progress is being made on that front, and that we are on the verge of an agricultural revolution, certainly in Asia, if the developing countries keep going the way they are, and if the United States and other aid-giving nations continue as they are.

I think this is a very important problem. I think it is essential it be solved, and I think it can be solved only by these countries with outside assistance.

ENABLING COUNTRIES TO DEVELOP ON THEIR OWN 

In addition to the question of food, the possibilities, the realities of economic development, are plain in a number of these countries that I mentioned. And if we look at this thing in terms of closing the gap, in terms of making the GNP or the per capita income of the developing countries anywhere near ours, I think we are looking at the wrong target.

Take, for example, Taiwan today. Taiwan still has a per capita output of slightly more than $200 per capita, and yet Taiwan is not getting economic aid any more. Taiwan is able to borrow money on commercial terms for its own development. Taiwan is clearly forging ahead. 

The Japanese today have an average per capita GNP I think, of $900 to $1000 as compared to our more than $3,500. But Japan, heaven knows, is one of the fastest growing countries in the world today, and has become one of the foremost industrial powers in the world. 

So that we are not trying to close the gap in terms of making these people as well off as we are. What we are trying to do is give them enough of a push so that they will be able to continue to develop under their own steam. And this, I think, is a realizable goal. It has been realized by a number of countries. A number of others like Turkey and Korea are coming close to it. Pakistan has made tremendous progress. If India continues with the sort of progress she is making now on the agricultural front we are going to see some very real changes there.

So we have invested a lot of money in this business. The job is nowhere near done. I think the job is do-able, and I think it is terribly important to do it. 

A FEW BRIGHT SPOTS 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Well, Mr. Gaud, I have heard that same argument for 15 years, that things are getting better and better and better. 

Mr. GAUD. And aren't they, Senator, in this respect?

Senator HICKENLOOPER. I am not so sure they are. I am not so sure they are. It seems to me the world is in more turmoil than it was. 

Mr. GAUD. Well, the world, yes, certainly economic- 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. You can pick out a few bright spots. Cer tainly the Marshall Plan was helpful to those countries, but I don't know that Europe is marching down the road with us very much right now. Greece and Turkey seem to be declaring themselves a little antagonistic to the United States periodically, and are not so friendly as they were. I don't know that Africa is responding very much. I think the Japanese have been cooperating in the Pacific on an industrial basis quite well, and Taiwan is proof to me, as is Japan, that if you really encourage self-help in a country that is willing to provide self-help, they can do wonders. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. And they have done wonders. 

Mr. GAUD. Yes.

Senator HICKENLOOPER. And yet there are people in this country who want to throw Taiwan to the mainland Chinese.

GAP BETWEEN RICH AND POOR WIDENS 

I think that the overall picture is not the most encouraging. I think there are elements of progress and perhaps elements of encouragement in some areas, but the idea of $130 billion spent in the last 22 years, that is a vast amount of money. I think there is a lot of agreement anyway, but whether it is universal or not, it is a majority agreement, but the gap between the rich and the poor is getting wider. Maybe that is not true, I don't know.

Mr. GAUD. Well, certainly, I think it is getting wider in the sense that we are getting more prosperous faster than the poor nations. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. I question that. I think that a pay day is coming pretty soon in this country.

Mr. GAUD. That may be. But as far as the $130 billion, as I say, it wasn't all spent on the kind of thing that we are doing today. It is quite recently that we got into this business of economic development. 

The $130 billion is the total investment that the United States has made in solving a series of problems, the effort of the communists to take over Europe, their effort to take over Korea, Turkey, and Greece, a whole series of different problems.

POINTS TO KEEP IN MIND 

But a couple of questions, a couple of other points I would like to mention if I may, Senator Hickenlooper. None of us is satisfied with what is going on in Europe today, but what would Europe have been

like without the Marshall plan? I think it would have been worse than it is today.

The second question to keep in mind, the second point to keep in mind, it seems to me, is this: economic aid, and greater prosperity in these countries, is obviously not going to solve all of their problems. 

The United States today-relative to most countries in the world-is extraordinarily well off. Yet heaven knows we have some ghastly problems right here at home. So that we can't expect foreign aid to solve all the problems of these countries. It can solve some problems. If people are fed, for example, you are going to eliminate one of the greatest threats to peace. If people can get an education, if they have an opportunity, if they can get jobs, if they are not subject to diseases, if the world population doesn't grow at too fast a rate, we are going to have fewer problems than if these conditions are not attacked and ameliorated. So that foreign aid is only a partial answer to the much greater problem of achieving some kind of stability and peace in the world. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Well, thank you, Mr. Gaud. 

I have taken more time than I should. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Senator SPARKMAN. Senator Williams. 

Senator WILLIAMS. Thank you. Mr. Gaud, I think we are all in agreement that there has been much good in the aid program, but what bothers you as an administrator and what embarrasses us as Members of Congress with constituents are these continued examples of abuse in the program, and I know you are interested in correcting them as much as we are.

But I would like to discuss just a few of these that were called to our attention in this report, which disturbed me and I am sure disturbed you.

Mr. GAUD. Yes. 

PETTY CASH EXPENDITURES 

Senator WILLIAMS. First, I refer you to page 30 of the report where it is pointed out that a considerable amount of your expenditures are made through petty cash funds and it indicates that more than $400,000 was used in petty cash in one fiscal year, with over $258,000 of that representing reimbursements to contractors that were doing work.

Now, I realize the term petty cash, I always thought that it meant small amounts and $400,000 in cash is not petty. I am at a loss to understand why such large amounts of petty cash would be available and apparently so little accounting for it. I note in here that the Inspector General, commenting on this, cited one example of what can happen. He said, as an example, "U.S. AID submitted schedules of cash payments to 'contract employees,"" and contract employees is in quotes, "who, so far as we could tell, did not work under any contractor or for any contractor." Those instances will happen as long as you have such loose management of petty cash. I am wondering if you would care to comment on what steps have been taken to prevent this from happening? Have you tightened up on that practice? 

Mr. GAUD. Senator Williams, so far as I know, I have never heard of it anywhere except in Nigeria. In Nigeria our setup is this: Our 

AID mission has its head office in Lagos, which is the capital. We have a number of provincial or district or regional offices in other 774

parts of Nigeria, and as you know, Nigeria is not what one would call a very cohesive place.

The contractors in some of these areas local merchants who were supplying us with food and other supplies as well as some of the local people who were working for us-were just not prepared to give us credit and wait for checks to come through to them from our disbursing office in Lagos.

Our district regional directors took this means of buying supplies and paying for local employees. When it was called to our attention here in Washington by the Inspector General, we did go after it to eliminate this practice. I have not got an up-to-date report on where it stands now, and I would be glad to get it and submit it for the record. I just don't know exactly where we are at the moment. 

Senator WILLIAMS. I appreciate that. I think you will agree with me that such a practice is wide open for abuse.

Mr. GAUD. I agree that it is not a proper practice to follow. 

Senator WILLIAMS. And when you figure $400,000 is fleeting through what they call a petty cash fund, for which I understand there is not always a detailed accounting, it is rather loose management and something which I don't think we can condone and carry on. 

Mr. GAUD. I agree. 

Senator WILLIAMS. And you will furnish to the committee a record of steps that are being taken to correct that abuse? 

 (The information referred to follows:)

PETTY CASH FUND-NIGERIA 

In the Northern Region of Nigeria, petty cash expenditures had been made for personal services of contract employees such as local-hire drivers, guards, and the payroll of certain other local-hire employees. In early 1967, following the visit of the Inspector General's staff which identified the problem. USAID/ 

Nigeria advised that new arrangements for eliminating these local employment and payment practices were in process by means of contracts with qualified firms. 

According to our AID mission in Nigeria on December 9, 1967, the following steps had been taken : (1) Payment of salaries of certain local employees from the Agency Cashier Account had been discontinued, resulting in a significant identifiable reduction in petty cash expenditures in the North. (2) USAID had entered into non-personal services contracts with qualified firms for guards for multiple dwellings, cleaning services for offices, etc. (3) USAID had converted some local employees, such as drivers, clerks and maintenance and repair artisans, from a contract to an official directhire basis.

Only a few local employees are now being paid through petty cash. These are occasional day-laborers employed when their services are needed. 

AID GOODS BEING AUCTIONED IN BRAZIL 

Senator WILLIAMS. I notice also that the report says on page 4 that in Brazil a local official called the Inspector General's attention to a warehouse where distress cargo including U.S. finance cargo was being auctioned off. The U.S. AID representative of the company took the Inspector General to the warehouse and when they went into the warehouse, with a location designated, here they saw considerable quantities of AID-financed goods which had just been auctioned off and a walk through the warehouse brought to light many other boxes with markings which were also presumably to be sold. Then he reports

that just as they were leaving the warehouse a large truck rolled up and unloaded another batch of boxes with similar markings that were likewise scheduled to be sold, but when they talked with your AID officials they didn't even know the warehouse existed. How could that happen and how could these AID goods get into a warehouse where they would be auctioned off in the free market without somebody knowing it?

Mr. GAUD. Well, we have here an example of the situation that I was talking about yesterday. In this case we made a loan to the Government of Brazil. Importers in Brazil ordered goods from suppliers in the United States. Those goods are shipped by the supplier through his freight forwarding agent to the importer in Brazil. They are the importer's goods. They are not our goods. We, like a bank, have financed the purchase of the goods, but they have been shipped by the 

American supplier to the Brazilian importer, and the Brazilian importer for reasons that we don't know, in a particular case, has failed to pick them up. We can only speculate as to why this happens in a particular case.

It was our understanding, it was the understanding of our people in Brazil at the time, that these goods were not auctioned. It was brought to light by the Inspector General, as you point out, that these goods, like all other goods shipped to Brazil, were auctioned when they had lain unclaimed for 180 days. We have now done several things: 

One, we have made provision for receiving notice when any of these AID financed goods will be auctioned. Two, we have been reviewing and taking inventory of all AID cargo at this and other sites on a monthly basis. And we are trying to expedite these cases, get after the importers, find out why the goods are not utilized, why they are not picked up, and the like.

Senator WILLIAMS. Well, in this instance the goods are paid for under our AID program and in effect charged to the American taxpayers, are they not? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes.

Senator WILLIAMS. I mean they represent gifts as far as the taxpayers are concerned Mr. GAUD. No, sir, they are not gifts. We make a loan to the Brazilian Government and that loan is ultimately repaid. There is no gift involved here. The importer- 

Senator WILLIAMS. The loan is repaid in dollars? 

Senator WILLIAMS. Always? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir.

U.S. LOANS TO BRAZIL 

Senator WILLIAMS. Then how does that differ from a normal transaction and why should it come under the AID program? If the Brazilian government is eventually paying for them in dollars would that not be transacted through the Export-Import Bank? Just where does the AID program come in on this? 

Mr. GAUD. Well, we make loans to Brazil because, as we see it, Brazil has not got the foreign exchange she needs for her development. 

We make these loans to Brazil to supply her with the foreign exchange which she does not have herself, to enable her to stabilize her economy and to get on with development.

Brazil, as you know, has been undergoing a very severe inflation for a number of years. The rate of inflation in the last few years has been reduced very substantially; I think it is now something like 24 percent in a year whereas it used to be a couple of hundred percent. 

These loans which we make to Brazil are a part of the stabilization program which we have worked out with Brazil and with the IMF. 

In consideration of these loans, Brazil undertakes certain actions in the monetary, fiscal and economic field which will stabilize her economy and prevent inflation. That is the basic purpose of these loans. 

They cover the import of goods into Brazil which can be used to combat inflation. 

Senator WILLIAMS. Do I understand that they are repaid to the U.S.

Government in dollars without relationship to any devaluation of their currency; but we are paid back in hard currency? 

Senator WILLIAMS. And as they are repaid, would you furnish us a list of such loans and the records of repayment, along with a comparable list of dollar grants which we have made to the Brazilian government?

Mr. GAUD. We don't make any grants to the government of Brazil except in connection with the technical assistance programs. We don't make any grants that would cover the importation of goods or anything of that sort. (The information referred to follows:)

rol solutiui 91979% MAINTENANCE-OF-VALUE CLAUSE 

Senator WILLIAMS. Now, I notice there was a suggestion in this report also that the Cooley loans that are being made without the maintenance-of-value clause, result in the repayment being made in currency substantially less than that which is borrowed. 

The Inspector General calls our attention at page 6 and 7 to the fact that in this particular instance there was a $685,000 loan and it is repaid with the equivalent of $200,000. Now do you think it would be advisable to have the maintenance-of-value clause included as a part of these loans?

Mr. GAUD. After the Inspector General got in touch with us, we did change it in Chile. I think it is a very debatable question and there is a good deal of difference of opinion on this subject in the 

Administration. 

Let's take Chile, for example. When we were making the Cooley loans without a maintenance-of-value clause, we were charging interest of 16 to 20 percent, and when we went on to a maintenance-ofvalue basis we reduced our interest to 6 percent. 

Now, pay your money and take your choice. It is pretty hard to say what is going to happen to the currency of any particular country over a period of years. It is better to collect a very high rate of interest without the maintenance-of-value clause or should you charge a much lower rate of interest and have a maintenance-of-value clause? 

That is the situation as we see it in Latin America. 

Now, in other parts of the world you get a different situation. In 

India and Pakistan, for example, where we have a great deal of local currency already, because of the enormous Public Law 480 sales that have been made for local currency over the past half dozen years, there the question of maintenance-of-value is presented in quite another frame of reference.

So that I think the answer depends. And it is a question of judgment as to what it is you do. Although we did shift in Chile, I am not sure that we were wise to. I think we might have been better if we had stayed with the 18 to 20 percent, but we did shift. 160 1 

EFFECT OF CHARGING HIGH INTEREST RATES 

Senator WILLIAMS. Perhaps the mathematical result would be the same, but does not the charging of 18 to 20 percent by the United States Government put us in the position of being a shylock? Has not one of the objectives of the aid program been to lower rates of interest in those countries so that the people of that country who need the assistance could afford to get a start? For example, as long as we take part in, or encourage, the 18 to 20 percent, just picture the instance of buying a home: for 5 years paying a hundred percent of the cost of the home in interest charges alone. 18 to 20 percent in effect are prohibitive rates unless the government responsible is almost forced to devalue its currency in order to protect itself. Are we not moving in the opposite direction? Would it not be better to put a realistic interest rate on the maintenance-of-value? At least it would prevent windfalls such as the Inspector General called to your attention.

Mr. GAUD. Here again I think you have a rather tough question. 

Our 18 percent interest or whatever we charge on a Cooley loan in any particular country is geared to the going interest rates of lending institutions in that country. 

Now, these loans, as you know, go only to the affiliates of American firms. 

It seems to me it would be quite impossible, as a practical matter, and it would be undesirable from a political standpoint, to make loans to American firms at very much below the going rate of interest in the country to give the American firm that advantage in the country itself. So that as we see it, we have to keep our interest rate fairly close to the prevailing interest rate in the country. 

Now, the local borrower, of course there is no maintenance-ofvalue clause in his borrowing from a bank or from anybody else. He borrows, and if there is a devaluation, he gets the benefit of it. That is the way it is.

So if we are going to insert a maintenance-of-value clause, something which is not present in the ordinary loans in that country, we feel we have to do something about the interest rate and we lower it. 

But, I don't think we can lower the 18-percent interest rate in the average case to the kind of interest rate that we think would be reasonable without making it virtually impossible for American firms to go into those countries."

For example, we have had the question in India in connection with the Cooley foan program. If we were to offer too much of an advantage to American firms by way of low interest rates the Indian Government would not give their approval to the loan. So that our interest rates have to be more or less the same as those which prevail in the country.

PREAUDIT PROCEDURE IN VIETNAM 

Senator WILLIAMS. As I suggested yesterday, could we have a provision, or isn't there some method, where we can recover the money? 

There is fraud here. 

Senator WILLIAMS. What can be done and what has been done?

Mr. GAUD. Well, we have done several things. One is the fact that we have instituted in Vietnam, as I believe you know, a procedure whereby every application for an import license is screened by the AID mission, and every application must be stamped by the AID mission before the transaction can go forward. 

In addition to that there is still the question, of course, of whether the shipment, the goods that were shipped, comply with the description in the application for the import license. We audit the transactions after the event to try to find this out. 

Now as far as penalties are concerned, there are now on the books statutes which provide for the criminal prosecution of individuals or companies which have been guilty of a false, fictitious or fraudulent claim against the United States. There are also civil remedies under the False Claim Act which provide for penalties, and also provide for double damages. There is no bill-I find since yesterday, and I think you and I both thought this was true-which provides for the kind of penalties which you were speaking of yesterday.

I have asked my General Counsel to draft such an amendment to the Act to work it out with the Department of Justice-and I will undertake to get something to you well before the Committee marks up this bill. 

Senator WILLIAMS. I appreciate that, and as I said yesterday, perhaps we are in a sense discussing the situation too late, we are

trying to lock the barn after the horse has been stolen, but in order to prevent this, we have to locate the horse thieves. 

Now, are steps being taken to enforce the existing law in this instance where the sea water was sold to the U.S. Government? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir, that case has been referred to the Department of Justice.

Senator WILLIAMS. When was that law passed to which you refer requiring this audit. I think it was Senator Mundt who referred to it yesterday. 

Mr. GAUD. You mean requiring the preaudit in Vietnam?

Senator WILLIAMS. Yes.

(The article referred to follows:) [From the Washington Star, Mar. 6, 1968] U.S. AIDE TELLS OF GRAFT IN SAIGON, ASKS ACTION (By Dick Barnes) 

A top U.S. civilian adviser says the United States must boldly take the initiative to root out corruption in South Vietnam's government because, he insists, there aren't enough honest Vietnamese officials to do the job.

"Corruption is permeating all echelons of government and society, corroding the vitality of this nation, eroding the framework of government and unnecessarily prolonging the war," the adviser said in private reports from Vietnam. 

The reports were made available to The Associated Press on the condition that the adviser, chief of a 22-man team in Vietnam, not be identified. 

Saying the United States "must take the initiative in this remedial action and, once having started must continue it," the adviser charged: "Vietnamese officials are so involved that very few have hands sufficiently clean that they can make an immediate major independent contribution." 

ONE REPORT GOT NOWHERE 

One report, which outlined a specific plan of action, was submitted Nov. 29 to the Agency for International Development in Saigon. It went to AID's then new Public Administration Ad Hoc Committee on Corruption in Vietnam. 

An AID spokesman said here yesterday: "The committee never really got started. And it's possible it won't get going. "When you get into the business of trying to make another government clean, under international law you get into the question of sovereign nations.” 

The U.S. adviser sent a copy of the report to his superiors in Washington, along with another complaining that he had previously proposed stern measures against graft only to encounter opposition from "hearts and minds purists" in the U.S. mission.

His reports told of corruption extending to the very top of the Vietnamese customs collecting organization and said unknown millions of dollars in revenue had been lost.

The reports have been kept private because some U.S. officials feared disclosure would compromise the efforts of American advisers who rely on cooperation from 

Vietnamese officials.

GIVES SOME EXAMPLES 

In his recommendations to AID, the adviser listed 17 types of corruption and said "the list could go on indefinitely." 

His examples included: "The court officials who can keep a person locked up for months on technical, trumped-up charges until a payoff is made, or who can secure the release of a tried and convicted criminal upon a similar, but higher payoff." "The police officials whose 'check points' are synonymous with 'shakedown points.' "The custom official who sells to the highest bidder the privilege of holding down for a specified time the position where the graft and loot possibilities are the greatest." "The petty official who can't 'find' an importer's documents for processing until a fee improves his 'finding technique.'” "The very high officials who combine, and engage in smuggling, not only of dutiable merchandise, but undercut the nation's economy by smuggling gold and worst of all, that unmitigated evil-opium and other narcotics." 

DOUBTS CONVICTION CHANCES 

But he wrote that "I still could take very few persons into a regular court of law with the solid evidence I possess and stand much of a chance of convicting them on that evidence. "The institution of corruption is so much a built-in part of the government processes that it is shielded by its very pervasiveness."

GRUENING SAW REPORTS 

Sen. Ernest Gruening, D-Alaska, chairman of the Senate subcommittee on 

Foreign Aid Expenditures, and a long-time investigator of corruption in Vietnam, said "our efforts are not effective because some of our agencies aren't concerned about corruption." 

Last week Gruening, after seeing copies of the adviser's reports, told the Senate of a gold and opium smuggling operation which he said "involved the highest South Vietnamese government officials." 

President Johnson, speaking Friday in Beaumont, Tex., said "certainly, they have corruption and we also have it in Boston, in New York, in Washington and in Johnson City. Somebody is stealing something in Beaumont right now."

ACCURACY OF CHARGES OF CORRUPTION IN VIETNAM; ACTIONS TAKEN TO COMBAT CORRUPTION 

Material made available by the Treasury Department to the Subcommittee on Foreign Aid Expenditures of the Senate's Committee on Government Operations, to which Senator Williams refers in his question on Corruption in Vietnam, is of two types between which, unfortunately, some confusion has arisen. 

The first consists of monthly reports submitted by the Chief of the Treasury 

Department's Customs team in Vietnam to his Washington Headquarters.

That team, working under a Participating Agency Agreement with the USAID, is assisting the Vietnamese Customs Service to develop an efficient Fraud Repression Service. In that connection, the team provides training, proposes preventive measures and supplies any information it receives as to possible customs evasions. Cases of actions taken by the GVN Customs Service are described in the Team Chief's monthly reports. It should be noted that the cases discussed in the Subcommittee's release of February 28 (Cong. Record, p. S. 1808) were of this type. They are old cases on which the Government of Vietnam has already taken action. For example: 

Seizure of a Laotian airliner at Tan Son Nhut Airport September, 1967 on which was found some 114 kilos of gold and 200 kilos of opium. The seizure was made by the GVN Customs Service.

Seizure of $10,000 worth of PX goods. This was a raid conducted by the 

GVN Customs Service and the goods were seized by GVN Customs Service. 

The U.S. Customs Advisor notified the appropriate U.S. military authorities in view of the fact it consisted of PX goods. This occurred in June, 1967. 

Discovery that slot machines imported for the use of U.S. military institutions had been imported without paying appropriate GVN customs duties. 

This was accomplished by the GVN Customs Service. 

Public charges that Director General Loc of the GVN Customs Service was extracting payoffs and was personally involved for profit, and negligent in performance of his duties in respect to smuggling activities. The GVN itself has investigated this situation and Director-General Loc has been removed from his position as head of the GVN Customs Service. 

Raid on the "American Club," revealing operations without appropriate licenses and illicit currency operations. This was an action conducted by the GVN Customs Service. The case ultimately concluded with the permanent closing of this club by the GVN. 

Seizure of eight trucks of an American equipment firm together with the cargo. This was an action by the GVN Customs Service as part of its normal procedure in matters of confiscation. The goods were auctioned off. 

Investigation of this particular case revealed a shadow of doubt as to the efficacy of the sale and the charge that the trucks and cargo were sold on the black market. No firm evidence is available to substantiate these charges. 

A raid on the home of one operator of a jewelry concession in a PX revealed that jewelry purportedly manufactured in the U.S. and sold in the PX was in fact manufactured in Japan. The GVN has taken appropriate action to levy duties.

Many people are now pointing to these cases as evidence that the GVN and U.S. are condoning corruption or have failed to take leadership in seeking to correct corrupt practices. Actually, the reverse is the case, for these are published examples of positive action.

The second type of material made available to the Subcommittee was also from the Treasury Customs Team: in addition to his regular monthly reports,

the Chief Customs Advisor prepared an internal memorandum for an ad hoc USAID committee of which he was a member. The committee's purpose was to explore possible and alleged corruption and to recommend, for Mission consideration, methods by which the GVN could sharply reduce it. The Customs 

Chief's memorandum is prefaced by the statement that "Despite four years of observation, I still could take very few persons into a regular court of law with the solid evidence I possess and stand much of a chance of convicting them on that evidence." The Treasury Department letter transmitting these records to the Senate Subcommittee on Foreign Aid Expenditures noted that the comments contained in these reports were impressionistic, uncorroborated and unevaluated. Moreover, the letter said the reports did not necessarily reflect the views of the United States Government. In the war atmosphere of Vietnam rumors and charges of corrupt practices abound. U.S. advisors at all levels hear of and report such cases, but the collection of hard facts upon which to base prosecution is extremely difficult.

When the Agency receives generalized charges of corruption or recommendations, as those advanced by the Customs Advisor, it investigates where U.S. financed commodities are involved, and, in many cases, has adopted unilateral changes of procedures or policies for AID supported programs, and, moreover, it has presented the evidence and recommendations to the GVN for its action. 

The Government of Vietnam has ennunciated a strong policy and has taken bold action to combat corruption. President Thieu in his inaugural address said: "The most urgent effort in the first months of the future government will be to eradicate corruption at all levels from central to local government, in the Army as well as in the administration, in a complete, though just, impartial and clear manner." 

Since November 1, 1967, and up to January 31, 1968, 38 military personnel and eight civilians have been sentenced and others are under investigation. 

Thirty-four have received disciplinary action, such as reduction in grade, dismissal, etc. Furthermore, some 60 importers who were involved in corrupt business practices were suspended during the past year. The former Director of 

Customs, against whom serious charges were made, has, as noted above, been removed from office. In 1967, 138 province and district officials were removed for corruption or inefficiency.

More recently in opening a training course for province chiefs, President Thieu reiterated his anti-corruption program in the strongest terms: "If we do not remedy our spirit and our behavior, we may lose our countrynot because of military or political defeat, not because we lack an adequate policy or line of action, not because we lack programs and means, but because of bribery and corruption."

He then went on to call this situation a "national shame" and promised to continue his efforts to eliminate this evil. It was just prior to this speech that he removed from office two of the most powerful regional military commanders who were known to have been involved in corrupt practices. In addition, President Thieu has just placed in office six new province chiefs to replace those who performed poorly or were known to have been involved in corruption. 

In a televised address on the evening of March 14, Prime Minister Nguyen Van Loc devoted himself exclusively to the anti-corruption issue. He stated that his government is determined to eliminate corruption "from top to bottom" by the process of investigation and the application of strict punishment. In addition, President Thieu in a letter to the Prime Minister indicated that severe action will be taken against those selling products intended for the relief of war refugees, prohibited merchandise, or PX products. In recent days, authorities have taken measures against such violaters. 

Though working in a war zone under the most difficult operating conditions, AID has pressed forward new procedures and tougher controls to combat corruption and reduce the loss and diversion of AID commodities: 

The USAID Mission's American audit staff has been doubled and the audit program greatly increased. 

An automated arrival accounting system for AID financed commercial imports is being developed to follow imports from the license application to the delivery of commodities to the importer. (A computer was installed in 

February 1968.)

The U.S. Bureau of Customs advisory team in Vietnam now assists the 

Vietnamese Customs in improving its procedures and makes spot checks of 

AID-financed commercial imports.

Commodity analysts and industrial advisors have been assigned in Saigon to continually monitor our Commercial Import Program to ensure that full value is received.

All applications for import licenses under the Commercial Import Program are reviewed by the USAID prior to their approval by the GVN. 

American logistics advisors are stationed in provincial and regional warehouses to supervise a much tighter commodity control and distribution system.

Fertilizer was previously distributed within a government system and was often sold above official prices or distributed on a basis of favoritism. 

Fertilizer has now been put in the commercial market and many of these malpractices have been eliminated.

When the Saigon port was badly congested, there were a variety of kickbacks and bribes by importers to get their goods out more quickly. This problem was attacked successfully by eliminating the cause of the payoffs, namely port congestion.

Traditionally, import licenses have been dispensed by the government for a small under-the-table charge. Through U.S. insistence in 1965 and 1966, new procedures were established and such charges eliminated. 

AID is confident that these and other measures have reduced the loss of AID funded commodities. The Agency is constantly searching for new methods and procedures to reduce it further.

INVENTORY CONTROL 

For example, there is another case in Turkey where goods were found to have been in the warehouse since 1951, and unknown to our AID official. Having operated a small business before I came down to Washington, I am aware that one of the requirements is not only to take an inventory each year, but to determine, the management of that business had to know the age of the goods on hand. They do deteriorate and leaving goods of any description on hand for 17 years is not only a waste of the taxpayers' money if they are not needed, but the goods themselves go bad.

What steps are you taking to tighten up your inventory control to where the manager of the AID program in a given area will know that it is his responsibility not only to maintain an inventory, but to have knowledge of what is going on. If he doesn't exercise his respon-. sibility perhaps we need new managers wherever they may be, as for instance in Brazil. I was somewhat shocked, and I am sure you were, that these goods were being sold as surplus without the AID director even knowing that the warehouse existed. Now, he must have been, well, the mildest word I can think of, negligent, in meeting his responsibilities.

Mr. GAUD. Well, the case in Turkey is just like the case in Brazil in the sense these were not AID goods in the sense of being owned by AID. They were goods which were ordered by a Turkish importer from an American supplier, and for some reason or another, the Turkish importer had never picked them up. They weren't consigned to us. 

We didn't own them. They were bought under a loan which we had made which will be repaid whether or not the goods arrive. to Now, I still don't condone the fact that the goods sit there for so many years. We should have an effective system of getting notice of arrivals and seeing that these goods are utilized because, as you say, there is no sense shipping goods which are not used. 

In Turkey, sir, we have worked out with the Turkish Government a new arrival, accounting system. We have instituted a new system of reports on this, and I hope and believe it is going to be more effective. 91-392-68—pt. 2- -7

As far as what we can do, or what I can do, about making all of our people aware of this and more conscious of this it is a constant business of harping on the importance of follow through and of implementing our transactions, and of not stopping with programing but getting enough auditors, enough inspectors, enough managers so that we follow after all these things. 

I have put a good deal of emphasis on that. I sent just within the last couple of weeks a personal message to all of our mission directors on this very subject, and I will be glad to put it in the record if you would be interested in it. 

We have instituted a number of new procedures in the last year or so directed toward this end, and I can assure you, Senator Williams, that I am doing my best, we are all doing our best to try to tighten up on these procedures. (The information referred to follows:)

WASTE OF AID RESOURCES MESSAGE TO MISSION DIRECTORS 

I am sick and tired—and I trust you are-of reading reports by visiting inspectors of warehouses, equipment yards and docks containing idle or misplaced AID-financed supplies and equipment, of AID-built schools without teachers, hospitals without electricity, etc. The recurrent question is, how can visitors find these situations and recommend ways of curing them if our own technicians, auditors, in-house inspectors and Mission managers are doing their jobs? 

I am sure the answer is that, to a considerable degree, these problems are being continually uncovered and dealt with by our own staffs. It is evident, however, that not enough attention is being devoted to this aspect of our business.

Too many Division Chiefs seem to be more concerned with planning next year's program than with assuring effective use of resources put into the program last year and the year before. Too many Ministers in the recipient countries are seemingly failing to fulfill their sides of the bargain made when these supplies were purchased. Too many Missions seem to be approving new procurement actions without regard to possibilities of using supplies and equipment rendered excess by program changes in other parts of the AID Mission or host government. Too many controllers and auditors seem to be spending too much time looking at financial records and too little looking at physical resources. 

The supply/logistics staffs in the larger Missions do not always seem to be adequately geared into the programing system to avoid procurement in excess of host government inventory and pipeline positions or to the recipient agencies' maintenance and stock control competence. 

I recognize that we are in a high-risk business. If the recipient governments and other local agencies were capable of managing perfectly, the country probably would not need much foreign aid. Moreover, the necessary and desirable reliance by AID on host government management of the resources we provide will inevitably result in some disappointing performance. 

Nevertheless, the reality we must live with in maintaining public and Congressional support for the AID program is that repetitive evidence of waste and mismanagement by the recipients of our aid will be taken as proof of AID's own failings. It is a valid criticism that AID could reduce the number of these black eyes by greater attention to the implementation side of our business-without abandoning the policy of keeping management responsibility firmly fixed on the recipient countries and agencies. This can and must be done. 

I urge you to devote more of your management attention to this area of our work and to insist that supply management and other aspects of implementation be a major responsibility of all your staff in any way connected with the provision of supplies and equipment. In some Missions it may be desirable to appoint temporary inspectors to assure that all the problem areas are brought to your attention and that both immediate and continuing systems of correction in these problems are enforced. An intensive effort over the next few weeks may be the appropriate approach in some countries. Continuing attention by the Mission 

Director will be required to avoid backsliding.

I am not asking for reports to Washington, but I would welcome information on methods of dealing with this problem which may be of value to other Mission Directors.

I am asking for vigorous action to reduce the number of inspection reports of failure to see our job through to completion.

INCREASE IN JAPANESE AID TO ASIAN COUNTRIES 

We have been looking more to Japan, haven't we, for assuming leadership in Asia, and I am wondering, has Japan been increasing her aid programs to other Asian countries, either loans or concessions of any kind? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, Senator Aiken. Japan has announced a policy of increasing her aid to the point where it will reach 1 percent of her national income. She has not reached that point yet. 

Senator AIKEN. I see. But it has been increasing.

Mr. GAUD. In 1964, for example, the figure of commitments was $183 million. In 1965, it was $317 million. In 1966, it was $351 million. In 

1967, I haven't got the exact figure here, I can supply it, I understand it was higher still. (The information referred to follows) 

JAPANESE AID 

In 1967, preliminary figures indicate commitments reached about $440 million. 

Private flows have been adding another $250 million or so annually. 

Mr. GAUD. She is definitely assuming more responsibility, and this has been particularly evident, I think, in connection with the Asian 

Development Bank. 

Japan subscribed 20 percent of the capital of that bank-$200 million-which is the same amount that we did. Japan was the first country to come forward with a pledge to make a contribution to the special fund of the Asian Development Bank-$100 million. 

These are two examples of what she has been doing lately in the way of increasing her influence in the aid business. 

Senator AIKEN. Would you say that with respect to carrying out these programs in Asia, that the Japanese presence is as welcome as our own?

Mr. GAUD. Yes, I think so.

Senator AIKEN. That might not apply to every country equally, suppose, but on the whole you would say that the Japanese presence is accepted and welcomed!

 We have had very good evidence of that in Indonesia lately. And you will remember a couple of years back_when there was quite a fracas over a peace treaty between Korea and Japan. 

There was some doubt as to whether large amounts of Japanese aid would be welcomed in Korea, and it has certainly developed that they were welcomed and it is being made good use of. 

Senator AIKEN. Well, that is an improvement.

INCREASE IN RICE PRICES IN VIETNAM 

I received a report recently that the price of rice had risen 25 percent in the last month in South Vietnam, particularly Saigon. 

If true, what is the reason for it? 

Mr. GAUD. The price of rice rose very fast and very dramatically as a result of the Tet offensive. The reason being that Route 4 to the delta was cut repeatedly by the Viet Cong, and supplies were not getting through.

Within the last week or 10 days the price of rice has begun to drop again. It has dropped to where it is still a little bit higher than it was

before the Tet offensive but not remarkably so, and we are all kind of holding our breath and keeping our fingers crossed as to what happens in the future. Because unless supplies of rice can reach Saigon from the delta, there is going to be much more pressure on shipments of rice into the country. This is going to present problems of utilization of the port. There may also be problems as to whether there is enough rice to go around. So we are very much concerned about what does happen on the rice front. 

Senator AIKEN. And they are looking to us for more rice than ever now, I believe.

Mr. GAUD. Well, we haven't received any additional requests since the Tet offensive. We expect to be able to avoid that if shipments from the delta are possible.

INFLATION IN VIETNAM 

Senator AIKEN. As I started to say to Secretary Rusk the other day-until I found that the topics relating to aid were not the topics of that day, that were acceptable anyway-that the question of inflation during a war particularly, makes a few people richer, and a lot of people poorer. I was wondering if that isn't taking place in Southeast

Asia and as they get poorer, what is the tendency, to come to us for relief, or are they more inclined to go to the opposition?

Mr. GAUD. Well, again I can only talk about Vietnam on this subject pre-Tet. But let me talk about that just a little bit with regard to inflation.

Inflation, as you say, has been a very serious problem there all through the war. Over the 3 year period between 1965 and 1967 the total increase was about 200 percent. 

Only 35 percent of that was in the last year. The rate has been pretty well kept down. And to give you some basis of comparison, in a comparable 3-year period during the Korean war, the inflation was not 200 percent, it was 2400 percent 12 times as bad.

Senator AIKEN. Where? 

Mr. GAUD. In Korea. 

Senator AIKEN. Yes.

Mr. GAUD. Twenty-four hundred percent, whereas it has been held in Vietnam to 200 percent, and in 1967 was down to 35 percent. 

Now, the people in the cities, the people in Saigon in particular, were well off-full employment, plenty of goods to buy, high salaries and all the rest of it.

The big question, of course, is what about the countryside? I was out there in January-it was the first time I had been there in about a year and a half--and the thing that struck me very much was the increase in the well-being, the economic well-being, of the peasants in a good many parts of the delta and in some of the other parts of the country. This was due primarily to increased agricultural production. 

The roads at that time-the roads from the delta to Saigon-were open. The Saigon government was maintaining the price of rice high enough so that the peasant in the delta could get a good price for his crop. There were plenty of consumer goods for the peasant to buy. He was getting fertilizer distributed by the farm groups and through the private sector, and there was a remarkable increase in the welfare of the peasant in the delta.

In a number of small town market places, for example, I saw motorcycles, bicycles, sewing machines and radios for sale. Our people in 

Saigon reckon that in the last year some 40 to 50 thousand, two horsepower pumps were bought by peasants in the delta and used for irrigation. With the new seeds they are using for rice, with the increased fertilizer, with these pumps, you were beginning to see more and more double cropping which greatly increased the prosperity of the farmer. 

Now, this was all before January 30th.

What the situation will be in the future is pretty hard to tell. Will the farmer be able to go ahead with his double cropping plans? Will he be able to get his rice to Saigon? Will he get a good price for it?

All of these questions are open today-you just can't tell what is going to happen.

Senator AIKEN. We don't know what is going to happen here, either. 

I have an article on this question which I would like inserted in the record. 

(The article referred to follows:) [From the Washington Star, Mar. 2, 1968] FARM TRAFFIC TO SAIGON CUT AS REDS HARASS DELTA ROUTE By Donald Kirk, Asia Correspondent of The Star 

SAIGON.-The main overland route through the rice-rich highly populated 

Mekong River delta is still largely unused due to Viet Cong terrorism and government harassment.

Traffic over Route 4 which goes from the southern coast to Saigon, is only 10 to 15 percent of its volume before the series of Communist attacks during Tet, the Vietnamese lunar new year, according to official sources here. 1 13 

BIEN HOA TAN SON 

NHUT ​4 عيد لد Saigon 2 عيد 

S. VIETNAM 

-United Press International

One official said that road was regularly mined and cratered by Viet Cong terror units even though both American and South Vietnamese troops have placed top priority on keeping the road open.

I. 

The official said that continuing problems along route 4 would have “most serious long-range repercussions" on the economy of both the Mekong Delta and Saigon, which depends on the delta for the bulk of its food supplies." "Saigon will become much more dependent on foreign-mostly Americanaid," said the official, "while production in the delta decreases and farmers are forced to dump their produce at lower prices.' 99 The net effect, according to sources here would be both to undo attempts at reviving the delta economy, lifeblood of the nation, and to turn Saigon into a "foreign dependency" kept alive only by large infusions of foreign food. 

The official said Route 4 was theoretically open from Saigon all the way to Ca 

Mau on the southern tip of Vietnam. But he qualified that remark by saying “if you were driving in a tank, you might be able to go all the way."

He said the road had been mined scores of times by Viet Cong terrorists, who had hit hardest in the crucial midsection along the Mekong River between My 

Thuan and My Tho 50 miles south of Saigon. My Thuan, 30 miles west of My

Tho, is a key ferry crossing through which, in normal times, several thousand vehicles pass each day.

FIRING FROM SCOOTERS 

Besides mining the highway, said officials, Viet Cong terrorists for the first time have taken to firing from the backs of Lambrettas, three-wheeled Italian motorscooters popular as a means of public transport throughout South Vietnam. "This technique is entirely new for the Viet Cong" said the official. "They pull aside the canvas in the back of a Lambretta and fire point-blank at passing trucks. That tends to discourage drivers from wanting to use the road."

Another factor discouraging the use of Route 4, even on the most secure stretch between My Tho and Saigon, is the enforcement by the government of different curfew hours on different parts of the route. "Sometimes the curfew is on from 6 at night to 8 in the morning, in other places its from 5 p.m. to 7 a.m.," said one source here. "Traffic doesn't get moving generally until around 10 a.m. instead of early in the morning as it normally did." 911: 

BRIBES SIZABLE 

Truck drivers charge, moreover, that they're compelled to pay sizable bribes at police checkpoints en route. One source complained that the Viet Cong, if they wished, could send arms into Saigon by bribing the police while small farmers were discouraged by the system from shipping badly needed produce. 

The problems created by the Communist attacks have had such an effect that one of Saigon's leading rice merchants, a Chinese with modern offices near the National Bank here, has ordered his buyers to stop all business until the situation becomes more clear.

The merchant, who asked to remain anonymous, said the rice crop this year was very good but insisted the Government or army provide security or transportation before he authorized his men to buy it for resale here. "Seventy percent of the newly harvested rice is still on the farm,” said the merchant. "The rice mills in the country do not have enough oil to operate, and the Viet Cong will make trouble for those bringing rice to the city. 

EXPECTATIONS ON RICE 

American officials said they had hoped Vietnam this year would produce at least 4.5 million tons of paddy rice, a small increase from last year's harvest of 

4.4 million tons. They now hear that rice production may go down still further, even though conceding this winter's crop was excellent. Until several years ago 

Vietnam was a rice exporting nation, growing more than 6 million tons annually. 

Economic observers here report that farmers raising pigs and other livestock have suffered even more than rice farmers. 

The reason is that most of the livestock is grown in the lower delta, more than 

200 miles from Saigon, and must travel farther to market. "We've had reports of farmers having to get rid of ducks, vegetables and fruit at cut-rate prices,” said an official. "Rural prosperity in the delta depends on good transport with Saigon."

The official said both American and South Vietnamese officials had considered the possibilities of airlifting food to the capital but had dismissed the idea as too expensive. It's cheaper to import from outside," he said “Airlifting costs something like a dollar a pound."

CONFIDENCE UNDERMINED 400 dedi. bine Toisille en 

The over-all effect of the Tet offensive, said the official, was to completely undermine the confidence of businessmen in all phases of Vietnamese life. He said the Saigon government was considering introduction of an insurance scheme, but admitted the project was far from fruition. 

Another idea for restoring confidence, said the official, was paying "war damages" for damage already done to merchant's holdings and business. But he admitted Vietnamese officials had not "decided on the exact form" for this effort and indicated the decision might lie far in the future. Eshiell bisz telonu edT 

While farmers get less for their goods in the countryside, Saigon food prices are still considerably above what they were before the lunar new year. 

The price of low-grade rice, most commonly used by the mass of city dwellers, is 25 percent higher today than a month ago, even though general retail prices have declined 6 percent from last week. Pork is still 12 percent above the pre-Tet level.

Officials attribute the gradually declining Saigon prices to the release of American and Thai rice on the market and the reopening of routes to farm areas near the city.

FREIGHT FROM DALAT 

sh of nodal ared unit

An official said vegetable products were moving here from Dalat, 160 miles to the North, and also pointed out that goods could move from vegetable-growing regions immediately south of the city. But he said the railroad, which had operated on a limited basis before Tet, was now virtually closed. 

As indications of the extent to which Saigon has become dependent on foreign food, the official noted that half of Saigon's daily pork consumption of 70 tons was now frozen pork, most of it shipped here from abroad. Before Tet only oneseventh of the pork eaten here each day was frozen.

The city is now so dependent on frozen pork, he said, that the government has announced plans to buy 2,000 tons of it. The city at the time of Tet had some 1,200 tons of frozen pork in storage.

Officials also said that more than half Saigon's daily rice consumption is from abroad, as opposed to less than 20 percent before Tet. The city had considerable rice stores before Tet but now needs to replenish them, said an official. "It seems the Viet Cong have changed their policies," said one official. "They are no longer observing the semi-truce that existed in the delta. Perhaps they were afraid we were making too much progress. No one realized what would happen."

SIPHONING OFF OF SUPPLIES MEANT FOR REFUGEES 

In connection with our discussion yesterday concerning the amount of aid we had siphoned off into the private pockets, I realize this is very hard to ascertain but I would like to read into the record this one paragraph from the speech by a colleague, Mr. Kennedy of 

Massachusetts: 

Let me detail some examples in the refugee field. We have now given South 

Vietnam $30 million a year for refugee relief. In my many conversations with the hard-pressed American refugee personnel, it was estimated that only half of the supplies ever reach the refugee. The officials of the government of South 

Vietnam, and the province chiefs supported by them, have the keys to the warehouses, and they keep much of the goods for themselves, Each refugee is supposed to receive the equivalent of $45 for resettlement. It was estimated to me by a United States official adviser to the refugee program that 75 percent of this amount is siphoned off before it reaches these people.

I just put this into the record as a matter of interest to me. I know 

Senator Kennedy put a great deal of effort on this and really dug in deep in that trip. I realize his estimates are different from your estimates and we will just let them stand that way.
