PUBLICIZING SENATE STAFF SALARIES 

I want to make another comment congratulating you on the fact that I think your presentation this time is the most informative and the most intelligent that you have ever made. You have said for the first time you have declassified certain matters which have been held classified, and that you have given us specific figures on specific countries. You recognized, as we all know, the handicaps that you have in doing that because one country knows its own needs and the needs of its neighbors and concludes they are not getting enough; I don't suppose they conclude they get too much, but not enough. I hope this is not like the reform we imposed on ourselves at one time. 

There used to be a great deal of excitement displayed by our friends in the press. They alleged that some kind of hanky-panky was going on because the Senators did not disclose the salaries of the members of their own staffs and while they could get it, it was a painful process in going to the disbursing office. They said they couldn't understand why the Senators didn't practice what they preached and publish personally the names of their staff with the salaries. 

So we decided to do it.

I regret to say to my friends in the press this has cost the American taxpayer many, many tens of thousands, I am afraid, perhaps hundreds of thousands of dollars. I will give you the experience on my own staff.

I put the list out, published in the little book they put out and all the members of my staff, and I suppose all the members of every other Senator's staff, looked around and saw what they were paying in somebody else's office for a job of the same classification. I come from a rural 

State where salaries are not as high as they are in metropolitan areas for understandable reasons, but when you transport a rural employee down into the metropolitan area they have the same living expenses as everybody else and they came in and said, "Senator, we have been here longer than Susie Jones over on Senator Pell's staff, or somebody's else's staff, and Susie is getting $1,500 a year more than I am. What's wrong with my work here? Aren't you satisfied? What reason have you got for not paying me as much as Susie Jones ?”

I couldn't think of any reason. I thought hard. I didn't want to lose them. So I had to make some quick promotions in salary for virtually every member of my staff and on occasion, I am still continuing to make them to some bright-eyed employee, if he finds someone on somebody else's staff doing the same kind of work. 

So I am not sure that this is going to result in economy in this area. 

I hope it does, but it failed us ingloriously in responding to the legitimate desires of the press to find out who was getting paid how much, but it was bad for the American taxpayer.

DISPARITY OF AID REQUIREMENTS OF INDIA AND PAKISTAN 

Now, getting to this breakdown of country by country items, I have a couple of questions.

Before we got into the digression which proved to be a fruitful one, as to what do you do when there is corruption, or what do you do when there is misapplication of funds or wrong distribution of supplies, you discussed in your main presentation the problem between India and Pakistan. This is a very tricky situation for our country and for you, for the Defense Department, so we don't get in the position of subsidizing a new arms race which simply accelerates and escalates the controversy which, unfortunately, exists between these two countries at a higher and more costly and more dangerous level. 

Now, I don't know whether the figures 408 versus 177 on page 2 indicate we are giving better consideration to the Indians than the Pakistani, or whether it indicates that the needs of India are greater simply on a population basis, but I would like something in the record explaining the disparity, the difference between those two unfriendly neighbors.

Mr. GAUD. Well, the difference is really based on our estimate—and it isn't our estimate alone of the relative requirements of the two countries, Senator Mundt. In each of these cases there is a consortium headed by the World Bank with roughly a dozen aid-giving countries as members.

The requirements of India and Pakistan for the coming year have been reviewed by the consortium, and these figures represent, roughly speaking, what you might call the normal U.S. contribution to the consortium.

India is, as you have suggested, a much larger country than Pakistan in terms of people, in terms of size, and so it is only natural to expect that their requirements for aid would be larger. Their budget is much larger. You have got a population of 500 million in the one case and a population of about 118 million in the other case. 

But these figures, as I say, have not been arrived at on an arbitrary basis. It is really a study made by the consortium under the leadership of the World Bank of the requirements of the two countries. 

Senator MUNDT. Is there anything in your budgetary request dealing with military assistance?

Mr. GAUD. As far as the military assistance part of the bill is concerned, I believe there is a small item for training as far as these two countries are concerned, and I don't know the details of it, but the Department of Defense will be testifying here on Monday and perhaps you can find out what you need from them.

ANALYZING DEFENSE EXPENDITURES OF RECIPIENT COUNTRIES 

Senator MUNDT. In determining the economic aid we make available, do you analyze the budgets of the respective countries? Taking these two as a laboratory test now, do you analyze the budgets of these respective countries to determine whether or not they are making a disproportionate contribution toward military support for themselves as against meeting their economic problems? Is that one of the criteria that you apply?

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir, we do, and we are specifically directed to do that by an amendment to the Foreign Assistance Act that was adopted last year and is now embodied in 620 (s), the Symington amendment. 

Under that amendment, we have to make such an analysis of the budget of every country to which we give aid. 

India and Pakistan are particularly important in this regard because of the amount of aid we give them. We analyze their budgets, we watch the trend in the budgets from year to year, see what percentage of the budgets are going to defense, whether they are increasing or decreasing, see what percentage of their GNP they are devoting to defense. We have made a rather elaborate study of this in the case of both these countries and have concluded that in the light of their situation, their expenditures for defense are reasonable. 

Pakistan's defense expeditures are decreasing fairly sharply. They were raised after the war between India and Pakistan. They are now dropping quite fast.

India did not increase her expenditures for defense after the India- Pakistan war, and on a real basis they, too, are not increasing at present.

So that as of now on the basis of all the information that is available to us, we are satisfied. And I might say, for example, to give you some idea of how we work on this, we have withheld going forward with our program loan for India this year until we were able to look at the Indian budget which just came out a few days ago. 

REPORTED INCREASE IN INDIAN DEFENSE BUDGET 

Senator MUNDT. Yes, I was leading up to that, and because a friend, in whose judgment I place considerable confidence, visited me recently to point out that it was his view on the basis of his knowledge, that what you say is precisely correct about Pakistan, they have decreased their military budget. It was his impression that India had increased its military budget.

Well, he said it has gone up. I know about the Symington amendment which I supported enthusiastically. This is his feeling and impression. I put the statement in the Congressional Record and he gave me an article from the London Observer of March 3, 1968, which was only 2 days ago. It is headed, "India." One of these curious British headlines, under which it says, "One rupee in three on defence." It is dated New Delhi, 2 March. 

I will read the first paragraph:

One out of every three rupees collected as revenue in India is being spent on the armed services. The Finance Minister, Mr. Morarji Desai, has now raised the defense budget by another 25 million pounds, to the highest figure since 

Indian independence-605 million pounds.

I will put the rest of the news story in the record. 

Now, if that news story is correct, and I have no basis for believing it is not correct, it would seem to indicate that under the Symington amendment something has gotten a little out of hand in New Delhi. 

Mr. GAUD. I would like to have a chance to comment on that article after reviewing the actual facts. 

Senator MUNDT. Right. It may be that these developments are fairly current.

Mr. GAUD. My recollection is that in real terms, i.e., discounting price rises and inflation in India, there has not been any real increase in Indian defense expenditures, nor any decrease in the proportion of the budget that is devoted to development. 

Senator MUNDT. I am sure you are in no position to argue the point.

Mr. GAUD. Right. 

Senator MUNDT. I have the four news stories he brought in and I will put them all in the record.

Mr. GAUD. Good. 

Senator MUNDT. I will just read one other paragraph from the one I quoted from the London Observer. 

It says:

India has recently bought four Russian submarines and means to acquire more. Some Russian frigates are expected by the middle of the year. Over 100 supersonic Russian aircraft are coming to Delhi shortly and, reportedly, some artillery equipment as well.

Now, my friend is neither particularly pro-Indian nor pro-Pakistan or anti-Indian or anti-Pakistan, and he says, "Look, what are you fellows doing? They are continuing to pour money in without any restraints." 

Mr. GAUD. The point is well taken.

Senator MUNDT. So I will put it all in the record, as well as the other articles and after you have had a chance to verify the newspaper stories or disprove them or check from other sources, would you submit your comments for the record later. 

(The articles and AID comment referred to follow :) [From the Observer (London), Mar. 3, 1968] ONE RUPEE IN THREE ON DEFENCE From C. P. Ramachandran 

New Delhi, 2 March.-One out of every three rupees collected as revenue in 

India is being spent on the armed services. The Finance Minister, Mr Morarji 

Desai, has now raised the defence. 

Even this is not the entire story, because quite a number of regular and paramilitary units are financed separately by the Home Ministry, and so are certain defence production units.

SUPERSONIC AIRCRAFT 

Some Russian frigates are expected by the middle of the year. 

This is all intended to offset any possible drying-up of defence equipment from the Western Powers, as happened during the Indo-Pakistan conflict. 

Although Members of Parliament bewail the lot of the common man, there is hardly any group or party or even an individual MP who will raise a squeak against this vast expenditure on defence. 

[From the Times (London), Mar. 2, 1968] PILGRIMS SET OFF WAR SCARE 

Delhi, March 1.-Angry M.P.s thumped their benches in Parliament today and demanded that the Indian Navy, Air Force and Army be placed on an immediate alert. An urgent Cabinet meeting has been called and Mrs. Gandhi, the Prime Minister, assured the House that all diplomatic measures would be used to resolve the crisis, which loomed suddenly today. 

The scene of the dispute is the inoffensive Roman Catholic church of St. 

Anthony on the uninhabited island of Kachadiva, 12 miles off the Indian coast and midway between India and Ceylon.

There has been a dormant dispute over the island between India and Ceylon for 50 years. Visited once a year by Roman Catholic pilgrims, Kachadiva offers vital strategic value. St. Anthony's Church has no drinking water, but there is a mile-long, 300 yd. wide stretch of white sand.

In Parliament today indignant M.P.'s cited reports suggesting that Ceylon had occupied St. Anthony's. Members of the opposition, still incensed over the 

Kutch award, called on the Prime Minister for immediate action. "It is no use protesting any more. What is our Army, Navy and Air Force doing. Send them in", several members argued.

FRIENDLY RELATIONS 

Mrs. Gandhi assured the House that the Government was taking immediate action. "I appreciate the anxiety", she said. "We do not have full information at the moment. We have friendly relations with Ceylon and I would ask the House not to force me into saying anything which might make things more difficult." 

Several M.P.s called on the Prime Minister to make a categoric statement that this was Indian territory. Mrs. Gandhi said the High Commissioner in Ceylon was taking up the matter.

It is suggested in Delhi tonight that the "occupying force" of Kachadiva probably consists of the Ceylonese pilgrims who sail to the island every March for religious festivals.

INDIAN BUILD-UP SOURCE OF CONCERN FOR NEIGHBOURS-DELHI'S POLICY WILL HAMPER DEVELOPMENT IN REGION 

(Source unknown)

From M. A. Mansuri. 

ISLAMABAD, March 1.-Experts on Indian affairs here said the actual outlay on India's defence for the next year, showing an increase of Rs 450 million, will be much more than Rs 10,150 million announced by the Indian Finance 

Minister in the Lok Sabha yesterday.

Last year also the Indian Budget included the total outlay on defence at Rs 9,700 million, but on closer analysis it was found that various items concerning defence were not shown in the defence Budget and the actual expenditure on defence was about Rs 11,500 million.

Official sources here were still awaiting a fuller report on the Indian Budget and would not offer any comment until they have studied its full implications. 

However, this is apparent that a rapid increase in India's defence Budget over the past five years has been a source of concern for smaller neighbouring countries because it forces them to enter into an arms race with India at the cost of their development plans which they can hardly afford. Even if the figures announced by Indian Finance Minister yesterday are taken on their face value, an increase of Rs 450 million over the past year's estimate indicates that India is determined to carry on with her plan to become the mightiest military Power in the region.

So far as Pakistan is concerned, it has explained to India on many occasions that it is wedded to a policy of peace and friendly relations with all neighbouring countries, especially India, because it believes that this is the only asset through which it can pursue its gigantic task of economic and industrial development. 

But naturally it has to safeguard the security of the country which is uppermost in all its policies.

Pakistan finds it hard to believe that any invasion of India is likely to occur through NEFA or Ladakh. On the basis of its past experience Pakistan believes that in the absence of any settlement of the Kashmir dispute, the massive military build-up in India is more likely to be deployed against Pakistan than for 

India's own defence.

SABRE-RATTLING BY CHAVAN AGAINST PAKISTAN 

(Source unknown)

NEW DELHI, March 1.-Home Minister Y. B. Chavan said in the Lok Sabha yesterday that India's attitude towards Pakistan would be on the basis of "flexible response", according to PTI.

Intervening in the debate on the motion of non-confidence in the India Government Mr. Chavan was quoted as saying: "We know Pakistan's foreign policy is based on hostility towards India. "We cannot forget her flirtations with China. We cannot forget she is trying to encourage subversive elements in the eastern part of India. Our attitude will be based on flexible response, if it is aggression, certainly we will have to respond in the same way. If it is subversion, it will have to be subversion".-APP.

FACTS RELATIVE TO ARTICLE IN OBSERVER PRESS RELEASE OF MARCH 3, 1968 (AID) 

800 The Observer is correct in saying that roughly one rupee out of three of central revenue in India goes for defense. However, India runs a large budgetary deficit and hence the comparison of defense expenditures to total expenditures (about 1/5) is the more relevant comparison. de highe

The proposed 1968/69 budget recently laid before Parliament shows defense expenditures to be 19% of total expenditures. They are up 4.6% from last year.

This is much lower than the rate of increase in the price level-prices increased 15% last year-and it is therefore likely that the new budget represents a decline in the actual use of resources for defense. The Indian Government estimates that the total Central Government expenditure on defense will amount to 3.2% of 

Gross National Product for the Fiscal Year which will begin April 1, 1968, a decline from the 4.4% level of 1963/64.

SELECTION OF RECIPIENT COUNTRIES IN AFRICA 

Senator MUNDT. One other question and I am through.

You can do this on specific terms or general terms, but I just wonder out of all the countries in Africa how you select the seven that you give aid to? How you approach it? There seems to be quite a difference between the amount the different African countries get. I have no quarrel with this, but I am wondering, since you must have a lot of applicants that you turn down. What are the general criteria that you use?

Mr. GAUD. The prevailing trend in our program in Africa is toward regionalism. Two or three years ago, Senator Mundt, you will recall that we were giving aid and had what you might call general aid programs in some 30-odd African countries.

This committee, in particular, took the lead in reducing the number of countries to which we could give aid. Partly in response to that specific requirement of the law and also in response to a general reassessment of aid policy which took into account a report done by Ambassador Korry, we decided it made sense, conditions in Africa being what they are, to shift more and more of our aid to Africa to a regional basis and to discontinue bilateral aid programs in most of the countries in Africa.

We are pretty well along in that now, and we have decided to continue with full bilateral aid programs in only nine countries in Africa and to phase out our regular bilateral aid programs in all of the others. 

Assistance to these countries will be through regional or multilateral projects as well as by continuing Public Law 480, the Peace Corps and the special self-help funds which finance small self-help projects in these countries. 

Senator MUNDT. Is there something permanently unique about these seven countries which still get bilateral aid or are you planning to

phase them out ultimately and make them part of the regional program too?

Mr. GAUD. Well, there is nothing final about the decision. The countries are the ones which, it seems to us, have the most opportunity, the best prospects, of moving ahead; where we feel that the bilateral program will pay off. 

Tunisia and Morocco, for example, are making quite a lot of progress, Ghana, Liberia, Ethiopia, the three countries in East Africa- Uganda, Kenya and Tanzania-these are all countries where we feel it makes sense to carry on bilateral programs. 

Mr. GAUD. It just so happens in fiscal year 1969 we propose to make development loans to only six countries, but there are three others which would be eligible for development loans. Now, we don't plan presently any for next year in those three countries. 

Senator MUNDT. I see. So the list will stop with Uganda.

Mr. GAUD. For '69. 

Senator MUNDT. Right. 

Mr. GAUD. But there are several other countries which we consider eligible for development loans, although we don't presently plan to make any next year.

CRITERIA FOR SUPPLYING AID Senator MUNDT. Are Tunisia, Morocco, Ghana, Liberia, Ethiopia and Uganda countries which you would consider friendly countries to the United States? For example, you used the criterion of friendliness, which may not be accurate, the general support of our American position in the United Nations, for example? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir, I would say these are friendly countries under that definition. 

Senator MUNDT. Yes. Because I was a little curious about Ghana. I don't criticize this, I have never been there. I don't know anything about Ghana, except what I read occasionally in the news stories, which are getting three times as prevalent as the Philippines. I know something about the Philippines. They are friendly people who are joining us in the fight in Vietnam. I was wondering why the need is greater in Ghana than in the Philippines?

Mr. GAUD. Well, Ghana has been in rather a mess as a result of the administration of President Nkrumah, who has not been president of the country since 1966, as you know. She is making today a serious effort, as we see it, to get out of the financial and economic mess that she is in. We have gone into an intergovernmental arrangement with other countries to try to help Ghana get on her feet. We have undertaken to come up with roughly one-third of the total amount of aid that is going to Ghana today, to support a stabilization program to get her out of this rather different debt situation she is in at present. 

And one would hope that at some time, after a period of a few years, with her natural resources, if she continues the self-help measures that she is following today, that Ghana will be relatively well off and will not need the amount of money that she needs today to continue her development.

We are working there with the IMF and with these other countries with this end in view. 91-392-68-pt. 2-

Senator MUNDT. Would it be a rather fair and accurate generalization to say, then, that your thinking on Ghana is something like your thinking on Indonesia, she is getting a new leader?

Mr. GAUD. Precisely. 

Senator MUNDT. So we are going to give her a little help?

Mr. GAUD. Precisely. 

Senator MUNDT. I couldn't quarrel with that.

LIMITING NUMBER OF RECIPIENT COUNTRIES 

Are you proposing or supporting or just accepting reluctantly in this presentation an amendment which I had the pleasure of offering, and I do support, specifying some kind of top limit of countries to which 

AID could go? We had that in the last bill.

Mr. GAUD. We have no objection to that at all, no problem. 

Senator MUNDT. Thank you, that is all. 

Senator PELL. Thank you very much.

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1968 TUESDAY, MAY 14, 1968 UNITED STATES SENATE, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN RELATIONS, 

Washington, D.C.

The committee met, pursuant to notice, at 10:05 a.m., in room 4221, New Senate Office Building, Senator John Sparkman presiding. 

Present: Senators Sparkman and Carlson. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Let the committee come to order, please.

The Committee on Foreign Relations is meeting today to continue hearings on S. 3091, the Foreign Assistance Act of 1968. 

This morning we shall hear testimony from representatives of various organizations interested in the foreign aid program. 

Because of the large number of witnesses scheduled to testify we would appreciate it if each witness would limit his testimony to a brief summary of his prepared statement. The full text of the statement will, of course, be printed in the record. 

All comments and suggestions will be given careful consideration by the committee.

Our first witness this morning is Mrs. Lois Van Valkenburgh, Citizens Committee for UNICEF, Washington, D.C.

Come around, Mrs. Van Valkenburgh. We are very glad to have you.

STATEMENT OF MRS. LOIS W. VAN VALKENBURGH, DEPUTY EXECUTIVE SECRETARY, CITIZENS COMMITTEE FOR UNICEF 

Mrs. VAN VALKENBURGH. Thank you. I have with me Mrs. Virginia 

Gray, executive secretary of the Citizens Committee for UNICEF.

Senator SPARKMAN. Very good. 

Mrs. VAN VALKENBURGH. I do not intend to read my entire statement, but I will summarize it. 

I am Mrs. Lois W. Van Valkenburgh, deputy executive secretary of the Citizens Committee for UNICEF. The Citizens Committee for 

UNICEF is an informal clearing house of legislative information serving a number of national organizations which support continued participation by the United States in the United Nations Children's Fund (UNICEF). Some of these organizations send their individual representative to present their views to you. Others, listed below, have authorized this joint statement. 

I will not read the organizations.

This year 26 national organizations have joined in endorsing the views which follow. Fifteen years ago, in 1953, the Citizens Committee for UNICEF testified on behalf of nine national organizations. (345)

This impressive increase in the number of organizations which has taken place is, nevertheless, only a small indication of the growth of citizen approval for the work which UNICEF is doing to aid sick and hungry children around the world. In addition, many Americans demonstrate their interest and support for UNICEF directly by their purchase of UNICEF's charming greeting cards at the holiday season and by the encouragement of their young people in the trick-or-treat for UNICEF drive at Halloween. 

$13 MILLION CONTRIBUTION TO UNICEF 

The members of the organizations endorsing this statement urge your approval of a contribution of $13 million to UNICEF in fiscal 1969. For the past 8 years (9 years if the State Department recommendation stands) the U.S. regular contribution to the Children's 

Fund has remained at the same figure: $12 million. In a period when most costs are increasing at an alarming rate, it would be equivalent to a decrease in material support if the U.S. contribution to the work of the Children's Fund did not make some allowance for this fact. An increase to $15 million could be well justified but we limit our request to a $1 million increase over fiscal 1968. 

We request your specific approval of a contribution of $13 million to UNICEF. In this year when billions can be spent without hesitation for military purposes, surely this sum can be found to assist sick and hungry children around the world, including emergency aid in 

Vietnam and the Middle East.

UNICEF's assistance policies have been reviewed and endorsed by its Executive Board, by ECOSOC and by the General Assembly of the U.N. Indeed, the soundness of its policies is reflected by the assisted governments themselves in the rising volume of requests for aid which are being submitted. All governments in the world's family of nations should not give effective substance to their own laudatory speeches of commendation. In 1967, 35 governments increased their contributions, compared with 1966. A number of these have made several increases since 1960, while the U.S. contribution has remained the same. In 1967, 123 governments contributed to UNICEF's central account. These contributions are, of course, in addition to internal matching funds provided by UNICEF assisted countries. 

Even after taking into account the growing volume of private contributions to UNICEF resulting from the increased activities of national UNICEF committees in 24 countries, the Children's Fund could usefully allocate $10 million more at the upcoming June session of its Executive Board, solely on the basis of valid project requests. ready for submission if the funds were available to finance them. 

It is estimated, for example, that 40 percent of the population in the developing countries is under 15 years of age. Of these, 450 million. live in countries with a national per capita income of under $100. 

In the face of the enormous needs of children in the developing countries, the question might well be asked whether there is anything effective that UNICEF could do, even if the funds were available.

UNICEF GRANTS ON A COUNTRY BASIS 

The Executive Board's response to this kind of question has been to base its grants to projects on a country approach: to support those projects most likely to advance the welfare and development of children according to the priorities of the country concerned. UNICEF assistance is focused on the problems of health, nutrition, responsible parenthood, and education. Its aid represents a contribution to both welfare and economic development, both for the individual and for the Nation. One of the main criteria for the coherence of a country's policy for children and youth is that it take into account the growth cycle of the child through various ages, each with successive and somewhat different needs. An appendix to this statement gives a table showing the main fields of UNICEF assistance and the amount allocated to each by the Executive Board in 1967 arranged by age grouping of beneficiaries. UNICEF is currently assisting 496 projects in 119 countries.

Although UNICEF's main role is to aid projects for the longrange benefit of children in developing countries, the present period of world tensions has brought added demands on UNICEF's resources. 

A needy child is a chronic emergency, of course. But, in addition, world events have forced UNICEF to provide assistance in three regions because of circumstances dictated by natural disaster or conflicts. These are India, the Middle East and Vietnam. An allocation of $1.4 million was made to drought-stricken India in 

1967. For the Middle East, $496,000 was approved last June for emergency refugee relief, and an additional $512,000 was approved by a recent mail poll of Executive Board members. For Vietnam, close to $1 million has been approved during the past year for health and social services, for education, for tuberculosis control and for emergency aid.

CONTRIBUTIONS OF OTHER NATIONS 

During the past 8 years when the United States' regular contribution has remained at the same figure, other governments and private contributions have accounted for a modest growth in UNICEF's income. 

It should be pointed out, however, that governments have made a very substantial increase in contributions to various other organizations. 

Since 1963 WHO's budget has more than doubled, as has FAO's; other agencies have increased almost as much. During the same period 

UNICEF's income has risen only 26.8 percent. Members of our organizations are reluctant to draw these comparisons. We have no intention of disparaging the excellent work done by these other agencies and hasten to point out the close cooperation which UNICEF continues to maintain with them. It is only realistic, however, to recognize the fact that some of these increases have come about because the financing of the agency is determined by assessments which are voted by member countries. Thus the individual member government has little control over the level of financial support which they must be prepared to render. We submit that the increasing demands upon many govern-

ments, with rigidly limited resources, by such increases in assessments have operated to restrict an increase in the amount of the voluntary contribution which such government is in a position to make to the Children's Fund. Indeed, it is primarily the main donors to UNICEF that have been compelled to face a higher volume of obligations through increasing assessments for the Specialized Agencies. We believe that there is merit in the system of voluntary support for humanitarian causes a principle which the United States has traditionally supported and we recommend that our Government now demonstrate its leadership toward the world's children by free choice instead of under the pressure of an imposed assessment. Essential as the programs are in all of these organizations, none is more fundamental than UNICEF's primary goal of aiding the health and preparation for a useful life of the oncoming generations of the world's children.

What the nations of the world do or do not do for the world's children in the next decade may well determine whether development becomes indeed a reality and the path to peace, or whether it is merely an empty slogan. UNICEF cannot, of course, aspire to do all that needs to be done but it could make a much greater contribution with additional resources. In his state of the Union address, President Johnson issued a clear call to action when he stated that "But we must also improve the lives of children who are already born in the villages, in the towns and the cities on this earth." It is noted also that he mentioned aid to the world's children at least three times in his foreign aid message "To Build the Peace." In the same vein, Mr. Labouisse, the Executive 

Director of UNICEF, said in his report to UNICEF's Executive

Board, "In an era when the words 'reaching for the moon' no longer means striving for the impossible, but simply describes a practical possibility, there is no longer any excuse for the world to permit its children to go hungry in body or in mind, to be deprived of the essentials of life-food, medical care and education opportunities." Can we do less in this session of the 90th Congress than urge a contribution. of $13 million to UNICEF this year? 

Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

A $13 MILLION CONTRIBUTION BY THE UNITED STATES TO UNICEF IS REQUESTED 

The members of the organizations endorsing this statement urge your approval of a contribution of $13 million to UNICEF in Fiscal 1969. For the past 8 years (9 years if the State Department recommendation stands) the U.S. regular contribution to the Children's Fund has remained at the same figure: $12 million. 

In a period when most costs are increasing at an alarming rate, it would be equivalent to a decrease in material support if the U.S. contribution to the work of the Children's Fund did not make some allowance for this fact. An increase to $15 million could be well justified but we limit our request to a $1 million increase over Fiscal 1968.

We request your specific approval of a contribution of $13 million to UNICEF. 

In this year when billions can be spent without hesitation for military purposes, surely this sum can be found to assist sick and hungry children around the world, including emergency aid in Viet-Nam and the Middle East. 

INCREASES IN UNICEF CONTRIBUTION ARE BEING SOUGHT FROM ALL GOVERNMENTS 

UNICEF's assistance policies have been reviewed and endorsed by its Executive Board, by ECOSOC and by the General Assembly of the UN. Indeed, the soundness of its policies is reflected by the assisted governments themselves in the rising volume of requests for aid which are being submitted. All governments in the world's family of nations should now give effective substance to their own laudatory speeches of commendation. In 1967, 35 governments increased their contributions, compared with 1966. A number of these have made several increases since 1960, while the U.S. contribution has remained the same. In 1967, 123 governments contributed to UNICEF's Central Account. These contributions are, of course, in addition to internal matching funds provided by UNICEF assisted countries.

JUSTIFICATION FOR AN INCREASE IN THIS PERIOD OF WORLD TENSIONS 

Even after taking into account the growing volume of private contributions to UNICEF resulting from the increased activities of national UNICEF committees in 24 countries, the Children's Fund could usefully allocate $10 million more at the upcoming June session of its Executive Board, solely on the basis of valid project requests ready for submission if the funds were available to finance them.

It is estimated, for example, that 40 percent of the population in the developing countries is under 15 years of age. Of these, 450 million live in countries with a national per capita income of under $100. 

In the face of the enormous needs of children in the developing countries, the question might well be asked whether there is anything effective that UNICEF could do, even if the funds were available. 

The Executive Board's response to this kind of question has been to base its grants to projects on a country approach: to support those projects most likely to advance the welfare and development of children according to the priorities of the country concerned. UNICEF assistance is focused on the problems of health, nutrition, responsible parenthood and education. Its aid represents a contribution to both welfare and economic development, both for the individual and for the nation. One of the main criteria for the coherence of a country's policy for children and youth is that it take into account the growth cycle of the child thru various ages, each with successive and somewhat different needs. An appendix to this statement gives a table showing the main fields of 

UNICEF assistance and the amount allocated to each by the Executive Board in 1967 arranged by age grouping of beneficiaries. UNICEF is currently assisting 496 projects in 119 countries.

Although UNICEF's main role is to aid projects for the long-range benefit of children in developing countries, the present period of world tensions has brought added demands on UNICEF's resources. A needy child is a chronic emergency, of course. But, in addition, world events have forced UNICEF to provide assistance in three regions because of circumstances dictated by natural disaster or conflicts. These are India, the Middle East and Viet-Nam. An allocation of $1.4 million was made to drought-stricken India in 1967. For the Middle 

East. $496,000 was approved last June for emergency refugee relief, and an additional $512,000 was approved by a recent mail poll of Executive Board members. 

For Viet-Nam, close to $1 million has been approved during the past year for health and social services, for education, for tuberculosis control and for emer gency aid.

UNICEF'S COMPARATIVELY MODEST GROWTH 

During the past 8 years when the U.S. regular contribution has remained at the same figure, other governments and private contributions have accounted for a modest growth in UNICEF's income. It should be pointed out, however, that governments have made a very substantial increase in contributions to various other organizations. Since 1963 WHO's budget has more than doubled as has 

FAO's; other agencies have increased almost as much. During the same period 

UNICEF's income has risen only 26.8 percent. Members of our organizations are reluctant to draw these comparisons. We have no intention of disparaging the excellent work done by these other agencies and hasten to point out the close cooperation which UNICEF continues to maintain with them. It is only realistic, however, to recognize the fact that some of these increases have come about because the financing of the agency is determined by assessments which are voted by member countries. Thus the individual member government has little control over the level of financial support which they must be prepared to render. We submit that the increasing demands upon many governments, with rigidly limited resources, by such increases in assessments have operated to restrict an increase in the amount of the voluntary contribution which such government is in a position to make to the Children's Fund. Indeed, it is primarily the main donors to 

UNICEF that have been compelled to face a higher volume of obligations thru increasing assessments for the Specialized Agencies. We believe that there is merit in the system of voluntary support for humanitarian causes-a principle which the United States has traditionally supported-and we recommend that our government now demonstrate its leadership towards the world's children by free choice instead of under the pressure of an imposed assessment. Essential as the programs are in all of these organizations, none is more fundamental than

UNICEF's primary goal of aiding the health and preparation for a useful life of the oncoming generations of the world's children. 

CHILDREN: THE BEST INVESTMENT IN THE FUTURE 

What the nations of the world do or do not do for the world's children in the next decade may well determine whether development becomes indeed a reality and the path to peace, or whether it is merely an empty slogan. UNICEF cannot, of course, aspire to do all that needs to be done but it could make a much greater contribution with additional resources. In his State of the Union address, President Johnson issued a clear call to action when he stated that "But we must also improve the lives of children who are already born in the villages, in the towns and the cities on this earth." It is noted also that he mentioned aid to the world's children at least three times in his Foreign Air message "To Build the Peace". In the same vein, Mr. Labouisse, the Executive Director of UNICEF, said in his report to UNICEF's Executive Board, "In an era when the words 'reaching for the moon' no longer means striving for the impossible, but simply describes a practical possibility, there is no longer any excuse for the world to permit its children to go hungry in body or in mind, to be deprived of the essentials of life food, medical care and education opportunities". Can we do less in this sesstion of the 90th Congress, than urge a contribution of $13 million to 

UNICEF this year? 

Thank you.

The next witness is Mr. Stacey Widdicombe, Friends Committee on 

National Legislation.

STATEMENT OF STACEY WIDDICOMBE, JR., REPRESENTING FRIENDS COMMITTEE ON NATIONAL LEGISLATION 

Mr. WIDDICOMBE. I hope we can equal the lady's record. Men are known to talk not as long.

Senator SPARKMAN. We have 15 witnesses. 

Mr. WIDDICOMBE. In that case, Mr. Chairman, I ask that the written testimony be made a part of the record. 

Senator SPARKMAN. All of the statements will be printed in full in the record. 

Mr. WIDDICOMBE. Mr. Chairman, first I would like to introduce Miss

Frances Neely, the associate director, who is with me here this morning. 

Senator SPARKMAN. It is very good to have you. 

Mr. WIDDICOMBE. My name is Stacey Widdicombe, of Darien, Conn.

Last October I returned from Brazil where I had served since 1965 as the resident representative of the Ford Foundation, previously having served as associate director of the Foundation's Latin American and Caribbean program. However, I am not here today to present either officially or unofficially the views of the Ford Foundation. 

As a member of the Religious Society of Friends, I am here representing the Friends Committee on National Legislation, a Quaker organization. Because of the society's democratic organization, and long-cherished tradition of individual opinion, it should be pointed out that the FCNL does not attempt to speak for all Friends. 

At a time like this when America's problems at home seem so overwhelming, it is difficult, we know, for Congress to concentrate on U.S. responsibilities overseas, and keep them in proper national focus. 

There is little question that the mood of America is turning inwardas well it should, with so many of our own people failing to share in national prosperity and failing to share in the governance of their own cities and towns. As President Johnson and other American leadership have said, Dr. Martin Luther King's brutal murder should make

all our citizens recognize the tremendous need for pulling together to solve the problems of our ghettos and our rural poor. 

Your committee, while charged with responsibility for legislation concerned with our Nation's foreign affairs, cannot help but recognize the desire to respond to this call to put our own house in order. Our 

Quaker group is also deeply troubled and concerned that we, as Americans, take action on the problems so clearly before us at home. 

Now is the time for restating our national commitment and increasing public and private action. However, our testimony before you today is a plea a plea that your committee will point out to the Nation and the world that U.S. ideals are indivisible and that those goals we aspire to for our own people we aspire to for our neighbors in the world as well.

It would be a tragic betrayal of our ideals—if in our zeal to attack our own problems at home we were to forget our less fortunate neighbors, many of whom are struggling with far more complex and divisive situations. Even with all that the United States has gone through in the past weeks, we have only to consider the plight of the people of 

Vietnam or the people of Nigeria.

SEVERAL POINTS OF EMPHASIS 

Your committee has already heard all the major, moral, humanitarian, and political arguments for the aid program. You doubtless have before you all the basic figures as well. Rather than repeat these arguments and these figures, our Quaker group would like to put forward several points of emphasis for your consideration. 

First, we urge very much that your committee do more than just routinely take up the 1968 Foreign Assistance Act. We recommend that you seize this opportunity to show leadership and insert in the 1968 bill a reaffirmation of our faith in the ideals on which the first U.S. overseas assistance programs were founded many years ago. 

Congress should challenge our people to strengthen all our foreign assistance programs, just as we grid ourselves to tackle with increased vigor our own internal problems. Moreover, the Congress must assure that all our foreign policies are in harmony with these ideals; for example, our trade policy should support rather than negate our aid policies. This we feel is very important at this time. 

You can feel, I am sure, in the letters to the Senators the American people saying, "Why don't we attack more of our problems at home?"

We feel your committee has a responsibility for showing these problems are indivisible.

AID FOR RECONCILIATION AND PEACE 

Second, we recommend that the committee, rather than using aid for another weapon in the cold war, recommend that aid be used for reconciliation and for peace. 

I will not repeat all the arguments here, because several Senators present have already spoken on this issue many times. But we would like to point out quickly four areas that come to mind. 

The first relates to the Mekong Basin. This is an area where 22 nations, foreign nations, are working to assist Thailand, Laos, Cam-

bodia, and South Vietnam in a cooperative venture. I will not go into the details, the Senators are already familiar with these. yoKE 

The second area of reconciliation relates to Nigeria, in Africa, a continent about which this committee and the U.S. Congress has said little recently, although the Senator from Massachusetts has presented a unique report in this regard. In a country like our own which is still recovering from the effects of a Civil War, it seems to us that we should be particularly sensitive to the desperate needs of Nigeria and the peace moves that are going on in that country now. 

Third, we would like to urge your committee to raise with the executive branch again the problem of the Middle East and U.S. participation in any kind of regional development program there that can begin to bring the sides there together on some kind of minimum level of cooperation. 

These examples are all trying to use aid as a method of reconciliation rather than a method of the cold war. 

Fourth, we would like to suggest that as a first step in reconciliation with Cuba that Congress authorize the use of U.S. funds by the United 

Nations for economic development of that island. We would hope that this first small step would lead to further direct reconciliation. 

MULTINATIONAL DEVELOPMENT PROGRAMS 

Our third major point, and one I would like to take a minute on, is strengthening the U.N. and other multinational development programs. 

Our committee recommends that this committee support the very modest increases which the Executive has requested for support of international organizations and programs. 

We heard about UNICEF this morning, and we certainly concur with this testimony. Indeed your committee should not be satisfied with the size of the increase proposed by the administration which amounts to less than 1.5 percent of the funds authorized last year. The administration is far too timid in this area. Over the past few years it has not requested enough funds for the U.N. development program to equal the 40-percent support level already authorized by Congress. U.S. contributions to international organizations and programs are about one-third of the funds requested for military assistance, a dismal reversal of priorities in our opinion. Perhaps the administration is overreacting to what it perceives to be the mood of the Congress. In any case, your committee has the responsibility of encouraging the administration to face up to the opportunities which lie before it. 

In these days of rampant nationalism; with many less developed nations beginning to turn inward, as in our own country, a strengthening of international programs by the United States is vital to reemphasize U.S. recognition that the fates of all nations are bound together in this one world and that we must continue to work together to solve common problems.

I will not go into the personal examples I have seen in my own experience except to speak of one, the northeast area of Brazil, which is the dust bowl area of Brazil desperately needing assistance. There is a multinational U.N. team working on irrigation problems in the 

San Francisco River Basin which hopefully will change the life of a

great number of citizens in that area, various UNESCO teams, and others. 

AUTHORIZATION FOR INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENT ASSOCIATION 

I would like to digress for a minute to point out something that will be coming before the committee very soon, and that is the question of the authorization for IDA, the $480 million 3-year authorization which is coming up. We hope this is supported. This figure, as some of you should know, is down from our previous percentage of 43 percent, and brings it down now to the 40 percent, which is the level mentioned before by the Congress. 

U.S. BILATERAL ASSISTANCE PROGRAMS 

Our fourth point is the strengthening of U.S.

bilateral assistance programs. 

At a minimum, this committee should support the administration's request for funds for the U.S. bilateral aid program. We recognize the complications caused by the balance of payments situation, as well as the enormous claims on our national resources brought on by the Vietnam war and problems at home. However, a nation which is spending several billions a month for a war clearly lacking the full support of the American people certainly cannot afford to pare down the request which Mr. Gaud has made for his agency. As your committee knows, the amount of U.S. gross national product going into relief and development activities has dropped from 2 percent in 1949 to less than one-half of 1 percent in 1967. 

These are the days when the aid program seems to be caught in a crossfire of charges of waste, corruption, lack of commitment by recipients, desires for economy, balance of payments problems, and last but not least, differences between the administration and the Congress on basic foreign issues. 

We hope that this committee will take a broad view and try to encourage the AID staff. So much of what I have seen abroad when 

Congressmen come through and Senators come through has been fact-finding a little bit, perhaps on some occasion, muckraking. We hope that your committee will commend the AID missions for many of the things they are trying to do. For example, there are priorities this year, as there were last year, on agriculture, education, family planning, programs which foster enterprise, self-help, and participation of the recipient as well as in education. It seems to us work overseas seems to have excellent priorities, but a lot of the men in the field, your AID mission chiefs, are discouraged and since Congress bears a major responsibility in this regard, it is our feeling that Congress itself, Senators, should encourage these men to do better in their jobs rather than what so often happens-we get a feeling they are just charges cutting back and a lack of inspirational leadership. 

Among the things we would like to mention on the strengthening of U.S. bilateral programs is the encouragement of the technical assistance and development grant programs for the Agency, not only by authorizing the funds requested but also by urging that these requests be resubmitted with higher totals. 

Douro bywest

U.S. technical assistance commitments should be put on a long-term basis, just like development loans. It is our understanding that all 

AID technical assistance contracts are negotiated on a short-term basis. The initial contract is for 2 years; later funding is on a yearby-year basis with numerous escape hatches. In past years the Agency apparently tried to give universities and other contractors some assurance that technical assistance projects funded under annual contracts would not be terminated abruptly, but Agency officials have abandoned this policy because of budget cuts, and I can give you some specific examples if you would like later.

Third, I stress this question of the appropriate fund mechanism. 

We feel very strongly this is a problem all of you are well aware of, this substitution of loans for grants under the foreign assistance program and a heavy burden that this throws on the countries of repayment. 

One digression here. I think in this committee's taking leadership in the Congress and with our citizenry in general in pointing out the importance of our foreign assistance program to this country, you might want to put in or recognize that some of the lessons we have learned overseas in our technical assistance programs are now being put to use in some of our programs here in the United States having to do with underprivileged children in the ghetto areas, et cetera. 

The number of AID officials now coming back to our country working in programs like those of the urban coalition and the U.S. Office of 

Economic Opportunity is very large indeed. 

GREATER CONGRESSIONAL RESPONSIBILITY IN AID PROGRAMS 

I would like to skip, if I may, to the last point of our statement. 

The Congress itself should take more responsibility for U.S. foreign assistance programs. We feel that the Congress should take more initiative itself. The Congress just cannot delegate to the executive all foreign affairs interests. There are areas in which Congress is uniquely fitted to contribute. For example, carrying out the objectives of title 

IX is one of these. The amendment of Representative Fraser of Minnesota is very much in the spirit of amendments on cooperatives that the then Senator Humphrey made some years ago. We believe that 

Members of Congress should travel more and spend more time in the areas they do visit. We think this is one area of difficulty in the field where Senators do have very heavy schedules and do get caught up more in talking to embassy personnel than in talking to citizens in the countries they come to visit. 

We would like you to talk more to your counterparts-that is, countries that you do visit-share some of the insights which are so important in the democratic participation in a nation-building process. 

Here this is a responsibility the executive cannot assume and Congress is uniquely fitted to carry out.

REAFFIRM IDEALS OF ASSISTANCE PROGRAM 

To sum up, Mr. Chairman, we urge your committee and the Congress to reaffirm the ideals of the Foreign Assistance Act in a strong statement of support-linking our renewed efforts at home with renewed efforts abroad. We urge your full support of the administra-

tion's authorization requests and suggest that these authorization requests be raised in several instances-like the U.N. development program-that these be increased to the 40 percent that you have already authorized, but we are particularly keen that these international organizations be strengthened so that we can show by our own example the kind of international cooperation that may encourage some of the smaller nations to come along. Lastly we urge Congress itself to take on more of the leadership role in our national assistance effort abroad. 

This is a cooperative venture, and we feel Congress bears as much responsibility as the executive in this regard. 

Thank you very much. 

(Mr. Widdicombe's prepared statement follows:) TESTIMONY OF STACEY WIDDICOMBE, JR., IN BEHALF OF THE FRIENDS COMMITTEE ON NATIONAL LEGISLATION 

I am Stacey Widdicombe of Darien, Conn. Last October I returned from Brazil where I had served since 1965 as the resident representative of the Ford Foundation, previously having served as associate director of the Foundation's Latin American and Caribbean program. However, I am not here today to present either officially or unofficially the views of the Ford Foundation. 

As a member of the Religious Society of Friends, I am here representing the Friends Committee on National Legislation, a Quaker organization. Because of the Society's democratic organization, and long cherished tradition of individual opinion, it should be pointed out that the FCNL does not attempt to speak for all Friends. However, the Committee includes in its membership Friends appointed by 19 of the 25 Yearly Meetings across the United States and by 10 additional 

Quaker organizations.

The Friends Committee appreciates the opportunity to testify today on the non-military phases of the Foreign Assistance Act of 1968. Most Quakers have for many years supported the U.S. government foreign assistance programs out of their religious concern for the dignity and worth of each individual. 

At a time like this when America's problems at home seem so overwhelming, it is difficult, we know, for Congress to concentrate on U.S. responsibilities overseas, and keep them in proper national focus. There is little question that the mood of America is turning inward—as well it should, with so many of our own people failing to share in national prosperity and failing to share in the governance of their own cities and towns. As President Johnson and other American leadership have said, Dr. Martin Luther King's brutal murder should make all our citizens recognize the tremendous need for pulling together to solve the problems of our ghettos and our rural poor. 

Your Committee, while charged with responsibility for legislation concerned with our nation's foreign affairs, cannot help but recognize the desire to respond to this call to put our own house in order. Our Quaker group is also deeply troubled, and concerned that we, as Americans, take action on the problems so clearly before us at home.

Now is the time for restating our national commitment and increasing public and private action. However, our testimony before you today is a plea-a plea that your Committee will point out to the nation and the world that U.S. ideals are indivisible and that those goals we aspire to for our own people we aspire to for our neighbors in the world as well. We hope that your Committee will declare that while we recognize the critical needs of our own poor, we also recognize the increasing needs of the world's poor. A richly endowed land such as ours can afford to concentrate additional resources on home problems and still be able to continue the worthy tradition of the decades since World War II during which the United States has taken leadership in providing food, relief, technical assistance and development funds for the less fortunate nations around the world. It would be a tragic betrayal of our ideals-if in our zeal to attack our own problems at home we were to forget our less fortunate neighbors, many of whom are struggling with far more complex and divisive situations. Even with all that the United States has gone through in the past weeks, we have only to consider the plight of the people of Vietnam or the people of Nigeria.

Your Committee has already heard all the major moral, humanitarian and political arguments for the Aid program. You doubtless have before you all the basic figures as well. Rather than repeat these arguments and these figures, our 

Quaker group would like to put forward several points of emphasis for your consideration.

POINTS OF EMPHASIS 

The points of emphasis on which we would like to speak are these: 

1. Congress should take this time of crisis to affirm our Constitutional belief that all men are created equal, not only within our own country, but in other nations as well. This is the time also to reaffirm our commitment to our own people and to the world. Our Aid and related programs, such as 

Food for Peace, are practical manifestations of these commitments and beliefs. Your Committee should encourage the Executive not to do less, but to do more. 

2. Congress should take this time of crisis to insist that our Aid program be considered a force for reconciliation and peace, and not just another instrument in the continuing Cold War. Certainly our Aid program should not be considered a way to buy friends in the world. Such a narrow approach is unworthy of a great nation, and, as we all know, seldom produces any lasting results. 

3. Congress should follow the advice of its own wise leaders, who urge that additional U.S. resources be made available to UN agencies, regional assistance agencies, and similar multi-national organizations. U.S. contributions to the UN Development Program are below the 40% level authorized by the Congress in previous years. The Congress should encourage the Executive to plan for and to ask for additional funds up to the 40% level. 

4. Congress should strengthen the U.S. bilateral aid program by providing the Executive with all the funds they feel are necessary to carry out economic development objectives, particularly funds to support the technical assistance and development grant programs. Certainly it is our moral duty as a nation to help less developed countries achieve more satisfactory living standards and political liberty. 

5. Congress should seek ways to make Title IX of the Act more effective, not in a Machiavellian spirit of political manipulation, but in the real spirit of Representative Fraser's amendment-which in its essence urges that the Aid program promote the pluralistic and participant polity which we aspire to here in our own country. 

6. Congress itself should take more initiative in our development programs abroad. Congress has leadership responsibilities which go beyond the passing of legislation and watch-dogging the Executive. Congressmen should visit other countries more often, stay longer, and take up the opportunities in 

Title IX themselves. 

None of these points of emphasis are new to the members of this Committee. 

But, in these days of national turmoil, they take on added significance. I am reminded of the comment made by a member of my Quaker meeting in Connecticut several Sundays ago when he said that our domestic problems and our foreign problems were all of one piece. I am also reminded of a well-known Brazilian journalist who responded to my query as to why the Brazilian press gave such extensive coverage to American involvement in Vietnam and to the violence in the United States itself. He responded, "What you North Americans don't realize is that you still represent the world's best hope. We want you to find solutions because, whether we like it or not, we still look to the U.S. for inspiration so we can solve our own problems.” 

1. Reaffirmation of our national commitment to foreign assistance 

Our Quaker group urges that your Committee do more than just routinely take up 1968 Foreign Assistance Act. We recommend that you seize this opportunity to show leadership and insert in the 1968 bill a reaffirmation of our faith in the ideals on which the first U.S. overseas assistance programs were founded many years ago. Congress should challenge our people to strengthen all our foreign assistance programs, just as we gird ourselves to tackle with increased vigor our own internal problems. Moreover, the Congress must assure that all our foreign policies are in harmony with these ideals, for example, our trade policy should support, rather than negate our Aid policies.

2. Reaffirmation that Aid be a force for reconciliation and for peace 

We strongly recommend that the Committee ask Mr. William Gaud to present to you firm proposals for using foreign aid as a force for national and international reconciliation. There are four areas which immediately come to mind: (a) The first relates to the Mekong Basin. You are of course familiar with the Mekong program in which our government has participated in an on-again, off-again manner. We would like to share with you the impressions which our group has just received from Russell Johnson, a senior staff member of the American Friends Service Committee, who spent January and February in Southeast Asia. "Today, despite the war, progress goes forward in the implementation of plans for the development of the lower basin of the Mekong. Twenty two foreign nations are assisting in Thailand, Laos, Cambodia, and South Vietnam. Whereas a year ago, it appeared that 

Cambodia might cease cooperation, because of adverse reaction to withdrawal of U.S. assistance in the building of the Prek Phnot dam, today, with increased Japanese financial assistance and a large contribution from Cambodia itself, the work goes forward. Dams in Laos and Thailand are being completed and work on the first dam in Cambodia is underway. "This work is of tremendous long-range signifiance in that economic relationships now being forged may provide a climate for other kinds of cooperation in the future, thus strengthening the region internally and permitting the countries of the Mekong Basin to overcome ancient antagonism and live together in peace and security. U.S. assistance in the Mekong Development 

Program is thus of fundamental importance and, in my opinion, a far better way of assuring peace and stability there than U.S. military bases and forces on Southeast Asian soil."

Our Quaker Committee sincerely hopes that the new peace efforts in Vietnam achieve positive results so that our Aid efforts in that strife-torn land can proceed without the corrupting effects of identification with U.S. military action. (b) The second area of reconciliation relates to Nigeria.—A nation that is still recovering from our own civil war, should be particularly sensitive to the desperate needs of Nigeria and its former eastern regions. While there is perhaps little that the United States can do until the fighting ceases, at least we can hold out the prospect that the United States would contribute quickly and generously to healing the wounds both with direct aid and through appropriate African regional organizations. The United States did this in Greece three years ago; we have been doing it in the Congo; and we are doing it now in the Dominican Republic. (c) In a related fashion, our group asks whether your Committee should not urge the Executive to take leadership in again promising U.S. participation in regional development programs in the Middle East so that both sides can be encouraged to engage in a minimum level of cooperation. The 

Eisenhower Plan is a natural beginning. (d) Our Committee suggests that as a first step in reconciliation with Cuba, that Congress authorize the use of U.S. funds by the United Nations for economic development of that Island. We would hope that this first step would lead to further direct reconciliation. 

3. Strengthening U.N. and other multi-nation development programs 

Our Quaker group recommends that this Committee support the very modest increases which the Executive has requested for support of international organizations and programs. Indeed, this Committee should not be satisfied with the size of the increase proposed by the Administration which amounts to less than 1.5% of the funds authorized last year. The Administration is far too timid in this area. Over the past few years it has not requested enough funds for the U.N. 

Development Program, to equal the 40% support level already authorized by Congress. U.S. contributions to international organizations and programs are about % of the funds requested for military assistance, a dismal reversal of priorities in our opinion. Perhaps the Administration is overreacting to what it perceives to be the mood of the Congress. In any case, your Committee has the responsibility of encouraging the Administration to face up to the opportunities which lie before it.

In these days of rampant nationalism, with many less-developed natons beginning to turn inward, as in our own country, a strengthening of international 91-392-68-pt. 2-

programs by the United States is vital to reemphasize U.S. recognition that the fates of all nations are bound together in this one world and that we must continue to work together to solve common problems. I have seen the excellent work of multi-national U.N. teams in irrigation projects in the San Francisco river basin of Brazil's Northeast, and I have personally worked with a multi-national UNESCO Educational team in the Caribbean. In addition to multi-national efforts such as the U.N. Development Program and the International Development As sociation, ties between nations can be strengthened through various regional programs such as the Asian Development Bank, and the Latin American Free Trade Area. In trying to encourage these regional programs, however, the United 

States should not lose sight of national programs which merit support. There are occasional tendencies to support a regional program as an excuse for lessening bilateral support of national programs worthy of concern and support. 

4. Strengthening U.S. bilateral assistance programs 

At a minimum, this Committee should support the Administration's request for funds for the U.S. bilateral aid program. We recognize the complications caused by the balance of payments situation, as well as the enormous claims on our national resources brought on by the Vietnam war and problems at home. However, a nation which is spending several billions a month for a war clearly lacking the full support of the American people, certainly cannot afford to pare down the request which Mr. Gaud has made for his Agency. As your Committee knows, the amount of U.S. gross national product going into relief and development activities has dropped from 2% in 1949 to less than one-half of 1% in 1967. 

These are the days when the aid program seems to be caught in a cross-fire of charges of waste, corruption, lack of commitment by recipients, desires for economy, balance of payments problems, and last but not least, differences between the Administration and the Congress on basic foreign issues. This Committee has the responsibility of keeping its "cool" and promoting the best interests of the United States. The morale in the AID staff, as this Committee must know, is not one of hope. Many AID executives will say in private that they fear that their program is certain to be the whipping boy again. Congress certainly must bring to the attention of the Administration its criticisms of the aid program. 

However the Congress also has the duty to inspire, to give encouragement and to make constructive suggestions to an Agency which has one of the most difficult tasks in the entire Federal Government. 

One of the unfortunate consequences of the many responsible and irresponsible attacks on the Agency is to make it timid and fearful of taking chances. There are a number of concrete steps which this Committee could take which might restore some of the flexibility and imagination which the Agency needs to do its job:

First, the Committee should encourage the technical assistance and development grant programs for the Agency, by not only authorizing the funds requested but by also urging that these requests be resubmitted with higher totals. You would be surprised to see what "encouraged" officials could come up with. In 

Brazil I know of several excellent teacher-training projects which have been curtailed because of the lack of technical cooperation and grant funds. 

Furthermore, U.S. technical assistance commitments should be put on a longterm basis, just like development loans. It is our understanding that all AID 

Technical Assistance contracts are negotiated on a short-term basis. The initial contract is for two years; later funding is on a year-by-year basis with numerous escape hatches. In past years the Agency apparently tried to give universities and other contractors some assurance that T.A. projects funded under annual contracts would not be terminated abruptly, but Agency officials have abandoned this policy because of budget cuts. 

This is extremely unfortunate, because research and basic agricultural development are long-term projects, and have to be funded on a long-term basis if they are to reach full fruition. To give one example, the Rockefeller Foundation has been experimenting with corn for 10 to 15 years. 

Second, your Committee could congratulate the Agency for its priorities, particularly its stress on agriculture, education, family planning, and programs which foster enterprise, self-help, and participation of the recipients and doers in planning as well as in execution. 

AID should also be congratulated for reaching out for the cooperation of voluntary agencies and educational institutions, and for assisting private business to take a more active role in our total development efforts. In this connection

1 am reminded of the excellent work carried on by Purdue University in helping develop a modern agricultural university at Vicosa in the Brazilian State of Minas Gerais, an institution devoted to teaching, research, and service in the best U.S. land grant college tradition. Also in Brazil, a private non-profit agriculture research organization, IRI, is doing a superb job in applied agriculture research on forage grasses and legumes. It is hard, expensive work and I have regretted to learn that a cut in aid funds will curtail these IRI activities. We need more not less agriculture training, research, extension, and service to people. 

Third, your Committee should encourage careful use of the most appropriate funding machinery. Just as the World Bank has had to develop several kinds of windows, so the AID program must develop similar grant and technical assistance windows. However, AID administrators should not have to make inappropriate uses of funding categories just because of the authorization procedures. For example, many experienced observers see a real danger in making large loans for such purposes as education. The economic effects of education are more long ranged than they are for other fields, but there is little calculable return to the recipient government which can be used to pay off the loan. In effect what happens is, the loan is a claim on the general revenues of the recipient country under a fancy politically-acceptable label. In the opinion of many, the private enterprise concept of loan and credit is being subverted by improper use because it is easier to get funds by using appealing terminology. 

Certainly the United States is sufficiently great that we can call a spade a spade and not engage in such window dressing. 

The gradual substitution of loans for grants under the foreign assistance program, as your Committee knows, has resulted in a problem of growing proportions. For some countries, the repayment of prior year loans is already larger than new funds anticipated. This repayment burden is a problem which must soon be grappled with in all its aspects. 

Fourth, some way must be found to put AID authorizations, where necessary and appropriate, on a long-term basis. Many of the problems with which AID seeks to deal are just not susceptible to easy solution in one or two years. 

Fifth, your Committee may want to point out to Congress, and to the citizenry that some of the know-how which we have gained in our foreign assistance program is now being put to use in the United States by the Office of Economic Opportunity and the Urban Coalition. In fact, I spent most of last Friday discussing economic development problems with members of the staff of the Coalition in New York City. 

5. Forceful, but careful implementation of title IX 

In the opinion of many of us who have worked abroad, Rep. Donald Fraser has made a significant contribution to U.S. efforts with his insistence that the foreign aid program be directed at the "utilization of democratic institutions in development." Our Quaker group applauds the high ideals behind Rep. Fraser's amendment. His amendment is in the same spirit as Vice President Humphrey's earlier amendment urging the stimulation of cooperatives under the aid program.

In adding these statutory social and political objectives to our aid program, it must be recognized that such goals can be misunderstood by recipient nations, that they can be self-defeating if considered to be purely manipulation, and that they complicate tremendously the work of AID. Title IX presents many long-term opportunities, but as Mr. Gaud's testimony before the House Foreign Affairs 

Committee on 20 March attests, AID must be encouraged and assisted in these new initiatives. As Field Haviland, of the Brookings Institution suggested at the Airlie Farms Conference on Political Development, a better name for these efforts might be "civic" development rather than "political" development. 

We know, fortunately, that most basic problems can be attacked by programs which stress the "pluralistic" and "participant" aspects of less-developed countries. We have examples in the highly effective J.C.R.R. program in Taiwan and in the SPREAD program in the Philippines. There are many unsung cooperative programs in Latin America and elsewhere.

Nowhere are the high ideals of the Fraser amendment better demonstrated to be possible and effective than in the work of the publicly-motivated, but privately run Asociacion Para El Desarrollo of Santiago de Los Caballeros in the Dominican 

Republic. This association for Development was started in the summer of 1962 by 

12 young community leaders. Since that time they have amassed a remarkable record of achievement:

(a) The establishment of a resident high school for boys to learn scientific agriculture. Of the first graduating class of 80 boys last year, 26 are at Texas 

A. & M. continuing their studies with AID assistance and more than half that number are at Mayaguec Campus of the University of Puerto Rico continuing their studies at the university level. The school, incidentally, continued operating throughout the fighting and the OAS occupation in 1965. (b) The establishment of health clinics in their region of the country. (c) Assistance in the establishment of a new private university. (d) Establishment of practical agriculture research programs and field trial programs which have significantly raised rice production and the yield of other food crops. 

The Association has based its program on the continuing support of Dominican businessmen (for example, Rotary Clubs in various cities and towns provide student scholarships) and of the Dominican government's Ministers of Education and Agriculture. The Association has carefully supplemented these national resources with assistance from such diverse sources as AID, the Peace Corps, the 

Heifer Project, the Rockefeller and Ford foundations.

To develop such Title IX programs takes imagination and dedicated staff in AID. This may take more rather than fewer people overseas.
