Speaking of Staff, our Quaker group would like to urge Congress to consider the effects of its own criticism and appropriations vacillations on the many able and hard-working AID staff members. Regular AID staff should be considered as much of a professional staff as the U.S. Foreign and Consular Services. AID staff should not be treated as just a group of technicians, who happen to be attached to our embassies abroad. Their work should, of course, be overseen by the U.S. Ambassador and his senior aides. However, the country program should certainly not be subjected to the capricious wishes of either short-term political appointees or cut to fit short-term political goals; neither should AID be stifled by the other senior foreign services, which have other jobs to do. 

We believe that a dedicated AID staff can be further supported by more extensive use of universities, private business, labor unions, student groups, church groups, and professional organizations of all sorts. 

6. Congress itself should take more responsibility for U.S. foreign assistance programs Congress must take more responsibility and initiative itself if it wishes U.S. foreign assistance programs to fulfill their purpose. Congress cannot just delegate to the Executive all foreign affairs interests. There are areas in which Congress is uniquely fitted to contribute. For example, carrying out the objectives of 

Title IX is one of these. If members of Congress travel more, spend more time in the areas they do visit, we believe they would be better able to fashion legislation and public opinion for the tremendous task before us. 

I have had the personal pleasure of meeting many members of the House and a few Senators in both the Caribbean and in Brazil during the past 5 years. 

While these visits normally add to the burden of the country ministries, and have made some AID executives apprehensive by questions and comments, these visits by and large, have produced a healthy exchange of views. Where the visits were of a constructive nature and of considerable length, they had an excellent effect on the host country as well. 

Some Congressman bring a technical and professional expertise to their discussions, which is particularly helpful. This is frequently the case with representatives from agricultural districts. Other Congressmen bring first-hand experience with urban problems, housing, transportation, health, and welfare. 

Most good mission chiefs I have known are secretly pleased to have professional politicians from the United States sit down with their counterparts in the host country. Not only do listening Congressmen learn first-hand of the political and civic implications of AID work, but, as elected officials they have an opportunity to discuss directly the workings of the democratic system in the United States in a way which few members of the Executive can do. 

To sum up, we urge your Committee and the Congress to reaffirm the ideals of the Foreign Assistance Act in a strong statement of support-linking our renewed efforts at home with renewed efforts abroad. We urge your full support of the Administration's authorization requests and suggest that these authorization requests be raised in several instances, particularly for international organizations and programs. And, lastly, we urge that the Congress itself take on more of the leadership role in our national assistance efforts abroad.

If I can amplify this formal testimony by responding to questitons, particularly from my experience in Brazil and the Dominican Republic, I will be happy to do so. 

Thank you. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Thank you very much.

Mrs. Marjorie Creighton Jordan. 

STATEMENT OF MRS. EDWIN P. JORDAN, REPRESENTING THE AMERICAN ASSOCIATION OF UNIVERSITY WOMEN 

Mrs. JORDAN. Thank you, sir. 

Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, I am Mrs. Edwin P.

Jordan of Charlottesville, Va. I am here today as a representative of the American Association of University Women, an organization with a membership of 175,000 organized into 1,624 branches in 50

States, Guam, and the District of Columbia, to support S. 3091. 

DEVELOPMENT ASSISTANCE SUPPORTED 

I greatly appreciate the opportunity to appear before this committee in support of development assistance. 

Approximately 700 of our branches have chosen for study this year the growing gap between the rich and the poor nations. This is a study of modernization, how it occurs and how it can be accelerated. It is a search for understanding of the forces of change in the developing world: changes in attitudes, social behavior, economic and political transformation. We are trying to understand what the complex process of development involves, to ask questions about the proper ways of judging development, and to focus on how to make U.S. aid more effective. Our program parallels the program of AID in its emphasis on agriculture, population problems, and education; popular education for the release of energies and initiative of the people-as well as education for an educated elite. AAUW is concerned that revolutionary pressures will build up if the gap between rich and poor nations widens, and if peaceful economic and social progress is impeded.

BILATERAL AND MULTILATERAL AID ARE COMPLEMENTARY 

It is in our national interest to respond to the rapidly changing conditions and problems that arise in the less developed countries. 

We need both bilateral and multilateral aid in a complementary fusion to further U.S. interests. We need bilateral aid to supplement multilateral aid when multilateral organizations are less sensitive to how conditions might affect our interests. Multilateral aid can minimize political problems that may arise when one nation assists another bilaterally. As this committee knows, the dollar drain has been cut by tying Agency for International Development financing tightly to the purchase of American goods and services. Now AID spending directly produces expert business for American farmers and industry. 

I would be unfortunate to erode development assistance through curtailment of this modest share of the Federal budget, less than 1 percent of the GNP. It would be shortsighted if the United States, through

cutting its development and technical assistance programs under AID, were to fail to develop future markets for its future productivity. 

We need this tool, this important instrument of development assistance to further U.S. interests. We are faced with substantial change in the whole view of economic aid. Enlightened development policies are more selective, concentrated, and more effectively employed; for example, the emphasis on internal effort, on multinational economic cooperation, on regionalism, on efficient administration, and the trend toward long-range planning. 

The need for development assistance is so compelling; for failure to understand the forces of change may lead to catastrophe. AAUW urges the full economic appropriations for the fiscal year 1969. The overwhelming majority of our membership concur in this request. 

Thank you. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Thank you very much, Mrs. Jordan. A very good statement. 

Senator Carlson?

Senator CARLSON. No questions. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Next, the Reverend A. Dudley Ward, National 

Council of Churches.

STATEMENT OF REV. A. DUDLEY WARD, REPRESENTING THE NATIONAL COUNCIL OF CHURCHES 

Reverend WARD. Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, I believe that you already have our statement. 

Senator SPARKMAN. We do have it.

Reverend WARD. Yes, and I am requesting also that into the record there be inserted the policy statement of the National Council of Churches dealing with "World Poverty and the Demands of Justice," adopted on February 22, 1968.

Senator SPARKMAN. Without objection, that will be included at the end of your testimony. 

Reverend WARD. Thank you, sir.

My name is A. Dudley Ward. I am general secretary of the Board of Christian Social Concerns of the Methodist Church, and I appear today on behalf of the general board of the National Council of Churches, of which I am a member and have been for 9 years. 

The National Council of Churches is composed of 34 Christian communions whose aggregate membership is approximately 42,500,000. 

No one can speak for all these people, and I do not claim to. I am speaking only for the general board, which is the representative governing body of the National Council of Churches, and which is composed of 255 members chosen by the member communions in proportion to their size. The National Council of Churches appreciates this opportunity to present its views on the proposed Foreign Assistance Act of 1968, and the testimony is based on explicit policy adopted by the general board after a lengthy and democratic process of preparation. That is the document we have asked to be inserted. 

We are all aware of the basic facts concerning the impoverished conditions under which the majority of men live. The rich become richer and the poor become poorer. Modern communications have spread the knowledge of this gap around the world, together with the

knowledge that the nations collectively have the technogical capabilities to lift the burden of extreme poverty from all those who bear it. 

The result is a seedbed of social and political revolution, rising from the volatile mixture of high aspirations for freedom and justice with growing frustrations over the perpetuation of the impoverished conditions in which most men continue to live. 

CONCERN OF THE CHURCHES 

The churches have been concerned with this "scandal of the century" for many years. Long before Government undertakings, the churches pioneered in technical assistance and aid programs in education, literacy, literature, medicine, agriculture, industry, and leadership training. Such programs are still in progress, carrying on efforts to aid people in need around the world, to distribute surplus food, give disaster relief, and maintain various community projects. 

The concern of the churches in this area has been reflected in various official statements by the National Council of Churches and by other religious bodies. This concern is based squarely on the fundamental tenets of the Christian faith concerning the nature of God and man, God's intentions for His creatures and His creation, the interdependence of the human family, the quality of man's stewardship in his care of the earth and his use of his resources, and the value of human life. That any man should be deprived of basic human needs in a world of plenty is a violation of God's intention in creation. Yet this deprivation is a fact for more than half of the human family. We cannot escape a heavy sense of obligation to do something about it. 

But the magnitude of the problem is far beyond the capabilities of voluntary and private agencies. Only a massive Government effort can be sufficient for the task of long-range development. This of course has been recognized for many years to a degree, by this and other governments. Since 1948 the United States has made a substantial contribution to reconstruction and development in Europe, Asia, Africa, and 

Latin America.

Mr. Chairman, parenthetically, I would insert that after my personal trips around the world in an attempt to evaluate the place which both voluntary and Government aid has had in the development of underdeveloped economies, I must say that I come back extremely proud of the work which our country has done in this regard, and the qualifications that we put in our statement here are only those qualifications which we think are necessary to bring to more effective fruition the aid which we have already implanted and continued and I think the American citizens ought to be proud of this record. 

In recent years, however, there seems to have been a sharp drop in American interest and effort in international development. A once proud effort has now become the occasion for an annual bicker. According to one experienced official, many donor nations are guilty of "giving amounts of aid sufficiently large to irritate their own national legislatures, but too small to help accomplish real economic progress." Illustrative of this trend is the fact that the percentage of the gross national product now provided for international development had dropped sharply. I am sure you have also heard from several sources the criticisms of connecting U.S. cold war objectives

to aid policies and of allowing aid to be used as a prop for the status quo in countries where social change is desperately needed. There are also criticisms of trade arrangements which tend to negate any positive effects of the small amounts of aid forthcoming. 

DEFICIENCIES OF THE GENERAL AID PROGRAM 

The following are examples, from responsible sources, of some of the problems stemming from the deficiencies of our general aid program-and we give these, Mr. Chairman, simply to illustrate the point that you have already indicated that we think a development of the positive aid that America has put into these countries can be further magnified by taking into account the kind of items we are now elaborating. 

First, the public debt of 97 recipient countries has gone from $10 billion to $45 billion in a decade. It is estimated that if volume and terms remain unchanged, and terms if anything are hardening, the net benefit by 1975 to the recipients will be zero. 

2. In West Africa the price of imports has increased 25 percent in recent years while the price received for exports has dropped 4 percent. 

3. The share of world trade for the less developed nations has dropped from one-third to one-fifth in the past 10 years. One major reason has been the production of synthetics by the Western nations. 

4. The United States ties 90 percent of its aid to buy American purchases. The average for all donors is 60 percent. This raises the price of goods that could have been obtained cheaper on a competitive basis. Tying aid in this manner, either bilateral aid or IDA contributions, deprives the recipient nations of fair value for their money and tends to make aid a disguised subsidy to domestic manufacturers in the export business.

These and other such examples give some credence to the charge that things seem to be rigged against the less developed nations. More and more the rich nations tend to live in a closed technological economy in which the revolution of rising expectations is more than anything else an intrusion. More disturbing is the lack of any strong sense of obligation or of urgency. The vision, drive, and will to make an adequate contribution in the struggle against world poverty, as well as a genuine awareness of the seriousness of the problem seem to be lacking.

This is evident, if in nothing else, in a comparison of a defense budget request of $80 billion with an economic aid request of $2.5 billion. We need, among other things, a broader perspective of the meaning of security. The present threat to U.S. security arises at least as much from neglect of social justice as from any other source, and reveals thereby a new and tragic dimension to the problem of security for which traditional concepts are inadequate. Narrow interests of the self and the Nation are overstressed, concern for justice and the common interest understressed. Our Nation has done much to develop the science and technology and to amass the wealth which provide the promise of triumph over world poverty. We must now make a

response commensurate with our capacity. The real issue is not so much a technical one as a moral one. 

POLICIES IN INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENT EMPHASIZED 

The National Council of Churches, in its most recent statement, "World Poverty and the Demands of Justice," which you have in your hands, emphasized three broad areas of policy and action in international development which it considers important. 

1. First, we believe the U.S. contribution to development should be maintained and increased primarily because of the needs of people, and not in order to secure lesser objectives. While recognizing the power and to a certain degree the legitimacy of these other interestsmilitary security, the maintenance or extension of power and prestige, economic advantage-it is necessary to go far beyond them, basing development efforts primarily upon the more ultimate claims of mankind for justice and peace. Our Nation, in its own best tradition long the champion of justice, should clearly distinguish its immediate political and military objectives abroad from its development efforts, providing development aid to peoples as justice requires. Lesser interests and objectives should be submerged to secure the common interest inherent in the full development of peoples. Since economic assistance is based on arrangements between 

Sovereign nations, it is both inappropriate and increasingly unpopular for rich nations to use foreign aid to influence the political behavior of aid recipients. Aid is enevitably an aspect of foreign policy and has political overtones, but it is subverted when it becomes an instrument of direct political relationship.

RELATIONSHIP TO SOCIAL CHANGE 

2. A second aspect of aid, which we feel is important, concerns its relationship to social change. Development efforts should be careful to avoid support of an unjust or corrupt status quo in the countries concerned and should be designed to facilitate needed social change. To do this, four guidelines which would be particularly helpful are suggested. (a) U.S. policy should encourage the emergence of indigenous economic power in the less developed countries. Responsibility for development lies with these countries themselves— and I would again say parenthetically, where this has been done we have a marvelous record, both to the credit of our aid and to those who receive it. No one can do the job for them, but they cannot perform this task without economic power. They cannot perform it if they are dependent solely upon the actions of other nations. (b) U.S. policy should try to build upon the just aspirations of the peoples involved. In this age of intense nationalism there should be no hint of our trying to impose our ideology or beliefs upon other cultures. (c) U.S. policy should be sensitive to trade arrangements which will work especially to the benefit of the less developed nations. While

aid is essential, it can be largely negated by discriminatory trade arrangements. This means, among other things, international commodity agreements to stabilize export earnings on primary products from these nations and the liberalization of trade policies to expand access of the less developed countries to markets of the developed countries. (d) U.S. policy should undertake a vast increase in its foreign economic aid program. The amount of the program should be no less than 1 percent of the GNP for governmental development assistance and a further 1 percent of the GNP for private development assistance. In recent years our aid program has been decreasing further and further away from this minimal figure. Along these same lines, we would support multiyear aid authorizations for purposes of longterm planning, an increase in grants, low-interest and interest-free loans in order to secure less burdensome terms, and a progressive removal of "Buy American" purchase restrictions which hinder more economical procurement by recipient nations.

INTERNATIONALIZATION OF AID 

3. The third major emphasis in the recent NCC statement is that development assistance should, to the maximum degree possible, be provided through international channels and institutions. There is a strong tendency on the part of developed nations, the United States included, to tie development aid to the narrower interests of national policy. It would help to free development aid from these ties if there were an increase in the extent and authority of international institutions for development, particularly the specialized agencies of the U.N. It is also likely that the objective of facilitating needed social change can be secured more easily, without feelings and charges of outside interference, if aid is provided largely through international institutions. Finally, adequately funded multilateral aid is more likely to avoid unhealthy relationships of dependency than is bilateral aid.

My comments have been directed chiefly toward general concepts concerning our aid program rather than to the specific bill before the Congress. I do not feel competent to comment on the specific financial recommendations for particular parts of the world. My aim has been rather to indicate the attitude toward development aid which the organization I represent has held for many years and still holds and to make you aware of its latest thinking of that organization concerning the direction in which it feels it should go. 

BASIC PRINCIPLES OF AID PROGRAM SUPPORTED 

I will say, however, that all in all there seems to have been significant progress over the past 20 years as a result of our aid program, and that the basic principles for aid administration, as outlined by the President, show a commendable effort to incorporate past experience into future plans. (a) We support the principle of self-help from the point of view that development is most effective when governments and individuals

join in the effort to determine their own destiny and to assume responsibility for their own betterment.

Again parenthetically, Mr. Chairman, I think one of the wonderful evidences we have is the way in which India is meeting its agricultural burdens. It is highly expected that with the Government aid and with the development through private foundations with the cooperation of the Indian Government this year the agricultural return in India will meet the national goals of 90 million tons. I think this is an excellent illustration of what can happen in such a situation. 

We support the policy of giving a larger portion of our aid through multilateral channels for the reasons mentioned earlier. On the other hand, it will be necessary for us to maintain a U.S. aid program for some years to come, and, as we do this, we must seek continually to improve its quality and to negate the dangers of bilateral aid programs. (c) We support regional approaches in which assistance is given jointly to two or more nations through a mutually helpful regional project. In this regard we hope the United States will take advantage of the new opportunities to add momentum to regional efforts through support of the Inter-American, Asian, and African Development Banks. (d) We support the President's stated priority for agriculture and population planning. We are all aware of the dire predictions of a food crisis of enormous proportions in the next 10 to 20 years in the absence of concentrated effort to avoid it. This dual emphasis in the bill before you deserves strong support, as does the food-for-freedom program. Inadequate agricultural development in the world, and the famine which will result from that inadequacy, could make all the other acts of this Congress and of this Nation ultimately futile. 

CONCLUSIONS 

My remarks to this point lead me to this now obvious but nevertheless most important conclusion. The already inadequate request for international development assistance contained in this bill must not be further reduced. We would of course favor increased funds but, in view of what seems to be considered political reality, we must content ourselves with seeking support for the $2.5 billion economic aid appropriation requested and with asking that no amendments be attached to it which would limit the effectiveness of the program for sound development purposes. 

In closing, let me quote from the recent NCC statement on "World 

Poverty and the Demands of Justice":

Economic and social development require mutual effort among persons and nations. To think that even a vast increase of United States development effort will "solve the problem" is to be greatly misguided. Ultimate responsibility rests with the peoples, institutions and governments of the less developed countries, and a large share of the task must be undertaken-as in many cases it now isby other highly developed nations. The United States, by virtue of its favored position and moral tradition, has the obligation, however, to take leadership in the great task.

Mr. Chairman, I appreciate the opportunity of giving this statement. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Thank you. A very fine statement.

(The policy statement referred to earlier follows:) WORLD POVERTY AND THE DEMANDS OF JUSTICE-A POLICY STATEMENT OF THE NATIONAL COUNCIL OF THE CHURCHES OF CHRIST IN THE U.S.A. 

(Adopted by the General Board Feb. 22, 1968) 

The broad facts concerning poverty on the world scene are well known. The rich become richer, the poor, poorer. The highly developed nations are predominantly white with a bad tradition and practice of race relations; the less developed nations are vari-colored and possessors of vivid memories of colonial and racial oppression. In countries where poverty is dominant, high aspirations of freedom and justice will not be denied, even though the slow pace of achievement leads to dangerous frustration. It is known that the nations together now possess the technological knowledge to remove much of the burden of poverty from the peoples of the world. Yet, in most parts of the globe, including our own country, poverty and underdevelopment have become the seedbeds of social and political revolutions. These facts have caused many informed and concerned persons to issue warning after warning: this inequity, this injustice is brewing a human trouble that will dwarf our present difficulties. 

Although we in the United States of America have a greatly favored position of wealth and security, we are not making a response to the challenge of world poverty that is commensurate with our privilege or our moral responsibility. Our nation has done much to develop the science and technology, and to amass the wealth, which provide the promise of triumph over world poverty. 

Our very affluence, however, tends to muffle the cries of human need and to stifle our response to them.

It is to be acknowledged that since 1948 the United States has made a substantial contribution to reconstruction and development in Europe, Asia, Africa and Latin America. Governmental, business, private and church expenditures of money and personnel have been made. But a once proud effort now nears the point where it is an offense. The percentage of the gross national product provided for development abroad has dropped sharply. There is now little more than an annual bicker in the Congress concerning foreign aid. A heritage connecting 

United States objectives in the cold war with foreign aid stifles imagination. 

Once bold plans for development have shrunk into little more than props of the status quo in countries where social change is desperately needed. Private investment too frequently does little to produce development that benefits the country concerned. Trade barriers remain and protectionism increases. 

At the present time, the vision, drive and will to make an adequate contribution in the struggle against world poverty are generally lacking among the people, including the church people, of the United States. The government reflects this deficiency. Narrow interests of the self and nation ride too high, and concern for justice and the common interest too low. The deepest issues in the matter are not technical but moral. The urgent questions are: What is to be the purpose of the nation in relation to the less developed countries? Are the people of the United 

States willing to take needed action to help correct the injustice inherent in present-day poverty and underdevelopment?

In this situation, Christian faith discerns God's judgment. The living God has revealed to us in the Biblical history and message that such gross inequities as those which we permit are against His will. In other times, He has raised up people and movements that judge the wealthy. We may expect Him to do so again, for God does not permit a people to neglect justice for ease in Zion. 

The Gospel provides clear direction. The Biblical revelation clearly states that the great inequities between the oppressed and the oppressor, the poor and the rich, the outcast and the accepted rulers are contrary to God's will and must be redressed. The justice of God is fulfilled by a dynamic movement among men that replaces these inequities with a relationship that can be found righteous in the sight of God. The justice of God restores the oppressed and downtrodden to full humanity, providing redemption from their enforced misery. To act justly, the wealthy must move beyond their own narrow self-interest, and act in the common interest to secure the well-being of those now in the grip of poverty and subject to injustice.

In the present situation, at least three broad areas of policy and of action are essential.

I. 

The United States contribution to development should be increased and maintained because of the need of people, and not in order to secure lesser objectives. Careful studies have shown that many interests have, in fact, controlled the operation of the United States and other western nations in providing development aid. Military security, the maintenance or extension of power and prestige, and economic advantage have been the chief. Nevertheless, while recognizing the power of these interests, it is necessary to go far beyond them, basing development efforts rather upon the claims of humanity for justice and peace. 

Our nation, in its own best tradition long the champion of justice, should clearly distinguish its immediate political and military objectives abroad from its development efforts, providing development aid to peoples as justice requires. Lesser interests and objectives should be submerged to secure the common interest inherent in the full development of peoples. 

II. 

Development efforts on the part of the United States should avoid support of an unjust or corrupt status quo in the countries concerned, and should, in scope and objective, be designed to produce needed and rapid social change. Four measures are of particular importance at the present time. 

First, United States governmental policy should encourage and support the emergence of indigenous economic power, whether national or regional, in the less developed countries. Mere dependence upon the action of others cannot secure justice; justice requires a distribution of power so that true equalities rather than inequalities are the result. Responsibility for development lies with and within the less developed countries; no one can do their job for them. But without economic power they cannot perform their task. It should be a purpose of 

United States policy to discourage any public or private economic practices that withhold indigenous economic power and growth from the less developed countries, and to encourage this growth by an appropriate aid. 

Second, development efforts should build upon the just aspirations of the peoples involved, seeking to achieve social change in terms of indigenous desires for development. In this age of intense nationalisms and varied ideologies, development efforts by the United States should not reflect a desire to impose our ideology upon different cultures. It is essential to take time to understand development problems from the inside. Any preoccupation merely with dollars given, with quick profits, and with "American know-how" should give way to the longrange nurturing of economic institutions and leadership conducive to the economic prosperity of the country or region involved. 

Third, early, vigorous and generous attention should be given, in both public and private sectors to trade arrangements which will work especially to the benefit of the less developed countries. Aid is essential, but it is generally agreed that without beneficial trade arrangements, aid to the less developed countries is largely futile. The following measures are important at the present time: 

The negotiation of effective international commodity agreements to stabilize export earnings on primary products from the less developed countries; 

The liberalization of tariff policies and positive trade assistance to expand the access of the less developed countries to markets of the developed countries.

Fourth, a vastly increased foreign economic aid program for development should be established by the people and government of the United States. The amount of this program should be not less than 1% of the gross national product for governmental developmental assistance and a further 1% (total 2%) of the gross national product for private developmental assistance. The quantity of funds, especially in view of the current decrease in funds available, is of critical importance, both in substance and as a symbol of the intent and interest of the people of the United States. Of perhaps greater importance, however, is the use of funds to secure social change in the less developed countries. The following measures are essential:

To secure long-term planning, Congressional authorization of long-term assistance for periods of up to five years. rol forno) ed 

To secure less burdensome terms, an increase in the proportion of grants, low-interest and interest-free loans in the distribution of United States development funds.

To secure greater benefit from aid money, the progressive removal of "Buy American" purchase restrictions which prevent more economical procurement in other countries, thus hindering development in the less developed countries to which funds are made available. 

To secure population control, a more substantial program of education and health facilities for family planning where desired by the less developed countries.

To secure education for development, enlarged assistance to literacy programs and to the educational institutions and programs indispensable to the progress of economic and political development. 

To secure the leadership and democratic processes necessary for development, the fostering of cooperatives, labor unions, trade and related associations, community action groups and other movements and organizations indigenous to the less developed countries that work toward this end. 

III. 

Development assistance, whether in the form of aid or trade, should, to the maximum degree possible, be provided through international channels and institutions. One problem, noted above, is the tendency of western nations, the 

United States among them, to tie development aid to the narrower interests of national policy. One way, although not an automatic one, of freeing development efforts from these ties is to increase the extent and authority of international institutions for development, particularly the specialized agencies of the United Nations. A second problem, also noted above, is to insure that aid actually effects social change. It is likely that this objective can be secured more easily, without feelings and charges of outside interference by another nation, if aid is provided through international institutions. 

The most essential matter, however, concerns dependency. To avoid unhealthy relationships of dependency must be a prime objective of development policy. 

There is no easy answer to this complex problem, but a multilateral, international structure which is equipped with adequate funds, personnel and authority is more likely to avoid the gross problems of dependency than is aid administered primarily through bilateral arrangements. 

The strengthening of international institutions-regional or United Nationsfor economic development is, moreover, essential to the creation of a just peace. 

The present international scene is characterized by intense nationalism. Development, if it is to succeed, must build upon, and to that extent augment nationalism. In this way, development efforts are caught in the dilemma that, by fostering national growth, international life and community are fragmented. It is, therefore, essential that national development take place within the context, and through the instrumentalities of, greatly strengthened international institutions of economic development.

Economic and social development require mutual effort among persons and nations. To think that even a vast increase of United States development effort will "solve the problem" is to be greatly misguided. Ultimate responsibility rests with the peoples, institutions and governments of the less developed countries, and a large share of the task must be undertaken-as in many cases it now is-by other highly developed nations. The United States, by virtue of favored position and moral tradition, has the obligation, however, to take leadership in the great task. That will require a constituency in the United States, now largely lacking, which is responsive to the need. Christians have many channels through which to help form that constituency: the programs of the churches abroad, the business and private development efforts of which many Christians are a part, and the governmental programs in which Christians participate as officials and as citizens. A new responsiveness to the human need created by poverty, more vigorous action and sustained education and study are required, to the end that an informed and sacrificial constituency be created to spur the leadership of our nation in its contribution to justice amid world poverty. 

For: 112; Against: 0; Abstaining : 1.

Senator SPARKMAN. The next witness is the Reverend L. Maynard 

Catchings, the Council for Christian Action of the United Church of 

Christ, Washington, D.C.

STATEMENT OF REV. L. MAYNARD CATCHINGS, THE COUNCIL FOR CHRISTIAN SOCIAL ACTION AND THE UNITED CHURCH OF CHRIST'S COMMITTEE FOR INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENT Reverend CATCHINGS. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Senator SPARKMAN. We are glad to have you, sir.

Reverend CATCHINGS. My name is L. Maynard Catchings. I appear on behalf of the Council for Christian Social Action and the Committee for International Development of the United Church of Christ. 

The views that will be presented today are based on policy statements that have been adopted by these bodies and approved by the general synod of the denomination. 

As associate for international development I am the director of an office here in Washington that was established by the United Church of Christ in January of 1967 for the purpose of maintaining a relationship between our churches and its members, the Congress, and other agencies involved in problems of international development. It was our hope that this would help to focus proper attention on this area of concern which our denomination thought was entirely too important to be thrust aside even as we consider other important issues of the day. 

We are very much aware of many of the problems that we have here at home especially the inequities that have brought about the serious situations in our cities and pockets of rural poverty and we have tried to direct constructive programs toward these issues. However, we find that it is not possible to teach compassion on a selective basis and the concerns that we feel for unfortunate people here cannot be divorced from the concerns we feel for poverty everywhere in the world. 

We also know that the unrest we have witnessed recently here is but a small sample of the frustration that is smoldering in many parts of the underdeveloped parts of our world. Our denomination has also expressed its deep concern about our involvement in Vietnam and is most hopeful that the peace negotiations now going on in Paris will be productive.

However, it is difficult to understand how we can hope to create a credible image of a peace-loving nation as we continue to spend $30 billion annually on our war effort and yet talk of whittling down the already disastrously low amounts that we are willing to budget for peaceful purposes. It is our conviction that the only effort that will lead to any lasting peace will be a concerted attack on the problems of world hunger and poverty and the hopelessness and desperation which results.

NOT BEYOND FINANCIAL CAPABILITY 

To make such an effort is not beyond our financial capability. Even if we met the U.N. goal of 1 percent or more of our GNP, more than twice what we now give, it would still be a very small investment for the wealthiest country in the world to make in the pursuit of peace. It is really only a matter of helping our people to see it in this perspective and to help them to realize that, though these needs are not always as visible as those close at home they are not less important. 

Cuts in foreign aid could only save a very small percentage of our budget at most. It would do nothing to help our balance of payments

position as AID's anticipated amount of actual outflow for economic assistance in 1969 of $130 million is more than offset by expected loan repayments in the amount of $220 million plus sizable aid stimulated commercial exports--both of which would be substantially less without continuing assistance. Cuts would, however, hurt the U.S. economy by eliminating jobs and constricting growing foreign markets for the products of U.S. farms and factories. 

Cuts must not be viewed as lump sum savings that will not be missed but as the curtailment of specific and vital programs. We must realize that it means neglecting educational programs that are the only hope for training leadership for a new nation or curtailing nutritional research that might save a whole generation of children from mental deficiencies. It means backing down on a malarial control program that could make the difference between an apathetic nation and one with vigorous health, or that the great opportunities before us of doubling the food production in many parts of the world must be foregone.

Cutting assistance, however, would discourage some of the other developed nations from the recently increased efforts to help the poor nations. It would most certainly spread a wave of despair among the many people who have looked to us as champions of their hopes for a better life and would encourage the Communists to capitalize on the misery in these countries. I would encourage the less developed countries to look more to Communist inspired solutions. 

PROGRESS IN CERTAIN AREAS 

Perhaps the most serious result of any cuts at this time would be the fact that much that we have gained in the last 20 years in the way of experience, progress, and capability to use assistance more effectively might be largely lost. We have been gratified to see some spectacular successes as some countries have "graduated" from our assistance programs but many more are still in midstream or just beginning to show real advancement.

The last few years have brought about undreamed of progress in some areas and we are on the threshold of some of the most significant technological and sociological breakthroughs in history. Already in 

Pakistan where we were told that the tradition-bound peasants could not be changed, in 1 year 3.5 million acres were planted with entirely new high yielding varieties of seeds. In India where it was difficult to even give fertilizer away a few years ago, it is now a black market commodity. One fifth of India's foreign exchange is being spent on fertilizers. With these radically new attitudes developing in relation to use of modern techniques and the increased effort being made by the governments and peoples of the less developed countries, self sufficiency in food could become a reality in much of South Asia in 4 to 5 years.

Now is the time we need to keep moving ahead, We need to make possible the necessary inputs of fertilizer and insecticides, the technical assistance, the water and tools, et cetera, for optimum results. We must continue research so that the new high yielding seed varieties will not become the victims of new diseases. Increased efforts must be made to involve U.S. private groups in this effort so that people 773

oriented institutions can be developed for distribution of credit and agricultural inputs, for improved marketing, and for generally broadening the base of local participation in the expanding economy. 

We are, therefore, at a critical time in history. Today there is a new optimism that if we push on vigorously in our efforts we might really be able to tip the balance in the race between the mouths and food and thus lay the base for continued development that will bring the basic necessities of life within the reach of the world's peoples. If we let up, even momentarily, that moment might be lost and we may as easily tip the scales the other way and doom civilization to even greater struggles in the face of growing scarcity. 

MULTILATERAL PROGRAMS 

We are encouraged to see other developed nations increasing their efforts in this direction and that even Britain is maintaining its level of assistance in the face of their financial problems. We are also encouraged to see an increasing amount of our own aid being channeled through multilateral programs and strongly support the $154 million that is budgeted for international organizations this year. We recognize that this has many advantages such as preserving the dignity of the recipient, freedom from the suspicion of political motives, and the ability to make firmer demands for better performance. However, knowing how great the needs will be in the years to come and realizing that our position of great wealth calls for us to bear a larger portion of that burden than can reasonably be channeled through international agencies, we are convinced that the United States must also continue its own aid program. We are glad to see many steps being taken to help negate the possible disadvantages and dangers of a strictly bilateral relationship, such as multilateral receiving plans, multinational advisory committees, consortium and consultative groups.

WAYS TO IMPROVE THE QUALITY OF THE AID PROGRAM 

While we support the present AID program we feel we must constantly work toward improving the quality and nature of these programs so that they might increasingly come to bear on the problems of the people at the grassroots levels in the developing countries. In order to do this we think that several things are necesary. 

1. We must increase the level of appropriations so that our efforts may be more commensurate with the pressing needs of the moment. 

2. We must give more aid in the form of grants and low interest loans realizing realistically that the real repayment we seek from such investments will come in increased stability around the world and increased trade opportunities for the future. 

3. To carry on this program as it should be done, there is need for much greater continuity to allow for proper planning and for developing a greater body of professional expertise. This would call for authorization for periods up to at least 5 years. 

4. We look forward to the day when it will be possible to remove "buy American” requirements to the extent that they make procure- 91-392-68-pt. 2- -10

ment more expensive and hinder the industrial development of the various developing countries themselves. 

5. We would hope to see the elimination of restrictions on making food and other forms of humanitarian assistance available to countries which may be engaged in trade with Communist states. 

Much as we would like to see a bill presented to the Congress that more accurately represents the full commitment we feel we should be called upon to make at this time, we would respectfully call upon this committee to stand firm against any further cuts in this vital program and to seek the passage of this bill without any amendments or restrictions that would limit its effectiveness for sound development purposes. 

Thank you.

Senator SPARKMAN. Thank you, Reverend Catchings. 

Senator Carlson?

You have given us a very helpful statement and we appreciate it. 

Next is Mr. W. B. Hicks, Jr., Liberty Lobby. 

STATEMENT OF W. B. HICKS, JR., EXECUTIVE SECRETARY, LIB- ERTY LOBBY, ACCOMPANIED BY MICHAEL JAFFE, GENERAL COUNSEL Mr. HICKS. Good morning, Mr. Chairman. 

I am accompanied again today by Michael Jaffe, our general counsel. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Mr. Hicks, will you wait for me to make an announcement?

Some who are going to testify have come into the room since I made the earlier statement. We have a long list of witnesses for this morning and we want to conclude. In fact I have another committee meeting this afternoon and, therefore, cannot be present. In order to finish, I appeal again to all witneses to submit their statements and summarize what is in it in as short a time as possible. 

All right, Mr. Hicks.

Mr. HICKS. Mr. Chairman, if it is possible to have our statement entered in the record as if read, I will summarize it by hitting the highlights. I would also beg your indulgence to allow me to make a couple of remarks that are not in the statement, having to do with some of the earlier testimony presented here today.

Senator SPARKMAN. Certainly. 

AID PROGRAM NOT SUPPORTED 

Mr. HICKS. To summarize our statement, we feel that the foreign aid program has been tried for 20 years and has failed. It has become an unacceptable burden on the taxpayer, a dangerous drain on our gold reserves. We do not feel that the United States can afford the luxury of a foreign aid program any longer. 

We believe this committee can perform a highly positive service both for the foreign policy and for the economic stability of the United States by refusing any further authorization. 

We feel that the program has not made friends and allies for the United States as promised by its advocates. Rather, one after another of the nations which have benefited from America's misguided

1 generosity have gone out of their way to hamper the achievement of our foreign policy goal.

Some of these nations are France, Burma, India, Pakistan, the United Arab Republic, Tanzania, Yugoslavia, Poland; the list is seemingly endless.

Clearly, then, the foreign aid program has been a failure from the standpoint of winning friends and allies to the United States. 

As to the underdeveloped nations of the world, if they are ever to become self sustaining, they need the development of private industry, the accumulation of private capital. We feel that American foreign aid has hindered rather than aided the development of free enterprise in the underdeveloped nations.

After receiving more than $7 billion in foreign aid, India's economic status today is worse than when the foreign aid program began, and we feel that one of the reasons for this is that the availability of huge foreign aid sums has encouraged the construction of costly social industrialization projects that have been built for reasons of prestige rather than for reasons of economics at the expense of their food production.

We feel that it has encouraged them to ignore the difficult task of developing private industry. 

Further, we feel that a good part of American aid is consumed in wars fought between our aid recipients. Congress was told that the nearly $10 billion given to India and Pakistan would be used for defense against communism, for development of the economies of those countries. Instead, they have used a large portion of that aid to fight each other.

American foreign aid has been used to further the aggressive ambitions of nations in every part of the world. 

The termination of the program, we feel, would be a useful step toward encouraging nations to settle their disputes peaceably. 

We also believe that termination of foreign aid will reduce the possibility of the United States becoming involved in future conflicts. 

Our experience in Vietnam has demonstrated that the dispensing of massive foreign aid can easily lead to American commitment in a foreign war.

EFFECT ON BALANCE OF PAYMENTS 

Further, proponents of the foreign aid program attempt to confuse the issue by contending that the program has a minimal adverse effect, if any at all, on our balance of payments. 

We think the record shows that the United States actually has had over the years a favorable payments balance with the rest of the world when you consider only private trade.

It is Government spending abroad, a large part of which is foreign aid spending, which has caused the balance of payments crisis. 

In 1966, the Department of Commerce reported that exports of goods and services financed by Government grants and loans amounted to nearly $3 billion.

In that same year, aid expenditures, excluding war costs, were $6.7 billion. Thus, we calculate that nearly 44 percent of aid funds were spent overseas.

It would appear to demonstrate that foreign aid is one of the principal causes of the balance of payments deficit. 

We feel that elimination of the foreign aid program would relieve the American taxpayers of a completely unnecessary burden, and remove a large part of the need for the administration's tax increase. 

Administration spokesmen have let it be known that the spending cut which is to accompany the tax increase will come out of such popular programs as highway construction, veterans' benefits, the school lunch program, et cetera, et cetera. 

How can any Member of Congress explain that he is for a program which will send food to India to a constituent whose child has been deprived of a school lunch program? 

How can he explain support for highway construction in Afghanistan to a constituent who needs a new interstate highway at home? 

Members of Congress can avoid these and similar dilemmas by refusing to vote for any foreign aid authorization this year, thereby sending the program to its long delayed demise. 

That summarizes our original prepared statement. 

MULTILATERAL PROGRAMS OPPOSED 

But in the statement of previous witnesses this morning there were numerous references to the desirability of multilateral aid, and at this point I think Liberty Lobby should go on record as opposing strenuously further extension of the multilateral aid programs. This includes not only those that go through the foreign aid program but also such as the Asian Development Bank, Inter-American Development Bank, the International Development Association.

We think that these multilateral aid proposals effectively remove foreign aid and the expenditure of the taxpayers' money from control by the taxpayer, through his representatives in the Congress. And we think this alone is reason enough not to go further into multilateral aid programs.

In closing, we think the record should reflect that Liberty Lobby is obviously outnumbered by the numerous organizations who come before the committee to support foreign aid. Their millions of members may superficially appear to outweigh our 200,000 subscribers, but we question this domination by supporters of the aid program. We think it superficial. Every poll of voter sentiment shows that the voters oppose foreign aid. Hence, we question the position of those organizations who come before you to say that the majority of Christians support foreign aid or the majority of women, or labor union members.

In view of all the surveys of public opinion this is impossible to believe.

Second, we urge the committee to take into account the vested interests of such organizations in continued foreign aid. Each of these groups should be asked to explain their own financial interest in foreign aid. Certainly in some cases such as church groups, you will find that the vested interest is small indeed, amounting to only a few thousand dollars in subsidized shipments of materials to their over-

seas mission. However, small relatively, this subsidy nevertheless contitutes a vested interest.

In other cases such as labor organizations, the vested interest amounts to millions of dollars annually. 

I must point out that Liberty Lobby no matter how lonely in its opposition to the foreign aid program nevertheless is, I believe, a group that comes before you to speak not for a vested interest but for the taxpayers of the United States. We do not enjoy the tax exemptions enjoyed by those who support foreign aid. Our supporters are the working people who have to pay taxes to support foreign aid, and they are weary of the burden. 

Thank you. 

(The prepared statement of Mr. Hicks follows:) STATEMENT OF W. B. HICKS, JR., EXECUTIVE SECRETARY, LIBERTY LOBBY 

Mr. Chairman and Members of the Committee, I am W. B. Hicks, Jr., Executive

Secretary of Liberty Lobby. I am appearing before this Committee today to present the views of our 12,000 member Board of Policy, on behalf of nearly 200,000 subscribers to our monthly legislative report, Liberty Letter.

On numerous occasions in the past, Liberty Lobby has appeared before this and other Congressional committees to point out the total failure of American foreign aid programs, and to call for their reduction or elimination. Nothing in the recent experience of the foreign aid program has altered our view that the program is not only wasteful and unnecessary, but has actually done more harm than good to the conduct of America's foreign relations. We therefore oppose 

S. 3091 and we suggest that this should be the year in which the burden of foreign aid is finally removed from the shoulders of the American taxpayer. 

Since the inception of the foreign aid program at the end of World War II through 1967, according to calculations by Rep. Otto Passman, the program has -cost the American taxpayers $152,533,000,000. This includes more than $37 billion interest on the money we have borrowed to give away to our numerous aid recipients. The funds appropriated for foreign aid last year, amounting to $2,295,635,000, bring the total close to $155 billion. 

S. 3091 would authorize the expenditure of an additional $2,962,245,000. As has been customary in recent years, the Administration boasts that this is the smallest request in the history of the program. And, judging by recent experience, we are confident that the Congress will cut this request. This, however, is not enough. The foreign aid program has had a trial of more than 20 years, and has been found wanting. It has become a totally unacceptable burden on the American taxpayer, and a dangerous drain on America's rapidly decreasing gold reserves. The United States can no longer afford the wasteful luxury of a foreign aid program, and this committee can perform a highly positive service for both the foreign policy and the economic stability of the United States by refusing any further authorization.

PURPOSE OF FOREIGN AID 

President Johnson, in his 1968 foreign aid message, described the fundamental American purpose in foreign aid as: "To help root out the causes of conflict and thus insure our own security in a peaceful community of nations." But, in fact, the billions of dollars the United States has expended on international handouts have done nothing to insure, or even advance, the security of the United States. 

The program has not made friends and allies for the United States as promised by its advocates. Rather, one after another of the nations which have benefited from America's misguided generosity have gone out of their way to hamper the achievement of our foreign policy goals.

With the United States presently engaged in a bloody seemingly endless war in Southeast Asia, a war which has cost 20,000 American lives, our aid recipients would have proved the validity of our foreign aid program by coming to our assistance at this time. And if the arguments by which foreign aid has been sold

to the Congress were correct, there would presently be more than 125 nations: fighting at our side in Vietnam.

This kind of repayment has obviously not been forthcoming. With very few exceptions, nations which have been heavy recipients of American foreign aid have not only failed to help this country in Vietnam, but have actually been of great assistance to the enemy. France, which has received more American foreign aid than any other nation (more than $9 billion) openly supports North Vietnam's war effort, and is engaged in an all-out effort to destroy the American dollar by draining our gold reserve. Burma, the recipient of $111 million in 

American aid, remains strongly anti-American. Its sole return for this huge giveaway has been U.N. Secretary General U Thant, whose performance in the past several years has been that of a virtual North Vietnamese agent. 

Gifts of more than one billion dollars to the United Arab Republic have not deterred Gamal Abdel Nasser from continually attacking the United States, and working against our interests. Nasser demonstrated his gratitude to the 

American taxpayer by providing the Soviet Union with a naval base in his country, thus allowing the fulfillment of the vital Communist goal of a Mediterranean base for the Soviet fleet. 

Gifts of more than $44 million to Tanzania have not prevented that African nation from becoming a Communist base for aggression against the pro- American and anti-Communist nations of southern Africa. Other African nations, recipients of close to $2 billion in American aid, ignore the betterment of their own people and use a good part of our aid money for subversive activities which aid international Communism. 

American foreign aid to the outright Communist nations of Yugoslavia and Poland has totaled more than $2 billion. This huge amount has done nothing to induce those nations to drop Communism, or to refrain from heavy support of North Vietnam.

FOREIGN AID HAS BEEN A FAILURE 

Clearly, then, the American foreign aid program has been a failure from the standpoint of winning friends and allies for the United States. This, alone, should be enough to warrant immediate termination of the program. However, it should also be kept in mind that, even from a "humanitarian" point of view, foreign aid has been a horrendous failure, and should be stopped before it does any more damage.

If the so-called underdeveloped nations of the world are ever to become selfsustaining, they will need the development of private industry and the accumulation of private capital. However, American foreign aid has hindered rather than aided the development of free enterprise in the "underdeveloped" nations, and has actively promoted the further growth of socialism. After receiving more than $7 billion in American aid, India's economic status today is worse than when the foreign aid program began. The availability of huge foreign aid sums has encouraged the construction of costly socialist industrialization projects, built for prestige, rather than for sound economic reasons, at the expense of food production. The difficult task of encouraging the growth of private industry has been ignored or discouraged.

We also suggest that the Committee give serious consideration to the disruptive effects the military assistance aspects of our foreign aid program have had on the peace and stability of large areas of the world. A good part of American aid is consumed in wars fought between our aid recipients. Congress was told that the nearly $10 billion given to Indian and Pakistan would be used for defense against Communism and for development of the economies of those countries. Instead, they have used a large portion of this aid to fight each other. 

The American taxpayer has given well over $4 billion to the various nations of the Middle East. This aid money is used for unending military activity which has so disrupted the stability of the area that the United States may, in the near future, be maneuvered into active military intervention unwanted by Congress and the American people. But the dispensers of American foreign aid want to continue giving away large sums of money to both Israel and the Arab nations. Such a course can only guarantee the outbreak of a fourth Arab- 

Israeli war.

It seems highly ironic to us that continuance of the foreign aid program can be advocated by organizations and individuals claiming to give the highest priority to peaceful resolution of international disputes. American foreign aid has demonstrably been used to further the aggressive ambitions of nations in

every part of the world, and termination of the program would be a useful step toward encouraging nations to settle their disputes peaceably. Termination of foreign aid giveaways would also reduce the possibility of the United States becoming involved in future wars. Our experience in Vietnam has demonstrated that the dispensing of massive foreign aid can easily lead to American commitment in a foreign war. 

EFFECT ON U.S. BALANCE OF PAYMENTS 

Even if the facts proving the failure of foreign aid did not exist, it should be clear to anyone concerned with the survival of the American economy that our present balance of payments deficit makes it imperative that no further foreign aid spending be authorized.

Proponents of the foreign aid program attempt to confuse the issue by contending that the program has a minimal adverse effect, if any at all, on our balance of payments. In his 1967 foreign aid message, President Johnson told the Congress that "This Administration will continue to see that our aid programs have the least possible adverse effect on our balance of payments. Almost 

90 percent of our economic assistance and more than 95 percent of our military assistance is now spent in the United States "And again in 1968, the President's aid message claimed that "More than nine dollars out of every ten dollars AID spends will buy American goods and services. And the repayments of prior loans will more than offset the small outflow from new loans." 

The facts, however, are different. Dr. N. R. Danielian, president of the International Economic Policy Association, states in his book The United States Balance of Payments that, "On the record, the contribution of foreign aid in all its forms to (annual) deficits is certainly close to $750 million. If the 'substitution effect' is added it is not unreasonable to assume that the total contribution of this sector of government programs abroad exceeds one billion dollars." 

By the "substitution effect" is meant the fact that countries whose imports from the United States are subsidized under the foreign aid program use the dollar proceeds of their exports to pay for imports from third countries, thus upsetting the American payments balance.

The United States actually has a favorable payments balance with the rest of the world when only private trade is considered It is government spending abroad, a large part of which is foreign aid spending, which has caused the balance of payments crisis. In 1966, the Department of Commerce reported that "exports of goods and services financed by government grants and loans" amounted to $2.944 billion. In the same year, AID reported its total expenditures, excluding war costs, as $6.751 billion. Thus, 43.6 per cent of AID funds were spent in the United States, with the remainder spent elsewhere. This would appear to demonstrate that foreign aid is one of the principal causes of the balance of payments deficit.

If foreign aid spending is eliminated, therefore, we will have gone a good part of the way toward correcting the deficit in our balance of payments. Elimination of the foreign aid program will also promote the cause of an anti-Communist foreign policy and of world peace. 

Not least important, it will relieve the American taxpayer of a completely unnecessary burden, and remove a large part of the need for the Administration's tax increase. At this very moment, the Congress is getting ready to enact that onerous 10 per cent tax increase into law, along with a $6 billion reduction in federal expenditures. Administration spokesmen have let it be known that most of this spending cut will come out of such popular programs as highway construction, veterans' benefits, and the school lunch program. 

REDUCTIONS IN GOVERNMENT SPENDING URGED 

Liberty Lobby has always advocated drastic reductions in government spending, and we would like to see even greater cuts than the presently contemplated $6 billion. We cannot understand, however, the justification for continuing exorbitant foreign aid spending while at the same time cutting programs which benefit-to some extent-a large number of American voters. 

How can any member of Congress explain a vote for a program which will send food to India to a constituent whose child had been depending on the school lunch program until it was curtailed with the approval of this same Congressman? How can he explain support for highway construction in Afghani-

stan to a Congressional district angry over the delay in construction of a new interstate highway at home? Members of Congress can avoid these, and similar dilemmas, by refusing to vote for any foreign aid authorization this year, thereby sending the program to its long delayed demise. Such action will earn them the universal esteem of the American voters and taxpayers, as well as an honorable place in the history of America's foreign relations. 

Thank you. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Thank you, Mr. Hicks.

Mrs. Kouzes. Mrs. Ryan is national chairman, but could not be here.

SUPPORT FOR UNICEF 

On behalf of our membership, we wish to reaffirm our established policy of support for the United Nations Childrens' Fund (UNICEF), and urge your approval for a contribution of $13 million for the fiscal year 1969. Costs are rising at an alarming rate and it would be equivalent to a decrease in material support if the U.S. contribution to the work of UNICEF was not increased this year. In fact, it is sincerely felt that an increase to $15 million could be well justified.

The National PTA comprises more than 11 million memberships in 50 States, in the District of Columbia, and in the American overseas dependents schools. It has been dedicated since its beginning, in 1897, to the health, education, and welfare of American children, and has been in the forefront in encouraging the development of UNICEF into a permanent organization concerned with the welfare of all children in the world.

It is reliably estimated that there are 1 billion children in the world today, and three out of four of them live in the devoloping countries. 

Six hundred million of these children suffer from hunger, disease, or lack of education, and frequently all three. Poverty entails ignorance, one of the causes of malnutrition; malnutrition brings disease and this, in turn produces poverty and want. This vicious circle is fearsome in its consequences. 

UNICEF has always been in the forefront of the fight for the preservation of the world's greatest resource-its children. It is known that 

UNICEF could usefully allocate $10 million more at the upcoming June meeting of its executive board. These are all valid requests if funds were available. This is even after considering the growing volume 4444

of private contributions to UNICEF resulting from increased activities of national UNICEF committees in 24 countries. UNICEF is currently assisting 49 projects in 119 countries. Its aid represents a contribution to both the welfare and economic development for the individual and the Nation.

UNICEF's assistance policies have been reviewed and endorsed by its executive board, by ECOSOC (Economic and Social Council), and by the Geenral Assembly of the United Nations. It is believed that all governments in the family of nations should now give effective substance to their many laudatory speeches of commendation in connection with the Nobel Peace Prize received by UNICEF in 1965. In 1967, 34 governments did increase their contribution and some of these have made several increases since 1960, while the U.S contribution has remained the same for 8 years. In 1967, 123 governments contributed to 

UNICEF's central account. The assisted countries, of course, provided matching funds, usually $2.32 for each UNICEF dollar.

UNICEF'S ROLE 

UNICEF's role has always been to aid projects for the long-range benefit of children and youth, but the present world tensions have brought added demands. Natural disaster and conflicts have created urgent needs in three areas-India, the Middle East, and Vietnam. 

An allocation of $1.4 million was made to India in 1967. Emergency relief of $496,000 was approved for the Middle East last June, and an additional $512,000 was recently approved by the UNICEF executive board. Nearly $1 million has been approved during the last year for health, social services, education, tuberculosis control, and emergency aid for the war-torn country of Vietnam. 

What we do here, along with the other nations of the world, may well determine whether development becomes indeed a reality and the path to peace, or an empty slogan. UNICEF cannot, alone do all that needs to be done, but it could, with additional resources, make an evergreater contribution above its already outstanding record of assistance. The consequences for good of this care and training is incalculable for these children are the potential leaders of tomorrow. These are the children, I would like to remind you, who will work alongside our children as adults in an increasingly interdependent world. 

It is a sad commentary on our world to note that UNICEF continues to help millions of mothers and children each year with less money than the governments of the world spend on armaments in less than 2 hours of a single day. Would that we could spend more of our money and talents in efforts to solve these health, poverty, and education needs. 

With this awareness of our country's leadership in building this better world for our heirs, we are grateful for this committee's careful consideration of this request for at least $13 million for UNICEF this year. 

Thank you very much.

STATEMENT OF HON. JACOB K. JAVITS, U.S. SENATOR FROM NEW YORK 

Senator JAVITS. Thank you very much, Mr. Chairman. 970 Mr. Chairman, I am grateful to the committee for the opportunity to testify and to fit the testimony into this hearing. 

I might point out that as long ago as 1965 Senator Fulbright assured me that this proposal which I shall lay before the committee, now incorporated in amendment No. 772 to S. 3091, would receive the committee's consideration.

Also today in support of this proposal, Senator Brooke is proposing to appear--he will be here very shortly-and the author of the proposal, who happens to be my own brother, has come down to make his expertise available to the committee, should there be any question which calls for it.

Now, Mr. Chairman, this amendment is identical to S. 3415 which 

I introduced on May 1 with 11 cosponsors and which is now pending before this committee. Along with many of my colleagues, I believe that the Senate is approaching the foreign aid bill with deep misgivings, even deeper than in previous years. And along with my colleagues who have cosponsored this legislation, I feel that the time has now come to press for new approaches in our efforts to aid the developing nations.

PEACE BY INVESTMENT CORPORATION 

This legislation which is incorporated in this amendment would establish a "Peace by Investment Corporation," a federally chartered corporation to engage in industrial development and technical assistance activities in developing nations, using both private and public funds.

Now, the corporation's principal function is to act as a catalyst, to tap the vast private resources, available in the United States and make a portion of it, as much as $1.25 billion, available for investment in profitmaking enterprises in the developing portions of the world. Such a corporation would be in a better position than our Government to assist developing countries in developing their industries, the skill of their manpower, their distribution facilities and to create new employment.

Under the terms of this legislation, $50 million in initial and temporary capital funds for the corporation would be subscribed by the U.S. Government through the U.S. Treasury as capital stock. 

During the first 6 years of its operation, the corporation would also be authorized to borrow from the Treasury in amounts up to $60 million in any one year, but not to exceed $300 million at any one time. 

IMPORTANT SOURCE OF CORPORATION'S FUNDS 

The most important source of the corporation's funds, however, would be obtained through the sale of peace by investment debentures in small denominations to the American public. These deben-

༠༠༥ tures could produce up to $375 million in any one year, but not to exceed $1.25 billion at any one time. During the initial 6-year period of the corporation's existence, it would function as an agency of the United States. Upon retirement of the Government's participation, whatever that might prove to be in loans and capital, the corporation would go through an orderly transition from an agency of the Government to private operation and management. 

PROPOSAL IS NOT A NOVEL ONE 

190 lux Now, Mr. Chairman, I would like to point out that this is by no means now the novel proposal which it was when my brother first made it in a book called "Peace by Investment," that he wrote quite a long time ago, in 1947, a copy of which is here, and the reason is the following: 

Other governments have now tried this concept and found it successful.

I have tried it in the development of the ADELA Investment Co. in Latin America and it is superlatively successful and it is now being suggested as a cardinal element in our foreign aid program by a subcommittee in the House presided over by Representative Farbstein. 

GERMAN DEVELOPMENT CORPORATION 

into Now, the foreign counterparts of this proposal are found in West Germany and in Britain. The West Germans have run part of their foreign aid program through something called the German Development Corporation, which they say is extremely successful, established in 1962 and now has roughly $60 million invested in various going projects, and generally speaking, would operate pretty much the same way that the Peace by Investment Corporation does; that is, go joint ventures with private enterprise as well as with public enterprise, with the right to raise additional money on the open market, and also giving technical assistance, together with its loans, within the corporate structure, and being able to dispose of its investments at such times as they mature for disposition, thus having a rollover of its corporate funds.

The West Germans like that plan very much, and I ask unanimous consent to include a brief analysis of the German Development Corp. as part of my testimony.
