THE GERMAN CORPORATION FOR DEVELOPMENT COOPERATION (DEVELOPMENT CORPORATION) LTD. 

(a) As a means to promote private investment in the developing countries and thereby to attribute to private economy a more important role in develop ment aid, the German Corporation for Development Cooperation (Development 

Corporation) Ltd., has been established in Cologne on 14th September 1962. This corporation has been founded under private law and has the character of a Handelsgesellschaft (trade company). Its capital stock amounts to DM75 million ($18.8 million) and will be made available out of the Federal budgets of the years 1962 to 1964. It may be possible that the Development Corporation will later on raise additional funds on the private capital market. (b) The Development Corporation is responsible for encouraging the economic development of developing countries in conformity with the principles of development policy and within the scope of the appropriate measures of the Federal Government by primarily facilitating partnerships between German firms and enterprises in developing countries and supporting such procedure. 

The Development Corporation is to concentrate its activity on the expansion and the development of small and medium-sized firms of the industrial economy, of handicraft and agriculture and thus to contribute towards creating both a solvent and able middle class as well as a sound social structure in the developing countries. 

The Development Corporation may pursue its various aims by acquiring participations in enterprises of developing countries or by extending such enterprises participation-like loans, by granting guarantees, by giving credit mandates and solving matters involving trusteeship. Finally, it is the Development Corporation's function to give advice to firms on possibilities of making investments in developing countries as well as to bring together those firms in developing countries which are interested in a partnership with the appropriate firms. It must be stressed that the Development Corporation can also participate in similar Development Corporations or like institutions in the developing countries. In accordance with the principles of German economic policy, the Development Corporation will sell its participations in domestic and foreign enterprises once it has jumped the "risk beam" and the participation yields a profit. (c) More than eighty proposals for projects from domestic sources and foreign countries have so far been received by the Development Corporation, and such proposals cover almost all fields of economic activity and are regionally distributed abong Africa, Asia, South-American and also European developing countries. As a rule, the Development Corporation will give priority to projects in which a firm of an industrial country participates with its own capital and furthermore takes the responsibility for the establishment and the operation of the plant. In this way not only private capital but also know-how skills and entrepreneurial initiative will be availed of for expanding the economy of the country in the course of development. The completion of the first projects is impending. In addition to the Commonwealth Development Finance Corporation

the German Development Corporation will participate in the development companies of the countries of Tanganyika, Kenya and Uganda whose functions corre spond to the objectives of the Development Corporation (of Germany).

(The material referred to follows:) [From the British Aid for Overseas Development, September 1965-Fact sheet No. 71 THE COMMONWEALTH DEVELOPMENT CORPORATION 

Electricity services in Dominica. fertilisers in Trinidad ... port facilities in Jamaica .. hemp and rubber and cocoa production in Sabah. . . oil palm, flour milling, land development in Malaya... development finance, power, tea production in Kenya . . . mining, milling, sugar production in Tanzania. electricity, water and tung oil in Malawi. cattle ranching and an abattoir in Bechuanaland large scale irrigated farming, pulp mill and a railway in Swaziland . . . hotels, steel and rubber manufacture and cement in Nigeria housing development in many countries industrial and agricultural estates in other Commonwealth territories. These are the sort of projects assisted by the investments of the Commonwealth Development Corporation (CDC), a statutory body set up by Act of 

Parliament. The Chairman, Deputy Chairman and Members of the Board of 

CDC are appointed by the Minister of Overseas Development. 

Terms of Reference: In general, CDC's task is to work as a commercial organisation, investing its funds in development schemes for the promotion or expansion of economic projects that will not only help to increase the wealth of the territories but will also yield a reasonable return on the money invested. 

The corporation is a non profit making organisation but is expected to pay its way taking one year with another.

Area of Operations: CDC operates in all Commonwealth countries which have achieved independence since 1948, and in the remaining dependent territories. It is not permitted to invest in the old Dominions, nor in India, Pakistan and Ceylon, but with the agreement of the Governments concerned CDC can act as managing agents and render advisory services in any independent Commonwealth country. 

Finance: CDC has powers to borrow up to £ million on long- or medium-term and £10 million on short-term. It may borrow up to £130 million outstanding at any one time from U.K. Exchequer Funds. 

Organisation: The General Manager, as chief executive, is responsible to the 

Chairman and Board for all CDC operations. He is assisted by an Executive Man agement Board comprising Head Office Controllers, Regional Controllers and other senior executives.

There are six overseas regions Caribbean, East Asia and Pacific Islands, East

Africa, Central Africa, Southern Africa and West Africa, with a resident Re-gional Controller in charge of each. 

Operations: Within the wide field of economic development there is great variety in the nature of CDC's projects. They include basic development such as power and water supply, transportation and housing finance; primary production incorporating agriculture, ranching, forestry and mining, and investments in commerce and industrial projects including factories, industrial development companies and hotels, CDC does not lend money for social services such as schools and hospitals or for government administration. 

Investment methods: The Corporation does not offer aid: it offers investment in the development of resources. Its investment methods are flexible. CDC has ininvestments in wholly owned projects, investments by way of ordinary shares, debentures and loan capital in subsidiary and associated companies and loans to Governments and statutory bodies: it favours particularly joint companies with local entrepeneurs and local capital. 

Pattern of CDC Projects: Within the range of projects referred to above, CDC investment has recently been increasingly directed to projects which involve association with and support of the peoples, collectively and individually, in the countries in which it operates. Such projects are smallholder schemes in agriculture (often attached to commercial nucleus estates), mortgage companies for the prospective house purchaser and support for the small entrepeneur through local development companies for industry. 

Management: CDC itself bears responsibility for providing management for many projects. A number are managed by experienced commercial partners. 

Association with other interests: Investments made by CDC over the years illustrate the wide variety of arrangements linking the Corporation with other investors. CDC is associated with territorial governments, with the West German 

Government and Netherlands Government development agencies, the World Bank and International Finance Corporation, Commonwealth Development Finance - 

Company and leading banks, insurance and commercial companies from Australia, Britain, Canada, Holland, New Zealand, South Africa and the United States of America.

Projects at end—1964: At 31st December 1964, CDC had 121 projects and in-vestigations in 30 Commonwealth countries involving a total estimated CDC® commitment of nearly £125 million of which nearly £100 million had been invested. 

Of the commitment 52.5% was in basic development projects, 26.5% in commerce and industry and 21% in primary production. 

CDC's investment in primary production and manufacturing enterprises alone was associated in 1964 with a turnover of £60 million of which goods worth £31 million were exported--a substantial part to countries outside the sterling area.. 

Training: CDC not only provides money for development. It also brings in skilled management and skill in training local people as the managers of the future. CDC attaches great importance to the training of the people of the territories in which it operates to undertake posts of responsibility in individual. projects and in the CDC organisation as a whole. A great deal of training is done on the job. CDC also offers a number of scholarships and bursaries in technical subjects to academically qualified local people and sponsors promising members of its staff for U.K. courses in management, accountancy, engineering, and industrial relations. 

1966 STATUS 

6. The Commonwealth Development Corporation continues as one of our most effective means of contributing to development and merits particular mention. Its sphere of action covers all countries which were dependencies of Britain in 1948. Many of these countries are now independent. Those included are effectively all the developing countries of the Commonwealth except India, Pakistan and Ceylon. Most of its finance has been provided by loans from the British Govern- ment and it invests usually in conjunction with commercial companies, governments of the less developed countries, or other aid giving bodies. It is required to pay back borrowing from the British Government with interest and to balance its income and expenditure taking one year with another, except insofar as interest can now be waived in the case of certain kinds of projects for a period not exceeding seven years when in the early stage of development the return from the project is low. Interest has been waived on advances for investment in development companies in Kenya and Northern and Eastern Nigeria. Arrangements for financing the Corporation are being reviewed.

7. The particular value of the Corporation is that it is able to attract into investment resources additional to its own and that it combines investment with expert management. Through its Regional offices it maintains close relations with 

Governments and development opportunities, and provides advisory services and training for local people. 

8. The Corporation has invested substantially in development corporations in the less developed countries. These companies are financed by contributions from the C.D.C., the local government and Germany. They promote industrialisation and investment in local industries. 

9. In 1966 disbursements by the Corporation totalled $23.7m. (£8.5m). The return capital flow was $5.5m. (£2m.) and interest payments were nearly $15m. (£5.4m.). Approximately a quarter of C.D.C. investment was in East Africa, a quarter in the rest of Africa and a quarter in the Caribbean. 

Source: Overseas Development Institute (London) THE COMMONWEALTH DEVELOPMENT CORPORATION 

So far, the most useful single instrument of joint government-private assistance to the private sector in developing countries is the Commonwealth Development 

Corporation (CDC). 

The Corporation was set up in 1948. Its funds come from the British Exchequer, as well as private sources. The purpose of CDC was (and still is) to bridge the gap between government development efforts (helped with direct official aid) and straightforward private investment (both local and expatriate). It can now undertake any type of enterprise open to a private concern; it may own and run projects entirely by itself, invest in enterprises in partnership with private or government concerns, or both, either through equity or dated-loan participation, or it may make investments of the 'finance house' type, i.e. loans to governments and statutory bodies, and loans guaranteed by governments. 

CDC maintains its own permanent staff in the countries and regions in which it lends or invests. It manages all its own direct and subsidiary projects; for this it provides either expatriate staff or trains local personnel. Special emphasis is in fact given to the building up of local management "cadres". The Corporation also conducts feasibility studies and operates an advisory and technical service which is generally available usually on a fee-paying basis-to countries in which its terms of reference allow it to operate. 

Many enterprises, as they grow, change in form from direct CDC projects into locally registered subsidiaries, with an increasing percentage of other shareholders as the undertakings become firmly established, and in time these other shareholders include not only firms and governments but also local individuals. 

Originally, CDC was to provide funds for projects not well suited for direct government aid, or which private investors would not tackle on their own. 

However, both the nature and the territorial distribution of its investments suggest that circumstances have forced it to depart to a certain extent from its original intentions. The rising cost of borrowing has been one important factor in inclining CDC towards the safer, "finance house" type of project. Because of this problem, the terms of Exchequer advances to it have recently been modified-on certain projects CDC will be allowed to waiver of interest for a period of up to seven years-so that the rise in its overall borrowing costs can be checked. Another important factor inhibiting CDC from participating in more "risk" ventures-the sort of ventures particularly useful from the point of view of strengthening the local private sector-has been its statutory obligation to finance its operations with fixed interest loans. If CDC could raise at least a part of its finance on an equity basis, it would be in a better position to invest in "risk" ventures, and its overall performance could be raised to an even higher level than has already been attained. 

In the first few years after CDC was set up, its operations accounted for almost 20% of total official aid. By 1966/67 the proportion had fallen to less than 

5%. CDC operations have expanded only modestly while other forms of aid have roughly quadrupled. The accelerated expansion of the CDC, both because it is well-tried instrument for strengthening the local private sector, and because of its overall record in development aid, should be high on the list of priorities whenever new aid funds are available.

ADELA INVESTMENT COMPANY Senator JAVITS. Finally, the ADELA Investment Company has $40 

million invested capital. I inspired that as Chairman of the Economic

Mostly, it is by participation. They give technical assistance, and the fact that they have placed their imprimatur on a project attracts other investors, private and public. For example, the leverage of $2,800,000 roughly recently in Brazil developed $33 million in capital for a so-called kraft paper plant. It is just an example of how this operates. 

It is superbly successful and will have a major impact, it is having it already, upon development in Latin America, and I ask unanimous consent that we may include a short analysis in the record of how the 

ADELA Investment Company operates.
