(The material referred to follows:) [From the ADELA Annual Report for fiscal year 1967] LETTER FROM THE CHAIRMAN OF THE BOARD OF DIRECTORS TO THE SHAREHOLDERS 

A report on ADELA's second full year of operations is not only a summary of how the company fared during the past twelve months, but it also reflects in some aspects the current investment climate throughout Latin America. 

ADELA seeks to obtain a reasonable return on its investments. By its charter, ADELA can invest only in Latin America. In addition to this geographical restriction, ADELA, according to its charter, "will endeavor to encourage trends favorable to private capital and will assist private enterprise of the Latin American countries in achieving conditions suitable for private investment”. 

In his report, the Managing Director reviews the achievements of ADELA and discusses the Company's problems and plans for the future. For that reason, I shall limit my report to a discussion of the investment picture in Latin America. 

From 1961 to 1965, total direct foreign private investment in Latin America, according to the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development 

(OECD), rose by $3.7 billion, or at a rate of approximately $740 million per year.

ADELA, since it started operations in January 1965, has participated in—and to an increasing extent sponsored-more than $300 million of private investment in Latin America. We estimate that within its first five years, ADELA can generate or participate in new private investments of $800 million to $1 billion. Moreover, with its present financial resources, backing and organization, ADELA will be able to sponsor and/or participate in $1 billion of private investment every 5 to 7 years, as investments are resold and the funds reinvested. 

In absolute terms, and measured against the volume of total private investment and, particularly, against the net inflow of long term foreign private capital, ADELA's impact in Latin America has been significant. However, in relation to its commitment "to encourage and support active participation of local and private capital in the development of Latin America", and measured against the great need for private capital and private initiative, ADELA's performance is modest.

The shortage of private capital and private initiative continues to be the single most important problem for Latin American economic development. Nevertheless, there has been little concentrated and integrated effort to encourage foreign investment. The successful promotion of the investment of private capital cannot

be accomplished by one company or by private enterprise alone. Governments must take measures conducive to the establishment of an environment which will attract capital. All forces active in this task must then ensure that such capital will be used effectively. Both, government and the private sector, must join together in making certain that the scarce resources are used in projects of high priority and not wasted on isolated ventures of how priority or for purposes unrelated to economic development. 

A favorable environment for capital formation and investment, domestic and foreign, cannot be achieved by only an expression of good intent. The demand for capital exceeds its availability and, therefore, it is attracted to areas where it is welcome, not by rhetoric, but by specific indicators. Worldwide competition for capital is strong and it will flow into countries where there is economic and monetary stability.

The most frequent risk is that of inflation and of devaluation of local currencies, leading to an erosion of capital. Chronic inflation inevitably results in the shortage and flight of capital, in particular the shortage of capital in local currency, the resulting high cost of borrowed money and constantly escalating direct and indirect taxation. All of these difficulties are in varying degrees impeding the process of economic development. Until inflation can be brought under control, progress will be slow. However, there are many indications that in 

Latin America the attitude toward inflation is changing. Many countries are engaged in determined battles against inflation, while others seem to embark on inflation, without learning from the experience of their neighbors. A stable currency, programs to reduce the rate of price increase, the reform of existing tax structures and improved tax collection methods, will enable Latin American countries to compete better for foreign capital. 

Another risk is the growing tendency toward excessive economic isolation and protectionism and the danger of an increasing invasion of the sphere of private enterprise by the public sector. Unilateral rescission of contracts entered into by previous governments, discrimination between local and foreign enterprises in the areas of public and private credit, and in taxation, exchange and transfer restrictions, expropriation and/or restrictions against foreign investors in certain industries which must be owned or controlled by nationals—or in some cases by the state-may be within the right of sovereign states, but are not conducive to a favorable investment climate and therefore not within their best interest.

There is no more important objective for statesmen in their determination to achieve economic development than to create an environment which will attract large scale investments, keep local capital at home and encourage the return of local capital from abroad. Through creating a favorable environment and incentives for private enterprise and new initiatives toward foreign investment, the governments can encourage private investment into projects of high economic priority.

Private enterprise has its responsibility in this effort, as well, By its action, it can convince governments that their confidence in private enterprise is justified. 

It must ensure that it makes optimum use of the limited financial resources available, and of its technological knowledge and managerial skills, Businessmen must begin to base their projects on the requirements of future economic integration, rather than on small isolated and highly protected domestic markets. 

Such projects may initially have to be able to exist on smaller domestic or subregional demand, but they also must remain feasible and must be able to survive in the larger trade areas of economic integration, which may be achieved in less than twenty years. This should be the objective of a partnership between government and the private sector-for the benefit of the economic development of Latin America, supported by adequate and systematic private enterprise activities.

Perhaps ADELA'S single most important achievement has been a clear demonstration that such a partnership is possible. ADELA has carried out its investment program in cooperation with the private business community within Latin America and outside and with national and international development organizations and takes into account the national interest. ADELA follows closely the planning efforts and the infrastructure investments of Latin American countries and the program of development assistance of the United States and other ADELA shareholder countries, and from international organizations, including the Inter- 

American Development Bank, the World Bank, the International Finance Corporation and the United Nations Development Program. It attempts to comple- 91-392-68-pt. 2—11

ment development efforts and infrastructure investment of the public sector by appropriate enterpreneurial action and investment of private capital, in order to attain optimal effectiveness and return on public and private investment. 

We, who believe in the validity of ADELA's concept, are confident that this approach will prove to be the best way to achieve large scale economic development. 

[From the ADELA Annual Report for fiscal year 1967] REPORT OF THE MANAGING DIRECTOR HIGHLIGHTS OF THE YEAR 

ADELA has completed its second full year of operations. 

The principal characteristic of this period has been continued growth. We have expanded the scope of our work, extended our geographical coverage, stepped up the rhythm of our investment activity, improved the effectiveness of our organization, and strengthened our cooperation with private and public entities operating in Latin America and with our shareholders. We have expanded our assets and increased our revenue and operating profit. 

Entrepreneurial Tasks 

The most significant advance in this past fiscal year is the increasing emphasis on the entrepreneurial aspects of our work-the identification of investment opportunities with good profit potential and high economic priority, the development of ideas and opportunities into feasible projects, and the implementation of such projects into profitable enterprises. 

We endeavour to maximize the resources available from outside our own organization from local partners in Latin America, from our shareholders and from international institutions and organizations. Through this, ADELA is able to mobilize resources for projects of far greater magnitude than it could undertake alone. In Honduras, ADELA is sponsoring the $72 million Olancho Forestry 

Project, with a commitment of only $3.5 million of its own capital. In Brazil, a commitment of $3.5 million of our own capital is complemented by our effort to raise an additional $43.5 million for a new rayon pulp mill in the state of Rio 

Grande do Sul. In the Dominican Republic, ADELATEC is completing an analysis and recommendations on the restructuring of industrial enterprises and of new agricultural developments, which are likely to result in projects which in magnitude will approach the afore-mentioned developments in Honduras and in Brazil and will surpass them in impact.

Much of our entrepreneurial work is directly related to the long-term objectives of Latin America, the advancement of economic integration, the creation of additional domestic and of subregional private financing institutions, which can serve larger market areas and contribute to the formation and integration of capital markets.

The ADELA system, comprising the businessmen and professional staff of the parent company and of our management services subsidiary, ADELATEC, have made good progress in acting jointly as catalysts for financial and human resources. Our objective is two-fold-high economic impact and return on investment. 

Investment management

Our organization had to be built from the ground within the past 22 years. 

In the last fiscal year, we have concentrated on completing its last and probably most important component-investment management. While maintaining a high pace in project appraisal, we have shifted the emphasis to investment management, comprising the systematic implementation of investment decisions, the close supervision of enterprises in development and in operation, assistance to the management of companies in which we have invested, and--as this is inevitable in our business sending out the fire brigade in a few cases. 

Our investment management function again emphasizes the intreprenurial character of our company. Rather than passively following up on their development, we are contributing to the companies in which we have invested with our knowledge and worldwide relations. Our functions have included the development of export markets, the diversification of their product-mix, vertical or lateral integration within the industry in one or between several countries, the coordination of complementary activities in different countries, mergers to

by achieve greater effectiveness and market penetration—all of it with the objective of higher sales and profits and with a view towards greater strength in the coming subregional or regional markets.

In providing these services, the ADELA/ADELATEC system-the combination of businessmen and a broad range of knowledge functions available in our grouphas been put to work with increasing frequency and effectiveness on improving the profitability or preventing losses in the enterprises in which we have invested and on which our own profitability is dependent. 

Investment commitments and disbursements Our geographical coverage of Latin America has been extended in this past fiscal year to what we consider close to its completion. ADELA or ADELATEC officers are now stationed in eight Latin American countries. They are assisted by officers, agents and advisors located in Europe and in the United States. This broader geographical coverage has improved our effectiveness in project analysis and in the entrepreneurial work required for project development and investment management. It has been achieved by decentralization and upgrading of quality of our organization-without increasing the size of our team. 

Investment commitments during the year amounted to $19.7 million, up by 

40% from $14.2 million in the previous fiscal year. We made commitments in four additional countries-Bolivia, Guatemala, Panama and Uruguay-thereby extending the number of countries in which we have made commitments from 

13 to 17.

Disbursements almost tripled from U.S. $5 million in 16 companies a year ago to U.S. $14 million in 40 companies at the end of the fiscal year. Of our investments in the equity capital of 37 companies, 20 projects, representing 43% of our equity disbursements, are still in the pre-operating or start-up phase. Two companies are currently losing money, and 15 companies, representing 45% of our equity disbursements are profitable, including five companies which are paying dividends.

During the fiscal year, we negotiated and initiated financial agency assignments in the amount of U.S. $130 million, which we are carrying out jointly with others.

ADELATEC has performed assignments in 14 Latin American countries. It has strengthened its ability of complementing ADELA's staff, as required by joint work on large projects and in project development and investment management. Its capacity was used increasingly for joint entrepreneurial assignments and for participating in task forces, as they are being sent out with increasing frequency for appraisal of economic conditions and investment opportunities or on investment management assignments. 

A detailed report on our investment activity is included under "Operations and Investments" on pages 22 through 32. The tables on pages 48 and 49 begin to show representative patterns of spreading and diversification of risks, geographically and by activities.

Cooperation Our own growth, in particular the expansion of our scope to include entrepreneurial tasks, and the constantly increasing magnitude of projects for which we become the sponsors and financial agents, have contributed to the further strengthening of our close relationship with private and public institutions active in economic development in Latin America. 

With the Interamerican Development Bank (IDB), we have signed in September 1966 the loan agreement on the U.S. $10 million, 15-year loan, negotiated earlier in 1966. An amount of $1.7 million has been drawn down and utilized in subloans to companies in which we have made equity investments. In the new fiscal year, we foresee an accelerated pace in putting these funds to work. On several larger projects, parallel loans from IDB are in the process of being studied or negotiated. 

The International Finance Corporation (IFC) and ADELA are partners in several enterprises, and additional joint projects are in an advanced stage of study. We have entered into agreements with IFC on joint efforts on financial agency assignments for large projects. We believe that this may become an increasingly important area for joint action. 

We have made further progress in our cooperation with private and public development banks in Latin America, to which, in most joint ventures, we can offer services and financing which are broader in scope and complementary to their own.

We are shareholders in six private development finance companies in Latin America, and another three projects are presently well advanced, including one for a first subregional private institution. 

Our philosophy of close cooperation has led to joint financing for several projects with the Deutsche Entwicklungsgesellschaft (DEG) in Germany, and we are presently discussing ways and means of joint action with other European institutions. 

Our close liaison with the United States Agency for International Development (AID) has led to the discussion of a formal cooperation in the field of identification and development of investment opportunities for private enterprise. We are also working with the United Nations Development Program (UNDP) and other U.N. organizations, the development studies of which can often serve as a basis and point of departure for our entrepreneurial work in relating available resources to potential markets within or outside Latin America. 

In our frequent contacts with private organizations, such as the Interamerican 

Council for Commerce and Production (CICYP) and the newly created U.S. 

Council on Agriculture and Food Production, we have initiated an active dialogue with representatives of the private sectors concerned with economic integration, subregional development, agribusiness activities and overall economic and industrial development programs in specific countries. 

We have been fortunate in using to greater advantage the resources of our shareholders, who not only provided ADELA's own capital, but who contribute to our work and to the overall growth and development of Latin America with an incomparable pool of knowledge and experience. In the phase of identification of opportunities and project development, as well as for investment management, we entertained in the past fiscal year hardly a project for which we have not drawn on the local knowledge in Latin America or the international experience and connections of our shareholders. In turn, the range of our services and information available to our shareholders has been significantly broadened. 

They include information on business, monetary and economic conditions in Latin America, identification of markets and investment opportunities, establishment of local contracts and location of potential partners for joint ventures, counselling on long-range development programs to meet the changing requirements of subregional and regional markets, and indication of business opportunities which may lead to sales, distribution and licensing contracts. 

Our cooperation with the governments and planning agencies, with the aforementioned institutions and organizations, and with our shareholders and others require a sustained effort at all levels of our organization. The result of this effort cannot always be measured in tangible terms. It is this cooperation, however, which permits us to pursue our course as an active entrepreneur for the promotion of private enterprise and to participate in the economic development of Latin America with a leverage of many times our own capital and human resources. Finances

The financial condition of the corporation on June 30, 1967, with comparative figures for the preceding year, is shown in the Balance Sheet and the Statement of Income and Expenses on pages 36 through 40, which are followed by summaries of the short- and medium-term notes and of the operational commitments and disbursements on pages 41 through 47. 

The report of the Board of Directors to the shareholders, recommending approval of the financial statements as of June 30, 1967, and setting forth the proposed allocation of the net profit for the year, is shown on page 33. It is followed by the reports of the statutory and international auditors. 

Our growth is reflected by an increase of our assets and income. 

Our total assets expanded by 30% from U.S. $37 to U.S. $48 million, primarily on account of the increase in disbursements on operational investments from U.S. $5 million to U.S. $14 million and, in particular, in equity investments, which went from U.S. $2.5 to U.S. $8.7 million during the year. Current assets have risen moderately to U.S. $26.6 million. Cash and time deposits declined from U.S. $11 million to U.S. $6.5 million, reflecting the growing disbursements on operational investments, while short-term investments increased from U.S. $14.4 to U.S. $19.2 million.

The portfolio of short- and medium-term investments increased by 25% to U.S. $26.6 million, of which U.S. $5 million were in two Latin American currencies. 

Seventy-five percent of the amount of notes held on June 30 falls due within one year, and maturities for the remaining 25% range from 12 to 36 months. With

an average rate of return of 9% p.a., the short- and medium-term notes are still our major source of income. Up to June 30, 1967, we had purchased shortand medium-term notes of Latin American debtors in the aggregate amount of U.S. $77.5 million, with one default on payment of U.S. $100,000 in Mexico. 

Our liabilities amounted to U.S. $9.6 million on June 30, 1967, as against U.S. $1.9 million at the end of the previous year. They include U.S. $1.7 million of drawings on the U.S. $10 million loan from IDB and U.S. $7.5 million of shortterm borrowings under established credit lines with shareholder banks. 

Total income increased sharply to U.S. $3.3 million, a gain of U.S. $1.1 million, or 50%, over the previous year. The sources of this income have undergone a major change. Operational investments brought U.S. $633,500, equivalent to 20% of total income, compared with only U.S. $159,100 (7.5%) in the previous year. 

Income from short- and medium-term notes and time deposits of U.S. $2,651,000 increased by 29% over the previous year's figure and still accounted for the bulk of total revenue. The substantial uptrend in disbursements on operational investments and the increasing number of investments which begin to generate income, will in the new fiscal year further change the proportion between revenue from operational investments and from notes. 

The U.S. $633,500 earned on operational investments include U.S. $385,300 interest on long-term loans and a first capital gain of U.S. $57,000, realized on the sale of part of an equity investment. Of the income of U.S. $2,651,000 earned on short- and medium-term notes and time deposits, U.S. $2,233,300 were derived from interest and commissions and U.S. $53,800 from trading profits on notes. 

Direct cost, comprising interest and commitment fees paid on borrowings, absorbed U.S. $431,000. In the previous year no direct costs were incurred, as we had not yet started to work with borrowed funds. 

The increase in operating expenses of 50% over the previous year reflects the cost of rapid decentralization for increased effectiveness and of markedly higher cost of outside professional services, caused by the accelerated pace of projects implementation and disbursements. Operating expenses are not yet comparable with the previous year's figures, as in that year we were in the process of building up our management and professional staff and expenses reflect normat strength only for the second half of the fiscal year. 

Net operating income for the year increased by 8% from U.S. $1,122,500 to U.S. $1,212,500, despite the fact that we are passing through our "lean years", by shifting substantial funds from income generating short-term investments to equity investments in new and expanding companies, which will not pay dividends for some time.

In accordance with the resolution adopted by the Board of Directors at its meeting of April 3rd in Rome, which calls for the establishment of a "Reserve for possible Losses on Investments" (previously called "Special Reserve") of up to an amount equal to 15% of the corporation's capital, the Executive Committee at its meeting of July 18, 1967, has provided for an allocation of U.S. $775,000 to this reserve. It has further made a provision for “Incentive Compensation to Personnel" in the amount of U.S. $60,000. 

After allocation of profits in accordance with the recommendation of the 

Executive Committee, the "Reserve for possible Losses on Investments" will amount to U.S. $1,150,000 and the Statutory Reserve and Surplus will reach U.S. $1,400,000.

Capital, reserves and surplus have increased to U.S. $38.5 million, from U.S. $35.2 million at the beginning of the year. Paid-in capital has risen during the year from U.S. $33.75 million to U.S. $35.9 million. As of the time of this writing, additional subscriptions have brought it to U.S. $37 million, based on the number of shares outstanding on June 30, shareholders' equity amounted to U.S. $10,712 per share of U.S. $10,000 par value. 

The total resources at our disposal amounted to U.S. $75.5 million on June 

30, comprising U.S. $38.5 million of capital funds and U.S. $37 million of short- to long-term credit facilities.

Organization and Personnel 

Despite the expansion in the scope of our work, the much improved geographical coverage and the accelerated pace of investment activity, there has been virtually no increase in staff. The total personnel of the ADELA/ADELATEC group increased by 2 to 84 on June 30, 1967, of which two thirds are in the employment of ADELA, They are stationed throughout Latin America and-very few-in Europe. 

The personnel of ADELATEC has experienced some changes. It has been considerably upgraded in quality and, at the same time, somewhat reduced in number.

ADELATEC's professional staff now consists almost entirely of qualified senior professionals with broad business knowledge and professional background, suitable for the responsibility of project managers. Specialists are being sought on a case-to-case basis from our shareholders and from outside consulting organizations.

The emphasis in this past year has been on increasing the effectiveness, by upgrading the skills of management and professional staff and by attempting to maximize on the characteristics of strength of each team member. 

A decentralization of senior personnel in ADELA and ADELATEC has brought our officers nearer to their task and projects. This facilitates the identification and development of new investments, but is particularly important-if not indispensable--for performing the function of investment management. 

The principal change in our method of operation is the introduction of "task forces" for achieving greater effectiveness and impact in a given country. The requirement of high flexibility and agility and the size of organization which we can afford within our budget of operating expenses will at no time allow us to make large-scale efforts throughout the subcontinent. We, therefore, decided to undertake concentrated efforts within limited geographical areas, to which we assign task forces for a period of 12 to 18 months, comprising 5-8 officers and professionals of ADELA and ADELATEC. 

Our organization structure at the end of the fiscal year reflects the shift of emphasis to entrepreneurial assignments and investment management. While 

ADELATEC will continue to operate independently and will be measured against its financial performance, the lines of command and communication with and within ADELATEC have been shortened and simplified. The whole system of communications was adapted to the greater decentralization and to the expansion and change in scope and functions, in order to assure a continued high degree of flexibility and agility, which have been our two most useful characteristics since we began our work in Latin America. 

Future Prospects 

We have proven that a private investment company like ADELA is workable, useful and capable of making a significant impact, and that it can be profitable even in its initial lean years. 

We know that our work and achievements during these first 22 years of operations are nowhere near enough when measured against the overall objective we have set for ourselves. While we do not intend to reduce the pace of project development and new investments, we will further shift our emphasis to the management of existing investments. A good part of our income and profits will, ultimately, depend on the earnings from dividends and capital gains from our equity investments. Our ability to mobilize additional resources-capital and men-in turn, is directly related to our profitability. We can only claim success if we can continuously expand our activities in line with the magnitude of the needs in Latin America. In order to do so, our performance in terms of return on our capital must be good.

Assuming a continuation of the current pace of investment activity, our present capital and surplus will be fully committed for equity investments sometime near the end of 1968. As subscriptions approach our presently authorized capital of U.S. $40 million, it has been recommended to our shareholders to increase the authorized capital to U.S. $50 million. Further increases are likely to be recommended over the coming 3-5 years, after which we expect to finance the major portion of new investments from the sale of shares of companies which have developed well and become profitable. To make these investments marketable, is again the task of imaginative investment management.

As a private company, ADELA must use all available ways and means of private enterprise to grow and to increase profits. One of them is adequate leverage on our own capital. Since financial resources for debt-financing are not likely to present problems in the foreseeable future, it would seem that we should substantially expand our debt-financing activities. This may prove to be indispensable for increasing our earnings and for bridging the gap in income, caused by increasing disbursements for equity investments, which will not produce income for two to five years.

It is important that we maintain or increase our present pace as the political and economic situation in Latin America continues to improve. Progress during the past decade and, particularly, during the two-and-a-half years in which www

ADELA has been operating, gives rise to optimism. The trouble spots are fewer and smaller today than at the time we started. 

The efforts towards accelerated economic integration were given renewed strength by the leaders of the Hemisphere at Punta del Este. The initiative for economic development and integration has clearly been assumed by Latin American statesmen. After the Central American Common Market has set the example of successful subregional integration, the blueprints are being readied for the "Pacific Club," comprising Chile, Peru, Bolivia, Ecuador, Colombia and Venezuela. Through the establishment of subregional markets of growing size and significance, which may eventually overlap and merge, a good deal of the economic isolation and excessive protectionism of smaller countries will disappear. The larger nations with ample domestic markets will find trading partners of similar or larger size and become amenable to the idea that integration and trading is a two-way street. Economic integration cannot be achieved without concessions and sacrifices from every participant, but the opportunities and challenge of larger market areas far outweigh these disadvantages. Economic integration is irreversible, despite all the obstacles which it faces. It would seem important for private enterprise to prepare for this change, which will require methods of operation and of organization which are fundamentally different from the present system of serving limited, isolated and protected markets. 

A pre-requisite for a positive attitude and active hole of private enterprise towards economic integration will be agreement between governments and legislation on such fundamental issues as free movement of capital between Latin American countries, elimination of double taxation of income, free movement of goods produced by integration industries and complemenary activities in two or more countries, and last but not least-elimination of red tape, which in many Latin American countries has taken very large proportions and has in some cases turned promotion laws and other incentives into deterrents against private enterprise activities. A consequence of the removal of restrictions on movement of capital, and an indispensable foundation for the establishment of larger trade areas, will be larger and integrated capital markets and multinational private financing institutions of sufficient potential to attend to the vastly greater financial requirements which economic integration imposes on private enterprise.

Another major area which will tax the combined efforts of the private and public sectors is that of food production and distribution. An increase in agricultural production of at least 3 percent per year is necessary merely to maintain the currently inadequate level of food supply for the growing population of Latin America. If production is to keep pace with the growth and an improving standard of nutrition in the area, it must expand at an annual rate of at least 5 percent. In order to achieve this growth, investment will have to climb by an average of U.S. $12 billion a year for the next ten years. Promotional efforts for agriculture and food production, incentives and appropriate legislation on conditions no less favorable than those established for industrial development will be required. Governments and private enterprise alike must discontinue to interprete the term "development" as "industrialization." Agricultural infrastructure, large-scale land reclamation and irrigation projects, road construction for the purpose of opening new fertile areas, and a realistic approach to agrarian reform which permits commercialized agriculture of scale, will have to be complemented by action and investments of private enterprise in setting up wellconceived food production and marketing systems, capable of bringing food from the soil to the mouth in good quality and at lower prices to consumers, while assuring producers of stable markets and adequate prices. 

The ADELA/ADELATEC system and organization is well suited to contribute to the progress of economic integration through private initiative by working with governments and with private enterprise in Latin America and by helping in the identification of the opportunities and risks which arise from a grouping of smaller domestic markets into larger subregional trade areas, which will lead eventually to LAFTA. We are also familiar with the causes of declining agricultural production and the measures required to reverse this trend. 

We believe that in Latin America, like everywhere in the world, major objectives like economic integration and substantially higher agricultural production and soil yields cannot be achieved by governmental action alone. Private enterprise should take the lead and must use its entrepreneurial ability and force to shape the future of an economically integrated subcontinent which can achieve

self-sustained growth within this generation. We are part of this effort and must use our own capabilities and potential resources in the pursuit of these objectives. 

OPERATIONS AND INVESTMENTS Summary of Operational Investments 

During the fiscal year ended June 30, 1967, the Executive Committee approved 25 investments in 24 companies, representing a total commitment of U.S. $19.68 million. Of this amount, U.S. $12.14 million is in the form of equity commitments and the balance of U.S. $7.54 million in complementary long-term debt financing. 

Of the 25 new commitments, 16 are for investments in new enterprises and 9 for the expansion and diversification of existing companies. Of the previously approved investments, two were cancelled during the year in the aggregate amount of U.S. $1.9 million.

By the end of the second full fiscal year, ADELA had made 61 commitments totalling U.S. $38.3 million, distributed among 56 companies in 17 Latin American countries. Equity commitments accounted for U.S. $22.23 million (58%), and complementary debt financing for U.S. $16.08 million (42%). Commitments for investments in new enterprises represent U.S. $25.5 million, two thirds of the total amount and number.

Disbursements on operational investments had reached U.S. $13.986 million on June 30, 1967, as compared with only U.S. $5 million at the end of the previous fiscal year. Of this total, U.S. $8.715 million were disbursed on equity participations and U.S. $5.271 on long-term loans. New enterprises (25) accounted for 

U.S. $6.04 million (43%) of total disbursements, while U.S. $7.94 million (57%) were disbursed to 15 existing companies. 

Of the investments in the equity capital of 37 companies, 45% is in 15 companies which have passed the break-even point and are operating profitably, 5 of which, representing 20% of disburements, are paying dividends. Two companies, representing 12% of disbursements, are currently losing money, while 20 enterprises (43% of disbursements) are still in the pre-operating or startup phase.

A breakdown of the commitments and disbursements in operational investments by countries and activities, and a list of the companies in which we have invested, are shown in the tables. 

Financial Agency Services 

Under an agreement entered into with the Government and the Central Bank of Honduras, ADELA is undertaking financial agency services for the formation of a company for the establishment of a pulp and paper and sawn timber industry in Honduras, which will serve the Central American Common Market and export timber to the United States, the Caribbean area and to Europe. 

IFC has agreed to join efforts with ADELA as co-financial agent for this U.S. $72.5 million project. The negotiations with prospective technical know-how partners are well advanced. It is expected that a pilot company will be formed soon, jointly with a technical partner and what Central American and foreign investors.

ADELA has also entered into a financial agency agreement with Aktieselskapet Borregaard of Norway, under which it undertakes to arrange the financing for a $48 million rayon pulp mill to be located in the State of Rio Grande do Sul in Brazil. The production of this mill will be for export to European markets through Borregaard. Also in this project, IFC is cooperating with 

ADELA in the development and implementation work. 

Financial agency services are being undertaken on a "best-effort" basis. In each project, ADELA has committed an amount of U.S. $3.5 million of its own capital.

Subsidiary Companies 

ADELA has three wholly-owned subsidiary companies, of which two are incorporated in Panama and the third in Brazil.

ADELATEC Technical and Management Services Company S.A. (Panama) 

commenced operations in September 1965. It has a paid-in capital of U.S. $250,000. In the past fiscal year, which was the first full year of operations for 

ADELATEC, the company was engaged in a broad range of activities, including resources surveys, market appraisal, feasibility studies, project implementation and management of companies, and business advisory services. The principal assignments were in Honduras, Peru, the Dominican Republic and Ecuador.

DS ad Smaller assignments were completed in ten other Latin American countries. 

The assignments were in the sectors of forestry, pulp and paper, metallurgy and steel fabricating, chemicals and petro-chemicals, textiles, fishing, agriculture and food production and in studying and advising development finance companies in several countries.

During the fiscal year, bookings of assignments amounted to U.S. $510,000, while invoicings reached U.S. $479,000. At year-end, ADELATEC had assignments pending on its books for total fees of U.S. $385,000, of which an estimated 

U.S. $193,000 had been completed on June 30, 1967. At the same time, the company had under negotiation new assignments, totalling fees of U.S. $662,000. Because of a high order book and an overload of work at the beginning of the fiscal year, ADELATEC's management could devote little time to development and promotional efforts. This led to a gap in assignments in the later part of 1966 and early 

1967. For this reason, and because of the increased use of ADELATEC's capacity for joint entrepreneurial tasks and investment management functions of ADELA, ADELATEC's income from third parties was insufficient to cover its operating expenses. The company is expected to become again self-sustaining in the third quarter of 1967.

ADELA Compañia de Inversiones (Panama) S.A. is incorporated with an authorized capital of U.S. $10 million, of which presently U.S. $2 million are paid in. It became active in the course of the fiscal year under review, as several investments were channelled through this company. It is the recipient of the U.S. $10 million, 15-year loan from the Interamerican_Development Bank (IDB), of which U.S. $1.7 million had been drawn down on June 30. Interest on time deposits of the paid-in capital were about equal to commitment fees paid on the undisbursed portion of the IDB loan. The capital, equity investments and loans of this company are consolidated in the financial statements of the parent company. 

ADELA Administraçao e Serviços Ltda. (Brazil) was incorporated in October 

1966 with an authorized and fully paid-in capital of New Cruz. 100,000. Its function is to provide a full range of investment and technical services to the Brazilian business community. Its work includes ADELA's activities of project evaluation and investment management and ADELATEC's professional services. 

It began to pay for its own costs six months after starting operations. 

ASIAN INVESTING COMPANY 

Senator JAVITS. Also, the chair is undoubtedly aware of the fact that that there is considerable discussion of an ADELA type Asian company to be called the Asian Investing Company which will be discussed in a conference in Australia which is imminently in process. 

In other words, there is a broad movement in the world of which this is a part and, Mr. Chairman, we have a rather informal way of doing things around here, but the informality should not obscure the historic importance of what we are doing. 

A HISTORIC OPERATION 

My testimony occurs in the midst of other testimony which may be vital and may not; we are considering another foreign aid bill. But this is a historic operation, and would be a historic breakthrough, and it is now proving its effectiveness. I emphasize that because I hope the committee will take this with the weight and seriousness which it richly deserves and require our Government at long last to come face to face with it.

The AID people thought there was something to this. On the other hand, various agencies of the Government have raised many objections to it, but I think that time has disproven their fears and their objections. In addition, they are under a terrible disability of being unable to come up with anything that has impressed the Congress or the world or the country in respect to foreign aid effectively enough. This is a

positive and constructive proposal and, therefore, I think the burden of proof is on those who would oppose it rather than the other way, considering the history of being unable to come up with some effective alternative to the present system which does not fill the bill. 

Now, Senator Brooke has kindly come in. 

Senator SPARKMAN. I wonder if I may ask you just this one question.

Senator JAVITS. Please. 

TRANSITION OF CORPORATION FROM GOVERNMENT TO PRIVATE MANAGEMENT 

Senator SPARKMAN. You say that during the initial 6-year period of the corporation's existence it would function as an agency of the United States. Upon retirement of the Government's participation, the corporation will go through an orderly transition from an agency of the Government to one of private operation and management. 

How does it occur? How is the Government's participation retired?

Senator JAVITS. The Government's participation is provided for financially in the way I have described but in management by the fact that the President will appoint the board. 

Senator SPARKMAN. I ask how is it retired? In other words, how do you get the private capital in there to replace the Government? 

Senator JAVITS. By selling debentures to the public. That is provided for in the legislation. 

Senator SPARKMAN. I am familiar with the idea that you advanced a few years ago with reference to Latin America.

Senator JAVITS. Yes. 

Senator SPARKMAN. And I listened to this with a great deal of interest and I am sure Senator Carlson has. Do you wish Senator Brooke to testify now? 

Senator JAVITS. Apparently he is under time pressure. I would like to interrupt my testimony and ask Senator Brooke to appear and then resume and answer questions. May I do that, Mr. Chairman?

Senator SPARKMAN. All right. 

Senator BROOKE. Good morning. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Senator Brooke, we are glad to have you with us.

STATEMENT OF HON. EDWARD W. BROOKE, U.S. SENATOR FROM MASSACHUSETTS Senator BROOKE. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Chairman, Senator Carlson, it is a pleasure for me to appear before you and to testify on behalf of the bill introduced by my able and imaginative colleague, Senator Javits, of New York, a bill to create a 

Peace by Investment Corporation. 

I have cosponsored this legislation in the firm conviction that a much larger investment is required in the developing areas of the world than can presently be provided through public sources alone. I have seen at firsthand the tremendous changes in the economy of a region, and indeed of an entire nation, which can be wrought through private investment and initiative. And I have reason to believe that greater achievements are possible where private individuals and public institutions combine to make capital and talents available on a sound and continuing basis.

FOREIGN AID PROGRAM IN JEOPARDY 

Our foreign aid program is in jeopardy at the present time. It is being attacked literally from all sides; proponents argue that it has not done enough, and that more funding is needed if it is to accomplish even minimal objectives; opponents contend that we have tried to do too much, and that we are pouring our scarce resources down a bottomless hole. Others argue from the understandable position of national priorities; we cannot afford to be aiding others when so many millions of our own people are living in poverty. And finally, the recipients of aid themselves are prone to criticize our handling of the program; there is too much red tape, too much time is expended in costly and repetitive studies, and the debts thus incurred are in fact imposing a serious burden on the economies of the host countries. 

There is something to be said for all of these arguments. AID has accomplished less than we would have liked, primarily because its resources have been too limited, and the restrictions which have been imposed upon it, from a bureaucratic, administrative, and legislative point of view, have inhibited its effectiveness. 

AID has accomplished tremendous good with a modest budget. But the time has come to supplement our public assistance effort with fresh and fruitful innovation.

As I see it, the creation of a corporation such as that proposed in the pending bill could go far toward making our aid effort more effective and more self sustaining.

APPEALS FOR INCREASED PRIVATE INVESTMENT 

In the course of my recent tour of Africa, I was repeatedly confronted with appeals for increased private investment. At the same time, I was told that in all too many cases private investment is geared to the profit motive of the investor rather than to the needs of the recipient country. I repeatedly defended private initiative; I pointed out to supporters and detractors alike that of course American companies were motivated by a desire for profit, that this was the heart of the free enterprise system, a system which has served our country well. But at the same time I became increasingly convinced that there must be a better way to coordinate the private interests of the investor with the public interests of the recipient country. 

To some extent our Government has attempted to do this. Investment studies have been conducted by the Department of Commerce and by the U.S. Chamber of Commerce to determine the most attractive areas for private investment in a number of the developing nations of the world. AID has established an office of private resources to expand the agency's contract with the private sector, and has created within this office a private investment center to administer the investment incentive program and to serve as a single point of contact between the Federal Government and present or potential investors. 

Investment guaranties of various kinds also serve to offer an incentive to private business to invest their resources and talents abroad. 

Such efforts have been effective. But I would contend most strongly that they have not been sufficient, either in terms of the real needs of

the developing nations, or in terms of the potential advantage and profit to be realized in these areas by American private enterprise. 

Numerous small and middle range investments are urgently needed if the developing nations of the world are to make full use of their resources and potential.

Such large concerns as Kaiser Aluminum and the Hilton Hotel chain have made valuable investments in many areas. They have provided jobs, they have paid taxes and duties into the treasuries of developing nations, they have contributed to the economies of the host countries by increasing the purchasing power of local citizens. But, by and large, their operations have been externally oriented: Their administrative and supervisory personnel are supplied largely by the foreign investor. Their operations are designed either to provide resources needed by the industrialized nations or to service visiting foreign personnel. They have had an impact on the economies of the countries in which they are located, but they have not always enabled those economies to become any more self sustaining. 

FILLING GAP BETWEEN FOREIGN CORPORATIONS AND SMALL RETAIL CONCERNS 

The Peace by Investment Corporation could begin to fill the critical gap between large foreign corporate ventures and small individual retail or crafts oriented concerns. 

Let me cite a few examples: In countries such as Liberia and Ghana there are extensive expatriate operations engaged in the extraction of raw materials. Yet neither of these countries have developed the type of supporting industry required to service the needs of these corporations and to supply their personnel. Textiles and canned goods are all imported from Britain or the United States. Machine tools, vehicles, even uniforms, ropes, and flashlights are brought in from abroad. Such everyday items as tin cans and paper bags are simply not produced in these countries where a large market for them already exists. 

The Ivory Coast wants to attract industries for processing petroleum products and scrap metal; Nigeria wants to increase its construction of housing units; Zambia desperately needs to manufacture its own machine tools, and wants to set up candy factories to reduce its imports of sweets. Some of these countries have even gone so far as to offer special tax incentives for investments in certain key areas. 

The Peace by Investment Corporation could fill many of these needs. 

By permitting small corporations, and even individuals, to become shareholders, it could involve them in an exciting and profitable venture in which they might not be able to participate otherwise. 

It could spearhead investment and development in areas where funds are needed, and where reasonable returns may be expected. 

It could provide desperately needed capital and technological know how without increasing the national debt of the recipient countries.

And it could put foreign assistance and investment on a businesslike basis which will satisfy both the practical interests of the donor and the self respect of the recipient.

Mr. Chairman, and Senator Carlson, I strongly urge that S. 3415 receive the prompt and favorable consideration of this committee and, hopefully, of Congress, which I believe it deserves. 

I thank you. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Thank you, Senator, for a very interesting statement. Senator Javits.

COSPONSORS OF S. 3415 

Senator JAVITS. Mr. Chairman, I would like to read into the record the cosponsors of this bill, because they show such a broad geographical range and such a broad range in political philosophy. 

They include Senator Brooke, who just testified; Senator Dominick of Colorado; Senator Hatfield of Oregon; Senator Hartke of 

Indiana; Senator Magnuson of Washington; Senator Miller of 

Iowa; Senator Morse of Oregon; Senator Pell of Rhode Island; 

Senator Percy of Illinois; Senator Scott of Pennsylvania; and Senator Yarborough of Texas.

It is very interesting to me that so many of these men who have such different views see a resourceful initiative in this. 

Finally, I wish to emphasize, Mr. Chairman, the desirability of this proposal as a structural idea. I have seen this thing nitpicked to death by the Commerce Department and the Treasury Department, and I think that it is high time that they came up with their ideas. 

ADELA INVESTMENT COMPANY 

Now, it may very well be that the committee might decide to move in the direction that I moved in with ADELA where the stockholders were large corporations and banks. This doesn't exclude public investing, but it would hinge very heavily upon large corporations and banks. The peace by investment concept of my brother was based upon the sale of securities, a share, as it were, in world development to the small investor, which could be made profitable, by the way. 

I would like to point out that the ADELA Investment Company is now operating on something in the area of a 9to an 11-percent return. 

It is by no means unprofitable. And the same would be true of this kind of a corporation.

But whatever may be the variant, Mr. Chairman, I do not know of a more resourceful idea than this one. 

AID FUNDS IN WORLD BANK 

Now, there is another proposal by Senator Fulbright which interests me greatly and I think interested the committee very greatly, the idea of using some of the funds in foreign aid, as agent for the United States, to be administered by the World Bank or similar agencies.

With all due respect and deference to the thrust of that idea, I think this is the same general principle but a better idea, and I think it would be more satisfactory to Americans because it would involve a big element of American management.

MIXTURE OF GOVERNMENT AND BUSINESS 

So I sum up, Mr. Chairman. I think the time has come for a new vehicle in the foreign aid field. I think the proven vehicle is now the corporate form which gives the flexibility of sales of securities to the public, joint venture investments with others, commercial and public, throughout the world, and expanding capital based on performance, private investments which all these countries want, which, therefore, is not added to the call upon them to pay principal and interest as capital or budgetary items and, finally, the admixture of technical assistance as an integral part of the investments process.

I hope very much that this time this will really have mature consideration, and with the aid of the enormous expertise which exists in State and Treasury and Commerce and AID, once the committee gives the word that it wants this kind of an effort written into the bill, that we will be able to arrive at an optimum structure and, of course, finally, Mr. Chairman, we are doing it ourselves in the Communications 

Satellite Corporation. This is the new mix of Government and business which I think is the most promising for the American social and political system.

If there is any other question the committee might like to ask of my brother, he is here. He came down specially, and will be glad to answer any question on the structure of the bill or anything the committee might have in mind. 

Senator CARLSON. Mr. Chairman, I have no questions but I do want to commend the distinguished Senator from New York for presenting this proposal. Those of us who have sat around this dias for 20 years have seen an expenditure of $110 billion of our taxpayers' money and we begin to ask questions where questions are now being asked by our citizens. I think it is time that we begin to take another look at this thing.

I think the Senator has presented a very helpful and imaginative proposal, and I certainly am one member who is going to give it consideration.

Senator JAVITS. I thank my colleague so much, and it is typical of him to be so gracious. I do hope he will find a source of support for his feeling that there has to be another approach in this bill. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Thank you very much, Senator Javits. I am intrigued by the idea as I am by many of your ideas. 

Senator JAVITS. Thank you. Occasionally, we have gotten together on some of these things. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Thank you very much. 

(The full statement of Senator Javits follows:) TESTIMONY OF SENATOR JACOB K. JAVITS, MAY 14, 1968-MIXTURE OF PRIVATE- GOVERNMENT INVESTMENT PRESCRIPTION FOR FOREIGN AID DILEMMA 

Mr. Chairman, I appreciate the opportunity to testify today on the amendment 

I have offered to S. 3091, the Foreign Assistance Act of 1968. The amendment is identical to S. 3415 which I introduced on May 1 with 11 cosponsors and which is now pending before this Committee. Along with many of colleagues, I believe that the Senate is approaching the foreign aid bill with deep misgivings, even deeper than in previous years. And along with my colleagues who have co-sponsored this legislation, I feel that the time has now come to press for new approaches in our efforts to aid the developing nations.

This legislation would establish a "Peace by Investment Corporation," a federally chartered corporation to engage in industrial development and technical assistance activities in developing nations, using both private and public funds. 

The principal function of the corporation is to act as a catalyst, to tap the vast private resources available in the U.S. and make a portion of it, $1.25 billion, available for investment in profit-making enterprises in the developing portions of the world. Such a corporation would be in a better position than our government to assist developing countries in developing their industries, the skill of their manpower, their distribution facilities and to create new employment. 

Under the terms of this legislation, $50 million in initial and temporary capital funds for the corporation would be subscribed by the United States Government through the U.S. Treasury. During the first 6 years of its operation, the corporation would also be authorized to borrow from the Treasury in amounts up to $60 million in any one year but not to exceed $300 million at any one time. 

The most important source of the corporation's funds, however, would be obtained through the sale of Peace by Investment Debentures, in small denominations to the American public. These debentures could produce up to $375 million in any one year, but not to exceed $1.25 billion at any one time. During the initial six year period of the corporation's existence, it would function as an agency of the United States. Upon retirement of the government's participation, the corporation would go through an orderly transition from an agency of the government to private operation and management. 

Funds from private investors would be attracted to the corporation because: (1) it would provide for a diversity of private investment overseas, thus minimizing the risks inherent in single project ventures or investment in one commodity field; (2) it would give small investors an opportunity to participate in pooled investment projects, far greater in scope than any thus far attempted, under the skilled management which investment of the contemplated magni tude and character would encourage; and (3) it would provide the American people with an opportunity to participate in a major, constructive effort to help their fellow men.

The corporation would be authorized to invest in economically sound undertakings of a profit-making nature with emphasis on the encouragement of small and moderate sized enterprises. Investments of the corporation would have to be generally consistent with U.S. foreign policy objectives, with the U.S. balance of payments program, and with the policy to maintain domestic employment, production, and purchasing power. 

The corporation would seek to develop new enterprises which have not been able to attract investment for one reason or another. It would take risks and it should. It could utilize existing U.S. investment guaranty programs or create new ones if its management feels they are needed. It would provide technical assistance along with the investments it makes. This facility has proven to be instrumental in the success of the ADELA Investment Corporation which operates in Latin America with private capital and with loans from private and public sources.

This new approach to foreign aid would be desirable, in my judgment, even if we were not facing difficult problems this year. I feel that the kind of effort to aid developing nations envisioned in S. 3415 and in this amendment should go hand in hand with the more traditional forms of aid. Raul Prebisch, Director 

General of the U.N. Conference on Trade and Development, for example, was quoted in yesterday's New York Times as submitting to the U.N. a report sharply critical of the inadequacy of the current efforts of the industrialized nations in aiding the underdeveloped areas of the world. 

This year, however, Congressional feeling against foreign aid is fortified by the many national crises we face. The budget deficit, Vietnam, and our balance of payments deficit are largely responsible for this feeling. But to an equal extent many Senators have concluded that foreign aid should be reappraised in the light of present conditions in this country, in other industrialized nations, and in recipient countries. I believe that we have reached the point where our basic foreign aid legislation should reflect new developments-those which did not exist when the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961 became law. Those elements of the program which appear relevant and effective, on examination, should be retained, those which do not should be replaced. 

Investment surveys, investment guaranties, loans to businessmen, aid to intermediate financial institutions, and the other programs of AID, the Export-Import Bank, and the Commerce Department have done a creditable job in sustaining

U.S. investor interest in developing countries. I am confident that without these programs the flow of private U.S. investment to developing countries would have been at a far lower level than it has been. An objective appraisal of these programs has been made difficult by tight monetary conditions at home and by the balance of payments restrictions which have been imposed on private capital outflows since 1963. Without question these restrictions have impeded private 

U.S. investment in developing countries, even though, technically, investment in these countries was not subject to restricions. This was pointed out, at least as far as commercial bank loans were concerned, in the report of the Advisory 

Committee on Private Enterprise in Foreign Aid in July, 1965. 

However, it is apparent that present methods to attract private U.S. investment to developing countries are very inadequate and U.S. Department of 

Commerce data supports this contention. Even though taken together, net U.S. foreign direct investment and reinvested earnings of U.S. corporations abroad have risen between 1961 and 1966 (the latest period for which such data has been published), investment flows to all developing regions have remained stable or declined. If U.S. investment in petroleum is taken out, this picture would be even more discouraging. 

Let me make myself clear. I am not advocating that there should be cuts in foreign economic assistance funds this year. On the contrary, U.S. economic aid funds are inadequate. I am asserting, however, that it is not likely that the development capital needs of developing countries can be adequately met from official U.S. sources in the foreseeable future or that existing methods will generate a substantial volume of private U.S. capital. A new tool is needed for that purpose and I believe—and those Senators from both sides of the aisle who are my cosponsors share my belief that the amendment embodies this new tool. 

The format of a federally chartered corporation is not the novelty it was when I first broached this idea as a member of the House of Representatives 15 years ago. Today this concept is embodied in the Communications Satellite Corporation; in the Corporation for Public Broadcasting, in proposals designed to cope with the problems of domestic poverty, and in the economic assistance programs of two industrialized countries-the Commonwealth Development Corporation in the United Kingdom and the German Development Corporation in the German

Federal Republic. 

Each of these proposals is predicated on the proposition that government can achieve important national objectives by utilizing its resources as "seed money" to attract vast private resources. 

In the case of the Peace by Investment Corporation, the initial funds of the Corporation are provided by government to attract those sources of capitalsmall and medium investors-for whom investment in developing countries would otherwise be too risky.

The concept embodied in the corporation has attracted the support of the Sub committee on Foreign Economic Policy of the House Foreign Affairs Committee. 

In its report of March 31, 1968, the Committee called for the creation of such a corporation. Earlier, the Advisory Committee on Private Enterprise in Foreign

Aid, chaired by Arthur K. Watson, chairman of IBM World Trade Corporation, said that this approach "deserves the most careful consideration as a way of helping to fill the capital gap with private resources." Also David Bell, while 

Administrator of AID, in a letter to Chairman Fulbright supported hearings by this Committee on this measure. 

Mr. Chairman, it would be the greatest folly for us to believe that we can continue to exist in peace in a world still heavily ravaged by hunger and poverty. 

The great bulk of mankind still lives on bare subsistance and envies for the opulence in which even our poor live by their standards, let alone those of us who are well off.

The growing gap between the flow of actual economic assistance from industrial nations to developing countries and their absorptive capacity is growing and cannot be overcome by aid from governments, by international lending agencies, or by private investment at the present rate. The requirement for development capital-estimated at between $5 to $20 billion a year above what is being provided now-is simply beyond what is obtainable from the industrialized nations today. Resources on such a scale are available only from major increase in private investment. 

The gap between rich and poor nations must be reduced. The American commitment to aid developing nations must continue, not for humanitarian reasons

but for the hard, practical reason that in an atmosphere of economic and social improvement political institutions in developing nations will be able to grow and will make possible the emergence of a stable international order. 

Senator SPARKMAN. I understand Mr. Charles H. Smith, Jr., has come in. 

Will you come around?

Mr. Smith, I made the announcement this morning when we started that due to the large number of witnesses-and we still have several more to go I have asked each one to submit his statement and then summarize it just as briefly as possible. We have about seven more witnesses to be heard from, so we would appreciate your proceeding. 

Mr. SMITH. I will proceed as fast as I can, sir.

STATEMENT OF CHARLES H. SMITH, JR., DIRECTOR, CHAMBER OF COMMERCE OF THE UNITED STATES 

Mr. SMITH. I am Charles H. Smith, Jr., president of the Steel 

Improvement & Forge Co. of Cleveland. My company has interests in Brazil, Argentina, and India. I am pleased to be with you to present the views of the Chamber of Commerce of the United States on the Foreign Assistance Act of 1968. For the committee's information, the national chamber is composed of some 3,900 organization members and more than 33,000 business members representing an underlying membership of more than 5 million individuals and firms. 

With me today is John R. Gilbert, senior associate of the chamber's international group.

I am a director of the national chamber and a member of its foreign assistance advisory panel which meets each year with key Government officials to discuss foreign assistance programs and to formulate recommendations on those programs. The committee may also wish its record to show that I am an adviser to AID's international private investment advisory council, and that I have been a U.S. employer delegate and adviser to several International Labor 

Conferences in Geneva.

FOREIGN AID SUPPORTED 

As in the past, the national chamber supports foreign aid as an instrument of U.S. foreign policy and as a means of stimulating the kind of private initiative that can put developing countries in the position of helping themselves. We are pleased to see that the President placed priority emphasis on self-help and selectivity in his foreign aid message this year.

Any American who has visited the developing countries, encompassing approximately 68 percent of the world's population, and has viewed firsthand the extreme poverty-the almost totally inadequate living conditons-that is the lot of the vast majority of the people of these areas of the world, is bound to return to the United States with at least two conclusions:

First, an even greater appreciation of the economic and political systems that have provided an opportunity for the people of our own country to create a standard of living that is materially better than anywhere else in the world. 91-392-68-pt. 2- -12

And second, a recognition that the odds against achieving true and lasting peace in the world are very great indeed until a means can be found to steadily improve the living conditions that are available to the majority of the people living in the developing countries. In the very small world that has been created by modern transportation and communication techniques, there is apt to be a very large explosion at some time in the foreseeable future if the vast majority of the world's population continues to merely exist, with no real hope for any measurable improvement in living conditions, while the people of North America and Western Europe steadily progress to higher standards of living.

The United States does not have sufficient resources to materially improve living conditions for the people of the developing world merely by sharing our abundance with them. We do have the capability, however, and perhaps the necessity in terms of "enlightened self-interest" to help the developing nations build their own economies in a manner that will enable these nations to achieve a better standard of living for their people.

CREATING SELFSUSTAINING ECONOMICS IN DEVELOPING NATIONS 

The Chamber of Commerce of the United States believes that a major purpose of foreign aid is to help the developing nations create a selfsustaining economy that will grow at a sufficient rate to enable these nations to create real opportunities for improving living conditions. To achieve this purpose, we should insist that our programs of foreign aid be fashioned in such a manner that they will contribute to the growth of the type of economic system that we know achieves results-the system of private property-free markets-competitionindividual liberty and opportunity that has worked so well in this country and in every other where it has truly been given a chance to work.

Priority is a keyword in evaluating approaches to the complex economic and social problems of emerging countries. Last year's alltime low foreign aid appropriations show that the Congress wants priorities to be set and that AID will have to redirect its programs so that goals can be achieved within budget limitations. Congress and the public, being fully aware of development needs on the one hand and of U.S. international involvements on the other hand, are demanding that existing private approaches to the international development process be strengthened and that less emphasis be placed on such direct government to government assistance; as grants for projects which are not directly related to economic development. 

FOREIGN AID IN REVIEW 

Proponents and critics of foreign aid have pointed out many times that the program lacks a constituency. Congressional reaction to foreign aid shows that there is a basis for concern about dollar outlays rather than program objectives. A similar concern was reflected in a national chamber membership poll. If foreign aid is to continue to be a nonpartisan issue-as we think it should be the administration must

assert itself by explaining the program to the public in terms of foreign policy objectives and financial benefits to the United States. 

Since the public demands a demonstrable return on its dollar investment on foreign aid, program objectives must be delineated as they relate to U.S. foreign policy. In the field of foreign policy, foreign aid is often used to stimulate regional and country development which may only be profitable materially in the long run. 

The other side of the coin is that foreign aid serves as a catalyst for expanding U.S. exports. In fiscal year 1967, "tied aid" provisions of the economic assistance program resulted in expenditures for U.S. commodities and services amounting to $1.4 billion. This positive feature of the aid program is an important contribution to current efforts to reduce our balance of payments deficit. Mandatory restrictions on direct foreign investment reduce private participation in the development process although "accommodations" are being considered by Government to permit expanded investment to move forward in the developing countries. The business community urges termination of controls as soon as possible, especially in those countries given high priority by AID. 

FUNDS FOR DEVELOPMENT 

Budgetary savings in foreign assistance programs can be realized through more efficient AID administration and by holding spending to last year's levels. The President has asked AID to cut overseas spending for commodity procurement and salaries by $100 million in addition to utilizing more U.S.-owned foreign currencies to finance local technical assistance costs. We are pleased to learn that AID plans to reduce its payroll by 1,300 employees in the next 14 months. Further reductions in overseas and Washington staff could also be made if aid type arrangements were made whereby private banking facilities replace government to government assistance and reduce administrative activities of mission oriented programs. 

However, efficient administration alone will not reduce U.S. outlays for foreign assistance. Population growth and mounting food needs coupled with increased demands for funds by developing countries as they reach the point where they can effectively absorb assistance, underscore the need for a more equitable sharing of worldwide assistance to emerging countries-assistance that should be conditioned upon performance. 

Since several items in the foreign aid budget are what might be called fixed programs in that the amount requested is approximately the same amount appropriated each year, the national chamber concentrated this year on items directly related to private enterprise. 

In general, economic assistance activities should not be expanded. 

We hope that the proposed congressional Joint Committee on Foreign Aid is established so that the impact of budget reductions can be evaluated, particularly as they relate to development loans and technical assistance which can pave the way toward balanced development. 

No position is taken on the military assistance portion of the President's total foreign aid request of $2.9 million because classified materials are not available. 

Although S. 3091 does not contain authorization requests for the 

Inter-American Development Bank, the International Development

Association, and the Asian Development Bank, the impact of programs financed by these organizations is of direct concern to this committee. Encouraging efforts are being made toward making burdensharing a reality in these organizations. Taking into account the current congressional and public attitude toward foreign aid and the economy push by Government, increased international participation in multilateral financing might be an alternative to substantial bilateral programs in 1968-69-or in the 1970's. 

PRIVATE ENTERPRISE AND FOREIGN ASSISTANCE 

Development loans, investment guaranties, and investment surveys are programs provided by the Foreign Assistance Act to stimulate United States and foreign private investment. The national chamber supports the incentive concept embodied in these programs as an approach to building an economic and political framework in which the profit motive can operate at full potential. 

As you know, the business community's concern about proposals to reduce the extended risk investment guaranty coverage from 75 percent to 50 percent resulted in maintaining the 75-percent coverage which is not available commercially. 

We want to urge particularly that this 75-percent ceiling coverage be kept and that the ceilings for this extended risk guaranty program be expanded and increased. 

I know that you do have other witnesses, Mr. Chairman, and I will try to paraphrase the last of this. 

We are particularly anxious to see foreign aid move in the direction of agricultural development which, we think, is extremely important in feeding the underdeveloped world. 

I think hand in hand with this we can support the concept that funds be used for family planning, and most of all, I think, in concluding, I would like to emphasize we would like to continue to urge that our foreign aid program be carried on with emphasis in the private field. We think this is what has made this country great. 

We think it can do more for the developing countries than any other form of economic establishment. 

I thank you, and I appreciate I have taken a little more time than you would like. 

(The prepared statement of Mr. Smith follows:) STATEMENT FOR THE CHAMBER OF COMMERCE OF THE UNITED STATES BY CHARLES H.
