However, efficient administration alone will not reduce United States outlays for foreign assistance. Population growth and mounting food needs coupled with increased demands for funds by developing countries as they reach the point where they can effectively absorb assistance, underscore the need for a more equitable sharing of world-wide assistance to emerging countries-assistance that should be conditioned upon performance.

Since several items in the foreign aid budget are what might be called fixed cost programs in that the amount requested is approximately the same amount appropriated each year, the National Chamber concentrated this year on items directly related to private enterprise. In general, economic assistance activities should not be expanded. We hope that the proposed Congressional Joint Committee on Foreign Aid is established so that the impact of budget reductions can be evaluated, particularly as they relate to development loans and technical assistance which can pave the way toward balanced development. 

No position is taken on the military assistance portion of the President's total foreign aid request of $2.9 billion because classified materials are not available. 

Although S. 3091 does not contain authorization requests for the Inter-American Development Bank, the International Development Association, and the

Asian Development Bank, the impact of programs financed by these organizations is of direct concern to this Committee. Encouraging efforts are being made toward making burden-sharing a reality in these organizations. Taking into account the current Congressional and public attitude toward foreign aid and the economy push by Government, increased international participation in multilateral financing might by an alternative to substantial bilateral programs in 

1968-1969-or in the 1970's. 

Private Enterprise and Foreign Assistance 

Development loans, investment guaranties, and investment surveys are programs provided by the Foreign Assistance Act to stimulate United States and foreign private investment. The National Chamber supports the incentive concept embodied in these programs as an approach to building an economic and political framework in which the profit motive can operate at full potential. 

As you know, the business community's concern about proposals to reduce the extended risk investment guaranty coverage from 75% to 50% resulted in maintaining the 75% coverage which is not available commercially. Particular emphasis centered on agribusiness projects. We urge that ceilings for specific and extended risk guaranty programs be raised to permit AID to process backlogged applications for coverage and to expand the programs. 

We are aware that some foreign aid critics think that the investment guaranty program is a business subsidy. It is not. It is self-financed. Most likely, the guaranty program is confused with the investment survey program whereby AID pays 50% of an investment survey if the investor decides against the investment. 

Business has felt for some time that AID programming often does not relate to the needs of United States and foreign business because there is a lack in certain areas of a day-to-day working knowledge and understanding of business principles. U.S. businessmen should be asked to participate with advisory boards in aid recipient countries before projects are initiated in the field and before those projects are submitted to AID Washington. The object of consultations

in drafting country programs would be to introduce a market concept designed to produce a profitable, interrelated economic system. 

To give emphasis to the role of private enterprise in the development process, the National Chamber maintains active liaison with AID's International Private Investment Advisory Council of which I am a member. The Council's two-fold purpose is to stimulate more private enterprise in developing countries and to shift an increasing number of development programs from the public sector to the private sector. Suggestions made by two ad hoc panels on investment and incentives point in the direction of reducing AID administrative functions while emphasizing business-like approaches to investment and opportunities.

Agricultural Development and Population Growth 

Major emphasis was made in the President's foreign aid message this year, as it was last year, on agricultural development and population growth. We cannot emphasize too strongly that such programs as investment guaranties must be maintained and broadened to attract investments that would not ordinarily be justifiable to a majority of stockholders. 

Development of agricultural techniques and productivity has been pointed to by the National Chamber and other organizations as a basic requirement for putting developing countries in the position where they can help themselves. 

Guidelines for implementing food programs must emphasize the use of private capital and free market transactions. Every effort should be made to reinvest local currencies generated by P.L. 480 food sales which only provide temporary relief.

Food aid which is now exchanged for commitments is a device to buy time. 

The other side of the equation is family planning. The National Chamber reiterates its position that funds should be available to provide information about voluntary family planning when such funds are requested by developing countries.

Effective agricultural development can only be achieved by tying rural areas to the overall economy. Several Chamber member firms are involved in the systems development of production and marketing procedures, on a regional basis, as an integral part of economic development. 

Conclusion 

Experience of Chamber member firms involved in the development process shows that those countries which are developing most rapidly are those where investment, public policy, and external assistance have worked together to bring private initiative into play.

The National Chamber has consistently supported foreign assistance as an important element of United States foreign policy, as a humanitarian obiligation, and as a means of developing the business community's own self-interest. 

We are encouraged to see a recognition of the need to shift more development programs from the public sector to the private sector. We favor channelling a larger percentage of available funds through international financial institutions that emphasize burden-sharing and condition assistance on performance. 

Profit is a driving force in our economic system. The profit motive must be allowed free play at home and abroad if private enterprise is to be encouraged to increase its contribution to the development process. 

In conclusion, Mr. Chairman, we request that the attached National Chamber "Statement in Furtherance of Policies on Mutual Security" be made part of the official records of your hearings. 

STATEMENT IN FURTHERANCE OF POLICIES ON MUTUAL SECURITY I. BASIC CHAMBER POSITION ON FOREIGN ASSISTANCE 

A. Foreign aid is an arm of U.S. foreign policy. Its objectives and principles have long been endorsed by the Chamber. Program objectives must be delineated as they relate to U.S. foreign policy. The U.S. public demands a demonstrable return on the foreign aid investment.

B. Refinement of the program must provide an effective base for the exercise of private enterprise initiative in foreign economic and social development. 

C. Program objectives should be continually clarified as they relate to U.S. foreign policy and to U.S. and foreign private enterprise.

D. Capital formation and accumulation by developing countries is essential to their economic development. Every effort must be made by these countries to create and maintain a climate that will attract development capital. 

II. CHAMBER PROGRAM PROPOSALS 

A. Long-range economic planning and development must be emphasized. To accomplish this goal, multi-year authorizations are desirable. 

Although long-term aid investment seldom has a dramatic impact, in general it can yield far higher returns than short-term "gimmicks". Economic planning need not mean a government "planned economy" in underdeveloped countries. 

Recent efforts at country development planning have shown the need for private foreign investment. Business should be consulted in the drafting of country programs so that a market concept designed to produce a profitable, interrelated economic system can be introduced. Foreign economic development programs must place increased reliance on private resources, and reduce government programs after these programs have served their catalytic purpose. 

Private enterprise and government should cooperate in their efforts to locate opportunities for the development of free and viable economies that can improve local conditions and provide markets for U.S. goods and services. In particular, foreign participation should be encouraged when feasible as a means of meeting nationalistic concerns. In negotiating such arrangements, business efforts to conclude equitable agreements should be fully supported by the U.S. Government. 

The expertise and recommendations of the International Private Investment Advisory Council on Foreign Aid should be fully utilized and implemented. 

B. Government incentives to U.S. private foreign investment serve a useful purpose. But a more positive approach is imperative, centering around the need to build an economic and political framework in which the profit motive can operate at full potential. Priority must be given to removal of disincentives that discourage private investment. Business concern about the implications and longer-term consequences of government restrictions on direct investment calls for termination of controls in those countries which are given high priority by the Agency for International Development. Certain provisions in the Revenue 

Act of 1962 on taxation of foreign income are punitive and self-defeating. International antitrust standards and an international patent system should also be developed. 

International principles relating to the rights and responsibilities of private foreign investment should be clarified. A multilateral system of investment guarantees could contribute to such clarification. 

The United States should suspend foreign assistance to any nation that takes discriminatory or confiscatory action against U.S. private investment in such nations without taking appropriate steps to discharge its obligations under international law including prompt and full compensation or relief. Courts in the 

United States should determine and apply principles of international law in appropriate cases to encourage the development of an international legal system that will contribute to the improvement of the investment climate, and the protection of private property abroad. 

C. Development loans should receive priority. The trend towards government lending to private institutions and organizations for the purpose of their development should be accelerated. Terms of development loans should take into consideration the overall debt management position of recipient countries. 

D. A limited program of military assistance to selected allies is essential to free world security. Military assistance should be held to a minimum in Western 

Europe and Latin America. The cost to the United States of such assistance should remain accountable to the Department of Defense. 

E. Multilateral development financing and technical assistance should be emphasized as an essential instrument of U.S. foreign policy. In many instances, this approach is more practical and effective than the bilateral approach. Private international consortia should be utilized to the fullest extent possible. Congress and the Administration should continue to place major emphasis on U.S. participation in international institutions, including regional development banks, provided the United States has voting rights commensurate to its contributions. 

Such programs must assign a major role to the private sectors of both donor and recipient nations, and the administrative cost of such programs must be equitably shared by the respective national governments. The Chamber recognizes the

need for selective United States bilateral programs that benefit the national interest, augmented by essential multilateral programs. 

F. The Administration must apply great selectivity in choosing which nations and projects are to be aided from among ever-increasing requests for assistance. 

Assistance must be based on results. 

Emphasis must be placed on technical assistance programs that can put aid recipients in the position of solving their own problems. 

G. The Food for Freedom Program is a temporary relief measure that serves, in general, U.S. foreign policy provided agricultural techniques and productivity are improved. Private initiative must be encouraged to undertake fundamental agricultural development and food production that can lead to more capital intensive industrialization. 

U.S.-owned local currencies should be used where available to finance investment surveys and vocational training facilities. Funds should be available to provide information about voluntary birth control programs when such funds are requested by less-developed countries. 

H. The Alliance for Progress must be moved forward along the sound lines recommended by its Inter-American Committee (CIAP). A broader concept of the Alliance should be developed by encouraging other member countries to contribute appropriate financial and technical assistance for Latin American development.

The objectives of the Alliance must be realistically determined with emphasis on regional market integration and the availability of external resources. 

Alliance programs must create a stable atmosphere in which private investment and initiative, local and foreign, can operate beneficially. 

I. The aid task must be justly shared. The United States should continue to enlist greater cooperation from other industrialized nations in assisting less developed countries. The Development Assistance Committee of the OECD has an important duty as an instrument to serve this purpose. 

J. Effective foreign assistance programs can be implemented only by compe tent personnel. Private enterprise urges, and is willing to cooperate with AID in, the maintenance of such standards. The administrators of the program must have the legislative authority to improve the quality and reduce the number of 

AID personnel, both domestic and overseas. 

Senator SPARKMAN. That is all right, Mr. Smith. You have hit it just right. That bell signals a rollcall, so I must go to the Senate. 

Now, if the other witnesses will stay here I will recess for about 

10 minutes and be back. We have four and possibly five witnesses left.

If any of you cares to hand in his statement we will be very glad to have each one for the record. Otherwise when I come back we simply will allot time in order to finish. 

Thank you very much, Mr. Smith. 

Mr. SMITH. Can I assume from that you have no questions to ask of me? 

Senator SPARKMAN. Yes. I have questions to ask you but I shall not take the time to do it.

I want to say this; I think your statement is very good. I think it is a well balanced statement. I am glad, very glad, to see the things that you do emphasize. I believe you know that this committee and, I think 

I can say the Congress, has been trying and has made considerable progress, I think, over the years, to move this to a development program and to bring in private enterprise just as much as we can, and I believe those are the things that you advocate. 

Mr. SMITH. Thank you, Senator.

Senator SPARKMAN. Very well.

Senator SPARKMAN. Let the committee come to order, please.

The next witness is Mr. Shipe of CUNA International. Mr. Shipe, we are glad to have you, sir.

STATEMENT OF J. ORRIN SHIPE, MANAGING DIRECTOR, CUNA INTERNATIONAL, INC. 

Mr. SHIPE. Glad to be here. I know how busy you are so we boiled it down considerably, Senator. 

I am J. Orrin Shipe, managing director of CUNA International,

Inc., a confederation of credit unions and leagues in the various 

States of this country, the Provinces of Canada, and in some 70 other countries. There are approximately 49,000 credit unions in the free world with over 30 million members. In the United States alone we have over 23,000 credit unions and 19.5 million members.

CUNA International, Inc., as you know, is a contractor under the 

Agency for International Development to provide technical knowledge and guidance for the development of credit unions in the developing areas of the world. As a contractor, we feel that we owe this committee a report, in broad general terms, of our activities, in other words, an accounting of what we have accomplished with the dollars you have committed to our contract. We welcome this opportunity to report, and we are appreciative of the courtesy you have extended us by allowing us to appear before you today. First, I want to express to this committee the thanks of the members of the credit union movement in the United States for the credit union investment guaranties which this committee supported in the Foreign Assistance Act Amendments of 1967. As you know, the legislation did not become law until 

November 14, 1967. Administrative procedures had to be prepared and approved, both by the Agency for International Development and by the credit union organizations. We are just about ready to negotiate the first U.S. credit union investment loan to a developing nation credit union; and, once the mechanics are established and running smoothly, we anticipate great success for the program. 

So, gentlemen, we are grateful for the investment guaranties you provided in last year's legislation; we think it heralds a big step toward a brighter future for our program.

ACCOMPLISHMENTS IN OVERSEAS ACTIVITIES 

Now let us look at some specifics in the accomplishments of our overseas activities under AID contracts since we reported to this committee at approximately this time last year. 

Our 12 AID contracts cover 12 countries of Latin America; Bolivia, Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru, Venezuela, Costa Rica, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua, and Panama. These countries, as all other developing countries, have an urgent need to increase their domestic savings. Credit unions are helping them to meet this need. 

By the end of last year, credit unions in these 12 countries had mobilized $48,800,000 in savings. In 1967 alone, they increased members' savings 23 percent; this amounted to $9 million. As the chart in the attached annex A (see committee files) indicates, it will take only a few years for their savings to exceed $100 million. 

It is a striking fact that for every dollar invested by AID through CUNA in this effort more than $20 of new capital has already been mobolized. This gives a benefit cost ratio which is constantly increasing. This is a fact of which we can all be proud.

In addition to mobilizing savings in 12 Latin American and three African countries, our present CUNA/AID program is supporting a unique form of inservice training for approximately 35,000 people who will serve this year as officers, board members, and committee members of credit unions in these countries. The great majority of these individuals are elected to these positions through the democratic process of one member, one vote.

The real and ultimate objective of the CUNA credit union effort under the AID program is to enable the people to attain selfsufficiency. CUNA's regional office for Latin America located in Panama aims at the development of specific programs to reach this objective of selfsufficiency in the shortest possible time. 

GOALS FOR THE FUTURE 

I have cited the foregoing figures and have detailed procedures under which credit unions operate in the developing nations. I have cited our program objectives for the future-objectives which if attained can mean economic expansion and increased production in the developing nations in which we operate. Although I believe our achievements in terms of hard figures involving dollars and numbers of individuals and credit unions are important and impressive, they are not in themselves the truly significant factor in this tremendous AID program of which we are proud to be a part. 

The real import of this program is the fact that it is bringing the indigenous financial resources of these nations out of hiding and putting them to work for the economic welfare of the people themselves. 

Credit unions are developing economic knowhow for people who have never had the financial resources to break out of the economic bondage which chains them to a substandard way of life. 

Credit unions are teaching these people not only how to manage their own financial resources, but also how to do it democratically. 

The principle of one man, one vote is inherent in the credit union; a shareholder has one vote regardless of the number of shares he holds. 

Thus, the credit union member learns a vital principle of democracy. 

Credit unions provide the basic foundation upon which these developing nations can build a more complex financial structure through development of cooperatives in various other fields of endeavor such as housing and related areas. 

Gentlemen, through title IX of the Foreign Assistance Act, this Nation is making a historic and bold attack upon the problems which are delaying the development of many of our sister nations. The technical assistance programs of AID are bringing hope; they are bringing economic independence; they are bringing an entirely new way of life to hundreds and thousands of our fellow men. I urge you to continue and, yes, to expand this phase of our foreign assistance program. 

Thank you, Senator Sparkman. 

(The statement of Mr. Shipe follows:) STATEMENT OF J. ORRIN SHIPE, MANAGING DIRECTOR CUNA INTERNATIONAL, INC. 

Mr. Chairman, and members of the Committee: I am J. Orrin Shipe, managing director of CUNA International, Inc., a confederation of credit unions and leagues in the various states of this country, the provinces of Canada, and in some 70 other countries. There are approximately 49,000 credit unions in the free world

with over 30 million members. In the United States alone we have over 23,000 credit unions and 19.5 million members.

CUNA International, Inc., as you know, is a contractor under the Agency for 

International Development to provide technical knowledge and guidance for the development of credit unions in the developing areas of the world. As a contractor, we feel that we owe this Committee a report, in broad general terms, of our activities-in other words, an accounting of what we have accomplished with the dollars you have committed to our contract. We welcome this opportunity to report, and we are appreciative of the courtesy you have extended us by allowing us to appear before you today.

First, I want to express to this Committee the thanks of the members of the credit union movement in the United States for the credit union investment guaranties which this Committee supported in the Foreign Assistance Act Amendments of 1967. As you know, the legislation did not become law until November 

14, 1967. Administrative procedures had to be prepared and approved, both by the Agency for International Development and by the credit union organizations. 

We are just about ready to negotiate the first U.S. credit union investment loan to a developing nation credit union; and, once the mechanics are established and running smoothly, we anticipate great success for the program. 

It is essential that we have a sound operational plan for this program because it holds such a great potential for the future. When we get organizations such as 

CUNA International and similar private organizations working directly with their counterparts in developing nations, we build durable bridges of international cooperation. We bring people together with people, fortifying their ability to solve their own problems and equipping them to deal more effectively with their own governments. We help establish more substantial foundations for international understanding and cooperation. 

So, gentlemen, we are grateful for the investment guaranties you provided in last year's legislation; we think it heralds a big step toward a brighter future for our program.

SPECIFIC ACCOMPLISHMENTS OF OVERSEAS ACTIVITIES 

Now let us look at some specifics in the accomplishments of our overseas activities under AID contracts since we reported to this Committee at approximately this time last year. Although our African program is still in an early stage of development, it is already gaining momentum in Tanzania, Kenya and Uganda. The number of credit unions has risen from 118 at the end of 1966 to 169 on December 31, 1967. Membership has increased from 14,857 to 22,749 during the same period. During the year's time, savings increased approximately 48 percent to $709,000 and outstanding loans increased by 51 percent to $534,000.

The development of credit unions in our Latin American program is much more advanced. Last year there was a 15 percent increase of credit unions to a total of 2,496. Membership reached 630,357, a 10 percent gain for the year. 

It is interesting to note that cumulative loans of the Latin American credit unions since July 1962 reached the substantial figure of $133,484,000. (See Annex A).

Our 12 AID contracts cover 12 countries of Latin America; Bolivia, Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru, Venezuela, Costa Rica, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua, and Panama. These countries, as all other developing countries, have an urgent need to increase their domestic savings. Credit unions are helping them to meet this need.

By the end of last year, credit unions in these 12 countries had mobilized $48,800,000 in savings. In 1967 alone, they increased members' savings 23 percent; this amounted to $9 million. As the chart in the attached Annex A indicates, it will take only a few years for their savings to exceed $100 million. 

It is a striking fact that for every dollar invested by AID through CUNA in this effort more than $20 of new capital has already been mobilized. This gives a benefit-cost ratio which is constantly increasing. This is a fact of which we can all be proud.

Of great significance is the fact that these credit union savings are coming from low income people who, for the most part, have never been previously reached by other financial institutions. Credit unions are proving that poor people have an untapped potential for savings.

In some countries the progress of these people in mobilizing savings is striking. 

For example, savings in credit unions in Bolivia now exceed the personal savings in all of the commercial banks in that country. Progress in other countries also indicates that low income people will accumulate savings whenever the necessary services for doing so are made available to them. We are convinced that credit unions can provide such services.

In addition to mobilizing savings in 12 Latin American and three African countries, our present CUÑA/AID Program is supporting a unique form of in-service training for approximately 35,000 people who will serve this year as officers, board members and committee members of credit unions in these countries. The great majority of these individuals are elected to these positions through the democratic process of one member, one vote. 

At the time of their election these individuals have had no experience and little training for such responsibilities. However, their election by fellow credit union members gave them the incentive to develop their potential for effective service. This is strikingly exhibited by their eagerness for a wide range of study materials and by their participation in various types of train-workshops. 

Their determination to increase their skills is most impressive. 

The results of this in-service training are equally impressive. These directors and officers not only develop the skills necessary to operate their credit unions successfully but by so doing they also prepare themselves for positions of responsibility in other organizations and institutions. Thus, they become active contributors to the increase of skilled manpower in each country. 

OPERATION OF CREDIT UNIONS IN DEVELOPING NATIONS 

Credit unions in these developing nations operate quite similarly to those in our country. However, our CUNA/AID assistance places special emphasis on the making of loans which will serve to produce additional income for the borrower. In Ecuador, a special type of credit union loan is made for the primary purpose of increasing agricultural production. Such loans are granted only after the member has shown exactly how he plans to use the money and after he has been given some training and advice by an agricultural extension worker. We operate on the premise that it would do little good to tell a poor farmer he needs better seed, fertilizer, insecticides, pesticides, fungicides, and vaccines if there is no way for him to purchase these things. By the same token, what good would a source of credit be to him if he had no background or training in using it wisely? Farmers who have participated in these special programs in Ecuador from their inception in 1965 have increased their production at an average rate of 30 percent per year-almost 100 percent in three years. 

The real and ultimate objective of the CUNA credit union effort under the AID program is to enable the people to attain self sufficiency. CUNA's Regional 

Office for Latin America located in Panama aims at the development of specific programs to reach this objective of self-sufficiency in the shortest possible time, in three basic steps.

First, the mobilization of national resources through the formation of credit unions at local levels. 

Second, the overall improvement of the management of credit unions to increase the effectiveness of their operation.

Third, the establishment of national credit union organizations which can help to provide the necessary resources for the continued growth and strengthening of the credit unions in that country.

I have cited the foregoing figures and have detailed procedures under which credit unions operate in the developing nations. I have cited our program objectives for the future-objectives which if attained can mean economic expansion and increased production in the developing nations in which we operate. Although I believe our achievements in terms of hard figures involving dollars and numbers of individuals and credit unions are important and impressive, they are not in themselves the truly significant factor in this tremendous AID program of which we are proud to be a part. 

The real import of this program is the fact that it is bringing the indigenous financial resources of these nations out of hiding and putting them to work for the economic welfare of the people themselves. 

Credit unions are developing economic knowhow for people who have never had the financial resources to break out of the economic bondage which chains them to a sub-standard way of life.

Credit unions are teaching these people not only how to manage their own financial resources, but also how to do it democratically. The principle of oneman, one-vote is inherent in the credit union; a shareholder has one vote regardless of the number of shares he holds. Thus, the credit union member learns a vital principle of democracy. 

Credit unions provide the basic foundation upon which these developing nations can build a more complex financial structure through development of cooperatives in various other fields of endeavor such as housing and related areas. 

Gentlemen, through Title IX of the Foreign Assistance Act, this nation is making a historic and bold attack upon the problems which are delaying the development of many of our sister nations. The technical assistance programs of AID are bringing hope; they are bringing economic independence; they are bringing an entirely new way of life to hundreds and thousands of our fellow men. I urge you to continue and even to expand this phase of our foreign assistance program.

INADEQUACY OF TIME TO TESTIFY 

I do want to take a minute to say we really regret that something as important as the U.S. foreign aid program was compressed into a half day of hearings for the nongovernmental organizations. 

I think this does not really represent an adequate appraisal of what you men feel the private sector of the American people can contribute to your thinking.

I know it was partly because of the time element, but I think it is especially unfortunate that in that half day your senatorial colleagues were included, which made still more pressure on those of us who represent the nongovernmental organizations. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Let me say this, I would certainly not quarrel with you about the inadequacy of the time. However, we work against very great difficulties here. We are all members of other committees.. 

Mr. VAN DEUSEN. Yes.

You take this last statement, for instance. I was amazed. I followed his statement as he went through, to see how he pulled out and condensed his statement into the facts that were really pertinent. There is no organization, no person, who has testified here this morning who did not leave a clear impression as to what his views or the views of his organization are.

Mr. VAN DEUSEN. Now, very briefly: The Lutheran Churches of the United States, and other churches, when they testify about foreign aid, speak out of a background of experience. For over a century, the churches have been dealing with these same problems in the developing areas, not only in evangelistic work but in medical, educational, and agricultural projects. 

So we have confronted the need first hand and know what the problems are. We realize that the need is so immense that only the governmental resources of our Nation and other industrialized nations can cope adequately with the problems. 

On a long-term basis, we approve strongly of what the Government has been doing. We recognize that in any program this large there are bound to be shortcomings, but in balance we think the Government has done an excellent job and needs to do more. 

Next year's program, we think, ought to be larger than this year's rather than smaller. We hope this can be given serious consideration. 

UNITED STATES HAS A MORAL RESPONSIBILITY 

In our prepared statement, we stress the moral responsibility of the United States. The difference in the standard of living between the 

United States and the rest of the world is on the conscience of a lot of Christians. We believe this gives a moral undergirding to the U.S. foreign aid program.

We do not feel that we ought to limit what our Government does abroad by what it needs to do at home. We are a rich enough nation to do both and do them adequately. We do not think foreign aid ought to be the "whipping boy" of the budget cuts.

I am going to let the rest of the statement stand on its own feet. 

As we say at the very end of the statement, we do urge you to act both responsively and responsibly in meeting world needs. We hope you will authorize the necessary funds, not only for the continuance but for the expansion of the foreign aid program. 

(The prepared statement of Mr. Van Deusen follows:) STATEMENT OF DR. ROBERT E. VAN DEUSEN, DIRECTOR, OFFICE OF PUBLIC AFFAIRS, LUTHERAN COUNCIL IN THE UNITED STATES OF AMERICA My name is Robert E. Van Deusen. I am the director of the Office of Public 

Affairs of the Lutheran Council in the United States of America. The Council is a cooperative agency of four Lutheran church bodies: the Lutheran Church in America, The Lutheran Church-Missouri Synod, The American Lutheran Church, and the Synod of Evangelical Lutheran Churches. The combined membership of these four church bodies exceeds 8½ million. 

The Council was organized in November 1966, as a successor organization to the National Lutheran Council which was organized in 1918. Among the functions delegated to the Council by its member churches is that of representing their interests in matters which require common action before the American public and national and state governments. 

The Executive Committee of the Council, in the fulfillment of this function, has authorized me to present testimony at Congressional hearings on the Foreign Assistance Act of 1968, expressing in that testimony the following concerns: 

1. In our world with its rapidly increasing population, famine in Asia, Africa, and Latin America is widely predicted by responsible experts. 

2. Leaders in many developing countries are stressing the needs of their people for aid and cooperation in economic, social and political development. 

3. The United States with its great resources has a deep moral responsibility to help the peoples of developing nations to help themselves. 

4. The fight against world hunger and poverty is of primary urgency and must be pursued vigorously in the interest of world justice and peace even while our nation helps its own who are deprived. 

5. Our government's program of technical assistance and social and economic development in the needy nations should be strengthened and intensified. 

6. Our government should increase its commitment of funds and personnel to programs of voluntarily family planning and economic development, particularly in food production. 

7. Our government should foster and strengthen international economic cooperation, including trade with the developing nations and increasing participation in the work of multilateral agencies. 

I should like to expand briefly on each of these concerns and to interpret the background out of which they are expressed. The Lutheran churches of the 

United States have for over a century been actively involved in serving people in the developing continents. While their primary purpose has been the proclamation of the Christian gospel, their understanding of that gospel has included a mandate to serve the physical and social needs of the people as well as their spiritual needs.

Thus the mission work of the churches has included medical, educational, agricultural, and economic aspects. In many places our churches pioneered in improving social and economic conditions. Today, in some of our churches, more than 50 per cent of their missionary personnel are now being sent for special services in education, medicine, and agriculture. 

The churches are sharply aware of the specialized problems in the less developed countries. While dealing with those problems in a limited way, the churches are conscious that the need is so immense that only the resources available to the governments of industrialized countries can hope to cope successfully with them. We therefore look to our government for constructive action in cooperation with other governments to deal with these problems that are truly global in scope.

The n 1. POPULATION GROWTH AND THE THREAT OF FAMINE 

prediction of a world population of seven billion by the year 2,000 is a frightening one, when coupled with the fact that, in large areas of the world, population is already outrunning food supply. Famine, already a critical problem for some nations, threatens to ravage major areas of the world. If disaster is to be averted, public and private agencies must cooperate in massive efforts to alleviate and to prevent famine. 

The Lutheran churches, through Lutheran World Relief, have shipped food, clothing and medicine valued at more than $193 million to 45 countries in the past twenty-three years. Almost half of that amount has been in the form of food donated by the United States government. We commend our government for its willingness to share America's resources with those who are in want, and we are pleased to cooperate in their distribution. 

Such programs, however, provide only temporary relief of the most critical needs. We feel that this long-range problem cannot be handled only on an emergency basis, but must be met through programs of development and of technical assistance such as are carried out by the Agency for International Development. We are deeply concerned over the current trend toward reducing the size of the U.S. foreign aid program, and believe that it should instead be substantially increased. 

2. NEED OF DEVELOPING COUNTRIES FOR AID 

The resources of the countries where the need for development is the greatest are not adequate to solve their problems. Technical and industrial know-how, investment capital, and trained leadership must come from other countries at the outset. With time and the development of their own human and natural resources, most of these countries can later assume a growing degree of responsibility for meeting their own needs.

The fact that many developing countries have recently become independent adds to the scope and urgency of the problem. Simultaneous achievement of political and economic stability and meeting the social and educational needs of their people is simply beyond the capacity of many new nations without outside help. 

3. MORAL RESPONSIBILITY OF THE UNITED STATES 

Since our nation has been so richly blessed, it bears a special responsibility toward other nations which are less adequately endowed. The gap between the standard of living in the United States and that in the developing continents weighs on the conscience of many Christians. To many people, the responsibility to share abundant national resources provides a strong moral undergirding for a substantial program of foreign aid. 

This sense of responsibility is especially valid when it results, not in the indiscriminate granting of largesse, but in a carefully planned program of helping the people of other nations to help themselves. The strong element of self-help in the U.S. foreign aid program commends itself as being compatible with the human dignity of the recipients, as well as being judicious distribution of resources. 

4. RELATION OF FOREIGN AID TO DOMESTIC PROBLEMS 

The prevalence of poverty in many American cities and the mushrooming costs of government programs aimed at improving economic and social conditions have led some to a feeling that foreign aid should be reduced in an attempt to bring the national budget closer to balance. The cost of the war in Vietnam has made this problem even more acute. 

We recognize that fiscal responsibility is important and necessary. However, we can not agree that foreign aid should be placed so low in the scale of national priorities that it must bear a substantial share of budget cuts. The fight against hunger and poverty in the world is so crucial that it should be ranked equally with the most urgent domestic programs.

Our Christian concern will not permit us to play off need abroad against need at home. Nor do we feel that the resources of the United States are so limited 91--392-68—pt. 2—13

that we cannot meet need both at home and abroad. We ask our government to be responsive to need wherever it exists and to marshal the resources for a vigorous attack on the root causes of need at home and abroad. 

5. NEED TO STRENGTHEN THE FOREIGN AID PROGRAM POSTBI SM102 101 

At the beginning of the present decade, the late President Kennedy named it hopefully the "Decade of Development." Instead, it is in danger of becoming the decade of disappointment. In spite of warnings by economic experts that the development needs of the nations of Asia, Africa, and Latin America are not being met, the proportion of the gross national product of the United States devoted to foreign economic aid has steadily shrunk. 

Rising expectations, when they are frustrated, can become dangerous. Today, the developing world is no longer content with inadequate diets, illiteracy, disease, and lack of opportunity. There is a growing clamor for rapid change, but the funds that could make it possible are dwindling. 

Two of the major tools of foreign aid are development loans and technical assistance. Both of these benefit the United States as well as the recipient nations. 

The loans are spent mainly in this country to purchase American goods, products, and services. Technical assistance relies heavily on American universities, labor unions, cooperatives, and financial institutions for providing the people and the expertise.

On the one hand, there is continuing and growing need for technical and financial aid in the developing countries. On the other, the United States has the human and economic potential to provide that aid, and to receive incidental benefits in the process. Both national interest and human compassion call for strengthening rather than weakening the U.S. foreign aid program. 

6. FAMILY PLANNING AND FOOD PRODUCTION 

The growing imbalance between expanding population and food supply underscores the importance of encouraging the exploration of methods of population control on the one hand and of increased agricultural production on the other. 

We welcome the policy decision made last year in the Agency for International 

Development to give these two emphases the highest priority.

Imaginative programs of family planning are under way in many countries with the assistance of the U.S. foreign aid program, and agricultural innovations are occurring with increasing frequency in countries where famine is a chronic threat. It is of the utmost importance that our government provide the financial support that will enable these effective programs to continue and to expand. The next few years will be crucial in the world-wide battle against the twin specters of overpopulation and famine. 

7. MULTILATERAL APPROACH 

Real progress has been made in securing the cooperation of other industrialized nations in providing help for the developing countries. The United States now ranks fifth in terms of the percentage of gross national product devoted to official foreign aid. Under the International Grains Agreement, other nations are contributing food and money to the world-wide war on hunger. 

This healthy trend toward international cooperation in the field of economic development strengthens the case for continued U.S. support of a program of world development.

CONCLUSION 

On behalf of the Lutheran Council in the United States of America, I urge you to act responsively and responsibly in meeting world needs and to that end to authorize the necessary funds for the continuance and expansion of the U.S. foreign aid program. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Well, I thank you very much, Doctor. You may be assured that the committee will give the most careful attention to your testimony.

You know, there is one thing that to me has been rather noticeable throughout the years that I have been working with this program.

There is a great deal of opposition to the foreign aid measure throughout the country; but somehow Congress always ends up approving a good foreign aid program. 

I think it is a recognition on the part of the public as a whole of some responsibility to help people in other parts of the world. 

Mr. VAN DEUSEN. We have felt that. We also recognize that it is incumbent upon the churches not only to speak to Congress but to speak to its own people and to get them understanding the reasons behind the foreign aid program and to get their support, too. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Yes. Thank you very much. 

Now, is Mr. Coleman Andrews here?

The other witnesses on the list filed their statements instead of testifying. (The statements referred to, as well as others submitted prior to the hearing, appear in the appendix.)

Senator SPARKMAN. So this brings the hearing to an end. I express my appreciation to all, and adjourn the committee subject to the call of the chairman. Thank you very much. (Whereupon, at 12:30 p.m., the committee adjourned, subject to the call of the chairman.)
