FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1968 FRIDAY, MAY 17, 1968 UNITED STATES SENATE, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN RELATIONS, 

Washington, D.C.

The committee met, pursuant to notice, at 10:10 a.m., in room 318, Old Senate Office Building, Senator John Sparkman, presiding. 

Present: Senators Sparkman, Gore, Symington, Pell, Hickenlooper, and Carlson. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Let the committee come to order, please.

Mr. Secretary, I was delaying the hearing briefly, hoping that we might have minority representation, but I understand a couple of Senators are on their way, so I think we had better get started. 

Let me say at the beginning, that I regret very much that the chairman of our committee was not able to get back for the hearings. He fully intended to, but due to circumstances beyond his control, he is not able to be here this morning.

The Committee on Foreign Relations this morning continues its hearings on S. 3091, the administration's foreign aid proposal. 

We are pleased to have as the principal witness today the Honorable 

Clark Clifford, Secretary of Defense. This is the Secretary's first ap pearance before this committee since he assumed office. On behalf of the committee, Mr. Secretary, I welcome you and expressly hope that your appearance here today and on subsequent occasions will be mutually beneficial. 

The military portion of S. 3091 contains a request for grant military assistance only. A separate administration proposal, S. 3092, would authorize a military sales program. Technically, this bill is not before us today, but because of the interest members of the committee took in the sales program last year, I am sure that questions will be raised about all aspects of our military assistance policy, present and proposed.

Mr, Secretary, we have copies of your prepared statement. The statement will be printed in full in the record. You may proceed with it as you see fit. We are glad to have you here. (427) 97

STATEMENT OF HON. CLARK M. CLIFFORD, SECRETARY OF DE- FENSE, ACCOMPANIED BY HON. PAUL C. WARNKE, ASSISTANT SECRETARY OF DEFENSE FOR INTERNATIONAL SECURITY AFFAIRS; JACK L. STEMPLER, ASSISTANT TO THE SECRETARY OF DEFENSE FOR LEGISLATIVE AFFAIRS; AND VICE ADM. L. C. 

HEINZ, U.S. NAVY, DIRECTOR OF MILITARY ASSISTANCE, OFFICE OF THE ASSISTANT SECRETARY OF DEFENSE FOR INTERNA- TIONAL SECURITY AFFAIRS Secretary CLIFFORD. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

I appreciate the courtesy of the committee. My statement is a short one and I believe there is advantage in my reading it, and reading it quickly.

Senator SPARKMAN. Very well. 

Secretary CLIFFORD. I welcome this opportunity to discuss with the committee two aspects of our Defense structure with importance far beyond their relatively small dollar cost-our military assistance and foreign military sales programs. These twin instruments of national policy complement each other in joint support of U.S. foreign policy objectives; they do so by supplementing other, more conventional forms of diplomacy and by substituting for what in many cases might otherwise be a vastly more expensive direct American military presence.

ROLE OF MILITARY ASSISTANCE 

For more than two decades, the military assistance program has been a vital part of the continuing active participation by the United States in an international system of collective defense against those who would make violence, and threats of violence, the substitutes for consensus and for mutual toleration. 

During the past decade, the scale of our grant assistance has decreased as the economic capabilities of our friends and allies have grown. While this trend will continue, the program cannot be phased down so rapidly as to deny them the support they still need if they are-as both we and they desire-to carry an increasing share of the security effort. In addition, drastic curtailment of U.S. military assistance might be misinterpreted by some as a general retreat from our commitment to the maintenance of an effective system of collective security. 

There is no way to determine precisely what any nation's fair share of the burden of collective defense should be. For nearly two decades of war and uneasy peace, the United States, because of its economic, industrial, and technological preeminence, has carried a large share of that burden, not only through the support of its defense establishment but also by providing weapons, equipment, other material and training for the forces of our allies. 

CHANGE IN CHARACTER OF MILITARY AID 

Over this span, the character of our contribution has changed significantly, and I am certain it can be expected to change still further in the years ahead. Grant material assistance, though still required in a number of situations, has for some time been declining in relative

importance in many countries. The sale of U.S. military equipment and services, in contrast, has grown along with our allies' increasing ability to provide for their defense needs from their own resources. 

We can prevent both a crisis in allied confidence and a degrading of our common defense posture by insuring continuation of an effective, but austere, military assistance program. The issue is a clear one, and the cost is moderate compared to the vastly greater resources-in both manpower and money-we would have to expend to preserve an international environment hospitable to free institutions and free choice without the contribution and cooperation of our allies. 

Clearly, military assistance makes an irreplaceable contribution toward reaching these goals. But I also recognize the importance to these goals of many other of our programs, both foreign and domestic, and of their equally valid claims on total U.S. resources. 

It is with full consideration of these competing requirements, and of the extraordinary demands made upon us both by the war in Southeast Asia and by the urgent need for coping with our increasingly critical problems at home, that we are requesting this year a minimal amount of new obligational authority. This amount reflects not only a paring down of ongoing programs, but the continuing process of eliminating materiel programs where countries no longer require such assistance.

FISCAL YEAR 1969 MILITARY ASSISTANCE PROGRAM 

The result is a program involving $420 million in new obligational authority for fiscal year 1969. Because, as you know, the administration has presented to the Congress a separate legislative request covering governmental military sales, the military assistance program itself is limited to grant aid. 

Authorization and appropriation of the $420 million requested for grant aid will provide us with the $500 million in total obligational authority upon which the proposed program is based. The difference between the two amounts represents $80 million in anticipated recoupments, reimbursements, and reappropriation of prior year funds. 

The manner in which we plan to allocate the minimal funds requested reflects careful consideration of national security and foreign policy interests with respect to each area and country, as well as relative priorities among competing objectives. I am satisfied that our proposals are fully consistent with the best use of available assets in support of our most basic interests and highest priority objectives. 

First, then, I would invite your attention to the fact that the lion's share of fiscal year 1969 military assistance program-$365 million out of $500 million-is going to five countries-Korea, Turkey, Greece, Iran, and the Republic of China-whose geographical location places them in a position of exposure to direct threat. 

Allocation to them of this high proportion is entirely consistent with the sense of the Congress, as reflected by a new paragraph added last year to the statement of policy at the beginning of part II of the 

Foreign Assistance Act. This provision states that "priority shall be given to the needs of those countries in danger of becoming victims of active Communist or Communist-supported aggression or those countries in which the internal security is threatened by Communistinspired or Communist-supported internal subversion."

PRIMARY ROLE OF MILITARY ASSISTANCE 

The primary role of military assistance has been-and continues to be to supply arms, equipment and training to fill the gap between what our allies need for deterrence and initial defense and what their economies can provide. The concentration of our military assistance investment in these few countries has produced very substantial dividends. It has helped to create and maintain a common defense capability which has been, and remains today, a major factor in deterring aggression and subversion. 

It enables them to maintain a position of strength from which they can effectively resist pressures from their more powerful Communist neighbors. It brings stability to regions of potential conflict by eliminating the possibility of successful external military adventure. And over the years, it has given the nations which we have supported the security and confidence to press forward with the development of their economies in peace and safety.

The existence of this substantial military strength in areas of strategic importance and potential vulnerability is an asset of great value to the common defense. The military assistance program is therefore an indispensable link between the self help efforts of our partners and our own self-interest in insuring that they have the means increasingly to do their part of the common task. 

So far as military assistance is concerned, indigenous effort has always been a criterion carefully applied to the evaluation of individual country and area capabilities and requirements. The programing of military assistance has not been directed toward doing for a country what that country could do for itself. Instead, it has been designed to stimulate development and use of the country's own resources, to provide equipment and training essential to full realization of the recipient's own potential for self defense, and to create a climate of confidence and cooperation conducive to the kind of reciprocity that collective security demands. Such reciprocity also enables the United 

States to count on continuing access to necessary overseas installations. 

INTERNAL SECURITY AND SELF DEFENSE CAPABILITIES 

A major part of military assistance is directed toward strengthening the internal security and self defense capabilities of developing nations. The specific purpose of such programs is to help equip and train local military forces so that they can play their proper role in maintaining a framework of stability as a precondition to political, economic and social progress in the face of externally fostered pressures threatening their societies.

Although the basic problems of "underdeveloped" nations call for solutions that involve much more than military measures, this assistance serves a useful purpose by helping to maintain minimum essential capabilities for dealing with insurgencies and maintaining internal security.

The training element of programs for developing nations, particularly when it is offered in the United States, serves as one of the more effective instruments of our foreign policy because of its impact on young officers, whose attitudes can be a significant factor in their nations' future courses of action.

Our budget request also makes provision for the U.S.

share of sup port of International Military Headquarters and for nonregional costs, including administrative expenses.

The military assistance program has but one purpose and one justification, which is to make the attainment of U.S. security and foreign policy objectives easier and more certain wherever this program can best serve as an instrument of the national interest. I believe that the proposed fiscal year 1969 military assistance program which we are presenting today measures up fully to that fundamental criterion. 

FOREIGN MILITARY SALES PROGRAM 

Finally, I would like to speak with you briefly about foreign military sales-the complementary program which, along with military assistance, makes a valuable contribution to our national security. 

The United States has been authorized to make foreign military sales since the Mutual Defense Assistance Act of 1949. You will recall that some Members of the Congress last year expressed considerable concern about U.S. military sales. It was thought desirable, therefore, to clarify the goals, authorizations, restraints and controls governing the administration of this program. Accordingly, the administration has proposed a separate "Foreign Military Sales Act."

Under the act, all the restraints and controls currently in the Foreign Assistance Act will be retained. Certain new ones, however, have been added. I would like to list the more important new requirements for you.

The "revolving fund," with which repayments from countries have been used to finance new credits, will be abandoned on June 30 of this year.

All funds for future credit sales will come from new obligational authority provided annually by the Congress. 

The Export-Import Bank will confine its financing of military credit sales to the developed countries. 

The Department of Defense guarantee authority will be limited to transactions with private banks and will no longer be used in connection with Export-Import Bank financing. 

There are important new requirements for semiannual and annual reports to the Congress on past credits and forecasts of future credits to less developed countries. 

This new legislation sets a limit of $296 million on governmental credit sales during fiscal year 1969. We believe that a program of this modest size will still enable us to continue to shift from grant aid to sales for those countries that are reaching economic self-sufficiency. 

As in the case of grant aid, moreover, the sales program will be carried out in a fashion completely consistent with our foreign policy objectives and under the direction and control of the Department of State. 

DEFENSE INTERESTS SERVED 

In summary, these two programs serve important defense interests of the United States. I urge this committee give them its full support. 

Mr. Chairman, I have brought with me today to assist me, Mr. Paul 

Warnke of the Office of International Security Affairs of the Defense

Department. He is an Assistant Secretary of Defense. He sits on my right.

I have also brought with me Admiral Heinz, who has been in this program for a number of years and knows many of the details. 

The program is exceedingly far-reaching and widespread. I have been in it for some time. There will be phases of it that I know I will wish to turn to my associates to assist the committee in getting the information they desire. 

That concludes my statement, Mr. Chairman. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Thank you, Mr. Secretary. We welcome the associates whom you have named, and we will be glad for them to participate at any time.

My questioning will be very brief, and then I will call on my colleague at the table to question you. 

FISCAL YEAR 1968 GRANTS AND SALES APPROPRIATION 

You state that the present bill that we have under consideration would provide $420 million for grants, and that the second bill that you discuss will provide for $296 million in sales. That makes a total of $716 million for your two programs. 

How much was appropriated for the two programs during the present fiscal year? 

Secretary CLIFFORD. I will ask Mr. Warnke to answer that. 

Mr. WARNKE. Well, Senator Sparkman, the figures are not quite comparable because of the change we have proposed insofar as foreign military sales are concerned.

In order to arrive at a comparable figure, you would have to include the amount that was in the President's Budget for export-import financing of foreign military credit sales last year. The final fiscal year 

1968 appropriation was for just grant aid. 

Senator SPARKMAN. May I stop you right there? 

Mr. WARNKE. Yes, sir. 

Senator SPARKMAN. I don't mean what was in the President's budget. I mean what Congress appropriated.

Mr. WARNKE. On the grant aid side, it was $400 million as compared to the $420 million, requested for fiscal year 1969. 

The $296 million that we are requesting this year for foreign military sales would replace a portion that was appropriated last year for credit sales, and would also replace the amount that was used out of the revolving fund and also out of the Export-Import Bank appropriation. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Is not the revolving fund to be used for this fiscal year?

Mr. WARNKE. No; it would not be, sir. It would terminate on June 

30, 1968. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Not to be used at all? 

Mr. WARNKE. That is right, sir.

Senator SPARKMAN. Of course, this is a problem with which we have had to wrestle year in and year out for a good many years, concerning the whole foreign aid program, and particularly the phase of it with which we are dealing today, the military assistance program.

EFFECT OF LAST YEAR'S CUTS IN MILITARY AID 

I want to ask you this question: every year we are warned of dire consequences that may result from our not voting the full military aid request. Last year, for instance, the Congress cut back rather drastically on military aid and on the sales program. In fact, I believe that the appropriation Congress finally made last year was some 35 percent less than what was requested.

Now, do you believe the United States suffered any during the present fiscal year by reason of the cut that Congress made? 

Secretary CLIFFORD. I might give an answer to that, Senator, we will see if Mr. Warnke has something to add. and It is my belief that with the military assistance program at this very low level, it performs an exceedingly useful function. 

You will recall, going back a good many years-maybe as many as 20 or 15-that this program has been very much higher. I can recall it being as high as two and a half to three, to three and a half billion dollars.

During the course of the years, it has been cut, and cut substantially and steadily, until now it has come from that high figure down to some $420 million, as we have gone over it, we feel that this year that $420 million will perform an exceedingly useful function. 

It is my own view that, in view of the circumstances that exist in the world today, we would have been able to do more last year had the cut not been put into effect. That happens to be my opinion. Others might differ.

I believe, as you look at the world situation today, with the difficulties that we face in the Far East, and the difficulties that confront us in the Middle East and in East Europe, that we have a need for this type of assistance to our allies. 

One reason, as has been said, that this program has been diminished substantially is that through other help we have given, particularly to our European allies, they are infinitely better able to look after themselves than they used to be. That is one reason we have been cutting down on aid.

I might make one other comment, there is a ferment existing today in Eastern Europe. We note the developments in Czechoslovakia; we have already noted developments in Yugoslavia, we get reports from Romania and Bulgaria and Hungary and East Germany. There is this ferment going on, and it seems to me at this particular time we should be very careful not to cut back this minimum type of program because it can perform an exceedingly useful function while this important development is taking place, certainly in that part of the world. 

LIMITATION ON FURNISHING OF ARMS SUPPLIES 

Senator SPARKMAN. One problem that we have had to deal with each year has been this question of selling arms or furnishing arms to new nations, many of which were really not able to afford the arms, and many of which we felt probably did not need the arms. 

Do you think it might be desirable to try to work out some type of arrangement with the Soviet Union and other arms suppliers among

the Western nations, to get some kind of a limitation on the furnishing of arms or military supplies to those countries? It may be that something is being done along that line and, if so, we would be glad to hear from you. 0951 Secretary CLIFFORD. I believe there are two areas involved. As the 

Senator knows, this country for some time has been urging on the Soviet Union that we start a series of conferences with reference to the opposing forces that exist in Europe and East Europe. 

On one side of the line, we have the NATO forces. On the other side, we have the forces of the Warsaw Pact countries. 

We have been urging the Soviets to consider a reduction in those forces. The time has come when there could be an exceedingly intelligent mutual reduction. That is one type of problem.

The other is the one that you referred to in which the new and underdeveloped countries desire defense equipment. It is a difficult problem, and one in which we hope that we would not have to take any part. But it is my view that the world, in the posture in which it finds itself today, dictates certain actions upon our part. 

If we are to maintain the type of relationship we want with some of these newer countries, we can do so many times through the use of military aid and in some instances through military sales. It brings us into a relationship with them that has proved valuable, particularly, as I have mentioned, when they send their younger men over here for training. We also have advisers and technicians overseas, so it results in a type of intercourse between the two countries which has proved to be valuable.

In those instances where both we and the Soviet Union have been involved, there has been a continuing dialog between our Ambassador in Moscow and the Soviet authorities on attempting to work out some kind of arrangement so that this competition which exists in the world might be minimized. might be minimize

I would say to you that we have made efforts in that regard. I would also have to report that up to this point they have been unproductive. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Thank you very much, Mr. Secretary. I will yield at this time.

Senator Gore. 

Senator GORE. Mr. Secretary, I think this is the first opportunity

I have had to express my very great satisfaction and enthusiasm at your appointment. That does not call for any comment on your part. 

I just express my own satisfaction.

Secretary CLIFFORD. Thank you, Senator. 

Senator GORE. I do have a few questions.

PURPOSES OF OUR MILITARY ASSISTANCE 

In your statement you say that:

Although the basic problems of "underdeveloped" countries call for solutions that involve more than military measures, this assistance serves a useful purpose by helping to maintain minimum essential capabilities for dealing with the insurgencies and maintaining internal security. 

In January I spent 32 weeks in South America, and in several instances I found requests on the part of countries in South America

which, if complied with, would have gone beyond the statement which I just read from you as descriptive of our program of aid to underdeveloped countries.

I do not raise any criticism of those countries, but I found in some instances that our own military attachés and ambassadors have recommended to Washington and urged upon the Government of our own country approval of requests that went beyond this description which you have given.

I cite, for instance, in Peru, as I understand it, our military assistance there is justified exclusively on the basis of helping that country detect and deal with insurgency and internal security. So far as our 

Government is concerned, so far as I know, there is no thought of Peru being endangered by aggression from any other country. There is concern about Chile, I do not know why, but there seems to be. 

I do not know whether the Department of Defense has administrative control of our military attachés or whether the State Department has. So it may be that this question is not properly addressed to you. 

But if the Defense Department has jurisdiction, I would like to have you express your views on it.

Secretary CLIFFORD. My views would be, of necessity, general in nature, Senator.

I think that the administration of this phase of the program has to be conducted with great care. I do not understand the meaning of those words to be that we shall perpetuate someone in office in a Latin American country against the will of the people. 

As you know, there have been efforts made by Cuba throughout Latin and South America to cause disturbances, and attempts to develop Communist cells and difficulties in those countries. 

We have made grants to those countries where we feel that there has been a solid and substantial government, to assist them in keeping a democratic form of government rather than a dictatorial form of government.

If there are instances in which military attachés apparently have gone beyond the scope of their function, we would certainly want to know it.

We do have a responsibility in that regard, together with the State Department, and it may be wise, if at your convenience, we might sit down so that I could get more details that, perhaps, you would not want to comment on in an open session.

PERU'S REQUEST FOR JET FIGHTERS 

Senator GORE. There may be justification for a military attaché to make recommendations with respect to a country which are contrary to the policies of the Government which he serves. I do not wish to be critical in that regard because I do not know just where the line can be drawn between the exercise of one's function in effectuating Government policy and the making of recommendations for a change in Government policy. I would be glad to discuss this with you in a more private way.

To be specific with respect to the issue there, as I understood it, our representatives in Peru had recommended that the United States sell jet fighter planes to Peru or grant them through the aid program,

when the sole threat to internal security in Peru seems to be entirely from guerrilla forces in some numbers in the mountains of Peru. 

The U.S. military authorities there conceded to me that a fast jet fighter plane was utterly unfitted to detect a guerrilla in the mountains. 

If they should happen to spot one, they would be over Brazil before they could turn around. Yet our military representatives there had recommended that the United States supply jet fighter planes to Peru. 

As I understand, the reason was that unless the United States did supply them, Peru was going to buy them from France or some other country.

As I understand it, that purchase has been made and economic aid has been suspended to Peru as a result. 

This brings up the whole question of just how far do we go in varying from a determined policy as to the needs of a country to maintain its internal security or to prevent their getting equipment from France or Great Britain or Germany or Russia. 

We have this problem not only in Latin America but in Pakistan and other countries.

I am not trying to burden you with an insoluble situation, but it is an important one on which I would like to have your views. 

Secretary CLIFFORD. It is extremely complex. 

In our relationship with Peru, the air equipment that they had was becoming obsolescent. We knew some years ago that they were considering the purchase of some very sophisticated new equipment. Last year they indicated a desire for the French Mirage plane after we informed them we would not authorize delivery before 1969 or 1970 of a new U.S. plane.

We have in this country, as you know, a plane that is relatively unsophisticated, much cheaper, and serves quite a useful purpose. It is our F-5.

It was felt to be the wise course of action to discuss with the Peruvians possible commercial sale to them of F-5s. It would replace their obsolescent equipment. They obviously were going into a more modern plane. The F-5 is a rather unsophisticated plane, and it would serve their purpose well.

Senator GORE. Perhaps better for internal security than the sophisticated one. 

Secretary CLIFFORD. Much better, we felt.

A recommendation did come in, and there were discussions between this Government and Peru with reference to the commercial sale to them of some F-5s.

They decided, however, that that was not a modern enough machine for them, nor available soon enough. They wanted something much more sophisticated, so they apparently started to purchase the French plane.

Now, this very likely will bring into focus the Conte-Long amendment and the Symington amendment, so that this Government will have to face up to that question.

It is a variable problem. If you know that a country is going to go ahead anyway and purchase very expensive and very sophisticated planes, it is, perhaps, a better course of action just to say, "Well, we will have nothing to do with you." Or perhaps it was more advisable in this instance to say, "We have a very good plane. It is not as modern as this other, but it is much better suited for your uses; and we, therefore, would urge you to take our plane."

In this instance, it did not prove to be successful. My reaction is that it was an advisable course of action on our part to offer the F-5 to Peru.

ROLE OF A MILITARY ATTACHÉ 

Senator GORE. I doubt it. The question I was really trying to get at is, just what is the duty of a military attaché, to recommend what he thinks is in the diplomatic interests of the country or what? What is his role in that area?

I take not only this case of Peru but I found in several countries a camaraderie between our military representatives and the local military. They were sympatico with each other, and I thought-maybe I was in error, but I thought-entirely too much so. I was disturbed that we were being alined with authoritarian governments and military dictatorships in far too many places, and that our own military were particularly pleased about it. 

But I will not ask you to comment in general on that.

I would like to know, though, just what is the duty of a military attaché. I am advised by the staff he is under the administrative con trol of the Department of Defense. 

Secretary CLIFFORD. It is his basic function to advise and counsel the 

Ambassador on military and defense matters. 

He also keeps the Department of Defense informed on military and defense matters in that particular country. 

His basic function really, however, is to serve as an assistant to the 

Ambassador. 

Now, I am sure that in some instances a military attaché develops too close a relationship. I think it is rare, but I am sure it exists. 

In your travels, I suppose you have also encountered some instances in which you thought that maybe our diplomatic representative established too close a relationship with the country. I know of some of those instances where I wondered after a while which country he was representing, and I think once in a while that may happen with reference to a military attaché.

We watch it. If it appears to come into existence in that manner, then we do something about it. 

Senator GORE. There is this affinity that seems to develop between our representatives to a country and military representatives of that country. It seemed to me to be particularly marked in South America as between our military attachés and the military establishments of those countries.

But, lest I trespass too far upon the time of my colleagues, I had better pass on to my other two questions. 

RELATIONSHIP OF EASTERN EUROPE FERMENT TO MILITARY AID PROGRAM 

I did not quite get the significance of your comment that the military assistance program should be extended because of the ferment in Eastern Europe. I am aware of that ferment and I think it is encouraging, but I do not know what relationship it has to our military assistance.

Secretary CLIFFORD. It would be a general one. 

If the nations of Eastern Europe at this time are beginning to feel a desire to exert greater freedom on their own part, then I believe we would do well not to rock our boat in that part of the world. 

Now, as I mentioned earlier, three of the nations that participate importantly in our military assistance program are Greece, Turkey, and Iran. I think it wise and advisable that other nations in that part of the world see a continuing relationship between this country and those countries.

I happen to believe also that it is important to us at this time to maintain a strong and close continuing relationship with our NATO allies. I believe that if we can present a united front through that part of the world, it might very well be a matter of interest and support to those in Eastern European nations who desire a greater measure of freedom.

I think if they see that the free nations of the world are working together in cooperation and unity, it gives them a greater incentive to become freer themselves. 

Senator GORE. Is there implied in this the possibility of their becoming sufficiently free so that they, too, might receive military assistance? 

Secretary CLIFFORD. I would not be in a position to comment on that, Senator, for, after all, it is the Congress of the United States in the final analysis that determines which nation shall participate in our military assistance.

NUMBERS OF NORTH VIETNAMESE TROOPS NOW IN SOUTH VIETNAM 

It is currently estimated that there are 72,000 North Vietnamese Army personnel in North Vietnam combat units located in South Vietnam. In addition it is estimated that there are 13,000-16,000 North Vietnamese filler personnel in Viet Cong combat units. Total figures then would reflect a strength of 85,00088,000 North Vietnamese Army personnel in South Vietnam.

AMOUNT OF RECENT INFILTRATION OF NORTH VIETNAMESE ARMY PERSONNEL INTO SOUTH VIETNAM 

Based on the information available to date, a total of about 30,500 North Vietnamese Army personnel have entered South Vietnam during the first three months of the year: 

Experience shows, however, that additional information which will become available over a period of several months will result in a substantial increase in the above figures.

EFFECT ON UNITED STATES OF SOVIET MILITARY AID TO UNDERDEVELOPED COUNTRIES Senator HICKENLOOPER. Mr. Secretary, have you formed any opin ion as to whether or not the United States automatically suffers a setback at any time the Soviet Union supplies arms to a neutral nation such as India, for instance? Or, in other words, by that act do we either lose face or lose position?

Secretary CLIFFORD. I believe it would be incorrect to make a generalization of that kind. I do not believe that we suffer a setback as a nation any time the Soviet Union delivers arms to some country. I believe that you have to differentiate between the various countries.

of st There are some countries in the world today to which I would be indifferent to have the Soviet Union deliver arms. It would make no difference to us.

There are others where such delivery would concern me deeply. 

So that I think we have to differentiate between countries as to whether or not it constitutes a problem. 

LOBBYING BY FOREIGN DEFENSE OFFICIALS Senator HICKENLOOPER. Would you care to comment on the propri ety of foreign defense officials lobbying the Congress of the United States for military aid? I refer specifically to the letters written by the South Korean Minister of Defense to Senators concerning the $100 million supplemental request for South Korea. That is a direct solicitation by foreign officials directed to Members of Congress which 

I refer to as a lobbying operation?

Secretary CLIFFORD. I believe it would be inadvisable, Senator, for me to give a personal opinion with reference to the actions of a high official of a foreign country in that regard. 

I believe that the Members of the Senate can attach importance or not to such inquiries. 

I believe, with reference to South Korea at this time, I perhaps might come to the defense of that particular individual, for they are in an exceedingly difficult and trying posture today; and if they seem overly energetic with reference to their efforts, I believe it is understandable.

As a quick illustraton, in 1966, the North Koreans led forays over the DMZ in South Korea that amounted to about 37 incidents. In 1967, that number increased by a factor of more than 10; and there were some 566 incidents in South Korea.

In addition to that, they put into South Korea a group of terrorists who attempted to assassinate the President of South Korea, and actually got within very close distance of what is called the Blue House there, which corresponds to our White House. 

They have been under increasing pressure from the North Koreans. 

The situation is exceedingly grave, and I use that as an excuse for what might otherwise be considered an unwarranted interference with the business of the Senate.

INCURSIONS ACROSS KOREAN DMZ BORDER Senator HICKENLOOPER. Speaking of those incursions across the border, I have read some stories-whether they are true or not, I do not know but, I think, perhaps you are the best authority-that most of these forays by the North Koreans into South Korea come through the particular sector that we are supposed to defend, and one of the reasons that they have been rather successful there is that we have given orders to be very careful not to shoot unless we are absolutely or practically overrun. The South Koreans are not quite that hesitant about defending the border.

As I say, I do not know about the truth of those reports, but they are extant.

Secretary CLIFFORD. I am not familiar with any such orders having been given. Our men who patrol our section of the DMZ in South 

Korea are well armed. They have our latest rifles, and their rifles are loaded. Their instructions are to defend themselves in any manner that they see fit, and I might say there have been some circumstances lately that show they have been doing a pretty good job of it. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Was it true that most of the incursions come through the zone that we patrol?

Secretary CLIFFORD. I am not familiar with that fact. We have had a number of incursions through there, but I would be surprised if the majority of them came through that area. I think they are rather widespread across the DMZ. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. I do not know. I am just asking about these reports that are circulating.

RELATIONSHIP BETWEEN GULF OF TONKIN INCIDENTS AND OTHERS 

Mr. Secretary, do you see any relationship between the incidents in the Gulf of Tonkin, which led to the resolution, and the incidents of the Pueblo and of the Liberty in the eastern Mediterranean? 

I may just amplify that a little so that you understand what I am getting at. Immediately upon the happening of the incidents in the 

Gulf of Tonkin, we had a resolution authorizing fairly drastic action which we have been stepping up ever since in South Vietnam. Generally speaking, it was those incidents on which it was claimed, lay the basis for our military actions in South Vietnam. Now, the Pueblo was captured, and so far as I know it is still held. 

There was a story in the paper this morning that, according to the writer, it might be close to a solution, but then that was denied. That probably was just wishful thinking on the part of the writer. 

The Pueblo was captured. So far as I know, I do not know of any deaths on the Pueblo.

But the Liberty, which was in the eastern Mediterranean, was the object of a direct attack by Israel, and 34 of our people were killed. I think well over 100 on that ship were killed and wounded, and yet you do not hear much about it. 

Regarding the Pueblo incident, everything seems to be quiescent at this time. I just wondered if there is any relationship between those incidents at all, as a matter of principle?

Secretary CLIFFORD. There would be some general relationships or similarities but, in my opinion, substantially more important dissimilarities.

Although I do not have much information about the Bay of Tonkin, I know that we had naval vessels there which were attacked by North Vietnamese naval vessels; and, as I understand it, torpedoes were launched against our naval vessels and retaliatory measures were taken. Then, as I understand it, that led to a request for a resolution from the Senate. "LIBERTY" The other two incidents, I believe, were really quite different. The 

Liberty was an intelligence ship of the United States which was in the Mediterranean at the time the war broke out between Israel and the Arab nations. The Liberty was there properly. We went into it with

great care. The Israelis apparently assumed that the Liberty was an 

Arab ship. They had been told that morning that there was an Arab ship with some of the same general configuration as the Liberty off the area where the war was taking place. 

They came in with planes and strafed it, and torpedo boats attacked it.

Fortunately, they did not sink it, but they did kill 34 American personnel. An investigation was made in depth, and we reached the independent conclusion that they attacked the Liberty in error. Arrangements are being made for proper financial and economic settlements to be made with reference to it. "PUEBLO" With reference to the Pueblo, that again involved an intelligence ship of the United States. It had some curious circumstances in it. I won't go into too much detail, but other intelligence ships had been harassed previously in East Asian waters.

On this one day, without any warning, North Korean ships, suddenly decided to board the Pueblo; and they did. 

They took it and the 83 members of the crew into Wonsan harbor in North Korea.

A policy decision had to be made by the administration. It was whether to go in with an attacking force and attempt to recover the Pueblo and its crew or attempt to do it through diplomatic measures. 

The decision was made to follow the latter course. 

Last week we finished the 16th meeting between the negotiators of the United States and North Korea. The report that we are close to effecting a resolution of the problem is untrue. We are not close to it. 

They have continued to be intransigent, but we are going to continue to try to work with them. 

We have been through some other situations of this kind, too, with North Korea, and in each instance, it took somewhere in the neighborhood of a year to get our men back. We have been through similar situations with the Soviets, and one time it took some 14 months to get our men back; but ultimately we did. 

One of the main reasons we did not go in there with an attacking force was that such action would not get our men back. It would pretty well assure their destruction.

So about the only similarity that might be seen is that these were naval vessels that came under attack by a foreign power. But the differences, I think, are very great.

Senator HICKENLOOPER. I am bound to say in all candor, I do not agree with you. The attack on a vessel in the Gulf of Tonkin triggered military operations in which we have over half a million ground forces now. I never have had a satisfactory explanation as to either the similarity or the dissimiliarity of the incidents. 

I realize the difficulties of the Pueblo, and so far as I am concerned, I have no quarrel with the way we are trying to handle that situation. 

I do not know that we would accomplish anything by trying to go in by force except get the men killed and the boat blown up, and maybe worse. 

I do not have any quarrel with that.

But I have read the Navy investigation of the Liberty, and the evidence adduced there, and I have read the Israeli court of inquiry records, and based upon their own records of the investigation, I cannot agree that it was accidental. 

I do not think this is the place to pursue that matter any further at the moment. 

Secretary CLIFFORD. All right. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. But it just is not compatible, in my view. 

I think I will desist, Mr. Chairman. Thank you very much.

Secretary CLIFFORD. Thank you, Senator.

U.S. PRODUCTION OF MILITARY HARDWARE 

In this connection, to try to make the complicated simple, and usually we spend so much time doing just the opposite, I am afraid, would it be a correct statement to say that as of now the United States is producing between two to three times more weapons and hardware, in dollar terms, than the rest of the world put together? This, I realize, is a broad and, shall I say, horseback figure, but I think it shows the problem that worries some of us if it is correct.

I would be astounded if it were so.

LIMITING SALES TO UNDERDEVELOPED COUNTRIES 

Has any thought been given at all in the Defense Department to the idea of some sort of United Nations' sponsored agreements among the principal arms suppliers, which would be the Soviet Union and us, and Britain and France?

Obviously, China would not be included for the time being, but some effort should be made to have some mutual understanding to limit sales or grants of arms to underdeveloped nations. I am thinking particularly in terms of conversations with the Soviet Union itself. 

Secretary CLIFFORD. I mentioned in an earlier answer that touched, perhaps slightly, on that problem. We have been attempting to get the Soviet Union into a series of discussions about the general field of arms reduction in the world. More specifically, it would have to do with a start that would involve the NATO countries on the one side, and the Warsaw Pact countries on the other. 

Here is a perfect opportunity for the nations of the world to save very important sums of their treasures. The Warsaw Pact nations maintain ready armed forces there in the magnitude of 960,000 men in central Europe and adjacent areas alone and are modernizing their arms. We, together with our NATO allies, have to maintain a similar type of force and keep them armed.

If we could start with a type of arrangement in that area with the Soviet Union, it could spread to a number of other areas. 

Now, they continue to supply other nations with arms. As you may know, since the Arab-Israel war, they have now practically replen-

ished all the arms of the Arab nations that were destroyed and lost in the Arab-Israel war. It is a continuing policy on their part. 

It would be our hope that the time would come when we could persuade them to do otherwise through arrangements with us. We would be willing to respond should they be willing.

Senator PELL. I believe last year our President called for negotiation of arms sales. Did anything happen in regard to that proposal, such as negotiations with the Soviet Union? 

Secretary CLIFFORD. Nothing other than the continuing request that is made to them by our Ambassador in Moscow to start such talks, and their disinclination to do so.

Senator PELL. I completely agree with your thought about the 

NATO and Warsaw Pact nations, and having participated in the civilian parliamentary side of those a good many times, I think the mood of the NATO parliamentarians, at least, is very much in favor of that idea. The great question you suggest is whether the opposition is in favor of it.

Secretary CLIFFORD. That is right. 

DISPOSAL OF OVERSEAS FACILITIES

It is not our intention to maintain military personnel in that part of the world and when the time comes, hopefully, that they can be withdrawn.

Now, insofar as the military equipment is concerned, I would assume that some of that equipment would remain in the hands of those persons who have been our allies, to be used there in their continuing defense. I am assuming that because I think it is so reasonable to assume it.

COMMUNIST INFILTRATION PROGRAM IN THAILAND 

Senator PELL. I realize we are putting in a good deal of money to help Thailand at this time with regard to its insurgency problems, I don't like to use that word "insurgency," so let us say its problems

with regard to infiltration. Is the situation improving or deteriorating? Are we better off now than we were a year ago in Thailand?

Secretary CLIFFORD. As far as the Communist problem within Thai land is concerned, I believe we are better off now than we were. 

Senator PELL. You mean there is less infiltrating, less military hostile activity within Thailand directed against the government today than there was a year ago?

Secretary CLIFFORD. I think not. I didn't mean that. I believe there is more insurgent activity today in Thailand than there was but I believe it is the feeling of the Thai Government that they have the situation better in hand today. They have more men. We have helped them. We have helped equip them. We have helped arm them, and we have helped train them; and they are better led than they were a year ago. So the danger is there, and the pressure is even greater from the 

Communist effort which, as you know, comes mainly from North Vietnam. As a Thai official told me some time ago, communism in that part of the world is like an octopus. One tentacle goes into Thailand, another tentacle goes into Laos, another into Cambodia, one into 

Burma, one into South Vietnam. But he said one thing you must remember is that the head of the octopus is in Hanoi, and that is where the orders are given and that is where the instructions are given and that is from where the equipment flows to the various tentacles. 

But the Thais have improved their capacity to turn back the Communist threat, and I believe that they feel they are doing a better job of it now than they were a year ago. 

Senator PELL. I see a certain contradiction between the fact that the incidents have increased but the situation is more in hand. I also see your point, and am glad that our assistance is proving of help. 

I would completely agree with your statement that the head of the Communist movement is in Hanoi and would hope we would keep that in mind.

EXTENT OF COMMUNIST THREAT IN GREECE 

I would like to turn, if I may, to the question of Greece, and then that will wind up my own areas of interest. 

In Greece, some of us have been very concerned at the continuation of any military assistance at all. As we know, the reason for the rendering of assistance there is in accordance with the NATO treaty where we discuss the principles of democracy, individual liberty, and the rule of law as being the governing factors for our participation in those treaties, and I know also in your excellent statement you mentioned "priority shall be given to the needs of those countries in danger of becoming victims of active Communist or Communist supported aggression or those countries in which the internal security is threatened by Communist inspired or Communist supported internal subversion."

Do you really believe that Greece is in danger of becoming a victim of Communist-supported aggression or is threatened by Communistsupported internal subversion at this time, sir? 

Secretary CLIFFORD. Taking the latter part first, I do not believe that Greece at the present time is threatened with internal Communist subversion. I do not believe that that is now a concern on our part nor does it constitute a basis for our rendering military assistance to 

Greece at this time. ​8

The answer to the other part of your question as to whether they are threatened by outside Communist pressure or possible subjugationis that Greece is a member of NATO, they are an ally of ours, and the fact is that they are in a rather exposed position right now. 

As you men know, there has been a very substantial increase in Soviet activity in the Mediterranean this past year, and its continues to increase. We must assume that their presence there is not a benign one. So that it brings Greece under greater pressure. 

Greece is a longtime ally of ours. Some of you will remember, of course, the action that President Truman took in March of 1947 when he enunciated what has become known as the Truman Doctrine. They continue to be a sturdy ally of ours. We do not have control over the kind of government Greece has or that any or our allies have. It is our hope that Greece will return to a constitutional form of government.

I might say there is some hope in that regard. At the meeting of defense ministers in Brussels just last week, the Greek foreign minister told me that they would have a constitution prepared to submit to the people before the end of this year. That is an encouraging sign. 

It is my feeling that we should continue to consider Greece as an ally and continue to render the kind of help we think is appropriate under the circumstances which exist in that country today, and as to an ally of NATO. 

Senator PELL. I remember a year ago the same assurance was given that before the end of that year, 1967, a constitution would be submitted.

The point that you are making, though, is a very valid one, that the threat now to military security comes more from the presence of the Soviet fleet than from invasion by or through Bulgaria. 

Secretary CLIFFORD. I think that is correct, Senator.

MILITARY THREAT TO GREECE 

Senator PELL. I have been struck on going to Bulgaria and asking them why they need such military competence as they have and such assistance from the Soviet Union. They are concerned with the possibility of a threat of invasion from Greece, and which one thinks is very unlikely.

Wouldn't that mean there should be a recasting of the military establishment in Greece if the threat or the concern is from the sea rather than from the land? 

Secretary CLIFFORD. Well, at the present moment, as you may know, we are not delivering any important military equipment to Greece, and I believe that the suggestion is a wise one, and that factor might well be taken into consideration when the time comes for reinstitution of our deliveries.

At the same time, one must accept with reservations what any other country says its intentions are. We have been through that many times, sometimes their publicized intentions are different from their real purpose, and sometimes you do not know how long a certain situation continues. But as an ally, Greece is in an exposed posture, and it is my belief we should continue both to consider Greece an ally and to render help to them.

of 

U.S. MILITARY ASSISTANCE TO GREECE 

Senator PELL. Would you be able at this time to give your own thoughts with regard to the extent of assistance to Greece? 

I noticed you mentioned five nations which will absorb the lion's share, which was, I think, $365 million, roughly, of the $500 million, Korea, Greece, Turkey, Iran, and the Republic of China. About what portion of this would be going to Greece or would this be classified? 

Secretary CLIFFORD. We have always followed the policy Senator, of not specifically mentioning in public the amount that goes to any one country.

We have mentioned in the past amounts that will go to a group of countries or to an area of the world. We will supply you with that figure in a memorandum, but it will be classified. (The information referred to is classified and in the committee files.) 

Senator PELL. Right. 

My recollection is from the press stories, and press stories are often inaccurate, but the press stories were to the effect there were about $20 million a year going there now and the full resumption would be in the order of $65 million.

You feel that the present amount, no matter what the figure is, is justified?

Secretary CIFFORD. Yes. We feel that it is justified. It was gone over with great care before it was submitted in our recommendation to the committee. 

Senator PELL. Speaking to you in your capacity as Secretary of Defense, is there an amortization rate, can you figure out what the depreciation is of military hardware? I believe it is 16 percent for machinery, 2 percent for a wooden house, 3 percent for a brick building. 

Is there any such figure as that for military hardware?

Secretary CLIFFORD. I am not conscious of any; and I doubt that there is one, because of the fact that military hardware rapidly becomes obsolete. It isn't so much the period of time that is involved; it is the question of what other weapons have come into being that make those weapons obsolete. It also depends upon the type of weapon that a potential enemy has.

Those are the factors that really determine what the replacement percentage or ratio would be.

PRESENT GOVERNMENT IN GREECE 

Senator PELL. My own strong hope would be that we would not restore any further assistance to Greece until it had actually gone down the road and restored democracy there because the promises, as you have mentioned earlier, have been repeated before. 

Would you share that view? 

Secretary CLIFFORD. I believe I would not, Senator. I believe that the obligation upon us as a member of NATO is such that I place that as a more important consideration than I do the present Government of Greece. I believe that we deal with a highly imperfect world, and if we were to confine our help to our allies on the basis of our approving completely the different types of governments that existed then, I believe that NATO would disintegrate; and I believe that would be a calamity.

Senator PELL. This, I guess, is the purpose of a public hearing, to illume the differences that exist. 

To my mind, there is a degree here. When a government has 2,500 people, political prisoners in jail, when the leaders of the opposition are in jail or house arrest, when torture has been used as a weapon, although I believe they are going easy on it now because of the publicity involved, I would disagree but this is what makes the world go around.

Secretary CLIFFORD. I believe, Senator, that we have a greater leverage and can play a more important part in helping Greece get a constitutional government which we might very well approve of, if we maintain our relationship with Greece rather than if we pull away from it. I believe that we have more influence with Greece if we treat it as an ally and continue our relationship. 

Senator PELL. I see your point. I also would like to extend the thought that Spain has a very constitutional government. You can have a constitutional government and still not have a government that reflects the will of the people, and this is the danger I see coming up in Greece. 

I thank you.

Senator SPARKMAN. Senator Carlson? 

Senator CARLSON. Mr. Secretary, it is needless for me to state that 

I was pleased with your appointment as Secretary. I am delighted you are here this morning for your first appearance before the committee.

I notice in your statement that $365 million of this $500 million will go to Korea, Turkey, Greece, Iran, and the Republic of China.

If you do not have it with you, you can submit it for the record, or if it is not classified, I would like to know the number of countries to which we give aid in addition to those mentioned and the amounts to each of them. Can we get that?

Secretary CLIFFORD. We can supply you with a memorandum that will show each country and the amount. This information has always been classified for future years, and we would wish that it remain so. 

Senator CARLSON. I understand that. (The information referred to is classified and in the committee files.) 

EXCESS ARMAMENTS AND MATERIEL 

Now, in addition, as I understand it, to the regular military grant aid program, I believe the Department of Defense gives foreign countries excess armaments and materiel from its own stocks. That value, I assume, was not included in this item. 

Secretary CLIFFORD. It is not, Senator. 

Senator CARLSON. Could you give us a figure on how much that would be? us.

Secretary CLIFFORD. I do not have it. Perhaps Mr. Warnke can help 

Mr. WARNKE. Actually, the cost of supplying excess is included as part of military assistance program, Senator.

Senator CARLSON. It is included?

Mr. WARNKE. It is not an additional amount. 

Senator CARLSON. It is included in the $500 million? 

Mr. WARNKE. That is correct, sir. 

Senator CARLSON. Do you know, Mr. Warnke, if this is the first year it has been included or has it been past policy? I thought previously we had had other items for that. Maybe I am in error. 

Mr. WARNKE. There are certain items which are furnished out of surplus without any cost. In other words, these are items that have been written off so that there is no cost assessed against the program. 

Senator CARLSON. There is none assessed against them, but they do have some value, don't they?

Mr. WARNKE. They do have some residual value; that is correct. 

Senator CARLSON. Would it be substantial?

Mr. WARNKE. It would not be a substantial part as compared to the $420 million that we are requesting. 

Senator CARLSON. I appreciate that, but could not that be quite an item?

Mr. WARNKE. It is an item of some size, that is right, Senator. 

Senator CARLSON. The reason I mention it is because we quite often think, "Well, it is $500 million and that is all it is." 

And I have noticed in past years, and I have not checked these recently, but some of these items, large pieces of equipment that have been given other countries, were rather substantial. That is the reason 

I brought it up.

Mr. WARNKE. Yes, it does vary from year to year, Senator; and we can supply you with the current information on that as well. 

Senator CARLSON. I think it would be well to have it. 

Mr. WARNKE. Yes, sir, Senator. 

Senator CARLSON. Because it is part of our military aid indirectly even if not through the regular appropriations or authorizations. 

Mr. WARNKE. That is correct, Senator. Just as the ship loans are also a form of military assistance. 

Senator CARLSON. That is right. ded 

Secretary CLIFFORD. I think you are right in that regard, and I would like the record to show that I thought my answer was correct, too. We do write off equipment from time to time so that it has no value on our books. After being written off, that equipment sometimes is delivered to foreign nations, and it obviously, as you suggest, does have value at the time. The acquisition cost of that type of equipment is not represented in this figure that we have presented to the Congress. 

Senator CARLSON. I appreciate that answer, because I know that was the situation in previous years but I wasn't sure whether it was this time.

Mr. WARNKE. The only figure, Senator, which would be included would be the rehabilitation and repair charges. 

Senator CARLSON. I see. 

Mr. WARNKE. That would be included in this figure.

MILITARY CONTRIBUTION TO VIETNAM PACIFICATION PROGRAM 

Senator CARLSON. Now, in this $500 million and any other funds regarding military assistance, there is none listed for Vietnam. How much is the Department of Defense spending this year on pacification programs and other, what I would call, civilian oriented programs in Vietnam?

Secretary CLIFFORD. Let us see if we have a note that breaks it down to that extent.

We have a note on the pacification program, but it is not broken down as to costs. I don't remember seeing such a memorandum. If there isn't one, we will prepare one and get it up to you. 

As of now, we do not have knowledge of the annual cost, I don't know what portion of it is attributable to the pacification program. I am not sure it has ever been broken down and I am not sure it can be. 

Senator CARLSON. I visited Vietnam last July and went into it to some extent. It is true, is it not, that the pacification program is regularly handled by the military? 

Secretary CLIFFORD. Yes, that is correct.

Senator CARLSON. The military not only furnishes personnel but contributes some money, I would think, and equipment and material. 

That is the figure I would like to have, if you can get it. 

Secretary CLIFFORD. I am sure that we make a contribution to it. 

I know, for instance, that the regional forces and the popular forces that play some part in the whole pacification program are provided

with rifles and other weapons by us, and the Defense Department pays for those.

I don't know whether there has ever been a breakdown, but we will find out and furnish you with what we have. 

Senator CARLSON. My point was just this: Of course we spend hundreds of millions of dollars in the military operation and I was just wondering how much could be or would be charged for what we would call military assistance. That was my only thought in this program. because you do have charge of the pacification program. If you can get something on that for the record I would be pleased to have it. 

Secretary CLIFFORD. We shall certainly try. 

(The information referred to follows. The classified portion is in the committee files.)

U.S. SUPPORT FOR PACIFICATION Department of Defense spending from 30 June 1966 to 31 December 1967 on pacification programs and civilian oriented programs in Vietnam are identified in Table 1. This table indicates the number of personnel and costs for overall support of pacification to include provision of territorial security. Military contributions are presented on the basis of the overall pacification mission. Costs for the 

National Police, Chieu Hoi, refugee, anti-infrastructure, military civic action and certain psychological operation programs are also a part of pacification. Moreover, many educational, agricultural, public health and public administration programs in the provinces contribute directly to the pacification program. 

The number of U.S. personnel of all agencies at district, province, corps tactical zone and Saigon levels who advise the GVN in the development of, implementation of, direct logistical support for and provision of security for pacification and the cost of their services are presented in Table 1.
