U.S. MILITARY OPERATIONS OVERSEAS 

Senator CARLSON. I was interested in your response to Senator Pell,

I believe, that we had no intention of maintaining military troops or bases or what is included in Vietnam when we return to the United 

States and I hope and pray that is right.

But thinking of past history, it was about 25 years ago that we concluded the war in World War II and we still have about a quarter of a million men in NATO. Fifteen years ago we finished the war in 

Korea and I think we have 50,000 or more men there and quite an installation. In my opinion, it may not be correct, but I believe a Member of the Senate made a statement the other day that we had 134 military bases or installations or operations on every continent on the globe.

Do you know of any that we have closed up with the exception of when we were told to leave France? Have we closed any of them? 

Secretary CLIFFORD. I do not know the specific answer to that question. I assume that there have probably been changes and shifts and transfers, and, I suppose, some of those bases that we have throughout the world are closed, while sometimes new ones are opened. I have the same concern about the future that you do insofar as the reflection that the past has on the futu I am very conscious of the fact that the

war was over 23 years ago in Europe and we still have a very substantial number of men over there. The same is true in Korea. 

The justification for this, as I understand it, is that in the 23 years since the war ended in Europe, we have not had another war in Europe. The contribution that we have made to the strength of the 

European nations and our allies, and the deterrent value of our presence there, Senator, has been very substantial; and, as you may know, we have a very substantial number of nuclear weapons in Europe. 

That, too, is a deterrent. But as time goes on, perhaps more attention is drawn to the possibility that any attack would be of a conventional nature rather than nuclear; and, therefore, we feel, that this constitutes insurance.

Now, I might say that again at the meeting of the defense ministers in Brussels last week, I advised them that while I cannot speak for future administrations they could not expect the United States to maintain indefinitely the level of troops in Europe we now have, that they must begin to face the realities of life, and that the time would come when we would begin a withdrawal of those troops. 

As you may know, at the present time we are bringing 34,000 of them back, but they are to be ticketed for use in NATO. 

As to Korea, I believe that the presence of our troops there has performed a very useful function. I would have hoped that they could have been brought back long before this because the war ended in Korea in 1953, and they have been there 15 years. But here, again, they have served a very useful purpose. There has not been another war in Korea.

When you estimate the cost of these programs compared to what the Second World War cost us, then I think it helps put them in a different light. I am sometimes impressed by the fact that the comment is made that in South Vietnam today we have lost-and I regret every one of them-23,000 men, and our cost is maybe $25 billion a year. The other day, I looked up, just for my personal reasons, the cost of World War II. We lost approximately 300,000 men in World War II, and the cost to us is in the neighborhood of over $400 billion-and that is in 1940 dollars, which I guess today would be close to a trillion dollars. 

So that I think, as you look at the investments we make in NATO and in Korea, this puts them in better perspective. We have gotten a dollar's value, I think, for every dollar we have put in by reason of keeping peace in the world since that time. 

BRINGING TROOPS BACK HOME 

Senator CARLSON. Mr. Secretary, I was not going to argue as to whether we should or should not be in NATO or in Korea. But I believe you have made the argument right now that we are going to be in Vietnam for years to come, and it hurts me a little bit to have a statement made in high places that our boys are coming home. I hope they will, but frankly, I don't have much faith in it and I don't believe the American people have. After all, we have invested billions of dollars over there in docking facilities, landing fields. In fact we have rebuilt the country. I think everyone would agree that if we do not maintain our position there that we should drop it for something else. It hurts me when we talk to the American people that boys are coming home, www

and nobody wants it more than I do, but I think we had best be frank for what looks to me as a very impractical situation based on the past. 

You say we haven't had another war in Europe, true. We didn't have any war in Korea and we probably won't have any in Vietnam if we stay there after we conclude this situation. I hate to bring it up, but I thought I ought to bring it up in view of your answer to 

Senator Pell. 

Secretary CLIFFORD. I believe it to be a relevant question, and I think it is one that the American people should be considering. I believe the time will come when we bring our present troops back from Vietnam, and I agree with you the quicker that day comes the happier we will be. I believe we will not bring them back in such a way as to leave a vacuum. I believe that we will gradually diminish the number of troops that we have there. 

It is our hope that we can begin to diminish the number of our combat troops so that the South Vietnamese can begin to take a greater share of the burden there. I would think our Air Force personnel would probably be there longer than our ground personnel. I think it is reasonable to assume, as you do, that we will probably have troops there until a situation exists, as contemplated by the Manila signatories, in which peace has been brought to the area and there is no need for our retaining troops there any longer.

INCREASING MILITARY EXPENDITURES 

Senator CARLSON. I just have one more thing, and I bring it up just this morning because of what seems to be an ever-increasing expenditure, military expenditure, worldwide, year after year. It keeps growing while the economic aid to all countries seems to be going backward.

Here is a research report, dated December 1967, issued by the U.S. Arms Control and Disarmament Agency. I just want to read for the record-three or four random paragraphs:

Worldwide preliminary data for 1966 indicate an accelerated rise in military spending to a new alltime high figure of $155 to $160 billion. Expressed in terms of some of the comparatives shown in the report, the indicated increase of $15 to $20 billion in the level of military expenditures in 1966 is more than twice the current annual total of foreign economic assistance extended by all countries to the less developed areas of the world, and is at least equivalent to a year's expenditures by less developed countries for public education and public health programs for a population of more than 2 billion. 

Military spending by the less developed countries, which have an average per capita GNP of less than $160 per year, reached a new high of $18 billion in 1965. 

The estimated increase of $2 billion in 1 year again indicated that the trend of defense expenditures among the poorer nations was rising at a faster rate than the world average.

In 1965 net official economic aid to developing countries was approximately $7 billion. This compares with their military outlays of $18 billion. 

I wanted to submit that for the record because it seems to me we are in a period where military expenditures, and I am not only speaking of our own Nation, but in the world, are going up. There is less spent for economic needs. In my opinion, we ought to try to reverse that trend, if we can. 

Thank you very much. 91-392-68-pt. 2- -15

Secretary CLIFFORD. Thank you, Senator. That, I am sure, would be the hope of all of us. We understand that it is a very imperfect world, but the ultimate answer under present circumstances lies in an agreement that could bring a new hope to the world. If the Soviet Union would choose at some time to agree with the United States to start a calculated series of disarmament moves, the whole picture could be changed. 

We have indicated time and again our desire to do so. I believe they pretty well hold the key. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Do you believe there is a hopeful indication that we may be making some progress? 

Secretary CLIFFORD. I believe we have to continue to be hopeful, Senator. There have been some straws in the wind that would indicate that there might ultimately be a détente. The agreement between the 

Soviet Union and the United States on the nonproliferation treaty was a definite step forward. We spent years at it and finally were able to reach agreement with the Soviet Union. There are some other areas. 

There is the consular treaty we had worked on for a good many years which has finally been signed. There are indications that they would like to see peace in some areas, as we do, so we hope that might bring us into a relationship which could be productive. 

So I would say that we must be hopeful, and the fact is that I personally do feel some degree of hope. 

TROOP WITHDRAWAL FROM EUROPE 

Senator SPARKMAN. Let me ask just this one question: You referred to the troop rotation or withdrawal, I suppose would be a better word, from Europe. You said there would be 34,000 men brought home.

It seems to me it was about a year ago that that policy was announced, and I had a feeling that it was to get underway before this time. 

Has there been a delay or is it underway now?

Secretary CLIFFORD. Yes, it is underway, and we are doing it gradually so that there would not be too sharp an alteration in the force level. 

My recollection is that out of the 34,000 close to 8,000, and maybe over that, have returned. They are being returned gradually, and I have some recollection that by the end of the year all of the 34,000 will have been returned. 

Senator SPARKMAN. I wonder if you can give us some idea as to just how much of an impact that move would have on our balance of payments deficit?

Secretary CLIFFORD. We would have to break it down. We have some figures on that, so we can start with them. 

During the 3 fiscal years 1965, 1966, and 1967, we incurred an average net adverse balance of $500 million per year on our defense account in Western Europe. That is less than one might suppose because our gross expenditures in Europe run about $112 billion annually, but out of that amount, approximately $1 billion a year has been offset by European purchases in the United States of military goods and services. So it leaves a net adverse balance of payments of $500 million per year.

80 Those are the last figures that I have, Senator. 

Senator SPARKMAN. We are very glad to have those. At least it gives us some idea.

Secretary CLIFFORD. Excuse me, but I find we have a further note which brings it down perhaps more specifically to the points you have in mind.

Now, as a result of taking our forces out of France, we have made substantial balance of payments savings reductions in our military expenditures in France which declined from approximately $200 million in fiscal 1965 to less than $50 million in fiscal 1968. The total was down from $200 million to $50 million, so there was a real saving. 

I have noted, also, that the operation of NATO in Brussels is not as expensive as it was in France. Furthermore, we have sold certain equipment and property to France, and that will be helpful. 

Now, there will also be a substantial balance of payments saving as a result of redeploying this group of 34,000 men and their families. 

An estimated $75 million a year will be saved by bringing those men back.

Senator SPARKMAN. Senator Gore? 

Senator GORE. Mr. Secretary, I think I concur in the policy of a gradual reduction of U.S. troops in Western Europe. I certainly concur that it would be unwise to make such a quick and sharp reduction that would have a severe psychological and political impact.

U.S. TROOPS IN SOUTH KOREA 

I am not sure I feel the same way about South Korea. South Korea is approximately twice as large as North Korea. So far as I have heard, North Korea does not have any troops other than its own. Why

South Korea, twice as large as North Korea, must continually be heavily subsidized by the United States and we must continue to have about 50,000 troops there I don't quite understand. I hope you will be examining that more carefully, Secretary CLIFFORD. I can generalize on it only. We have a treaty with South Korea, as you know. We believe it is important to the peace of East Asia that there not be another war in Korea. The armed forces of South Korea are well trained and well equipped. They are at a disadvantage, however, because of the substantially superior air. force that North Korea has.

At the time of the Pueblo incident, we did not know what else might take place in Korea. We sent some units of the Air Force out to South 

Korea. It is my present feeling that, in view of the uncertainty of affairs in East Asia, we would do well at this time not to upset the balance there. Our presence there is a deterrent to North Korea, and we have indicated to South Korea that we have a treaty with them and that we understand that we have that treaty. We believe that constitutes an important deterrent. That is the thinking that goes on in the administration today. 

Senator GORE. That thinking could be engaged in by both Russia and China. Both have treaties with North Korea, and if both sent troops into North Korea perhaps they could argue that it would be a deterrent against war from the south. I invite this to your attention.

STRENGTH OF NORTH VIETNAMESE FORCES IN THE SOUTH 

What I wanted to advert to was a matter on which I asked you one question, and the answer to which did not satisfy me. I had already used so much time I did not want to press the matter at that moment. 

I asked you about the strength of North Vietnamese forces in South Vietnam, and the degree of infiltration. You said the information had been or would be supplied to the committee, but would be secret. I don't know why it need be secret. 

I read here from the Washington Post of May 6 that the Defense 

Department, after Ambassador Ball had said that the infiltration from North Vietnam to South Vietnam had run on the order of 80,000 to 

100,000 in the last 4 months, issued the following statement:

The figure of 80,000 to 100,000 over the last 4 months is an intelligence estimate of troop movement southward toward and into South Vietnam. 

I am not sure of what "toward and into" means, because when Secretary Rusk last appeared before this committee on March 11 he gave the committee an estimate of North Vietnam combat troops in South 

Vietnam. That is on page 23 of the hearings.

This statement, inserted in the record by Secretary Rusk, shows 54,000 combat troops of North Vietnam in South Vietnam, and then there is an estimate of administrative services, et cetera, but this is at variance with this statement given by the Pentagon, according to the press. Maybe I should ask you if the Department of Defense, which you head, made such a public release?

Secretary CLIFFORD. Yes, sir. The question came up our new Ambassador to the U.N., Mr. Ball, was on a TV program, and he said on that Sunday that 60,000 to 100,000 men were on their way through the panhandle of North Vietnam into South Vietnam. The Pentagon gave out a clarification statement saying that that many men were estimated to be moving through the panhandle of North Vietnam. We cannot say that they all were going to go into South Vietnam. That was the reason for the clarification. 

Some of them might very well have dropped off en route through North Vietnam; some could very well go over into Laos; some could go over into Cambodia. We merely wanted to clarify the fact that we cannot say with any exactitude that all of those men were going down to South Vietnam.

I have no trouble from a classification or security point of view about an estimate of the men moving through that area. If I indicated that I did, then I misled you. That wasn't the part that bothered me. 

What bothers me are the open statements about the North Vietnamese who are in South Vietnam. I think there is a very real sensitivity there, because the means by which we know about those men—which units they are, which divisions, and so forth-are matters that we would wish to protect. We in the Pentagon try to keep the lid on that, if we can.

FIGURES GIVEN OUT BY DEPARTMENT OF STATE 

Senator GORE. I don't quite understand then how the Secretary of

State can give us these figures when the Secretary of Defense cannot. 

The administration's estimate of the number of North Vietnamese troops in South Vietnam has been given to the Congress repeatedly.

The highest estimate that I had ever heard given was the estimate which Secretary Rusk gave to the committee when he was last here. 

I think perhaps it was natural that as time goes on and the violence of the war increases, that the number would increase. 

Secretary CLIFFORD. I have no trouble, Senator, with the fact that 

Secretary Rusk-I think some time in March, in the middle of March gave the Senate a figure for what the estimate of North Vietnamese troops was back in January. I would have no trouble giving an estimate of what we thought the troops were in January. 

I would hope that if you want our opinion as to the troops who are there today, you might permit us to give it to you in the form of a memorandum. 

Senator GORE. This was a January 1968 estimate that he was speaking of, say, two months later?

Secretary CLIFFORD. That is right. 

Senator GORE. All right. 

Can you give us your estimates two months prior to today? 

Secretary CLIFFORD. I would have to use a general rule of thumb. I would say that the figure he gave, which represented an early January estimate, is perhaps from 15 to 20 thousand lower than it would be the first part of April. That is a very general estimate, but it would be higher in April. 

Senator GORE. Yes. So if you add 20 to 54, that would be 74,000 as of two months ago.

Secretary CLIFFORD. That is correct. 

Senator GORE. I won't ask you to explain it, but I just can't understand how North Vietnam would insist that they have no troops in 

South Vietnam. 

I don't understand it and I am sure you don't. What is the fiction they have tried to maintain?

Secretary CLIFFORD. My comment will be exceedingly general, for this is a subject that is very much in dispute now in Paris, as you know. So I would not want to comment with any specificity on it. But what we do know is that units of North Vietnamese are there. We have said that time and again publicly.

CAPTURE OF NORTH VIETNAMESE SOLDIERS 

Senator GORE. Haven't we captured North Vietnamese soldiers in large numbers? 

Secretary CLIFFORD. Yes, that is correct.

In addition to that, we know that there has been a very substantial buildup of the movement of men since the first of the year through the panhandle of North Vietnam southward. We assume that a certain number of those men also will end up in South Vietnam. As I say, I refer to it only generally. We know that they are there. The means by which we know are the identification of the units that are there. It would be inappropriate for me to go into that because it is a subject of discussion right now in Paris. 

Senator GORE. Again referring to a 60-day prior date, would you significantly change the South Vietnamese forces? What I am trying to get at is the extent to which they have been able to mobilize forces

from the area over which they regained control as a result of the Tet offensive. 

Could you give us an estimate of the size of the Vietcong as of 60 days ago and how would that differ from Secretary Rusk's statement in March as of January?

Secretary CLIFFORD. It would be difficult to be accurate about it. 

There are certain factors that are present. The Vietcong sustained exceedingly severe casualties in the Tet offensive. I have seen various estimates given; but, whatever estimate is correct, we know that their losses were great.

Since that time, they have had two and a half months to fill up vacancies in their units. They have been doing so by relying to some extent, much less than before, on young men in South Vietnam; but, to a great extent, they are filling vacancies in their military units with North Vietnamese. So that my estimate would be that the level of 

Vietcong is down some, but, as of two months ago, probably not down very much because it would have been replenished, mainly from the north.

Senator GORE. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Senator Hickenlooper? 

PERSONAL SECURITY QUESTIONNAIRE 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Mr. Secretary, I have just had come to my attention something I wanted to ask you about it because I think this should be pursued until we can get a satisfactory answer. I understand that your Department has issued instructions that the private employers who do business under contract with the Government are forbidden to ask questions or to receive answers from prospective employees as to their arrest records or anything of a derogatory nature in their background. And I have the documents here which seem to indicate that. So the main question I am interested in, and I will not pursue it now because time is late, but the question I am interested in is how can an employer be able to gauge the qualifications fully and the background and the desirability of a man for whom that private employer has the responsibility of paying and supervising even though he has a contract with the Government and the Government requires a PSQ on him?

Secretary CLIFFORD. I never heard of it. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Somebody in your Department has, because 

I have a document. I have the instructions here. 

Secretary CLIFFORD. Good. Does it have a date on it, Senator?

Senator HICKENLOOPER. May 1, I believe. It is apparently a new approach and, as I understand it, for top secret jobs, the information required of the applicant is sealed in an envelope and the employer never sees that.

My question is, how can the employer gauge the overall qualifications and desirability and the hazard and security risks of an individual where a private employer has the responsibility for paying that individual and supervising him?

Now, this information probably is in the hands of the Government under a sealed envelope someplace, but it seems to me that that completely flies in the face of the responsibility of the private employer

and under the guise of his Government contract. That is the reason the Government moves in and says it will put on these inhibitions or prohibitions.

As I understand it, it has been the policy up until this time that the regulations were that on the personnel security questionnaire those questions were asked which were designed to develop any possible derogatory background of the individual prior to his employment. 

But now that has been changed and it becomes suddenly a matter of great secrecy and sealed envelopes are deposited with the Defense 

Department. 

I would like very much if we could get a complete detailed and accurate explanation of this because, as I say, I have the forms right here in which there are instructions to seal these up and the private employer is not given this information at all. 

Secretary CLIFFORD. From what you have said, Senator, I am unable to deduce the reason for such a curious action. It may, in some manner, have to do with some top secret type of work, and maybe a man's background is being protected in some manner. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. I think the reason given is that it protects his right of secrecy or the invasion of secrecy. 

Secretary CLIFFORD. Yes.

Secretary CLIFFORD. Yes. I would be as interested as you are in finding out what the facts are and what the reason for it is, and I will do so and prepare a report which will give a full explanation of the matter.

Senator HICKENLOOPER. I would appreciate it.

Senator HICKENLOOPER. And, as I say, I have the new forms which have been issued.

Secretary CLIFFORD. We will get all the facts and give them to you.

(The information referred to follows:)

STATEMENT OF DOD POLICY CONCERNING DISCLOSURE OF CERTAIN DEROGATORY INFORMATION 

During my appearance before your Committee on May 17, 1968, I was asked whether the Department of Defense had issued instructions forbidding private employers who do business under contract with the Government from asking questions or receiving answers from prospective employees as to their arrest records or other derogatory information about their backgrounds. 

The Department has not issued instructions prohibiting contractors from asking such questions relevant to employment on their own employment forms. 

There is no intent to interfere with company hiring policies. 

The Privacy Personnel Security Questionnaire form was developed after considerable study of the subject following numerous inquiries by members of Congress in connection with invasions of privacy of individuals. The new forms were developed to enable contractor employees to submit certain information of a privacy nature directly to the Government as "privileged" information. As such, the privacy information may be imparted to the employer by the individual employee if he waives his privilege.

One of the main reasons for the introduction of the Personnel Security Ques tionnaire was the obvious misuse of the Department of Defense Industrial Security Program by certain contractors who were generally denying employ-

ment to persons, based on the allegation that security clearance could not be obtained from the Government because of the evidence of derogatory information furnished by an applicant. Applicants for employment have been advised that they are not eligible for hire because DOD security regulations prohibit such contractors from hiring people with criminal records and that such record is an automatic and absolute bar to the issuance of a security clearance. This, of course, is not true. Department of Defense security regulations do not preclude contractors from hiring individuals with criminal records. It is recognized that some firms will have a company policy of not hiring people with criminal records. 

However, it should be made clear where such company policies exist that these are company policies completely unrelated to DOD security policies. 

The Department of Defense considers the adjudication of derogatory information furnished on security forms for clearance purposes is the responsibility of the Government, utilizing its established due process hearing procedures, and not a matter to be determined by the employer. In addition, since the clearance procedure follows the precedent action of hiring by an employer, such denial for security reasons is an improper application of industrial security policy and requirements.

Additionally, the requirement for furnishing such information through contractors has often resulted in omissions or misstatements on questionnaires by employees. Such falsifications, if discovered, require extensive effort on the part of our investigators to obtain the true facts. Upon confrontation in such cases, contractor employees plead that the falsifications were the result of fear of not obtaining, or losing, their jobs and the threat of criminal penalties for misrepresentation.

It is anticipated that the use of this revised form will result in the submission of more accurate information for Government security determinations and reduce the investigative requirements in such cases. Of course, there is no intent to prohibit contractors from asking any such relevant questions on their own employment forms as being within their responsibility. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Senator Pell? 

Senator PELL. No more questions.

Senator SPARKMAN. All right.

(The article referred to follows:) 

[From the Washington Post, Wednesday, May 8, 1968] 7evin

NORTH VIETNAMESE INFILTRATION PUT AT 100,000 SINCE TET 

(By Chalmers M. Roberts)

More than 100,000 North Vietnamese troops have been sent into South Vietnam since the Tet offensive in January and infiltration during the first five days of May was between 6500 and 7000 men, an Administration official said yesterday. 

The figures, the highest yet made public, were given to the White House correspondents of the Associated Press and United Press International.

Both newsmen reported being told that something will have to be done about the massive movement South. The AP's Douglas B. Cornwell wrote that it was being suggested that Gen. William C. Westmoreland and the Administration "are going to have to decide something quickly."

The UPI's Merriman Smith wrote that "officials expressed the view that Westmoreland could not be expected to continue much longer in the face of continually increasing buildup without demanding the right to interdict the Red infiltration routes." 

At present infiltration routes are being heavily bombed along the Ho Chi Minh trail in Laos and in North Vietnam up to the 19th parallel. The implication of the statement yesterday was that bombing might be resumed further North, a reversal of President Johnson's March 31 limitation which led to the impending 

Paris talks with North Vietnam.

It has been widely reported that the North Vietnamese stepped up their infiltration after the Tet offensive which began on Jan. 31. But as late as last Wednesday Defense Secretary Clark M. Clifford said that he was "not aware of any increase in infiltration" since Mr. Johnson's March 31 speech.

However, last Sunday, George W. Ball, the new ambassador to the United 

Nations, said that infiltration toward and into the South had run between 80,000 and 100,000 in the last four months, presumably January through April, with 

April the highest month on record.

The two newsmen yesterday were told the April figure was 35,000 and that this was about 7,000 more than in March, the previous high. 

The AP account said that "the whole situation poses what some officials consider to be the gravest problem now confronting the nation." 

The new figures presumably were discussed, along with what should be done about it, at the White House lunch attended yesterday by the President, Clifford, Secretary of State Dean Rusk, Gen. Earle C. Wheeler, Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff, and CIA Director Richard Helms. 

The AP dispatch spoke of "possible retaliatory steps" while the UPI referred to possible "difficult military decisions."

Both newsmen wrote of concern that Hanoi is showing no sign of military restraint on the eve of the talks in Paris. It is widely believed in Washington, by sources other than the one quoted by the wire services, that the Communists hope to influence the Paris talks by gains on the battlefield. The American hope likewise is to be in a strong position by virtue of military successes. 

Having finally almost reached the conference table, it would be difficult to resume full bombing of the North, many officials here feel. Some believe that step would lead Hanoi to break off the talks.

Thus it was uncertain whether yesterday's release of figures, even though they can only be approximate because of the difficulty in estimating infiltration, was a psychological warfare step to impress Hanoi or to be the forerunner of some new 

American military move.

Meanwhile, the State Department released the official list of American delegates to the Paris talks, most of whom will fly there on Thursday. Led by Ambassador at Large W. Averell Harriman, the group includes Ambassador Cyrus R. Vance,

Lt. Gen. Andrew Goodpaster, due to be the number two military commander in 

Vietnam next month, Philip C. Habib, a deputy assistant secretary of state and former political officer in the Saigon embassy, William Jorden of the White House staff who will handle press relations, and Daniel I. Davidson, Harriman's special assistant.

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Does it say the official who gave the information?

Senator GORE. It does not identify the officials and it is poor business for a man who must live with the friendship of the news media to lecture them. I will not undertake it, but I am tempted to raise some questions about this constant quotation of unidentified officials, particularly when the unidentified official here is described as a high official in the White House. There is a sharp contrast between 

80,000 to 100,000 troops infiltrating into South Vietnam, and the description which the Secretary gives us today as that estimate moving into the panhandle, some of which may have, many of which may have, so far as I know, none of which or all of which may have. This

is compounding confusion, and I hope the press will be more I am about to lecture I hope they will be more diligent in finding and identifying the source of these rumors. 

Senator HICKENLOOPER. Don't you get lectured by the press every once in awhile?

Senator GORE. Yes, but they are invited in again the next day.

(The article referred to follows:) [From the Christian Science Monitor, Apr. 20, 1968] HEAVY HAND IN GREECE TESTS U.S. 

(By Saville R. Davis) 

WASHINGTON.-The United States is extremely uncomfortable about its policy of compromise with the severe military autocracy in control of Greece. 

It is one year since the April 21 military coup by a group of army colonels in Athens. United States officials concede: 

Democratic institutions are still in virtually complete eclipse in Greece. 

Some 2,400 political prisoners remain in jail as part of a policy of very severe repression.

Political parties are still abolished. 

There is no assurance that the colonels' promise of a new constitution and a plebiscite will repair the situation. It is not known what the new constitution will look like or how it will be applied by an arbitrary government. 

CONTACT RETAINED 

This situation, it is said here, "has ruined the relations of Greece with the democratic world."

The United States has admittedly temporized in the face of this situation. 

Officials say this country "must work for the basic principles of democracy." 

After an early condemnation of the first excesses of the "colonels" regime, a decision was made to "use our influence for a return to normalcy" and "not to cut ourselves off" from the Greek Government, closing the "option" of trying to urge it, rather than force it, back to freedom. 

Officials also say that "high emotions" surround this issue. The Greek-American communities in the United States are seething. "The situation in Greece remains terrible," said one internationally prominent Greek in the United States. "All my friends are in jail under the most grievous circumstances."

REPORT CONFIRMED 

The official statement that there are some 2,400 political prisoners in Greek jails, despite a proclaimed "amnesty," confirms at least part of a firsthand report on this matter that has been widely read in Washington and caused much concern. It was prepared by Amnesty International, an official consultant to the 

Council of Europe which also sent observers to Athens and which has put the Greek Government on a form of probation with respect to its membership in the council.. 

Amnesty International sent Antony Marreco, a member of the British bar, and

James Becket, a member of the American bar, to make a four-week investigation early this year in Greece. At that time they estimated more than 2,700 political prisoners "detained without trial by the Greek Government."

AFFILIATION ASSESSED 

There are "numerous others," the report says, under some form of arrest. "It is believed that some 500 [of these] may have been active or potentially active Communists. The remainder cannot be described as 'Communists' in any accepted 

European sense of the word.... "The delegation has assembled the names, addresses, and particulars of about 800 of those detained on the islands of Leros and Yaros. They come from all walks

of life and include parliamentarians, professional people, intellectuals, and artists.... "The delegation found that the relatives of prisoners have been subject to government persecution. Children of the prisoners are excluded from entering a university. Friends have been arrested for giving money to needy families. 

Relatives have lost their jobs. The police pay frequent harassing visits to the families of prisoners at all hours of day and night.” 

STATEMENTS TAKEN 

The two lawyers personally took testimony from 16 persons who said they had been tortured, and second-hand testimony with respect to 32 more who are still in prison, "which they found convincing because it was in many cases corroborated." 

The lawyers concluded in their report: "On the basis of first-hand evidence and oral testimony, on the basis of scars on the bodies of those tortured, and on the basis of testimony of professional people and relatives, the delegation can objectively state that torture is deliberately and officially used. [It was] convinced that the use of torture is a widespread practice against Greek citizens suspected of active opposition to the government." 

The report gave places of torture and names of the security and military police stated to have inflicted it. It detailed forms of torture that it concluded had been used, and methods of inducing mental breakdown of prisoners, giving many specific instances.

CONCLUSIONS STATED 

The two lawyers concluded: "The security police and the military police are unrestricted today in Greece. Since . . . 'the laws sleep,' the police may arrest anyone, in any place, at any time, with no obligation to charge him or inform anyone of his arrest. Believing that their own position is threatened by opposition to the government, they have reacted brutally to those engaged in opposition." 

A second report was made by Amnesty International two weeks ago. Mr. Marreco had returned to Greece after criticisms of the first report by the Greek Government. In the presence of Greek officials he was permitted to visit prisons and interrogate anyone he wished.

On March 30 and 31, he said, “out of a total of 12 prisoners seen (in the presence of prison and police officials) nine said quite definitely they had been tortured, and one said he preferred not to answer the questions. In each case, the story they told corroborated all previous evidence as to place and names of the torturers."

PRISONERS IDENTIFIED 

Among those interviewed, Mr. Marreco said, were Gerassimos Notaras, an internationally respected economist and research fellow at the Social Science Center of Athens and formerly an assistant lecturer in the political-science faculty of the University of Lausanne. Others interviewed were Constantine Sophoulis, economist and grandson of a former prime minister, and Charalambos Protopapas, assistant secretary general of the Creek League for Human Rights. 

Mr. Marreco had the following conversation thereafter with Deputy Prime 

Minister and Minister of the Interior Stylianos Pattakos. Mr. Marreco said that unless the allegations are disproved Greece must inevitably face expulsion from the Council of Europe-to which Amnesty International was making its report. 

COMMUNISTS ASSAILED 

Mr. Pattakos cried, "Let them throw us out." Mr. Marreco said, "Is that what you wish me to go back to London and say?"

Mr. Pattakos replied, "You force me to say it. The Greek Government has to protect its people against its Communist enemies. A Communist is not a Greek. 

We must put our own security first."

One frankly stated reason for the United State's policy of limited cooperation with the present Greek Government is that of the United State's own special interests in Greece today.

Greece has been a staging area for some 25,000 Americans removed from the Middle East area during the Arab-Israeli conflict. It is a stopping point for "strategic flights" to the Far East where the United States now is engaged in war. There is no American military base there, but there are landing rights for this sort of operation.

GREEK FACILITIES USED 

As the southern part of the Mediterranean has become "impossible" for American activities of these sorts, Greek facilities have been increasingly used. The present Greek regime has made a point of cooperating with these American needs. 

Nevertheless, it is obvious here that the American policy is "open for review," and is not considered satisfactory. At present officials seem to be waiting to see what direction events take in Greece, and what the present Greek Government's pledges of a return toward democracy turn out to be in fact. The placing under house arrest of former Premiers George Papandreou and Panayotis Kanellopoulos on the eve of the first anniversary of the April 21 coup appears to be a negative step.

It also appears from here that the Council of Europe is in a similar waiting position. The council's own observers in Greece were not in a position to verify the scope and treatment of political prisoners, but the report of Amnesty International is said to have had a strong effect on council members. 

However painful it is to have widespread and conspicuous political repression charged againt a prominent member government of the Western defense system, there is a growing impression here that it will have to be faced. The issue is increasingly agitated by groups concerned with freedom. Decisions both here and in the Council of Europe appear to hang on events in the fairly near future.

Admiral Heinz, your associates, we appreciate all of you for being here this morning. This has been a very helpful session, and we shall look forward to the time when you may return.

Secretary CLIFFORD. Thank you. 

Senator SPARKMAN. Thank you very much.

Secretary CLIFFORD. Thank you.
