APPENDIX STATEMENT OF THE COOPERATIVE LEAGUE OF THE USA, MAY 14, 1968 

Mr. Chairman, we thank you for letting us talk about the role America's customer-owned businesses is playing in bringing help to the less developed countries included in our foreign aid program. Of course what I say refers only to the economic side of that program. 

But when the cooperatives of this country which include over 20 million families, opened their own modest foreign assistance program 14 years ago, before there was an AID program, they earned a right to be heard on this subject. Our earliest effort was in India and the passing years have shown India to be a wise choice for reasons to be discussed later in this statement. 

These first efforts were financed wholly from contributions of American cooperators, but as this Committee knows from our testimony in previous years, the Cooperative League now works with AID directly under contracts which have enabled us to broaden work we were already doing and go into other undeveloped countries which had asked for help in starting or strengthening cooperatives. We have seen enough and experienced enough as our efforts have taken root in Asia and Latin America and made some promising beginnings in Africa to regret exceedingly that AID's FY 1969 request for $2 billion in economic aid is not more.

Of course, we share the concern of all other Americans with regard to the budgetary and balance of payments positions of our country. We know about the impact of foreign aid upon both of those positions. We nevertheless endorse foreign aid as a critical instrument for promoting U.S. interests abroad, particularly insofar as the aid is directed toward promoting the objectives of Title IX. 

The League believes that an increased, rather than reduced, allocation of resources to Title IX purposes would be warranted, even if this could only be done by a decrease in the allocation of resources to other purposes. 

The large-scale establishment in many less developed countries of new agricultural cooperatives, and the strengthening of existing agricultural cooperatives, would be especially helpful. Such cooperatives can be a highly effective instrument for enabling the low-income farmers of those countries to benefit directly from the "green revolution" to which the AID Administration has referred. 

We earnestly hope this Committee does not pass over lightly the significance of what is being said here. After years of biting our collective finger nails and wringing our hands over the classic inability of several densely populated Asian and Latin countries to feed themselves, now responsible agricultural economists are telling us we stand on the brink of a breakthrough an agricultural revolution in the making-where countries like India and Pakistan may become selfsufficient, not in an generation or two, but in the next four or five years. This is not rhetoric and hope alone but the sober judgment of men who should know. 

It is right here that cooperatives can contribute greatly, and at relatively little cost to the U.S. taxpayer, toward expansion of agricultural production abroad and therefore toward a decrease in their dependence upon U.S. foreign aid. No other instrument can so effectively bring these Asian and Latin farmers into a true appreciation of, and genuine devotion toward, the basic ingredients of the democratic process. There is no alternative instrument which can so effectively train the farmers in the attributes and the rewards of private enterprise, with minimum dependence upon governmental support or influence. Nothing else can better facilitate individual and mutual self-help among low-income farmers without resort to the socialistic or communistic device of collective farming. 

The contribution which the intensified development of agricultural cooperatives could make toward political development in certain countries warrants special emphasis. The rural sectors of those countries have derived little or no benefit from such general economic growth as has taken place in their countries. (467)

Insurgency and other manifestations of political alienation have become dangerously pronounced in the rural sectors. At the same time, there has been massive migration to the cities which spreads their squatter settlements for miles, thereby aggravating political agitation among these closely bunched and economically dispossessed people. Successful agricultural cooperatives can-by the very fact of their success-contribute not only toward improvement of the economic and social situations in the countryside, but in so doing can stem this migration and defuse its inevitable explosive consequences.

We do not mean that the intensified development of agricultural cooperatives is anything like a panacea. Farmers need many forms of help. They often need improved access to credit. They often need roads, irrigation, improved seeds and fertilizer. They need technical assistance in learning about, and applying the benefits of, the many forms of agriculture research which has already taken place in their own and other countries. And they need rural electric facilities. However, the League believes that the development of agricultural cooperatives can be helpful even with regard to facilitating the fulfillment of many of these needs. 

Moreover, the cooperatives can be especially helpful in promoting successful farm management. Farms are business enterprises, and as with all other business enterprises no ingredient for success is more important than sound management. And there is no mechanism for promoting sound farm management among the low-income and poorly educated farmers in less developed countries to match the mechanism of the agriculture cooperative. 

It should not be assumed, however, that the intensified development of successful agricultural cooperatives depends exclusively upon the desire that they be developed. The success of an agricultural cooperative ordinarily depends, in most of the less developed countries, upon a relatively small amount of seed capital plus technical assistance in their formation and in the early stages of their operation. The Agency for International Development can be helpful in both respects. 

The Cooperative League and other U.S. cooperative groups can be helpful in the provision of the necessary technical assistance, particularly if AID facilitates their work. AID has effectively collaborated with the League and other U.S. cooperative groups for this purpose, and it is expected that this collaboration will be continued and even expanded. 

The League believes that its role, and the role of other private, nonprofit, U.S. cooperative groups can be very important in the promotion of cooperatives abroad not only because of the knowledge and experience which they have in this area but also because the private, non-profit, groups can often achieve far better results than could be achieved by direct-hire officials of the U.S. Government. 

The effectiveness of U.S. Government officials may on occasion be impaired merely by recognition of their official capacity. They are further handicapped, moreover, by the obligation to work with officials of the host government-officials who sometimes are less than fully anxious to promote independent cooperatives, especially among low-income farmers. 

To an extent greater than is true of the U.S. population generally, the membership of our cooperatives-numbering in the millions-continue to show a deeper commitment to the objectives of the American aid program and a livelier interest in how that program is faring. This is so because American cooperators have had a sense of participation in this program and, being better informed about it, they see the crucial stakes for which we are playing in a world that is changing faster than any of us can fully comprehend.

This support by American cooperators for foreign aid runs the gamut from formal resolutions at the League Congress and in local cooperatives to contributing to our own foreign aid program (called Worldwide Co-op Partners), and having as guests in their homes visiting cooperators from other countries here for training. Scores of co-op leaders with a particular needed form of expertise have gone cheerfully on arduous assignments to places where amenities are few to serve as advisors or consultants on cooperative projects. These consultations can last for a few weeks to a year or more, and they are carried out at personal sacrifice and inconvenience which very few of them ever mention when they return home.

To get down to specifics, here is what happened in the community of Julio Andrade in the province of Carchi, Ecuador. There are a thousand farmers, 60% of whom own their small farms while the others rent. Being owner or tenant in 

Julio Andrade makes little difference for both lack modern agricultural tools and know-how. The wheat yield is about thirteen bushels per acre. There is no system of crop marketing or agricultural credit. Farmers had to sell their crops at low prices well in advance of the harvest, just to get enough cash to survive.

In March 1965, leaders there asked AID/Quito to map a program to improve their hard lot. An agricultural credit production loan service was devised. An existent, but dormant, credit union was used as a base for the experiment. 

Specially trained Peace Corps volunteers together with community leaders and three farmer committees set about an educational promotional campaign which proposed to explain how supervised credit would help them. New membership was recruited for the credit union, and intensive training in credit union operation was given to the Board of Directors and the credit committee. 

In three months, by June 1965, the credit union had been revived and 320 members had contributed a capital of 22,000 sucres ($1100 U.S.). With this capital base, the cooperative was able to obtain an additional line of credit in the sum of 200,000 sucres ($10,000 U.S.) from the Cooperative Bank of Ecuador. 

In March 1966 savings had increased to 350,000 sucres ($17,500 U.S.). Now in addition to credit, members are getting advice and help with better farm practices. They have held classes in farm management and rudiments of farm accounting. Surprisingly, some brought their children with them to class so that they would also learn and because they "remember better." What started as an experiment in supervised credit is growing into a great deal else, but all of it spells a closer, more prosperous rural community of people who are less likely to take off for Quito or Guayaquil to swell those overcrowded cities.

Now, on the other side of the world, let me give you a snapshot of the Anand dairy cooperative in Gujerat State, India. Here 75,000 villagers with one or more cows market their milk cooperatively. What is the advantage of this? For one thing they get the money the day they deliver the milk. This way they also see clearly the place of the non-productive cow and this makes it easier to trim the herd to include only the producers. Very considerable side benefits of belonging to this cooperative take the form of savings programs, health clinics, water systems-all made possible by income derived from the first sensible milk marketing program these farmers ever had. A market for pasteurized milk in Bombay became practical because Anand Dairy Cooperative could guarantee a reliable daily supply. It would take a book, however, to recount what is attempted here in a paragraph: listing all the ways life has been made better for these 75,000 small producers.

Our League has been in India for 15 years and such success as we have had is based on the principle that all our programs first have support of Indian cooperatives and people plus assurance of their wholehearted backing and participation. 

This has taken a lot of the guess work out of the proposals taken to AID and other sources of capital, sharply separating the truly promising ones from those which "look good on paper." As a case in point, feasibility studies are just completed on the first fertilizer production plant to be owned by Indian cooperatives and construction should begin this summer. U.S. cooperatives through Cooperative Fertilizers International (CFI) will manage the plant until local people are trained and can take over. U.S. cooperatives pledged a million dollars as seed financing to employing CFI staff the first four years plus $400,000 worth of their own management and technical personnel. For this they ask and expect no return and no ownership. Financing in the amount of $119.000,000 is coming from Indian cooperatives, from the Government of India, and U.S. private banks. Indian cooperatives will buy out the share of the other two capital sources. 

This facility and others like it can play a decisive role in carrying India over the hump to full food production sufficiency as it increases availability and lowers prices. It will also save India $350 million in foreign exchange between now and 1980. This was a joint program of AID and U.S. cooperatives now at a stage when we confidently expect in later years every one who was connected with it will look back with a solid prideful feeling of real accomplishment. 

Please note, Mr. Chairman, for it is most important in the context of what I am trying to tell this Committee, that comparatively small amounts of money are required for cooperatively-oriented AID projects. This is so because here we are essentially exporting a technique which long ago proved itself as workable to the satisfaction of all reasonable men. This technique enables men of goodwill to combine their resources and energy along sound economic lines to master problems standing between them and a decent living. This approach not only supplies opportunity for self-help, it requires it, and won't work without it. In undeveloped lands this approach sets in motion a chain of circumstances which brings to bear all the limited resources of mind, muscle, and buying power, releasing a group potential far greater than the sum of its parts. It also helps find and use natural community leaders of a constructive bent, often buried in the group, or overshadowed by the loud incendiaries.

This Committee did a very wise thing two years ago when it added Title IX to foreign aid legislation, calling for maximum use in the program of the private resources of the U.S. Let no one forget cooperatives in the list of our private resources. To make certain they are not forgotten you should in your report call upon AID to so staff its overseas missions that there will always be in each mission someone who knows and appreciates the part cooperatives have played in our own country's development and the part they can play in lands still facing the uphill development road. When there is no one in the mission so qualified the result is indifference, misunderstanding, and—tragically-lost opportunities. 

24 Administrators naturally find it easier to hand the keys to a new school building to a government minister or cut the ribbon on a new road. People's institutions grow to success in less dramatic circumstances but are meant to endure, and in the long run they will make a greater difference. 

A solid plus factor in the cooperative approach is that there is one segment of American private sector which can get out and leave responsibility in local hands when the job is done. A former president of the Cooperative League and 

President Kennedy's Food for Peace task force chairman, the late Murray D. 

Lincoln, called it "graceful disengagement" when talking about cooperatives and foreign aid five years ago. It is important that no vested interest should stay on or should develop, and with so few cooperative specialists and all of them needed so many places, there is no danger of that. 

The cooperative approach is versatile. We are talking about farm marketing specialists, builders of rural electric lines, credit union men knowledgeable in credit and savings, experts from the Foundation for Cooperative Housing eager to help with desperately needed housing programs, experts in grocery cooperatives, and every other form of business with its co-op counterpart. Mos 

With skills like these, when co-op specialists go out they are able to get away from the capital cities, past the government functionaries, and into the villages and countryside where the needs are all with a minimum of time-wasting red tape and protocol because they have come to do a job and then to go home. 

Space precludes elaboration here, but I would like to say for those interested in amplification of my thesis that it has been set forth in detail and with cogency in a paper entitled "Sound Cooperatives: Their Use as a Major Development Instrument." This work of our Latin American administrator, Mr. 

Daughters, documents persuasively how agricultural cooperatives can help take the undeveloped world over the hump to complete food sufficiency in a far shorter time than we were dreaming of in the early 1960's. With the Committee's consent 

I should like to present it for the record. 

The great new fact of foreign assistance in 1968 is that the food gap which threatens famine to millions if left uncorrected is not the insurmountable problem we were thinking a little while ago. If we will but use with compassion and intelligence the opportunities open to us in revolutionary farm technology and rural organization in other lands as we have used them in the U.S.A., we can lick this challenge on which millions of lives depend. The keystone in this breakthrough is not just technological but socialogical. What the reaper was to the 19th century wheat field, the agriculture cooperative can be to backward farmers and primitive villages on every continent. 

It is unthinkable that we could stint on this phase of foreign aid which promises at bargain rates food and dignity for those we help and the blessing for ourselves of a more stable and orderly world. 

DONALD L. DAUGHTERS, ADMINISTRATOR, LATIN AMERICAN PROGRAMS COOPERATIVE LEAGUE OF THE USA, MARCH 7, 1968-SOUND COOPERATIVES: THEIR USE AS A MAJOR DEVELOPMENT INSTRUMENT INTRODUCTION 

March 1968 finds two basic questions being posed with persistent and increasing urgency by USG foreign policy planners, development specialists, the Congress, the American private business sector, and representatives of these foreign governments and multilateral lending organizations concerned with economic and social development in the LDC's. 

Simply stated, the two questions are: (1) How do we get agriculture moving in the LDC's? (Or, in some countries, how do we get it to move faster or how do we eliminate specific weaknesses in the agricultural sector?)

07 (2) How do we get "the people" moving, or how can we help the maximum number of "little people" as well as others, to become psychologically transformed so that they assume the primary responsibility for their own progress? 

Stated another way, there is general agreement worldwide among development authorities that we must organize and implement broad-based rural development programs in the LDC's with the objective of creating more prosperous, selfreliant local communities, effecting a gradual change in their economic and social structure by transferring political/economic power and decision-making to larger numbers of people. Such programs would (a) emphasize increased food production in response to a threatening world hunger crisis in the LDC's; (b) increase economic opportunities in the rural sectors which would assist in reducing the massive migration toward, and serious congestion in, the urban centers; and (c) provide greater political and social stability in both the urban and rural sectors. 

As recognized by the U.S. Congress, through the Humphrey and Title IX 

Amendments to the Foreign Assistance Act, the effective promotion of sound cooperatives in the LDC's would involve maximum numbers of people in the mobilization of their own resources; stimulate a more conscious development and self-help psychology at the grass-roots level of the population; strengthen, as both a cause and effect, the participation-and the feeling of participationby the low income farmers and urban dwellers in the economic and social life of their countries.

But cooperatives are first and foremost businesses, albeit small businesses in the more primitive rural and in the low income urban areas of the LDC's. 

They therefore require the usual inputs of successful business enterprise: capital investment; trained management; some technological knowledge; the assurance of a market for the product to be sold; ready availability of credit; a structured organization to carry out the business enterprises; etc. To foster cooperatives seriously, these elements must be included in any cooperative development program. 

AID, to its credit, has dedicated some, but unfortunately very limited resources to the promotion of cooperative development. While it has confined its support to sporadic, isolated projects, it is imperative that at the very least such support be continued and even amplified, i.e., the Mogiana cooperatives in Brazil, marketing cooperatives in Ecuador, etc. However, the Cooperative League after deep study of the problem considers it imperative that AID provide the necessary support for the formulation and subsequent implementation of broader scaled more imaginative cooperative development programs in certain LDC's in harmony with AID's efforts to promote their overall economic and social development activity. 

The League proposes that development authorities employ the accumulated experience of the late years in LDC cooperative development and move forward from a project to a program approach at the earliest possible date. 

This will require the use of experienced program analysts familiar with wide varieties of cooperative operations and will involve the use of well-trained cooperative technicians and administrators from the U.S. cooperative movement. 

Our suggested approach not only makes available experienced cooperative technical backstopping but involves major segments of rural America in, and obtains their endorsement for, U.S. foreign assistance activity. 

Our USG development missions to date have fallen far short of providing to cooperative activity in the LDC's either the financial or technical assistance resources required to make any real economic impact on the development of the communities concerned-more especially in the rural areas. They have relied too heavily upon the expectation that the massively underemployed and unemployed, low-income farmers of the rural areas will eventually become better employed as an indirect consequence of agricultural lending, physical infrastructure investments (i.e., roads, schools, irrigation, electrification, some additional rural industrial installations), etc. We do not argue against increased investment in these activity areas. On the contrary, they are most useful if not essential to the proper development of rural communities. We propose not an "either. or" but a "both .. and" approach, that is, a continued support to physical infrastructural investment, but not to the exclusion of introducing integrated, cooperative, agri-business activity on an expanded scale. 

Consonant with the above, the League sets forth in Section II of this paper proposed procedures for formulating cooperative development programs. We emphasize that the League and other cooperative contractors are prepared to provide programming guidance or assistance to USAID's throughout the LDC's in determining: 91-392-68-pt. 2- -16

(1) The types, dimensions and locations of urban and rural cooperative institutions which might make the greatest contribution to development; (2) What should be done to determine the probable cost (in terms of both financial support and technical assistance) of promoting this high priority institutional approach; (3) What specific types of technical assistance are needed to assure the success of each project; (4) Where can or should that assistance be obtained; and (5) What should be the time schedules for the establishment, operation, expansion or modification of the selected programs and/or projects. 

Section III of this paper contains various recommendations as to specific actions which AID should take to support effective programming for cooperative development.

The recommendations are generally applicable to the problems of programming and implementing cooperative development in all of the LDC's but we have tended to focus primarily on Latin America for ease of presentation. While AID-financed cooperative development projects are being pursued in a number of fields (housing, credit, rural electrification, insurance, urban consumers' needs, etc.), this document relates primarily to agricultural cooperative programs because of the present grave concern with the world food problem and because of the need for AID personnel to have access to practical suggestions for programming agricultural cooperative development. Furthermore, efficiency, technological know-how experience, and trained management are certainly as abundantly available in the U.S. rural cooperative field as in perhaps any other cooperative activity. 

I. A new approach to programing for agricultural cooperatives 

A. Why a New Approach? 

The League has observed that the major emphasis of the USAID cooperative development efforts in the LDC's is usually upon specific projects of a rather sporadic and isolated nature. Almost invariably there is no systematic, rationally planned, programing approach to this important activity. For example, emphasis may be given to the promotion of specific cooperatives-i.e., rural electrification (NRECA projects in Ecuador, Peru, and other countries), or credit unions (CUNA projects in several countries), or housing development, FCH projects in several countries), or coffee diversification and the creation of a domestic market structure (a CLUSA project in Brazil), or the establishment of cooperative banks (FICD in Ecuador and Chile).

In some LDC's the USAID's have supported activities of a somewhat more general nature (cooperative training centers, university fellowships, etc.) to strengthen all or most existing cooperatives and/or to stimulate the formation of new cooperatives. Sometimes the emphasis is on measures to stimulate the formation of particular kinds of new cooperatives or of cooperatives in particular areas. 

Until 1967, the League had been engaged almost exclusively in promoting projects of the foregoing types. It continues to be very much interested in them, as reflected in its current participation in the Mogiana coffee diversification project in Brazil, for example, but we feel a distinctly new approach is necessary. 

In fact, this "new approach" was utilized by a League team in India as early as 1961 which developed an eight-point, broad-guaged cooperative program from which has emerged a very excellent fertilizer production and distribution project now receiving wide attention in Asia.

B. Nature of the New Approach 

Since early in 1967 the League (with certain AID/W staff encouragement) has devoted increasing attention to the desirability of formulating cooperative development programs of a broader nature responding to the more critical and high priority problems in the LDC's. In particular, it has been concentrating upon the possibility of formulating programs designed to meet the objectives of Title IX of the Foreign Assistance Act on a substantial scale. While the promotion of specific cooperative development projects also helps meet the objectives of Title IX, the League has concluded on the basis of its experience in Latin America and the Far East that there is both a great opportunity and an urgent need in several LDC's for cooperative development which will have a direct impact upon much larger percentages of the population, especially middle, middle-low, and low-income farmers. 

Of course, the components and even the general direction of each cooperative development program must be adapted to the distinctive economic, social, and

political situations of the pertinent LDC. No one type of program can be offered as a prototype for all LDC's. Nevertheless, you may find it helpful to review 

Annexes #1 and #2 as illustrations of broader-gauged programing to which the League has given increasing attention despite the fact that these Annexes pertain only to the very special situations prevailing in the Dominican Republic and Brazil, respectively. You may also find it helpful to review Annex #3, which enters into considerable detail in explaining how the League's evaluation of the special circumstances prevailing in the Dominican Republic led to formulation of the tentative program proposals contained in Annex #1 and represent an important means for achievement of USG policy objectives in that country. 

The League's tentative program proposals with regard to the Dominican Republic and Brazil differed from one another in many important respects, but each emphasized the objective of establishing large numbers of intergrated, multi-purpose agricultural cooperatives on cultivatable land which is at present owned by the governments of those two countries and which is not now being cultivated at all or which is being cultivated very inadequately. In each case it is proposed that there would be a small amount of governmental financial assistance ($400-$500 per family) plus technical assistance to large numbers of farmers to enable them to establish viable farming operations with each actively participating in cooperatives. Integrated agricultural cooperatives could be oriented, in their early stages, toward production activities of an essentially self-sufficient nature, as in the Brazilian proposal, or toward a complex of activities, including the cooperative purchasing of supplies, production and marketing, as in the Dominican proposal. In each case, some form of governmental assurance of land titles would have to be given to the farmers in each agricultural cooperative.

The League believes that the similar establishment or strengthening of such cooperatives would be highly deisrable in almost all of the LDC's. They have flourished, when properly supported, in Europe and Japan as well as in the United States. The economic functions they could serve would be at least as great in the LDC's, especially in Latin America, where other business management devices in the rural sector have on the whole attained a far too limited social and economic success. These integrated rural cooperatives would be organized for the purpose of: (1) Increasing agricultural output while reducing its cost of production; (2) Storing, processing grains and other agricultural production to reduce spoilage and improve quality; (3) Transporting, marketing, and distributing to consumer centers with minimal dependence upon middlemen at such times and under such conditions as to permit higher net returns to the farmers and lower costs to the consumers. The social functions they could serve would be at least as important, especially insofar as they would increase the farmer's attachment to the land, while training him in farm management and providing understandable demonstrations of the material and spiritual benefits of private enterprise and democratic group collaboration. Accordingly, the League believes that wherever the opportunity exists to promote integrated agricultural cooperatives on a large scale, as in Brazil and the Dominican Republic, it would be very much in the interests of the United States to encourage and actively support such activity.

On the other hand, the League recognizes that the basic conditions in many LDC's might be quite different from those in either Brazil or the Dominican Republic. Sound programming in many LDC's would doubtless lead to emphasis upon, say, strengthening already-established cooperatives in the urban as well as the rural sectors, or upon establishing or strengthening specific types of singlepurpose cooperatives, such as marketing, supply, insurance, credit unions, housing, rural electric, health, consumer cooperatives, etc. 

The League also recognizes that AID might find it necessary to limit its support to smaller programs than those proposed by the League or consider it desirable to start with smaller programs, with the objective of expanding them as it gains experience in the implementation of such programs. 

C. Advantage of the New Approach 

In addition to the advantages of increased agricultural production, improved marketing and consumption (which have been mentioned above and discussed more fully on pages 1-6 or Annex #3), we believe it demonstrable that the large-scale development of agricultural cooperatives in most LDC's is far less

costly than various alternative means of improving the economic and social conditions in rural sectors.

We believe also that, by making the rural sectors more attractive regions in: which to live and work, the development of integrated agricultural cooperatives will make a major, though indirect, contribution to relieving economic and social distress in the urban centers of the LDC's. Assisting masses of rural people toorganize and operate meaningful economic enterprises where they are can aid in stemming the tide of migration to the cities, a continent-wide problem of first magnitude in Latin America. The emergence of rural-born, slum-created political. extremists can be greatly ameliorated by absorbing their energies in rural institutions in the first instance. Europe, the United States, and Japan have found cooperatives an excellent device for assisting in maintaining a more favorable urban-rural balance Another direct consequence of our proposals for integrated rural cooperatives would be the furtherance of meaningful and effective agrarian reform. This could be considered a by-product of such programs and one which is not properly a responsibility of the League. Nevertheless, we consider it to be of major significance in light of the great need for agrarian reform in many of the LDC's and in light of the fact that very little progress toward such reform has been achieved in most of the LDC's through employment of other, less practical (including less politically practical) measures. 

Agricultural cooperatives can also contribute greatly to many highly essential social, as well as economic objectives. They can greatly facilitate the distribution of land, including the provision of secure land titles. They can guide the peasant into appreciating the merits of self-help and effective self-reliance, together with the advantages of noncompulsory collaboration with his neighbors. They can be an effective means for transmission of the benefits of agricultural research and extension services, for rural education, improved health facilities, population control. 

Furthermore, we believe that the introduction of integrated, multipurposecooperatives will develop improved farm management among lower income farmers and their management of farms as successful business enterprises. 

This is especially important for the purpose of deriving the maximum benefit from the other measures which are presently being introduced in LDC agricul-tural communities worldwide, e.g., stepped-up technical assistance through improved extension services, improved agricultural credit systems, provision of farm supplies and equipment at lower cost, construction of farm-to-market roads.

Again, as explained in Annex #3 (especially pages 22-26), it is not ordinarily reasonable to expect that farm management by the lower income farmers in most LDC's would be improved nearly as well by any of the four major alternatives to agricultural cooperatives, i.e., by establishment or perpetuation of (a) excessively small, inefficient family-sized farms (minifundia); or (b) large state collective farms; or (c) large corporate farms; or (d) government-sponsored and government-controlled agricultural settlements. 1 It should be pointed out that these integrated multipurpose cooperatives can provide credit access to individuals who have never in history enjoyed such benefits and who still cannot obtain it from the newly created Latin American industrial fomento banks or traditional agrarian banks since the lower interest, longer-term loans of these banks are usually almost exclusively directed toward the more affluent classes. 

In summary, some important advantages of the large-scale establishment of integrated multi-purpose cooperatives would be: (1) Increased agricultural production; (2) Improved marketing and lower food costs; (3) Reduction of the migration to urban centers in LDC's; (4) Creation of an institutional structure within which effective agrarian reform can move forward; (5) Development of improved farm management; and (6) Provision of credit to many thousands of small farmers who have no access to it at present. 

D. Problems and Suggested Solutions Relative to the New Approach 

However much the reader may be impressed by the intrinsic merits of promoting cooperative development along the lines of the proposals contained in the preceding paragraphs of this document and in Annexes 1 and 2, he may nevertheless be disturbed by the fact that such proposals would require: (a) sub-

stantial funding by AID or alternative multi-lateral lending institutions, (b) the assignment of additional technical advisors to the LDC's, (c) that the great bulk of necessary funding be in local currency with possible negative impacts on the U.S. balance of payments.

The League is not unmindful of the unfortunate fact that the Congress has sharply reduced the funds available for assistance to the LDC's. This fact alone has doubtless necessitated cutbacks in some of AID's current and important programs and projects. Initiation of any new and major cooperative development programs might require that other on-going activities be cut back as areas of less priority. We know of no way to circumvent this problem. We can only hope that you will agree for the many reasons advanced that very high priority should be given LDC rural cooperative development. 

It may be appropriate to observe in this connection that while there is no necessary correlation (speaking strictly in terms of programing concepts) between the total dimension of the assistance program for any country and the priorities attached to its separate components, it may be true in the case of some of the LDC's that the smaller the total assistance program, the greater will be the need for those sub-programs which directly affect a large percentage of the population. 

The fact that integrated cooperative development programs would require the assignment of some technical advisors to the LDC's may be considered undesirable in light of the USG's desire to minimize the number of U.S. personnel abroad. 

However, some weight might be given to ameliorating facts: (1) that most advisors would be living and working in the immediate vicinity of the agricultural cooperatives, rather than conspicuously in the larger urban centers; (2) that most of the advisors would be members of the private U.S. cooperative movement rather than USG personnel; and (3) that those of the advisors who are not members of the U.S. cooperative movement might well be members of the private cooperative movements of other countries, such as Sweden, Denmark, Israel, Argentina, or other Latin American countries. 

We cannot gainsay the fact that there might be some negative impact on the U.S. balance of payments as a result of the funding of local currency expendi- -ture. However, we do not know of any other way of implementing Title IX that would not raise the same problem. We hope that the USG's understandable concern about our balance of payments will not lead to neglect of obviously high priority activities while emphasis is placed upon projects or programs which are of lesser priority but which appear to be raise no balance of payments problems. 

The cost of foreign technicians in cooperative development programs and proj- -ects warrants special attention. We have encountered a predisposition in some -quarters to assume first, that the greatest impediment to cooperative development in the LDC's has been their inadequate understanding of cooperativism and to assume, second, that cooperative development would result more or less automatically from an energetic educational and promotional campaign concerning cooperativism in the universities, in government circles, and among the public at large. Unfortunately, these assumptions are not realistic with regard to most 

LDC's. There certainly is a need in many of them for education and promotional campaigns concerning cooperativism. In fact, such campaigns may often be a prerequisite to the adoption of a cooperative development program or project, and the League has already devoted considerable, and effective, energy to this objective. It will continue to do so. 

However, understanding of cooperativism is a necessary, but not a sufficient, condition to developing cooperatives in the LDC's when the prerequisite capital, technology, management, ability, attitudes of self-reliance, etc., are absent, as they frequently are. Furthermore, if a cooperative is formed under these circumstances, there is a substantial danger that it will not be economically successful. It hardly needs emphasis that the social and political consequences of cooperatives that fail are more likely to be negative than positive. Accordingly, the promotion of cooperatives, especially agricultural cooperatives, in the LDC's depends—indispensably, though regrettably-upon governmental financial assistance and upon the provision of technical assistance by specialists experienced in the management of cooperatives. Frequently professionals of sufficient experience can be obtained only from outside the national areas of the LDC's; hence, the technical assistance cost increases.

On the other hand, the necessary cost of cooperative development programs should not be exaggerated. As explained in pages 9-15 of Annex #2, it is a basic principle of our approach that the formation and operation of agricultural

on the cooperatives should depend principally upon self-help measures of the cooperative members, with the lowest indispensable amount of governmental support. 

For example, the total cost of the USG and the Dominican Republic Government of the program proposed for that country would be $6 million. In absolute terms this may appear substantial but only because of the very large number of farmers (15,000) who would receive the direct benefits of such a program. The cost per farm-family would be a small fraction of the costs incurred, for example, in the grandiose (and otherwise undesirable) settlement projects which have on several occasion been sponsored by many LDC governments. Far from considering such a program expensive, the League stresses its low cost, as well as its high desirability.

II. Procedure for Formulating Cooperative Development Programs 

It is one of our basic tenets that the League should never try to formulate cooperative development programs in a vacuum. They should be formulated so as to harmonize fully with the overall objectives which the USG considers appropriate for each country. The League can best do its job right only if it takes its basic guidance on priority objectives from the USG and the national development authorities, and the private cooperative sector. On the other hand, we believe that the USG can derive great benefit in formulating its assistance programs for certain countries, by drawing on the cooperative sectors and the League's expertise concerning the contributions which certain specific cooperative development programs can make. The following analysis will therefore include reference to some subjects which are significantly, though only indirectly, pertinent to cooperative development.

We suggest that cooperative development be formulated in accordance with the following general guidelines:

A. Preparation of Basic Reports 

1. Whenever the USG or the League, with the assistance of local cooperative leadership, determines on the basis of the available information and in light of the policy objectives of the USG-that promotion of cooperative development in a country might reasonably be expected to further the achievement of USG objectives, the League would prepare a preliminary report to AID of how cooperative development could best be promoted in that country and of the economic and social benefits that could realistically be anticipated. In preparing this report, the League would try to harmonize any suggestions for cooperative development with the current Country Assistance Program of the pertinent USAID. 

In particular, the League would try to minimize the need for additional technical assistance personnel or additional funding in dollar or in local currencies which would constitute a burden on the U.S. balance of payments. 

The proposal that the League could prepare such a report on its own initiative warrants special attention. The League would, of course, give priority to any reports requested by the USG. However, there are several countries where the League has independently determined that cooperative development programs might be highly fruitful. The League should be free to evaluate those situations and to transmit its evaluations to AID even when AID has not asked it to do so. AID need not agree with the evaluations. On the other hand, it might find that the reports of experts who are dedicating full time to this subject are valuable contributions toward the formulation of sound cooperative assistance programs. 

Special attention should also be called to the fact that the preparation of even a preliminary report may require a prior visit by League analysts to the country concerned. The information available in Washington may seem to suggest the desirability of a cooperative development program, but that information is often not sufficiently complete or reliable to serve as the basis for either policy or operational decisions. Additional investigation in the field will frequently be necessary for the purpose of arriving at preliminary recommendations. 

2. Whenever the government of an LDC or the leaders of its private cooperative movement request the assistance of the League in formulating proposals for promoting cooperative development, the League should send the necessary personnel to that country for this purpose, unless advised by the USG that such assistance would be clearly undesirable. In every case, the League would seek the initial and continuous policy guidance of the USG concerning the scope and directions of cooperative development for which the USG might be willing to consider some form of support. After providing the requested technical assist-

ance (and, when appropriate, even during the period that the assistance is being provided), the League will submit one or more preliminary reports to AID reflecting the League's views with regard to the promotion of cooperative development in that country. Such reports would reflect a careful analysis of cooperative activity and potential with appropriate contact being made with domestic cooperative organizations and key personnel for guidance. Where a 

League cooperative generalist is attached to the pertinent USAID, the number of personnel sent to that country may, of course, be reduced. 

B. AID Response to Basic Reports 

As soon as possible after the League has submitted a preliminary report to AID concerning a suggested cooperative development program, AID should give serious Agency consideration to that report from the following points of view: (1) the soundness of its general conclusions and recommendations in the light of USG policy objectives; (2) the extent to which any USG endorsement of the recommendations could be effected without requiring significant modification of AID's current and prospective assistance programs; (3) the extent to which any USG endorsement would require modification of AID's current or prospective assistance programs; and (4) the desirability of asking the League to undertake whatever additional work may be necessary for the purpose of refining its preliminary recommendations so as to convert them into detailed recommendations for a specific action program or project. Such "refinements" could include revisions which AID considers desirable in order to reflect USG policy objectives more accurately or in order to minimize modifications in AID's current or prospective assistance programs. 

C. Refining of Basic Reports 

As soon as possible after AID has evaluated a preliminary report, it should invite the League to a detailed discussion of the subject. Such discussions would hopefully culminate in requests that the League refine its preliminary recommendations as suggested above, although they would be useful even for the purpose of providing improved guidance to the League in the preparation of subsequent preliminary reports. If asked to refine its recommendations, the 

League would then enter into detailed consultations with AID officials in Washington and/or with the pertinent USAID officials. If considered desirable—as may usually be the case-the League would be requested to enter into detailed consultations with the government of an LDC or its private cooperative movement before, or during, the League's consultations with the USAID. 

D. Adoption of Program to be Implemented 

Agreement having been reached between AID and the League concerning the components of the desired program for cooperative development in a given LDC, AID would determine the most appropriate strategy for encouraging the LDC to adopt the program. No single strategy will be appropriate for all the LDC's. 

In certain situations, for example, USAID might consider it desirable to negotiate directly with the host government, without participation of either the League or the private cooperative movement of that country. It appears likelyand this usually has been the case that AID will ordinarily desire some form of direct or indirect participation by both the League and the local cooperative movement. The League would participate to the extent desired both in facilitating the adoption of a specific program and in providing the technical assistance necessary for its implementation, except in cases where implementation would more obviously be appropriate by other cooperative contractors (viz., CUNA, NRECA, FCH, the Farmers' Union, ICDA, etc.) or even by groups from other countries, such as Sweden, Argentina, or Israel. 

III. Action required by AID 

The League is a non-profit organization. It cannot reasonably be expected that it could succeed, without USG support, in successful promotion of cooperatives in the LDC's on the scale and for the purposes which are important to USG policy objectives abroad. If the League is to be successful as AID apparently desires that it be, it must have AID support of the following types: 

Salt A. Circular Instructions to the AID Missions urging: 

1. That, upon preparing country assistance programs, they give high priority to sub-programs for cooperative development, especially in the rural areas. It is recognized that AID/W has encouraged the Missions to program for cooperative development among other efforts to fulfill the objectives of Title IX. However,

the League does not know of any AID/W instruction which adequately stresses the high priority warranted for sound rural cooperative development programs. 

2. That whenever any Mission believes that promotion of cooperative development in the country to which it is accredited might reasonably be expected to further the achievement of USG policy objectives, the USAID should ask the League to prepare a preliminary report of the type indicated in Sections I and II above, unless the Mission has well-qualified, direct-hire personnel on its staff or has access to qualified contract personnel who can devote the necessary time to the preparation of such a report. 

3. That each Mission provide all necessary facilitation to visits by League personnel whenever (a) the League finds such visits to be necessary for the purpose of preparing preliminary reports in accordance with II, A; or (b) the host government or the leaders of its private cooperative movement request the assistance of the League in formulating proposals for promoting cooperative development, unless the USAID believes that such assistance would be undesirable. It would be understood that the necessary facilitation would ordinarily-but not necessarily in all cases-include USAID financing of the visits which are requested either by the USAID itself or by the host government. Visits undertaken at the initiative of the League or upon the request of the private cooperative movement of an LDC would ordinarily be financed out of the League's worldwide or regional task orders. 

4. That each Mission collaborate closely with the League. This collaboration would include (a) guidance to the League, as indicated in II-A; (b) full and prompt consideration to any League reports, as indicated in II B, (c) prompt transmission of its views concerning any League reports to AID/W or directly to the League, for the purposes discussed in II C, and (d) furtherance of any AID-League understandings as indicated in II D. It may be noted also that the 

League held a brief discussion with USAID Dominican Republic in October 1967 concerning Annex #1 and Annex #3 and that the USAID expressed a desire to hold more definitive discussions shortly thereafter, but that the League has not yet received any further advice in this regard. 

B. Instructions to Specific AID Missions, Recommending: 

1. Such modifications in their country assistance programs as AID/W may consider desirable for the promotion of cooperative development. The League has no desire to diminish the programming authority of the USAID's. However, it is understandable that some USAID's may be very reluctant to reduce on-going projects or sub-programs beyond the limits of the reductions being forced upon them by Congressional action. However, much as USAID may be impressed by a proposed cooperative development program or any other new program, it is understandable that the USAID might be reluctant to allocate part of its reduced funds for such a program. For example, the League has received informal and unofficial indications that it is their concern over budgetary limitations that has deterred USAID/Dominican Republic from serious consideration or approval of the League's proposals for general or additional cooperative development in those countries. Transmission by AID/W of the circular instructions recommended under III A might correct this situation. However, specific instructions to separate USAID's might be necessary in certain situations. 

2. That USAID/Brazil, USAID/Colombia, USAID/Peru and ROCAP (and possibly other Missions subsequently) ask the League to assign a representative to work with each Mission (at the Mission expense for the contract service) in the formulation of general programs and projects for cooperative development, including programs and projects which might be undertaken by CUNA, FCH, NRECA, Farmers' Union, ICDA, and other U.S. cooperative groups.

C. Direct Support of League Activities by AID/W as follows: 

1.

Approval of the budget proposed by the League (Annex 4) to permit effective fulfillment of its responsibilities in Latin America under Task Order No. 2. We have assumed, in our budget approval that AID/W will soon approve amalgamation under T.O. No. 2 of the cooperative bank development program, previously covered by the Latin American regional contract with the Fund for International Development. As shown in our proposal, we believe that this amalgamation will not only improve the procedures of programming for cooperative development, 'but will permit more effective use of the personnel heretofore directed to separate, and somewhat overlapping activities We have made a sincere effort to hold down our budgetary request for the purpose, among others, of adapting the request to the exigencies of AID's overall budgetary situation. However, our experience

with regard to Latin America has demonstrated that, while some personnel savings can be made (as we are proposing), there must be an increase in other personnel costs (as we also are proposing), if we are to be able to do the job that 

AID would expect of us. 

2. Prompt authorization should hereafter be given to League personnel to travel to any of the LDC's under T. O. No. 2 funding, whenever (a) the League considers such travel necessary for either the determination of whether there are reasonable grounds for consideration of a cooperative development program or project in a given country or for the sound preparation of a preliminary report concerning such a program or project, or (b) the leaders of the cooperative movement in a given LDC ask the League (without USAID dissent) to help them formulate a proposal for promoting cooperative development. 

The ability of the League to schedule its own activities efficiently, including its ability to collaborate effectively with the private cooperative movements of other countries, often depends upon prompt receipt of travel authorizaions from AID/W, but such authorizations have not always been approved as rapidly as necessary. We sympathize with the desire of some AID/W officials that USAID funds be used to finance travel by League personnel to the extent practical. However, we do not consider it practical to require that whenever the League wants to send anyone to an LDC-even for the purposes indicated here the pertinent USAID must first be persuaded to spend its own limited funds for such travel, even though the travel allocations in the T. O. No. 2 budget are adequate for those purposes. There may be some significance in the question of whether a given trip is financed from USAID funds, but it does not seem to us to be reasonable to delay or otherwise to impair the effectiveness of travel by League personnel because of this kind of issue. 

13. AID/W desk officers, economic officers and other interested officials, including those concerned directly with cooperative development problems, should be encouraged to focus more intensively, than has been the case with some of them, upon the details of the League's work in this field. We believe that this would' have the effect, among others, of enabling AID/W to arrive at more accurate evaluations of the high priority warranted by sound cooperative development programs. Moreover, the readiness of USAID officials in the field to recommend' the allocation of funds for cooperative development programs may be increased, or reduced, by their impressions as to the receptivity which such recommendations are likely to encounter among the responsible officials in Washington.

STATEMENT OF ANGUS MCDONALD, DIRECTOR OF RESEARCH, NATIONAL FARMERS 

UNION Mr. Chairman and Members of the Committee, I appear here in support of 

S. 3091, the Foreign Assistance Act of 1968, which would authorize the expenditure of two and one-half billion dollars for the Agency for International Development. Members of this Committee are aware that the Farmers Union has supported foreign aid programs for many years. Although we are aware that there have been many mistakes and some inefficiency, we think, in such an enormous program, that some mistakes are inevitable.

We emphasize the point that this Nation should not shirk its responsibilities: to other people around the world. We live in a world which, as has been pointed out possibly thousands of times, is a world of hunger. Two-thirds of the people on this earth suffer from malnutrition and in millions of instances from acute starvation.

About sixty-five million human beings are added to the world's population every day. This amounts approximately to the population of Great Britain or 

West Germany. I repeat, our Nation has a responsibility to do all in its power to bring food to these hungry people and to make it possible for them to develop an agriculture which will supply sufficient food for their needs.

We are also aware of the pressures that this Committee, other Committees and all Congressmen are subjected to. The Administration, unfortunately, has been unable to balance the budget in recent years and this coming year faces a bigger deficit than ever. We are not only aware of the facts of budget deficits and the need for economy, but we feel strongly that short-sighted, false economy is not the course which the Congress should take at this time. 

The Congress no doubt, and particularly this Committee, is aware of the hysterical outcries which have emanated from an individual in a very high place in the administration of the Nation's monetary system. It has been suggested by this individual that because of the prospect of bigger and bigger deficits that we faced uncontrollable deficits and an uncontrollable depression. 

We face, in other words, financial bankruptcy as well as economic ruin. These statements come at a time when the Gross National Product is the highest in history and at a time when the GNP, during the first quarter of this year, exceeded the most optimistic expectations. 

I know that the Members of this Committee will want to take a hard look at the facts relating to our financial, monetary and economic situation before they take at face value such statements which, if acted on, would wreck the foreign aid programs as well as other domestic programs. 

According to a Committee Print titled, "Background Material, Foreign As sistance Act, Fiscal Year 1969", dated March 21, 1968, the Agency for International Development appropriation recommended by the President amounts to only a little over three-tenths of one percent of the GNP of the Nation. Since this calculation was made, the GNP, according to Economic Indicators, April 1968, prepared for the Joint Economic Committeee by the Council of Economic Advisers, has risen during the first quarter to an annual rate of 827.3 billion dollars. Disposable personal income, according to this publication, has risen to 571.7 billion dollars for the same period. Corporate profits have risen to a record of 85 billion dollars.

Assuming that the proportion of profits after taxes remains the same, corporations are receiving in undistributed profits, considerably more than 25 billion dollars a year. During the year 1967, after all taxes were paid and dividends distributed, corporations, according to the President's Council of Economic Advisers, realized 24.7 billion dollars in undistributed profits. 

I have made calculation based on the 827.3 billion dollar estimated Gross 

National Product, and the amount recommended by the President comes to 0.00302 percent. In other words, the amount recommended amounts to only three-tenths of one percent, plus 2/100 of one percent of the GNP.

The amount recommended by the President in relation to the 571.7 billion dollars of disposable personal income comes to .00438 percent-or four-tenths and 

38/100 of one percent.

To put the matter of deficits and expenditures in perspective, the Committee should take cognizance of the facts in the following tables which pertain to the GNP, public debt as a percentage of the GNP and annual deficits for the years 1941-47 and 1961-67: [Dollar amounts in billions] Gross national product
