As is evident from the figures in these tables, the GNP was, during the period of World War II, about one-third of the GNP during the last few years. Annual deficits during the World War period ranged from two and one-half billion dollars in 1942 to a high of 53.9 billion dollars in 1945. Yet we are supposed to be frightened by prophesies of chaos because our present deficit is expected to reach between 20 and 30 billion dollars in fiscal 1969. Farmers Union does not suggest that the Congress should not enact a tax bill to bring about a better relationship between income and expenditures. 

The Farmers Union Executive Committee has endorsed the President's recommendation in regard to taxes, but we do not think that any Member of Congress should be scared by false economy appeals. Neither do we think that domestic programs should be sacrificed nor that American farmers should be penalized because of the export of food in international programs. 

In a recent address, our National President Tony T. Dechant commented on the question of "What the World Food Problem Means to the Farmer," as follows: 

“Agriculture gains every year in terms of farm commodity exports and use of food in international programs. Yet farmers still are forced to settle for income levels that lag far behind other sectors of our fast-moving economy." 

We have reviewed a great deal of testimony presented to the House Committee on Foreign Affairs during the period February 28 through March 25, 1968. We are particularly pleased that the Administrator of the program is attempting to make loans at as low a rate of interest as possible. In response to a question from 

Congressman Gross, Mr. Gaud, Administrator of AID said: "I think the interest rate ought to be three-quarters of one percent, just as the IDA rate is." 

Mr. GROSS. "Three-quarters of one percent?" 

Mr. GAUD. "Yes, sir." 

Mr. GROSS. "An interest rate?" 

Mr. GAUD. "Yes, sir." 

Mr. GROSS. "On a 30-year loan, or a 40-year loan?"

Mr. GAUD. "Right. We have had a good deal of discussion this morning about the debt burden which hard loans impose on these countries." 

We were also pleased at the reports which were made in regard to agricultural efficiency. In several countries it is expected that in a few years the efforts of AID will result in sufficient food being produced to feed the people in countries where malnutrition and starvation have been chronic for many years. 

We note that there is a continuing emphasis on attempts to encourage democratic, private and local institutions. The Congress has spelled out such a policy in Public Law 89-583 as follows: 

"TITLE IX-UTILIZATION OF DEMOCRATIC INSTITUTIONS IN DEVELOPMENT 

"Sec. 281. In carrying out programs authorized in this chapter, emphasis shall be placed on assuring maximum participation in the task of economic development on the part of the people of the developing countries, through the encour agement of democratic private and local governmental institutions " 

The National Vice President of Farmers Union, Edwin H. Christianson, testi fying before the House Foreign Affairs Committee on May 1, 1967, pointed out that the House of Representatives had set forth an explicit policy of the encouragement of cooperatives and labor unions in the administration of AID programs. This policy was first inaugurated in a provision inserted by Vice 

President Humphrey when, as a U.S. Senator, he had included in the Foreign 

Assistance Act of 1961 a section that provides that ". it is declared to be the policy of the U.S. . . . to encourage the development and use of cooperatives, credit unions, and savings and loan associations . . as part of our foreign aid program. In the cooperative idea, we are exporting one of the finest products of a democratic society. And the cooperative program is not one of dollars, but of people." 

This policy is entirely consistent with resolutions adopted by the National Farmers Union over a long period of years. We have not only endorsed such policies, but have attempted to bring about their implementation by actively participating in AID programs. 

On April 1, 1963, the Agency for International Development signed a global contract with the National Farmers Union under which that organization agreed to provide technicians for cooperative management, organization and development, and for assistance to democratic farmer institutions. 

Under this program the Farmers Union has provided 54 such technicians and has served directly through programs in five countries in Africa (Uganda, Kenya,

Tunisia, Liberia, and Dahomey); five countries in Latin America (Chile, Peru, Bolivia, Venezuela and Colombia); three countries in the Far East (Philippines, South Vietnam, and Thailand); and two countries in the Near East South Asia (India and Nepal). In addition, indirect service has been provided by training within the United States to those same Latin American countries, specifically to their campesino organizational leadership, and to seven countries in Africa (Togo, Senegal, Ivory Coast, Upper Volta, Dahomey, Mauritania, and Niger) in the training of cooperative management. 

During the past five years 315 leaders from the named Latin American countries have received training in rural community development, cooperatives, and farm organizational practices, with the National Farmers Union contributing to this program of training over $200,000 in a combination of direct cash contributions, services and in-kind support. boot a bronze 80 10

The "Report to Congress" prepared by the Agency for International Development, Department of State, on the fiscal year 1967, the Sixth Annual Report to the Congress, documents in detail the scope and the accomplishments of both the in-country programs and the training programs in the United States in which the National Farmers Union has been involved. 

However, providing a more demonstrable and detailed report which would be of interest, I am sure, to this Committee and the people of the United States, are illustrative reports of progress the first, a program of assistance to the rapidly developing cooperative movement of Uganda to which this organization has provided a team of technicians under AID contract since 1964. During that period of time the membership in primary societies providing cooperative marketing and supply machinery has grown from 400,000 to 549,000 members. The total amount of cooperative marketing of crops through these societies has increased from $45,000,000 in 1964 to well over $55,000,000 as an estimated figure for the 1967 crop year. The amount of marketing of key crops percentagewise by the cooperatives in the Country has grown from 60 percent cotton in 1964 to an estimated 80 percent in 1967. The Aribaca coffee marketed cooperatively, largely for export in the Country, now exceeds 93 percent. The sales volume of the supply cooperatives has grown from $746,000 volume in 1964 to $1,746,000 in 1967. 

A broad program of agricultural credit extended through credit societies has resulted from the number of societies from 279 in 1964 to 318 (less than 100 in 1962) with increases in the average loans of 50 percent in those four years and a loan capital increase of 35 percent.

The depth of the cooperative action in Uganda is providing an increasingly sound economic base in the countryside involving farmers in their own economical future, providing a capitalizing process through rural credit societies, effectively yardsticking prices to producers, and has served to stimulate an increase in the production of food crops, including dairy and livestock. 

A second illustration of the effectivenes of this type of international assistance can be drawn from the Philippines. Since the early 1950's, the Philippines has made a great effort to encourage cooperative marketing structure, primarily in rice. Included in these efforts was a program of agricultural credit through the 

Agricultural Credit Administration of the Philippine Government. Over the years this program has been confronted with many difficulties, both in terms of marketing cooperatives (FACOMAS) and the effective distribution and collection of agricultural credit. The Farmers Union/AID program of assistance in early 1965 was confronted with a history of failing FACOMAS (Farmers Cooperative Marketing Associations), and an adverse collection record through the agricultural credit administration.

The most recent report (after these three years of assistance in re-building from the Agricultural Credit Administration of the Philippines) indicates that both the institution and credit procedures have been greatly improved, and over the past two years the Agricultural Credit Administration has operated in the black with a great reduction in the percentage of delinquent loans, particularly among those linked to the market associations.

Even more significant progress was made in the area of institutional building, where for the first time in the Philippines 28 of the strong viable farmers' marketing associations in 1967 voluntarily joined themselves together to create a non-governmental regional marketing cooperative called GRAMACOP, now beginning to function effectively for producers. 

A third capsule report involves the significant program in North East Thailand involving 455 clubs and a combined membership of over 17,000 farmers. This is a sensitive area of the country and is the beginning of the involvement of farmers,

through their own marketing credit and supply associations, in which their economic position is being improved and their credit is beng expanded through commercial channels with an estimated 95 percent repayment of 1967 loans to over 11,000 farmers.

The significance of this program in Northeast Thailand (only moderately developed agricultural section of the Country) is that these some 455 local associations are developing their own economic interests on a democratic basis the type of program which might have some years ago prevented some of the problems which have grown up in South Vietnam. 

These three capsule reports are representative of significant gains in the building of peoples' institutions that have occurred in country after country where such cooperative assistance, as is being performed by this organization, has become part of our foreign assistance efforts. 

In reviewing testimony presented before the House Agriculture Committee we note that not only have cooperatives made considerable headway on a small scale in various countries as these examples indicate, but that in one country they play a leading role. I quote from a report presented by an AID official entitled "Cooperatives in Tanzania": "The cooperative movement plays a central role in the Tanzanian economy, handling almost all of the country's exports (except sisal) and a substantial portion of the domestic economy. By far the largest portion of its activities are involved with the marketing of agricultural produce but preliminary excursions have also been made into the transport, wholesale, retail, and import fields. Cotton ginning, coffee and tobacco curing are entirely carried out by the cooperatives." 

We commend the Agency for International Development for its proposed increasing use of agricultural technicians from the United States. Employment of technicians has increased greatly according to AID officials in the last few years. 

Specifically, at the end of 1967, employment of overseas agricultural technicians stood at 1,385, of whom 607 were AID employees, 227 U.S. Department of Agriculture employees, and 551 university contract employees.

Technicians from the University of Illinois, for example, will concentrate on plant protection; University of Kansas on grain handling and marketing; University of Missouri on plant breeding; University of Ohio on soil fertility and water relationships; University of Pennsylvania on crop production and seed technology and the University of Tennessee on agricultural economics. 

During all of the history of the administration of foreign aid programs, much information has been developed in regard to inequitable land systems in backward and undeveloped countries. It appears that one of the major causes of retarded agricultural development resulting in malnutrition and starvation has been the fact that ownership of land has been concentrated in a very few hands, rents have been exorbitant and interest rates very high. We feel that the problems of land ownership, agricultural production and distribution must be solved in various countries if the goals of agricultural efficiency and physical wellbeing are to be achieved.

It is reported that in various instances land owners and other vested interests systematically block reforms and keep peasants in a state of bondage. Administrators of the AID program must find a way to bring about various reforms not only in land distribution and ownership, but in education and social and political organization.

According to Administrator Gaud, Latin America is in the midst of a revolution. He discussed at some length before the House Committee, reforms and social and political relationships, but devotes little attention to land reform which, in agricultural societies must form a basis for all other reforms. Although some progress has been made in agrarian reform, in Chili, where 8,360 new settler families have been established since 1965, the silence of administrators in regard to land reform in many other countries leads us to the inference that little or nothing has been done. Significantly, in the proposed budget for 1969, only 1.7 million dollars has been allocated for land reform purposes. This amounts to only about 2/100ths of one percent of the funds requested for the entire program.

We hope that the Committee will approve the program recommended by the President as expeditiously as possible, although we are not prepared to justify other than agricultural parts of the program. However, it is well known that limited industrial development must go hand-in-hand with agricultural progress. 

As agricultural efficiency is increased, more and more workers should be able to find places in industry. The way should be paved for a shift from agriculture to industry as countries become more self-sufficient.

STATEMENT OF WILLIAM H. DRAPER, JR. WO vient dg 

Mr. Chairman, my name is William H. Draper, Jr.

, I serve as National Chair man of the Population Crisis Committee, as Honorary Vice Chairman of Planned 

Parenthood in this country and as Special Representative of the International Planned Parenthood Federation. However, I am testifying today as an interested individual who has been working in this field on a purely voluntary basis for a number of years and not as an official spokesman for any organization. 

Last year when I testified before this Committee with Richard Gardner, my colleague and I were deeply concerned over multiplying world population problems and somewhat critical that the United States was not doing all it could to help the developing nations recognize and deal with the problem. 

At that time, Richard Gardner, who had chaired a White House Committee on Population, testified that there was a tremendous "family planning gap" in the world. He estimated that about 140 million women in the less developed countries wished to limit their family size but did not have the information, facilities, and medical guidance necessary to do so. 

To help reach these women and provide them with voluntary family planning services has been essentially the objective of the United States Government, through AID, and of all the other public and private assistance efforts in family planning. To this end AID obligated $5,487,192 for family planning and population programs in FY 1966. In 1967, only $4,252,988 was obligated. We were understandably much concerned that AID was spending less than a quarter of one percent of its appropriation on population programs at a time when population growth rates of 2½% to 3% were preventing both national and individual prosperity in two thirds of the world.

I am happy to report today that this situation has changed. Under the capable leadership of the AID Administrator, William S. Gaud, and with the enlightened encouragement of the Congress, and especially of this Committee, strong population programs are being developed, with AID's help, in a large number of countries.

To make the best possible use of the $35 million earmarked by the Congress for this purpose last year, Mr. Gaud circularized Embassies and AID Missions around the world to determine which governments and indigenous family planning efforts desired to expand their programs in this area. The results of Mr. 

Gaud's initiative have been impressive, indeed. I understand that more than $50 million has been requested to expand the family planning activities in a great many countries. Mr. Gaud has emphasized, as I do, that family planning programs must be voluntary. They must be undertaken and run on local initiatives by the government or citizens of the country involved. These programs should not and cannot be forced upon people by foreigners or even by their own governments. Family planning must develop with grass roots support and as a result of individual free choice.

AID is now, I believe, very competently filling the role assigned it under Title X of last year's Foreign Assistance Act. Mr. Gaud testified before Senator Gruening's Sub-committee recently that family planning and increased food production have been assigned the highest priortiy among all of AID's programs. He has also increased the staffing for population activities and has expedited the processing of new programs in the population field. AID has now established a pattern of assistance that may well be a model for future AID programs. Mr. Gaud, his War on Hunger and the entire Agency are to be congratulated. 

Already, five other governments are offering assistance internationally in population and family planning. Sweden, which was the first, had a $5 million overseas assistance program in population and family planning in 1967. On per capita basis that would have been the equivalent of a U.S. Program in excess of $100 million. The governments of Great Britain, Denmark, Norway and the 

Netherlands have also helped by making grants to the International Planned Parenthood Federation. IPPF, which is recognized by the United Nations as an official spokesman for the family planning movement, now has 54 members in 54 countries and provides private assistance on a worldwide basis. Often this help is given in countries where there is as yet no government program and therefore no opportunity for AID to give government to government assistance. 

In this way, AID helps governments with population policies or programs by direct government to government grants, and assists IPPF, the Population Council, and other private organizations to carry on important family planning work in many other countries where only private programs are yet underway.

Altogether, the last year has shown a real awakening on the part of governments, prviate organizations, corporations and individuals to the terrible and growing threat of the population explosion, together with a new willingness to do something about it.

I think that this Committee, which has taken such a leading part in this awakening, the Congress itself and the AID Agency, under Mr. William Gaud, Mr. Rutherford Poats, and Dr. Reimert Ravenholt, who heads its Population 

Division, and all of their associates can be proud that a good start has been made.

Recently, Mr. Gaud testified that 26 developing countries have adopted official family planning policies. The United Nations have reported a comparable number, with 15 having some form of governmental program and 10 governments giving support to private programs. Ten additional countries are also listed by the 

United Nations as now moving toward definite action in this field.* The 35 countries are listed below and include 55% of the world's population and 73% of the developing areas' population. 

The United Nations General Assembly has unanimously urged an expanded UN program; Secretary-General U Thant has strongly endorsed this objective. 

He stated recently that "the most urgent conflict confronting the world today is ... between the pace of growth of the human race and the insufficient growth in resources needed to support mankind in peace, prosperity and dignity." 

He has already established a special Trust Fund to stimulate and assist programs relating to population growth, and I look for a growing share of the UN budget and the budgets of its associated agencies to be devoted to this purpose. 

Since I became Chairman of the Population Crisis Committee on January 1, 1966, less than 2½ years ago, I have personally visited 29 countries to check on their progress in dealing with their population problems or to attend conferences on this subject. I can attest, from my own observations, that great progress has been made, particularly during the past year, and particularly in Latin America. A considerable share of this progress can be credited to the increased interest and activity of the United States Government. 

But the problem is still far from solved. Population growth averages 22% each year in the developing countries and up to 3% and 4% in parts of Latin America. Food production in this decade has been increasing by barely 1%. 

This year, with ideal weather conditions, crops will be good-better than ever before but, as Dr. B. R. Sen, former Director-General of the Food and Agriculture Organization of United Nations has said: "One bad season of droughts and poor harvests, such as we had in 1965, is enough to show the precarious condition in which hundreds of millions must always face the morrow."

Death rates are still falling in many developing countries as disease eradication and better health knowledge spread. Unless birth rates can fall even more rapidly, the so-called Development Decade of the 60's may well give way to a Decade of Starvation in the 70's. Or, equally alarming, the human race may face a future in which a large percentage of its members will face permanent mental and physical retardation because of constant, inescapable malnutrition and protein deficiency.

There is hope. The early family planning programs initiated on a voluntary basis, but now receiving effective government and increased private cooperation, are beginning to show real results. United Nations statistics show that in Taiwan, birth rates have fallen from 44 per thousand in 1956 to 32 in 1966; in Hong Kong, they have fallen from 39 in 1956 to 24 in 1966; in Singapore from 48 in 1956 to 

29 in 1966; in Ceylon from 36 to 32; and in Korea, a developing country with over 

30 millions of people, birth rates have fallen from 45 to 39 under the impetus of a strong government program, supported by a broad and still growing private effort. Here in our own hemisphere, in Puerto Rico, the birth rate has fallen from 

34 to 25 per thousand in the last decade. A comparatively few years of careful 

*(1) Countries with official policies or programs: India, Pakistan, Mainland China, 

South Korea, Malaysia, Ceylon, Singapore, Tunisia, United Arab Republic, Kenya, Mauritius, Morocco, Turkey, Fiji, and Jamaica; (2) countries with private programs receiving government support: Taiwan, Barbados, Chile, Colombia, Guatemala, Honduras, Hong

Kong, Peru, Thailand, and Venezuela; and (3) countries moving toward greater interest and action: Afghanistan, Costa Rica, Ghana, Indonesia, Iran, Nepal, Nigeria, Philippines,

Puerto Rico, and Rhodesia. 

Source: Commission for Social Development, United Nations Economic and Social Council, 1967 Report on the World Social Situation, New York, February 1968.

programming, education and medical improvement in these countries have reduced the rate of new births by an average of about 1%. 

There is increasing evidence that family planning programs are welcomed by more and more potential users in developing countries, and that they do serve their patients and their overall national objectives well. India and Pakistan are also moving toward effective programs, but their populations and their problems are on a much larger scale and will take longer to show overall results. In both countries the family planning programs have stronger governmental support and are headed by able and enthusiastic leaders who are deeply convinced of the importance of their work. 

Last year the Congress, as a matter of policy, gave to family planning and to agricultural production programs the highest priority in AID legislation. $35 million was earmarked for programs relating to population growth. You may recall that three years ago, in 1965, a White House Committee recommended the allocation of $100 million a year for three years for AID's worldwide family planning program. But worldwide programs cannot move into high gear overnight. In my personal judgment, however, at least $50 million could be wisely expended in 1969 to continue the expansion and effectiveness of family planning programs in the developing countires. On the domestic side, for comparison, about $35 million is also being programmed this year for family planning work and research here in the United States. But President Johnson has now requested $61 million for services and $12 million for research as a part of the overall 

U.S. health program for fiscal 1969. 

Family planning as a part of our overall economic assistance to developing countries has been given a very high priority both by the Congress and by the Executive Branch. There is no question in my mind that substantially lower birth rates are a necessary element or catalyst in bringing about satisfactory rates of economic development. However, lower rates of population growth cannot by themselves solve the many agricultural and industrial problems faced by the "have-not" nations, nor will they alone bring about satisfactory economic progress. It is essential that programs for improved education, agriculture and industrialization be also carried out if the developing countries are to share in the prosperity that modern technology makes possible. While population programs must continue to have high priority and to expand steadily, I nevertheless believe that our national objectives throughout the developing world can only be achieved with a strong program of overall economic assistance. In our own best interests, we should all give greater support to the entire foreign aid program than we have in recent years.

STATEMENT BY JOHN T. CALDWELL, NATIONAL ASSOCIATION OF STATE UNIVERSITIES AND LAND GRANT COLLEGES 

This statement in support of the Foreign Aid Authorization for 1969 is presented on behalf of the National Association of State Universities and Land-Grant 

Colleges whose membership comprises all of the Land-Grant universities, Sixtyeight in number, and thirty-two of the major state, but non-Land-Grant univer sities. From the time of President Truman's announcement of the Point IV pro gram in January 1949, followed immediately by a pledge of full support from John Hannah as the then President of our association, there has developed an increasing commitment by a growing number of our member universities in foreign assistance activities. 

The Land-Grant universities, in particular, are by historical design and experience dedicated to the performance of research, resident instruction, and extension in agriculture to the end that our food supplies may be abundant, nutritious, wholesome, varied, and economical. Their success in the United States has been so notable in these respects that it is clearly understandable why the less developed countries have eagerly sought their counsel and assistance. Moreover, it is generally accepted that the development of a sound economy in a less developed country requires a firm and efficient agricultural base. 

With widespread recognition that world food shortages are destined to become even more acute despite outstanding recent achievements by agricultural scientists, there is urgent need for intensification of the effort to help the food deficit countries find the means to become much more nearly self-sufficient. This need will persist until family planning is widely accepted and adopted.

In the arena of educational development and of technical assistance the universities can offer to the developing countries invaluable assistance of a kind that will contribute greatly to improvement of the lot of vast numbers of the world population and concurrently add much to world-wide political stability and prospects of our own long-term peaceful existence. 

However, the universities would be impotent without a Federal agency with adequate financial backing to provide a functioning mechanism. At present this mechanism resides in the Agency for International Development authorized by the Foreign Assistance Act. As in earlier years we again urge your thoughtful attention to both the need and the prospects of achievement that make imperative the continuance of a major foreign aid authorization. Indeed, in certain areas a significant expansion in the authorized funds is urgent. We would, in particular, encourage greater emphasis on technical assistance and an increase in the authorization for those activities covered in Section 211(d). You may recall that this was the subject of a formal recommendation adopted by the Senate of our Association during the annual convention held in November 1966, and transmitted to your Committee shortly thereafter. Inasmuch as the recommendation is no less important today than it was at that time and since it has not been implemented we restate it here. "That the Association take note of the enactment of important new national policies in the field of international education and technical assistance by the last Congress. The International Education Act of 1966 and Section 211(d) of the Foreign Assistance Act of 1966 contain important new provisions for enhancing the competence of American higher education to serve in the building of international understanding and carrying out of international programs. We point out, however, with respect to Section 211(d), which provides grants to institutions to strengthen their competence to perform technical assistance activities, that the $10 million authorized is small in proportion to the real need. 

We would recommend, therefore, that the Congress and the President undertake at the earliest opportunity to enlarge this authorization and appropriate funds many times the $10 million, as presently authorized." 

Although emphasis has been placed on agriculture and the urgent need to bring world food supplies into a present and future balance with population as a first priority effort, it would be a gross oversight not to mention the reservoir of talent available in the member universities of our Association that pertains to other vital professions. Under AID contracts many of the universities have been assisting less developed countries in such areas as intermediate education, health professions, engineering, marketing, political science and others. All of these can contribute to the enhancement of social and economic well-being in the less privileged countries of the world and thereby provide much greater assurance of a peaceful world in which we can continue to enjoy a life of significance and satisfaction.

We are confident that our universities can and will use their best efforts in the overall foreign assistance program of the United States if the Congress will provide the necessary authorizations and funds. 

NEWARK, DEL., May 7, 1968. 

Hon. J. W. FULBRIGHT, U.S. Senate, 

Washington, D.C.

DEAR SIR: In reference to the Foreign Assistance Act of 1968 (H.R. 15263S. 3091), the World Problems Study Group of the Newark Branch (Del.) of the 

American Association of University Women would like you to know that we are in support of the economic and social development programs made possible under this legislation. We believe there should be a continued emphasis upon selfhelp, multilateralism, priority on agricultural and population planning, and efficient administration.

We would like this statement to be included in the record of the hearings for this bill. Thank you very much. Sincerely,

ANNETTE GRUBER, Chairman.

STATEMENT BY MRS. ROBERT J. STUART, PRESIDENT, LEAGUE OF WOMEN VOTERS OF THE UNITED STATES 

The League of Women Voters of the United States is a volunteer citizens' organization of over 145,000 members in 50 states, the District of Columbia, and Puerto Rico. We devote our energies to governmental issues on local, state, and national levels.

The League wishes to express its vigorous support for the development assistance aspects of the Foreign Assistance Act of 1968. We ask this committee to recommend a bill authorizing the full amounts requested for the heart of the aid program: development loans, technical assistance, the Alliance for Progress, and contributions to international organizations. 

This is not an ordinary year in the history of aid legislation. Circumstances not linked with aid goals are forcing contraction in aid funds. We are also aware of the dilemma in priorities. Nevertheless, we do not want to see this country lose sight of its long-term development goals or erode the gains of previous efforts. We cannot be content with what amounts to any further drastic curtailment of a program we consider to be sound national and international policy. 

We believe that the culling process has been done more than adequately at the budget-making stage.

We would also like to express our disappointment that economic and military aid are once again covered in the same legislation. 

The League is primarily concerned with "development." We would heartily endorse congressional consideration of development assistance separate from military assistance because we believe that there is great public confusion and misunderstanding of the differences between them. If development assistance were given separate consideration, the fundamental issue of the proper use of human and material resources for international economic development could then be judged in its own right.

In view of the critical atmosphere surrounding foreign aid in 1968, we wish to focus our statement on three pertinent points: the continuing and pressing need for development aid; the justification for the program which has emerged in the successes achieved in the last decade; and the relationship between the aid program and the U.S. balance-of-payments deficit. 

The U.S. Gross National Product was $785 billion in 1967 and $743.3 billion in 1966 a gain in one year of nearly $42 billion. Our GNP is far larger than that of any other industrial nation, and the total annual increase is larger than innumerable developing nations' total GNP. The United States, still the richest nation, has dropped to fifth among members of the Development Assistance Committee of the OECD in share of net income devoted to official aid. Despite the U.N. Development Decade goal of 1 percent of the developed nations' Gross 

National Product to go for development aid, U.S. aid now totals 0.5 percent, less than one half of the goal to which this nation pledged itself. It is not surprising, therefore, that the genuineness of the U.S. commitment to international development is being questioned.

That there is a very real need for economic and technical assistance to the developing countries can hardly be denied. The World Bank estimates that an additional $3 billion to $4 billion a year in new capital investment could readily be absorbed by the less developed countries. Statistics, however, are cold facts. The real needs become apparent only when one recognizes the despair resulting from an increasing gap between aspiration and living standards of those who populate many of the developing nations of the world. This situation is not only intolerable but dangerous. Speaking to the United Nations Conference on Trade and Development (UNCTAD) just before his departure as World Bank president, George Woods warned of the possibility of an "intolerable economic and political fission of the world." The logical alternative is to stimulate the growth of viable economies in developing nations, to reverse the growing split between the rich and poor nations. 

The need for development aid is overwhelming. The momentum of growth in the developing nations must be maintained, lest all past efforts be wasted. 

Continued commitment to the development effort is imperative. U.S. contributions can help make the difference between a margin of hope and utter despair. 

Our second point concerns the effectiveness of development assistance. The 

League is encouraged by two things: (1) the evidence that the international effort of the past 20 years has indeed brought improvements in the plight of

some less developed countries and (2) the growing skills with which aid projects and programs are geared to the requirements of development. 

That aid is and can be successful is evidenced in the substantial economic growth among some recipient nations where there have been large and consistent infusions of aid. The degree of success varies, of course, and in some areas the gap between aspiration and achievement is still very wide. But at least 25 developing countries showed an increase of 5 to 10 percent in their GNP in 1966. A few less developed nations have reached the take-off point-South Korea, Taiwan, and Iran, for instance. The United States has contributed greatly to the progress of these countries through both bilateral and multilateral contributions. 

The United States has learned from its relatively short experience with aid to less developed nations. Today's approaches are a far cry from the simplistic measures of the early years. As this country has gained skill in fitting its contributions to the requisites of the country being helped, development aid efforts have become increasingly effective. The United States has also learned the value of being selective and of concentrating its efforts. It has discovered that it is important to encourage less developed countries in their own efforts and to aid them in undergirding economic advancements with efforts to build social and political viability as well. It has come to acknowledge the race between food production and population increases in these countries, and thus is now seeking to help strengthen the agricultural sectors of the less developed countries and to offer, on request, aid in family planning. 

The United States has also learned the value of coordinating its efforts with those of other contributing countries, either through multilateral agencies or by means of devices (e.g., consortia and consultative groups) to coordinate the aid flowing into a particular country from various bilateral programs and international organizations. 

But despite our backlog of experience and catalog of success stories, this country still has much to learn about working with people whose cultural and institutional patterns are so different from our own. There is also much to learn about the major obstacles to development and the most fruitful approaches to overcoming these obstacles.

The stubborn problems of development in the emerging nations must be viewed as a challenge to the ingenuity of the industrial world. That development problems are not susceptible to quick and easy solutions is clearly evident. Changes in institutions and attitudes take time. Even in our own country where communications systems are excellent, educational levels are immeasurably higher, and much more money and technical and social skills are available, changes in the traditional ways of doing things take an astonishingly long time. In the less developed countries, quick results are even more unlikely. 

We now turn to our third point-the relationship between foreign aid and the balance-of-payments deficit. In recent years U.S. aid has increasingly been tailored to balance-of-payments requirements. Expenditures under the aid program for goods and services abroad declined from 27 percent in 1963 to 10 percent in 1967. Under the tied-loans policy begun in 1959, all development loans go to finance purchases in the United States; in FY 1969 over 90 percent of AID funds will be spent in the United States. 

This year balance-of-payments considerations are even more paramount. 

Cuts of another one third in 1968 in the remaining dollar expenditures overseas are required, in line with the President's New Years Day Balance-of-Payments Message. Since AID payments which affect the U.S. balance of payments totaled around $290 million in 1967, the cut will approximate $100 million. 

It seems clear that the economic aid request for FY 1969 will not adversely affect the balance of payments to any significant degree. Therefore, further cuts for balance-of-payments reasons would be unreasonable, unnecessary, and in addition dangerous.

Once the balance-of-payments crisis is past, the League hopes the United States will take steps to ease its tied-loan restrictions on development aid. 

We think this policy results in an uneconomic use of funds. The aid dollar is in effect devalued when a developing nation is not allowed to purchase goods at the lowest price it can.

In short, the League holds that in spite of setbacks along the way, in spite of the stubbornness of the problems encountered, aid development assistance deserves not only continued support but renewed committment of the United States.

STATEMENT BY THE NATIONAL COUNCIL OF JEWISH WOMEN, INC. 

The National Council of Jewish Women, an organization established in 1893, with a membership of over 100,000 in all parts of the United States, has been testifying in favor of the Foreign Assistance Act ever since its inception in 1948. This testimony has been based on two of our major interests: first, our concern with the disadvantaged everywhere, reflected in our service projects at home and our overseas project in Israel; and second, a resolution adopted in 1946, which expresses the belief that conditions for peace depend on free institutions, self-determination and social and economic progress. This resolution has been reaffirmed at each of our biennial National Conferences. But this reaffirmation is not automatic. The U.S. Foreign Aid program is reviewed and discussed at regular intervals, both in our Sections and at National meetings, in order to up-date the consensus of our membership and to reconsider matters as important as U.S. Foreign policy in the context of current national and inter national developments

It is no secret that the image of the Foreign Assistance Program has become increasingly distorted in recent years. This has been reflected in the gradual decrease of economic aid appropriations' request to Congress, this year at its lowest ebb, in Congressional response to these requests, as well as in the barrage of criticism aimed at the program by the citizenry. In view of this, we have given serious consideration, this year, to the arguments offered by the critics of the program and to the validity of this criticism. Our Organization has come to the following conclusions: 

1. Re the argument: The concept behind economic aid is no longer valid and the program is no longer morally justifiable. Our consideration was concentrated on two points: (a) Is foreign aid ethically right? (b) Is it beneficial to the donor and to the recipient? Our members, keeping in mind the change in character of the program from rehabilitation to long-term development, concluded that the basic philosophy of the richer helping the poorer remained an indisputable moral obligation, and suggested that this, perhaps, was the point which needed most reaffirmation. That Foreign Aid has been, in the past, beneficial to its recipients, is also indisputable, and is demonstrated in the dramatic results in such countries as Iran, Greece, Israel, Korea, Thailand, Venezuela and many others. That such demonstrable progress has not continued in recent years, unfortunately, is also true. It is more than coincidence, however, that the lessening of progress corresponds to the lessening of AID allocations. Economists have said that for the last six or seven years, insufficient capital and foreign currencies have been made available to the developing countries to maintain the momentum of their economic growth. "Development is suffering from financial anemia" to quote 

Mr. Leonard Rist of the World Bank. Even to a layman, logic would when you are treating successfully a long-term illness, you indicate that stop the treatment until the patient is cured or, at least, well enough to recuperate by himself.

The question of benefits to the United States has been the topic of many debates, most of them deadlocked, because of a misunderstanding of terms. 

One can say, for example, that we are making inroads in a long and difficult self-imposed assignment; that an eventual economically and socially healthy world is imperative to the well-being the United States; and that we must continue our efforts to create such an environment. On the other hand, one may take the position that the program has had enough time to prove itself; that famine, poverty and unstable governments are still rampant; that the world is ungrateful and that our image has suffered and that we should therefore curtail severally, if not completely abandon the program. What has not been convincingly stated is that the aforementioned conditions are due directly to our Foreign Aid rather than to an insufficiency of Aid. The National Council of Jewish 

Women, therefore, wishes to reaffirm the validity of the first position. 

2. Re the argument: We are involved in a war in Vietnam. We are suffering from civil-strife at home. We have enough troubles of our own. We should no longer concern ourselves with the problems of the whole world: 

In considering this statement, it occurred to us that the one positive result of our civil disorders so far, is that the urgency of the need for economic and social changes have become self-evident. Regardless of our opinions, we have all admitted that our domestic upheaval, be it violent or non-violent, is an

obvious symptom of economic, racial and social illness. In the same vein, is it not equally as obvious that wars and revolutions abroad are symptomatic of a great need for effective economic and social development? 

William S. Gaud said recently: "To thwart the drive of the new nations for progress is to invite unrest and violence, to encourage hostility towards the developed half of the world, and to jeopardize the possibilities for peace." It appears that we have reached a time in History when the same words and the same guiding principles can be applied equally as well to our domestic and to our international concerns. There is but one essential difference. Internationally, we are dealing with new, developing nations, many of which, in spite of their colonial background are not steeped in despair or prejudice any longer. They have had their revolutions, and they now have no place to go except forward. 

Our Organization has concluded that to assist them to their ultimate destination by contributing to the establishment of stable institutions and economic and political independence is an imperative of our commitment to freedom, and should be a major aspect of our Foreign policy. 

3. Re the argument: The United States cannot afford a Foreign Assistance 

Program. Look at our national debt, our balance of payments, etc. Our Organization asks in return: Can we afford not to have a Foreign Assistance Program? Is it not a false economy to save our economic resources to fight wars rather than to create a climate intended to prevent them. If we are able to achieve a domestic peace, can we then isolate ourselves from a world in turmoil? 

Will we not eventually be repaid in many ways for the 0.5% of our Gross National Product which we are investing in a few other nations? 

As to our balance of payments it has been authoritatively stated that it benefits rather than suffers from foreign aid, since nine out of ten dollars are returned to the United States for the purchase of goods and services and since we are opening up new export markets by assisting global economic development. 

In view of the above conclusions, the National Council of Jewish Women endorses the following recommendations: 

1. It wishes to commend the Agency for International Development for the continued efforts in improving the operations and administration of the Program. 

2. It considers the U.S. Economic Aid Program a necessary and valuable part of Foreign Policy, rather than an additional burden. 

3. It expresses its full support for the Foreign Assistance Act of 1968 and urge this Committee to give its favorable consideration to this bill. 

STATEMENT OF HON. T. COLEMAN ANDREWS, MAY 14, 1968 

I am T. Coleman Andrews of Richmond, Virginia.

I am an accountant by profession, and during the course of more than 35 years of practice I took leave of my practice to respond to calls to: 

1. Reorganize the Finance Dept. of the City of Richmond, Va.; 

2. Reorganize the internal auditing department of the Commonwealth of Virginia; 

3. Serve on the staff of the Fiscal Division of the War Dept. and the Contract 

Renegotiation office of the Navy Dept., immediately prior to, and during, the first year of World War II; 

4.

Organize and serve as director of the Corporations Audits Division of the 

U.S. General Accounting Office; 

5. Serve as Chairman of the Accounting and Audit Study Group of the First 

Hoover Commission; and 

6. Serve as the first Commissioner of Internal Revenue under President 

Eisenhower.

Prior to being called to the Office of Commissioner of Internal Revenue I served as President of the American Institute of Certified Public Accountants. 

I also served as an officer of the Marine Corps for 22 years during World War II-the first year in North Africa on loan to the State Department, Office of Lend Lease, at the request of the Secretary of State; the rest of the time, after training at Quantico, as a member of the General Staff of the 4th wing in the Central Pacific. 

91-392-68-pt. 2-18

Upon becoming Commissioner of Internal Revenue I withdrew from my accounting firm, and from other professional associations, in order to avoid possible conflicts of interest. Upon leaving the office of Commissioner of Internal 

Revenue I took an executive position in the insurance business and was so engaged down to the end of 1967. Since that time I have been engaged in activities designed to assist worthy men and women to get into business for themselves. 

I am here as a member of the Taxpayers Committee to End Foreign Aid, formerly the Citizens Foreign Aid Committee. 

The Citizens Foreign Aid Committee was organized in 1958. From 1959 down to date it has made approximately 150 appearances before committees of Congress in opposition to foreign aid. We have sent here to represent us a long list of distinguished citizens-outstanding business men with extensive operations throughout the world; distinguished economists and monetary experts; dedicated former military experts of long and toplevel experience in crucial overseas operations; and a host of others, including, of course, some who, like the present speaker, could not make any claim to distinction beyond trying to be good citizens.

Every concern we have expressed in our many appearances here, and every action and position we have urged, has been validated by subsequent events; instead of buying friendship, promoting peace in the world, and generating trade, as the proponents of foreign aid have claimed, our ill-advised generosity has created deep-seated animosities toward us, led us to support our enemies against our friends, financed revolutions and wars, and may very well become responsible for even more wars yet to come; and it has brought us to the brink of fiscal and monetary disaster, according to President Johnson, the President of Federal

Reserve Bank, and the Secretary of the Treasury. Secretary Rusk's recent claim that our aid is a sound investment, in that as emerging countries proposer they will enhance our export markets, is a hollow one; the fact is that confiscatory taxes and wages increased far beyond the levels justified by production are pricing us out of foreign markets.

The situation is indeed serious, gentlemen, when the Secretary of the Treasury says, as he did recently, "Our economic future, and that of the entire free world, are at stake in this hour of fiscal responsibility." 

What the good Secretary had in mind, of course, but perhaps did not feel free to say, was the sad fact that we have begun to feel the pinch of a change from a trade surplus to a trade deficit, and that this unhappy development had joined with the adverse flow of gold to compound our balance-of-payments problem; we have indeed already arrived at the point of pricing ourselves out of the world's markets.

The good Secretary perhaps also was thinking about the true state of our gold supply, which one knowledgeable expert says stands at little more than half of the amount at which his Department is reporting it, which matter we shall return to later.

What is important to say at this point is that it is not for the reason that the President gives that we are in the situation that now confronts us and that the solution for our big problem is not necessarily the President's remedy of an increase in taxes.

Even granting that foreign aid as it has been conducted has been a good thing-which we do not concede, of course-there wouldn't be any gold crisis today if the proponents of foreign aid had been just a little bit reasonable; had we given away up to now just 10% less than we have our gold supply would be $15 billion greater and there wouldn't be any monetary problem. 

Yes, that's right; we have spent $150 billion¹ on foreign aid, give or take a few billion; 10% of that amount is $15 billion; and $15 billion more gold in the till than we have would enable us to thumb our noses at Mr. DeGaulle or any other "I-am-the-law" who might aspire to take over and rule the world from his Mt. Olympus. 

1 Congressman Otto E. Passman, chairman, House Foreign Operations Subcommittee, states that including interest on the money we have borrowed to give away, foreign aid has cost taxpayers $171 billion.

As to the President's proposal of additional taxes as the way out of our present predicament, it is necessary only to point out that there is no need for any further authorization for foreign aid; our information is that approximately $15 billion is still available from prior years' authorizations. 

Hence, to grant the $11 billion of additional authorizations that the administration has requested would make a total of $26 billion could be made available. 

We find it difficult to believe that Congress would give serious consideration to increasing taxes by $10 billion to finance such an unnecessary expenditure as this. 

We are constrained to say, therefore, that for Congress to accede to the administration's request for the additional authorization of $11 billion, and to vote for a tax increase of $10 billion, without taking a second and very penetrating look at the whole foreign-aid and balance-of-payments situation, would be utter folly and an act of unpardonable irresponsibility. 

It is one thing to be generous when one has the means with which to be so; it is quite another to give away money raised or held in trust for others, or what one does not have or cannot afford. 

We cannot afford the luxury of catering to those whose ideological leanings outweigh their respect for the rules of prudence and trust and the need for recognition of the ageless principle that self-preservation is the first law of nature.

The ultimate end of carrying the power to tax that far is disaster-total and complete disaster-economic, political and social. 

We hope, therefore, that Congress will do some soul-searching before approving further authorizations or appropriations for foreign aid to carry on the bridge-building programs that provide our enemies with the means of killing and maiming our sons and grandsons, pre-empting our overseas markets, and destroying with intolerable taxes our will to achieve and make a greater and greater America.

Twenty years ago our gold reserve stood at $24 billion; the Treasury Department says now it stands at $10.4 billion, a figure which, though suspect, let us accept for the moment.

In the meantime, we have abolished gold-and silver-as completely as the Supreme Court has abolished God, all in the name of restoring confidence in the dollar.

But have we restored confidence in the dollar? It does not seem so; we apparently have succeeded only in relieving the pressure for a while; at any rate, the drain continues.

One of the most knowledgeable men in Britain, a former staunch supporter of Britain's Prime Minister has broken with the Prime Minister and published to the world that Britain's fiscal and monetary situation is twice as bad as the Prime Minister has admitted it is, implying clearly that he thinks the Prime Minister has been lying to the people of Britain and the world and that he believes another devaluation of the pound to be inevitable. 

Here at home one of the country's very distinguished bankers says that the actual gold available in the United States is somewhere between $5.5 billion and $6.5 billion, not $10.4 billion as the Treausry Department reports; and he points out that claims in the hands of foreign holders against the approximately $6 billion aggregate about $35 billion and that if only about one-sixth of these claims are actually presented we will be completely out of gold, since, having removed the gold cover on Federal Reserve notes and bank deposits with the Federal Reserve our money now has no gold support at all. 

And says this banker, it is his understanding that a part of the little gold reserve remaining is of questionable redeemability, since it was melted down from gold coins taken from American citizens during 1933-1934 and these coins contained some alloy that might make the bullion derived from melting them down unacceptable in settling International balances. 

It is not surprising, therefore, that experts say that in spite of the Treasury Department's eloquence in speaking of the new "paper gold", this so-called gold is based upon phoney bookkeeping and at best will do no more than stave off the inevitable for a while.

Heaven only knows, therefore, what will happen if there is another devaluation of the pound.

To make matters worse, a distinguished member of the Senate tells us, in a report to his constituents and other interested citizens, some things about the reporting of trade balances that makes the bookkeeping employed by the Department of Commerce in arriving at these balances as phoney as the "paper gold”. 

It is said that "figures don't lie, but liars figure". Says the Senator, the Department of Commerce has managed to convert an adverse trade balance of $1.8 billion to a favorable balance of $3.8 billion by the process of under-valuing imports and including in exports food given away under governmentally subsidized programs for which there is no payment to us, of course. 

In former years when our taxes and wages and other production costs were at levels that enabled us to compete successfully in world markets, we who opposed foreign aid derived some comfort from the fact that what might have been an early disasterous consequence of such aid was largely offset by favorable annual trade balances.

Now, that cushion having disappeared, the day when our misguided generosity will deliver the final coup de grace may be nearer than any of us think. 

Thus, gentlemen, our problem is not only an economic and monetary one but, as in so many other things these days, it also is one of credibility; and confidence in the dollar, at home and abroad, continues to fade. 

Do you not think, therefore, that regardless of any other consideration Congress should stop and ascertain the extent of the credibility gap as to the amount of our available gold and the status of our trade balance before accelerating further our mad dash to economic and monetary disaster by continuing to bet on a losing horse and imposing additional taxes upon the American people to pay for it?

I remind you, gentlemen, that while succeeding administrations have initiated the programs and practices that have produced the present precarious state of the nation's affairs-non-defense expenditures are 65% above those of the previous administration and there are 500,000 more people on the government's civilian payroll-yes, while succeeding administrations have laid out the course that has brought our country to its present sad and humiliating plight, it could not have happened without Congressional acquiescence. 

Will you firmly resolve to get at the truth of the situation and set things straight, and call a halt and let the administration live on its accumulated foreign-aid fat until this is accomplished; or will you let the United Nations, through its Conference on Trade and Development, continue to nudge us into ever greater and greater giveaways until our "financial resource transfers", as that group calls them, are quadrupled, which is in effect what the group recently proposed at the conclusion of its nine-week meeting at New Delhi, India? 

We emerged from World War II the most powerful nation the world has ever known and with unparalleled acceptance and confidence in our leadership by other nations.

We have lost that power and acceptance because those in control of our political affairs have allowed themselves to be blackmailed, in the name of "new economics" and "new politics", into following fiscal and monetary policies that have indicated that we either do not understand the rules of good husbandry, prudent management and successful government, or that we don't have the character to apply these rules; and the most flagrant evidence of these policies has been our stubborn practice of spending far beyond our income. 

Our country is full able to finance every reasonable need of its people within the framework of a balanced budget.

We are convinced, therefore, that the budget can be balanced whenever Congress develops the will to balance it and throws out all the vote bait, and all the concessions to that rule-or-ruin cabal that spawned the "new economics" and "new politics" and that will not be satisfied, no matter how much is done to meet their demands, until the country is on its knees and begging for admission to a world government dominated by her enemies.

Since the development of the recent gold crisis spokesmen of foreign governments have commented, one after another, upon our mismanagement of our fiscal affairs in terms that have indicated clearly that if we would only balance our budget we might restore a high degree of confidence that we take seriously our obligation to keep the dollar on a sound basis. 

There is amazing irony, therefore, in Treasury Secretary Fowler's statement that our economic future and that of the entire free world as well are at stake in our present situation.

Surely to live within our income is not too great a price to pay to reassure the people of our own country and regain the confidence of the rest of the world in our economic good sense and of our worthiness of the responsibility for 

International leadership that has been thrust upon us.

The responsible governments of the world, therefore, expect us to put our financial house in order, and we believe they would not be surprised or disappointed-indeed, we suspect that they would be highly pleased-if we began doing so by applying the axe to the $11 billion of unneeded further authorizations for foreign aid proposed in the budget now under consideration. 

But time is running out on us, gentlemen; in fact, the time is now; the future of our country is being decided today; and the responsibility for deciding it is yours.

Our fate as a nation, and as a people, therefore, is in your hands; it depends upon your decisions. 

May God grant that these decisions will be the right ones.

STATEMENT OF DR. N. R. DANIELIAN, PRESIDENT, INTERNATIONAL ECONOMIC POLICY ASSOCIATION 

The International Economic Policy Association has been presenting testimony on foreign assistance before the Senate Committee on Foreign Relations ever since 1958. It is natural, therefore, to take a backward look and assess the usefulness of this 10-year effort. The very magnitude of this program (see Table 1) deserves thoughtful reconsideration.

We have supported the principle of foreign aid all through these years with a number of recommendations for change of emphasis and improvements. The major elements in our recommendations have been concerned with the following areas: The impact of foreign aid on the balance of payments; the necessity of emphasizing self-help on the part of recipients; the importance of private investment in economic development; the effect of foreign aid on the U.S. economy; and the importance of international sharing of the burden of foreign aid.

White House message on foreign assistance programs, Feb. 8, 1968.

BALANCE OF PAYMENTS Testifying before the Senate Committee on Foreign Relations on March 31, 

1960, we commented:

As to the effect of the economic-aid program upon our domestic economy, there is now much doubt that the present machinery for the transfer of credits and purchasing power to foreign accounts can be continued indefinitely without a sharp and serious reaction upon the U.S. financial position and stability of the dollar, in view of the fact that our current accounts in goods and services exports are inadequate to cover the private and public capital transfers that we are making internationally.¹ 

Following this diagnosis, we have made a number of specific recommendations to the Congress and to the Administration to reduce the impact of foreign aid on the balance of payments. We are pleased that many of these suggestions have been accepted, but some of them have not been implemented. 

We recommended "Buy American" at a time when it was not popular, and in 1961 the Congress in its wisdom amended the Administration's proposed bill by requiring in Section 604 that procurement abroad be allowed only if it did not adversely affect the U.S. balance of payments. The implementation of this principle has reduced the direct impact of bilateral foreign aid upon the balance of payments by $710 million in 7 years, from $1 billion in FY 1961 to $290 million in FY 1967. 

Another factor, however, has crept into the situation because of increased emphasis on so-called program loans, which in effect are nothing more than balance of payments and budgetary loans to other governments on which they can issue import licenses for products bought in the United States. This new factor is the substitution of aid-financed exports for what would otherwise be cash or commercial exports. The effect of this on the U.S. balance of payments is not measurable, but is substantial. We have opposed this means of giving foreign aid and have advocated a change in administration which would put the emphasis on project loans of an incremental nature; that is, financing projects which would not otherwise be undertaken without foreign aid. 

There are those who claim that import assistance is the best means of helping aid-receiving countries. We feel that in this manner of administration aid loans and grants are like long-term consumer credit, used up more often in increased current consumption—often subsidizing unproductive employment—than in economic development. It also leads to increased U.S. balance of payments deficits because cash resources put at the disposal of governments for import as sistance tend toward substitution of aid credit sales for U.S. commercial sales, A delay in the implementation of the "Buy American" policies in connection with our contributions to international agencies has of course been deleterious to our balance of payments. Only last year the Inter-American Development 

Bank and this year the International Development Association have faced up to this issue and made commitments to abide by some restrictions on the use of dollars. The World Bank has not.

As a consequence, both the Treasury Department and the Commerce Department agree that in 1966 the direct balance of payments impact of all bilateral and multilateral aid was still in the range of $700-$750 million. There is no estimate of the deleterious effect of the substitution of aid for commercial business. 

In short, in this area considerable progress has been made, but much needs to be done to put the aid program on the basis of transfer of real resources from the United States to aid-receiving countries for incremental projects over and above the dollar resources which these countries may earn by other means. We doubt that this can be achieved under the present administrative structure with its growing emphasis on import assistance loans and grants. 

SELF-HELP CRITERIA 

We have always felt that foreign aid is only an incremental contribution of capital to the resources of aid-receiving countries, and that their own efforts in self-improvement are the determining factors in success or failure. We have, therefore, cautioned against assumption of responsibility by the United States in 

1 Hearings on the Mutual Security Act of 1960, p. 563. 

2 There are situations, of course, such as South Vietnam, where this approach would be inapplicable.

public opinion here and abroad for the economic health and progress of other countries. In this area not so much capital, but guidance and technical help are of much greater value. 

Here again most progress has been made since 1961 and the principle of selfhelp has been accepted as a vital ingredient of economic assistance. The emphasis on housing, on agricultural development, on tax reforms, the establishment of building and loan associations and other financial institutions have all been initiatives in the right direction. We have recommended that, in addition to these factors, fair and national treatment of private investments must be considered as elements of self-help, because in this area lies the promise of agricultural and industrial development. The Senate Committee on Foreign Relations and the 

House Committee on Foreign Affairs at one time or another have incorporated recommendations along these lines, but not in the same year, and they have never survived a conference committee agreement. We still believe that specific provisions should be included in the Foreign Assistance Act as follows: 

Delete the proposed Section 208 (b) and replace it with the following language: (b) the extent to which the government of the country is creating a favorable climate for private enterprise and investment, both domestic and foreign, as a necessary element in economic development by: 

1. providing for maximum private ownership and initiative in new as well as existing industry, including manufacturing, financing, distribution, and merchandising; 

2. assuring nondiscriminatory treatment between national and nonnational, and between public and private, enterprises and products; 

3. providing for unrestricted remittance of amortization of investment, interest and dividend payments; 

4. providing adequate protection for industrial property rights; 

5. enacting tax laws with rates of taxation designed to promote optimum utilization of private investment capital, both foreign and domestic. 

Countries which have adopted such measures have demonstrated how effective they can be in increasing the inflow of foreign private investment. When Brazil took such steps just a few years ago, the results were immediate. Other countries, such as Taiwan and Thailand, have achieved similar results. 

EMPHASIS ON PRIVATE INVESTMENT 

It is well known to the Committee that we have unreservedly advocated emphasis on private investment and private economic activity in the development of less developed countries. The reason for this has been wholly pragmatic, to achieve both the economic and the long range political objectives of the aid program. The economic objectives, of course, are rapid development of productive capability; the political objective, the evolution of less developed countries in the direction of national independence, individual freedom, and representative forms of government. 

Investment in the private economic field has the following advantages: It is capable of mobilizing capital if the enticements are sufficient, without resorting to the taxing power of the U.S. Government; it can mobilize the best technical and managerial talent in meeting the material needs of the people; it is a training ground for the able and ambitious elements and gives them both the opportunity and incentive to devote their energies to solving production problems in their own countries. Private development, in short, is the most economical means of utilizing both capital and manpower, and of training a generation of future managerial leaders.

In political terms, private investment and economic activity is the opposite of authoritarianism based upon state control of the means of production. Private economic activity generates the middle class, historically the foundation of stable, representative government, and the protector of personal liberties. The development of such a middle class should be the main objective of our economic programs in less developed countries and this can best be achieved by encouraging a multiplicity of privately owned economic enterprises in those countries. 

Again, the Congress and the Administration have made considerable progress in this direction. The investment guarantee program which had a ceiling of $1.3 billion in 1962 has been expanded to $8 billion. 

Guarantee agreements have been signed with most of the major aid-receiving countries and the program has been expanded to cover housing and situations where conditions of political instability require special risk coverage; this is, the

extended risk coverage. The aid agency has also instructed its country missions to engage private contractors in the implementation of aid programs. 

The Congress went a step further in 1963 by amending Sections 202 and 252 requiring that 50 percent of Development Loans and Alliance for Progress loans be used for economic development through private enterprise. Although a certain percentage of funds have been channeled under this provision to industrial development corporations and investment banks, it is far from satisfying the statutory requirement. (See Table 2.) There is a tendency on the part of the administrators of the program to consider the import assistance loans and even government-sponsored public works as satisfying this requirement. This is misreading of the intent of those who proposed it and of those who secured the passage of this legislation in Congress and, therefore, the statute needs further refinement.

It is recommended, therefore, that the authorizations for Sections 202 and 252 for Development Loans and the Alliance for Progress should each be split into two separate funds, one-half to be made available to AID for lending to governments for suitable public projects; the other half should be made available to a separate Private Investment Fund to be set up for this purpose in AID. Provision should be made that the Private Investment Fund could use its funds only for the purpose of lending dollars to national or regional development banks or institutions to be used by them in turn for the sole purpose of meeting the dollar import requirements of private business enterprises which they finance in their countries or regions. It should be made perfectly clear that such loans can be made on the condition that the development institution would undertake to assist business enterprises on a nondiscriminatory national basis. 

THE EFFECT ON THE U.S. ECONOMY 

The ostensible and justifiable purpose of foreign aid as well as private investment in the less developed countries is their economic development and the improvement of per capita income, hopefully leading to political stability and cooperation within the family of independent nations. If successful, this should lead to greater international commerce which would benefit the United States as well as other countries.

Whether such a result will ensue depends upon the conditions under which economic development and international commerce are organized. It has become obvious that the United States cannot sustain a one-way flow of capital and resources, whether for military purposes or for foreign aid, without adequate earnings abroad to cover such transfers. (See Table 3.) These earnings must come from the sale of goods and services or from the income on investment. 

There is no doubt that in a free international economy the United States, through investment income and exports, could earn sufficient income abroad to redeploy in foreign aid, capital exports, and other objectives.
