Unfortunately, however, the rest of the world does not always offer easy access either to U.S. investments or to U.S. exports. Most aid-receiving countries, for example, have a policy of using financial resources obtained abroad in the production of import substitution and export promotion industries. This means in simple language that they would like to use U.S. foreign aid resources to establish industries in order to displace imports from the United States and other countries. It means also that they wish to establish industries which would increase exports to the United States. 

The creation of common markets would make this policy even more injurious to the U.S. international economic competitive position if the same principle of import substitution and export promotion were applied on a trade bloc or continental basis as, for instance, in the emerging Latin American Common Market. 

If this attitude of aid-receiving countries is even further encouraged by unilateral trade concessions without reciprocity, then, of course, our position in the future would become even more untenable. To make it worse, if economic development is undertaken in those areas with limitations or exclusion of U.S. private investments, then our earning power in those countries would be even less. 

In short, foreign aid and investment policy in less developed countries can be sustained only within the framework of a consistent and clearly defined industrial development and trade policy. 

This means that the industrial development plans of the less developed countries, either singly or in trade blocs, must be aimed primarily at the development of the local market in the improvement of the internal consumption levels of the most necessary products in food, clothing, housing, and other consumer items. 

The export effort should be devoted to those areas in which the country has some comparative advantage besides low wages, and trade relations between the United States and the aid-receiving countries, singly or in blocs, must be on a reciprocal basis. It is only within this framework that we can be sure that our capital resources are being most efficiently employed and that our staying power to pursue this task of economic development abroad is of a long duration. 

It is obvious that there is much new conceptual initiative needed in this area by our own policy makers as well as those of aid-receiving countries. The inhibitions against imports from the United States and against U.S. investments in effect in many countries will surely limit or diminish the capacity of the United States to render aid unless we develop a viable theory of investment, aid and trade.

INTERNATIONAL SHARING OF THE BURDEN OF FOREIGN AID 

It is now realized that our ability to spend large sums of money abroad for government programs is limited by the capacity of the private economy to earn convertible foreign exchange in foreign commerce and investment. Essentially, when we give a billion dollars to Latin American countries, what we are doing is returning to those countries the income we receive from them in investments and services. In many situations, such as India where our aid is of equal dimensions as in all of Latin America, we do not have earning power to compensate for this return flow of capital. There we must make a net contribution from earnings in other parts of the world. We have discovered now that our total earnings abroad are not sufficient to sustain this redeployment of resources. There is serious doubt that the private economy can earn the necessary surplus on investment and exports to sustain the scale of operations commanded by the 

Executive Departments. 

If these programs are to continue, a program of sharing of this burden among the developing countries must be originated and agreed upon. This is of such overriding importance that it cannot be accomplished at any level lower than the Heads of States. All efforts at the lower levels through the OECD have failed to produce an agreement. Other countries have preferred short or medium term project or exporter credit loans at relatively high interest rates. (See Table 4.) 

Their repayment has been facilitated by the easier terms of U.S. aid grants and loans. This cannot continue.

Pending a voluntary high level agreement among the developing nations, what the United States can do unilaterally is to shorten the term of its own foreign aid loans. This will force the aid-receiving countries to be more discriminating in the use of foreign exchange resources and it may force them to reschedule the debts owed to other developing countries. To the extent that this is done, it will have the same effect as a net addition to foreign exchange resources of the debtor countries. This may be one way of forcing other developing countries to realize that they cannot indefinitely ride on U.S. generosity in making 40-year loans on soft terms, and force them to face up to the fact that this responsibility is one that can be carried only on a shared basis. It is for this reason that we recommend a 20-year repayment period for development loans. 

We further suggest that serious consideration be given to reorganization of aid agencies on an international level, where foreign aid budgetary requests are developed and presented to an international agency, possibly under OECD auspices, with claimant countries and donor countries sitting together evaluating needs and allocating responsibilities on comparable conditions. 

LIMITS OF POWER 

An observation of the results obtained in the 8 years of the Decade of Development must lead one to a sense of humility as to the capacity of the United States singly to solve the economic and security problems of the whole non-Communist world. In the area of development the limits are not only those imposed by shortage of capital but also of human resources and the lead time required in political evolution and public education. 

The United States Congress inescapably must choose priorities in public expenditures at a time of financial crisis. If the same scale of reductions in foreign aid should result from current deliberations as in 1967, then it is to be hoped that the priorities would be established on the basis of strategic importance of areas to U.S. security; the ability of the recipients to absorb and effectively utilize foreign aid; the readiness of the governments to cooperate on an agreed set of principles in self-help. In such a reassessment and allocation of resources we hope that Latin America will emerge as a deserving claimant for priority consideration.

Where balance of payments considerations lead to reduction of dollar aid, substitution of increased aid under P.L. 480 can relieve situations of dire human suffering.

Above all, emphasis on education and technical training, agricultural production and fair and national treatment of foreign investments must be cardinal principles of self-help.
